# Bezel: Design Rationale

**Companion to the Bezel Language Specification, v0.357 — November 2026**

The specification says what Bezel *is*; this document says *why*. It covers the places where two reasonable designs were possible and one was picked, where a familiar-looking rule rests on a less obvious one, and where something common elsewhere was deliberately left out. Each decision is recorded with the alternative it declined, what that alternative would have bought, and why it lost, so that decisions are revisited deliberately rather than relitigated from memory. Read it when a rule surprises you; each section stands alone.

This document is not exhaustive. Choices that simply follow settled convention (`:=` for assignment, word keywords for boolean operators, non-virtual by default, `case` for pattern matching) are not discussed; the precedent is the rationale.

<a id="sec-the-big-picture"></a>
## 1. The big picture

Bezel is a compiled language for services and tools, designed around three commitments.

- **Predictable performance.** Native code, no garbage collector, and costs that follow from what the source says: an object is released at a point the reader can find, and nothing runs in the background to reclaim it.
- **Memory safety the compiler checks.** A resource-holding object has a single owner responsible for disposing it, ownership moves are visible in the source, and the compiler's ownership tracking guarantees disposal exactly once. Leaks, double frees and use-after-free are mostly rejected at compile time; most of the remainder faults deterministically at run time in checked disposal mode; and the few admissions the model can back with neither are recorded individually in the spec rather than hidden ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows) of the spec). The guarantees extend to concurrent code through execution domains, which share no mutable state ([§12](#sec-async)).
- **Few surprises.** What a program does should not depend on rules the reader cannot see. Types are non-nullable unless written with `?` ([§6](#sec-nullability)), operators do not silently accept a missing value ([§6.4](#sec-operators-do-not-lift)), integer overflow is an exception rather than a wrap, no user-defined conversion runs implicitly ([§14.5](#sec-no-implicit-conversion-operators)), and name lookup never backtracks into a different meaning ([§2.4](#sec-name-resolution-position-not-meaning)).

The ownership model bears some similarity to Rust's — single ownership, explicit transfers, borrowed references checked against their owners — and the resemblance is deliberate: compile-time ownership is how Bezel stays memory-safe without a collector. Where Bezel differs is in how much its checker demands. There are no lifetime annotations to write, and no exclusive-writer rule: code that shares and mutates freely inside one execution domain compiles as written, because freedom from data races comes from the concurrency model — serial domains, with transfer or immutable sharing at their boundaries — rather than from aliasing restrictions. The price is stated as plainly as the bet: a small class of mistakes a stricter checker would reject at compile time surfaces in Bezel at run time — deterministically, as `DisposedAccessException`, under checked disposal mode; as undefined behavior in an unchecked release build (spec §28.2). In return, the ownership rules stay few enough to learn in full and cheap enough to apply everywhere.

Most of this document is the same kind of exchange made again at smaller scale: what a stricter or more familiar rule would buy, what it would cost, and why the balance landed where it did. The memory model carries the largest share of it (§4, §5), followed by nullability, equality, generics and concurrency (§6, §7, §10, §12). Where Bezel looks familiar, it usually is; the chapters that follow are mostly about the places where it departs from familiar practice, and the reasons each departure seemed worth making.

<a id="sec-modules-and-files"></a>
## 2. Modules and files

<a id="sec-dotted-module-names-are-one-name"></a>
### 2.1 Dotted module names are one name

`module Foo.Bar.Baz;` declares one module. The dots are punctuation in the name. `Foo.Bar` and `Foo.Bar.Baz` are unrelated modules, with no implicit visibility between them.

The alternative — nested names that relate to each other, as C# namespaces do — promises more than it delivers. In C#, only name lookup follows the hierarchy: code inside `Foo.Bar.Baz` can name types from `Foo.Bar` and `Foo` without qualification, because the compiler searches each enclosing namespace in turn. Nothing else does. `internal` scopes to the whole assembly and ignores namespaces entirely, and `using Foo;` imports the types declared in `Foo` but not those in `Foo.Bar`. The lookup rule invites the reader to treat the dots as a containment hierarchy, and the access and import rules then contradict that reading. Bezel removes the invitation: the dots are spelling, and the obvious reading — no relationship — is the correct one.

The cost: no sub-package privacy. Use `internal` and put related code in the same module.

<a id="sec-no-top-level-functions-or-fields"></a>
### 2.2 No top-level functions or fields

Every method, field, and constant lives inside a class, record, or enum. Classes already group code and own static state; a parallel module-level grouping mechanism doubles the surface area without solving anything. A `public static class` of static methods is the entry point for what other languages call free functions.

Extension blocks are the one exception. They live at module level because their identity is the type they extend, not a surrounding class.

<a id="sec-auto-imported-extension-modules"></a>
### 2.3 A type's standard surface follows the type

Spec §2.3 auto-imports the standard library's extension modules over always-available types, which today names one module, `System.Collections.Linq`. For those modules it sets aside §26.5 rule 2, which scopes extension-member candidacy to imported modules, because applied to the standard library that rule cuts against two others: §2.3 makes the standard library's core type names visible in every file with no `import`, and §2.8.3 rule 2 lets a program *call* the public members of a value whose module it never imported. Under the gate a program could hold an `IEnumerable<T>` and call its instance members with no import at all, while `xs.Where(p)` required `import System.Collections.Linq;`, a module naming neither the receiver's type nor the receiver's module.

**What decided it was not the design argument but three facts about the rule's standing.** It had never been argued: the citation the standard library carried dated the *sentence* that stated it, not the rule, and the changelog entry it pointed at is a contract sweep containing nothing about extensions or imports. The compiler had never enforced it for the standard library — §5's operators are builtins matched by name, the import set is not consulted, and forty-three test files called `.Where(` with no import and passed the real compile-link-run path. And the specification's own examples did not follow it: six fenced examples called §5 operators with no import — in §14.5, §21.10, §27.4 and §27.6 — alongside two guides and a conformance case recorded as compiling.

So the question was never whether to relax a rule; it was which of two things the project already did should be written down. That reframing is what disqualified the option that looks conservative — keeping the rule and correcting the documents would have meant *enforcing* an unargued restriction at a cost of thirty-four tests, nine document sites and new compiler work, for a benefit no record states. Ratifying practice made nine sites correct without editing them.

**The gate is kept where it earns its keep.** An extension injects member names onto a type its author did not write, and §26.5 rule 4's ambiguity remedy — *remove or narrow a conflicting import* — is the language's only tool for choosing between two such blocks. That hazard is real for third-party modules and absent for the standard library, which is singular, compiler-known, and cannot collide with itself. Every comparable language draws the line in the same place after shipping the opposite first: C# made its `using System.Linq` implicit in the default template a decade on; Rust puts `Iterator` in the prelude while requiring `use` for other traits; Kotlin auto-imports its collections.

**The cost, which is not hypothetical and is not solved here.** §2.3 has no opt-out, and Bezel's extension blocks are nameless — so unlike C#, where a colliding extension can still be called through its static class, a user block declaring a member named `Where` over `IEnumerable<T>` has no qualified spelling and no import to remove. Renaming is the only remedy. This was accepted rather than answered, on three bounds: the escape did not work before either, since the gate was unenforced; the failure is an ambiguity diagnostic naming every origin, not a silent wrong answer; and the affected population was believed to be zero — which the landing gate falsified, finding the collision in §26.5.1's own worked example, where two blocks spelling `First()` met a §5 block that declares the same name. The example was renamed, and §26.5.1 states what rule 4's remedy list does not reach: against an auto-imported standard-library candidate, *narrow an import* is unavailable, leaving renaming. The cost is therefore instantiated, not hypothetical. Two fixes are recorded and deliberately not taken — a provenance tiebreak ranking a user block above a compiler-known one, which is not the auto-ranked *specificity* §17.10 deleted, or an import-suppression form on §2.3 — because neither is designable without knowing which of them a real collision wants.

**Extended to the collection module itself (proposal 0122 L3).** The same three facts decided `System.Collections.Generic`. Unenforced: the compiler had never gated the module (its name resolver says so in a comment beside `KeyNotFoundException`). Unfollowed: 84 `.bzl` test files used a §4 collection type and 4 carried the import; 216 embedded programs and the specification's own fences — ten uses against two imports — did the same; no guide carried the line. Unargued: no decline record, and the two proposals that restated the rule did not argue for it. The row-by-row hoisting of §2.3 — `Array<T>`, `OwningArray<T>` and fourteen interfaces always available while their module was "not auto-imported" — had also produced a live contradiction (spec §14.2's sorting row against §2.3's own invariant). The cost is stated at full strength rather than as "one more module": twenty-nine names enter the bare-name space of every file — `Dictionary`, `Set`, `Stack`, `Queue`, `Span`, `BitReader`, `BitWriter`, `LinkedListNode`, `Counter`, `FrozenArray`, `KeyNotFoundException` and their kin, every one listed in §2.3's row — with no opt-out, so a future user module exporting one of them makes the bare name ambiguous wherever both are imported (§2.2's collision rule, which compares names together with their generic arity, so a non-generic `Counter` does not collide with `Counter<K>`; a current-module declaration of the same name and arity shadows it). That is the ground on which the decision could have gone the other way, and it was taken at that price. The extension-module invariant above is untouched and still names one module.

<a id="sec-name-resolution-position-not-meaning"></a>
### 2.4 Name resolution: position, not meaning

**Member vs type at a chain head (#2097/#2125).** When a bare name inside a type's body could denote either an accessible enclosing-type member or a same-named type, Bezel resolves by the name's *syntactic position*, not by trying each meaning to see which one the rest of the chain accepts. A chain head (`Color.Red`) prefers the type, so the `property Color: Color` self-naming idiom keeps working across modules; every other head — a value read, an assignment target, a bare callee, a `?.`/`!`-continued head — prefers the member. The declined alternative is C#'s Color-Color rule, where the head's meaning depends on whether the member reading can host the next segment — that is backtracking on semantic failure, which §2.7's "leftmost-first, type-wins, no backtracking" design refuses (adding an import can un-error a chain but must never silently re-mean one). The positional split buys the idiom without the backtracking; a warning covers the one residual case where a chain head resolves to a *different*-typed shadowing type — or to an import alias, which outranks the member there as a type does ([#7343](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7343)) — so the safety is delivered at zero cost to idiomatic code (spec §2.7).

([#7121](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7121), [#7085](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7085); proposal [0202](proposals/0202-type-position-qualified-head-is-not-a-chain-head.md)) **A nested type ranks as a member at an expression chain head, and a type position's qualified name has no chain head** (spec [§2.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-name-resolution-order), the table's two rows and the chain-head paragraph; [§11.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-nested-types)). The chain-head demotion exists for one reason, the `property Color: Color` idiom, where a member and a type share a name at a head that could mean either; that is an expression's question. A nested type stands at that head as any member does, last, so a same-named import or module declaration wins there, loudly, under the warning row, and the enclosing type's name — with its arguments where it is generic, `Outer<int>.C.M()` — reaches it, as [§22.12](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-static-members) reaches a static. In a type position only types are candidates (proposal [0189](proposals/0189-a-type-position-names-a-type.md)): a bare `C` finds the nested type innermost, and the head of `C.N` finds the same `C`, since reading it as a chain head would make `C` and `C.N` in one annotation name two types and would leave a nested type of a generic enclosing type with no spelling at all once an import takes its name — the hole #7121 measured. Declined: suppressing the warning where no spelling reaches the nested type (a warning whose main message, that the type wins, stands whether or not a remedy exists); type arguments on an enclosing segment in a type position ([§9.10](#sec-method-local-type-declarations)'s deferral stands: a top-level declaration still meets the need); and the status quo, a type position's qualified head ranked as a chain head.

<a id="sec-syntax"></a>
## 3. Syntax

Most syntax follows Pascal/Modula-2 conventions; the precedent is the rationale. The choices that needed more than precedent are recorded below.

<a id="sec-always-semicolons"></a>
### 3.1 Always semicolons

Pascal famously had places where a semicolon was wrong — typically before `else` — and places where it was required, with the rule depending on what came next. The Ada designers fixed this by making semicolons unconditional: every statement ends with `;`, every block ends with `end;`, no exceptions.

Bezel follows the Ada rule. The rule is "always put a semicolon," which is impossible to get wrong by accident. The cost is occasional visual noise; the benefit is one fewer thing to remember.

**A third category: the contract clauses (proposal 0055).** A `requires`/`ensures` clause carries no terminator — not because a semicolon would be wrong there in the Pascal sense, but because the clause's own parentheses are structural and already delimit it (spec §22.16), so there is nothing for a terminator to do. The rule above is therefore about statements and blocks, which is where it was always aimed: every statement ends with `;`, every block ends with `end;`. A self-delimiting *clause* is neither. The `invariant` member is the check on the reading — it is a member, so it keeps the member-terminating `;` exactly as the rule demands. *(Whether the clause region should also have carried a visible closing cue is the question proposal 0100 examined and closed: the answer is a recommended blank line between the last clause and the first body statement — style, not grammar.)*

<a id="sec-the-discard-_"></a>
### 3.2 The discard `_`

`_` carries C#'s discard meanings — wildcard in patterns, discard for `out` arguments, and the expression-statement form `_ := expression;` (C# spells it `_ = expression;`), which evaluates `expression` and throws the result away. What Bezel changes is the weight the form carries, and its reach: the bare token is reserved (spec §1.3, §17.13), never a declarable or readable name in any position.

In C#, discarding a result is the default — an expression statement drops its value silently, so `_ = ...` is optional documentation of intent (most visibly for a deliberately-unawaited task). Bezel warns on every expression statement that discards a non-`()` result (spec §21.9), so the discard is the sanctioned opt-out: without it, a programmer who means to drop a value must either declare a throwaway local (which trips the unused-variable warning) or live with the discarded-result warning (which trains them to ignore warnings). The discard keeps the deliberate case syntactically distinct from the accidental one.

Ownership gives the same form a second job: applied to a named owned binding, `_ := x;` is a marker-free consume — the explicit "dispose this now" spelling, standing beside `throw x` and `owned` returns in the marker-free family ([§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model); spec §28.3). And one result type stays out of the discard's reach entirely: dropping a `Task` is a hard error, not a warning, because abandoning a running operation is a semantic decision — that case is routed to `Task.FireAndForget`, and the anonymous `using var _ := task;` binding, which nothing can await, is rejected with it.

**Why `_` is reserved (proposal 0083).** The alternative is approximately C#'s contextual rule, which C# keeps for backward compatibility Bezel does not have: `_` a discard in three positions and an ordinary identifier everywhere else. Bezel had that rule before reserving the token, and the overlap produced corners no one designed, each individually ruled and jointly indefensible: a declared `var _` was readable but never re-assignable (the write-once corollary, forced by the discard statement's lexical shape); `_ := e;` and `var _ := e;` were different constructs with different disposal timing, distinguished by lexical shape alone; and `ref _` needed a dedicated owner ruling (#2679 part B) just to decide *which* thing it was. Reserving the token deletes the corner class outright: there is one meaning everywhere, and every future position answers itself — a discard is valid exactly where receiving-and-ignoring is coherent, an error everywhere else. The corpus made the change free (zero reads of any `_` binding existed anywhere; the only rewrites were bind-and-ignore test sites with a mechanical spelling). Kept, honestly redefined: `using var _` as the anonymous owning binding — a real, corpus-proven idiom that binds nothing readable. Sanctioned as true ignores: lambda parameters and `for var _` (under the contextual rule these were bindings *named* `_`, with `lambda(_, _)` a duplicate-name error). Rejected rather than made ignores: method/constructor parameters named `_` — parameter names are call-site-addressable API surface (spec §22.5) and carried by the artifact interface, so an unnameable slot would be a hole in that surface; lambdas have neither concern. One rule closes the family: a bare ignore takes its type from context, never an annotation. The #2679 `ref _` ruling is completed, not reversed: it said `_` after `ref` is not a discard; reservation agrees and removes the identifier fallback too.

`_` is a sink, not a binding. It cannot be read.

<a id="sec-the-value-position-conditional"></a>
### 3.3 The value-position conditional is condition-first and paren-delimited (`(if c then a else b)`)

The expression form of selection is the parenthesized, condition-first atom — `(if c then a elif c2 then b else fallback)` — the same word order as the statement form, with the atom's parentheses as its delimiters and no `end` (spec §20; proposal 0057). It replaced Python's postfix conditional (`value if condition else fallback`), and the replacement is a genuine reversal, recorded as one: the postfix form's deliberate word-order split had been re-examined and confirmed in 2026-07 (issue #1373) on the strength of the value-first reading for the default-with-override idiom, and a later re-weighing — by the same decider — overturned that preference: one selection word order across the language retires the asymmetry's erratum class (#748); first-class `elif` replaces the postfix form's right-nested chaining; the branches of a delimited atom derive full expressions, dissolving the postfix form's true-branch `throw` parse trap and the conditional's precedence entry outright; and the value-first idiom's strongest case keeps `??` for the nullable default, with the boolean-conditioned default reading condition-first — a real, accepted residual cost. What made the reversal available rather than a re-ask was the delimiter design: the #1373-era condition-first candidate was `if c then a else b end`, declined for `end`-noise inside nested expressions; the paren-delimited atom adopts the statement's word order while deleting exactly that cost.

The atom's parentheses earn their keep three ways: `(` + `if` commits the parse at the second token; the delimiters make dangling-`else` and extent-by-precedence questions unaskable; and the form composes in any operand position like the `(case …)` atom. The recorded ergonomic cost: in call- and index-argument position the atom supplies its own pair inside the argument list's (`f((if c then a else b))`).

Alternatives declined: the postfix form (retired v0.208, above — reopening it requires new evidence against this section's reasons, per the standing discipline); `if c then a else b end` (the end-noise objection stands; the adopted form is its repair); C's `c ? a : b` (two more sigils in a language that spells `and`/`or`/`not` as words); the bare unparenthesized `if c then a else b` (statement-position prefix collision with the statement form, dangling-`else` under nesting).

<a id="sec-compound-assignment-spelling"></a>
### 3.4 Compound assignment keeps the C spellings

Assignment is `:=` and equality is `=` (the Pascal family), yet compound assignment uses the C-family spellings `+=`, `-=`, `*=`, `/=`, `&=`, `|=`, `^=`, `<<=`, `>>=`, `??=`. The Pascal-pure alternative (`+:=` and friends) has no precedent in any language programmers arrive from; omitting compound assignment entirely (the Ada position) buys no safety and gives up the evaluate-the-target-once guarantee the compound forms carry (spec §6.6) — the expanded `x := x + 1` spelling evaluates its target expression twice. Familiarity won over purity; the cost is aesthetic only. Nor is there ambiguity to pay: the compound forms exist only in statement position, where an equality expression cannot begin.

<a id="sec-arrow-body-family"></a>
### 3.5 The `=>` body family: one addition, three recorded divergences

Unit-returning methods take the assignment arrow (`method SetX(v: int) => _x := v;`) that accessors have — the two contexts are the same declaration shape, and an asymmetry between them would have no reason (review finding #1345). The remaining divergences are deliberate, with reasons:

- **Lambdas** do not take a bare-statement body: a statement's mandatory `;` collides with expression position (`Apply(lambda(x) => _total := x;, 5)` puts the arm form's `;,` seam inside an argument list; `var f := lambda(x) => _total := x;;` doubles the terminator). Multi-statement lambdas use `=> do … end`.
- **Operators** are expression-only: an operator must produce a value; a statement form could never typecheck.
- **Methods and constructors** have no `=> do … end` form: both already have the lighter bare-block body (`… end Name;`), so the arrow-block would be a second spelling of the same thing (declined for methods as far back as v0.84).

<a id="sec-destructor-takes-no-parens"></a>
### 3.6 `destructor` takes no parameter list

`constructor()` carries parens because parameters may follow — the parens are the parameter list, present even when empty for uniformity across constructors. A destructor can never take parameters (it is invoked by the compiler-generated `Dispose()`, §28.4), so a mandatory empty `()` would be pure ceremony attached to nothing. The asymmetry is intentional (confirmed 2026-07, review finding #1343): `destructor` is a keyword-introduced block like `try` or `extension`, not a callable signature. (The §1.6 closer taxonomy already treats `constructor`/`destructor`/`operator` as one keyword-labeled family.)

<a id="sec-case-scrutinee-of-delimiter"></a>
### 3.7 The `case` scrutinee is delimited by `of`

`case` separates its scrutinee from the first arm's pattern with `of` (#1370). Without a boundary, since patterns may begin with `-` (a negative range bound), `..`, `..<`, or a relational token, the scrutinee has to parse at a restricted precedence level — multiplicative and postfix only, with a mandatory-parenthesization rule for anything lower (`case (x - 1)`) — which is how it parsed before `of` was adopted. That restriction served no semantic purpose: it substituted for the missing boundary, surprised users whose scrutinee was a perfectly ordinary arithmetic expression, and required a normative paragraph (and issues #1320 and #1233) to keep the rule honest.

`of` supplies the boundary explicitly: `case score of 90..100 => …`. The scrutinee is a full `Expression` with no restriction; the leading-pattern-token ambiguity vanishes by construction rather than by precedence surgery; and the spelling is the language's declared §3 Pascal heritage verbatim (`case x of` is Pascal's form). One extra keyword-token per case was weighed against the removed restriction, the removed parens rule, and the removed parser delicacy, and judged the better trade at every scale of program. Alternatives declined: a colon (`case x:`) reads as a type annotation; a mandatory newline resurrects layout-sensitivity, which #744 deliberately removed from this construct family.

<a id="sec-pattern-alternatives-pipe"></a>
### 3.8 Pattern alternatives are spelled `|`

Pattern alternatives have their own separator (#1371). Before it, the comma did triple duty in pattern context — arm separator, alternative separator (`1, 2 => "low"`), and positional-slot separator — disambiguated only by scanning ahead for `=>`, with three consequences: alternatives had to be banned inside positional destructure slots (the #1300 fix), `catch` grew its own comma-based multi-type list that read differently from case alternatives (#1341), and every tool that parses patterns needed the lookahead.

`|` gives alternatives their own spelling: `1 | 2 => "low"`, `FormatException | OverflowException => …`, and the per-slot form `TwoColor(Color.Red | Color.Blue, var x)`, which the comma could not express. The comma means exactly one thing in pattern context; no `=>` lookahead is needed; case and catch share one alternatives syntax; and the spelling matches the Rust/Scala/Python-`match` precedent readers already know. The bitwise-or collision is dissolved structurally rather than by rule: the only expression positions inside patterns (relational operands, range bounds) parse below `|`'s precedence level, and the scrutinee is `of`-delimited (§3.7), so a pattern-level `|` is always the separator. Alternative members exclude types and bindings in case patterns (a type member stays a compile error; grouped type tests are separate arms) — because constant and type alternatives have different exhaustiveness and binding semantics. The admitted members are constants, ranges, and relationals: those are binding-free pure membership tests, which that reasoning never targeted; the exclusion of types and bindings is the decision.

<a id="sec-owning-prefix"></a>
### 3.9 The owning family is prefixed `Owning`, not `Owned`

The owning family's prefix is `Owning` (#1372; the stronger remedy #1334 hedged toward). The earlier spelling, `OwnedArray<T>`, grammatically read as the wrong axis: "an array that *is owned*" (the container axis), when the family actually denotes "an array that *owns its elements*" (the element axis). The misreading was not hypothetical — §28.3 and Appendix C.3 both had to carry defensive parentheticals explaining that a bare `OwnedArray<T>` return is a borrow, and `owned OwnedArray<T>` stacked one word across two meanings. The rename gives each axis its own word: `owned` is the container qualifier; `Owning` is the element prefix. `owned OwningArray<Dog>` reads correctly; a bare `OwningArray<Dog>` does not *sound* owned.

Folded in (the #1359 deferral): the owning interfaces carry the standard `I` prefix — `IOwningEnumerable`/`IOwningEnumerator` (and the async pair), `IOwningList`/`IOwningCollection` — since they were the stdlib's only unprefixed interfaces; `GetOwnedEnumerator()` followed as `GetOwningEnumerator()`. Method names that describe an owned argument — `FromOwnedResult` — keep `Owned`: there the word correctly names the qualifier axis (the value being passed is owned), not the element axis. Where a borrowing and an owning form of one operation have the same parameter types they take distinct names, since `owned` distinguishes no overloads ([§3.10](#sec-call-site-transfer-marker)'s record of [proposal 0185](proposals/0185-owned-overload-pair-retired.md)); return-side `Owned` names were never this convention's subject. The inline-array slot marker is deliberately not touched (a separate decision, recorded here as deferred, not declined). Historical changelog entries ([`Bezel_Changelog.md`](Bezel_Changelog.md)) retain the names as they were at each revision.

<a id="sec-call-site-transfer-marker"></a>
### 3.10 Ownership transfer is marked at ordinary call sites

A consume of a named owned binding is marked at the site with `move` (#1348; respelled by proposal 0090, [#3056](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3056)): `M(move x)`, `var y := move x;`, `case move x of`, arm-result `move x`, alongside `move x.f`/`move coll[i]`. **Fresh producers** (`new`, owned-returning calls, `move obj._f`) stay unmarked because no live binding is invalidated — the marker spells invalidation, not the parameter's mode. `owned` is purely declarative (parameters, fields, returns). The gap the marker closes: `ref` and `out` require call-site keywords, `move` spells a field take-out at the read site (§5.10), yet without a marker, passing a named owned binding to an `owned` parameter — the mode that leaves the argument *moved-from* — is invisible: `M(value)`. The spec paid for that invisibility twice: §22.6's owned-overload ban and the stdlib's dual naming (`Append`/`AppendOwned`) both existed because the call site could not distinguish the forms. The marker came from #1348, an adversarially-verified review finding, not a rejected-then-revived design; no prior recorded decision had weighed a call-site marker.

**Why `move`, not `owned` (proposal 0090, [#3056](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3056); owner-ruled 2026-08-11).** The marker was first spelled `owned`, defining the orphaned bare-`owned` alternative that Appendix A.7's `ArgModifiers` carried with no prose semantics. `owned` was preferred over `move` then because it filled the reserved slot, mirrored `out owned var x`, and avoided relaxing MoveExpr's deliberate field-only operand restriction (§5.10). The *marking* survives exactly as that record argued; the verb was reopened on evidence it never weighed, the reading-model cost of one token carrying a *state* meaning at declarations and an *action* meaning at use sites — `TakeOwnership(owned resource)` was a callee describing its parameter's obligation and, character for character, a caller performing an invalidation. Each of the first record's grounds is answered in the study (0090 §2–§3): the reserved `ArgModifiers` slot is deleted rather than filled — the marker is part of the argument expression; the coherent mirror of `out owned var x` was `owned p: T` (declaration ↔ declaration), not an action wearing the same token; and relaxing MoveExpr's operand restriction is the point, not a cost — §5.10's three reasons for an explicit take-out verb are reasons at every take-out site, none field-specific. The flip is spelling-only: every kernel rule reads unchanged with the marker re-pointed (spec §28.3, §28.8.1).

**The redundant-marker asymmetry: examined and declined (proposal 0102 §2, ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation).** The declaration side tolerates a redundant `owned` — spec §28.1 admits the marker on a move-only value type as admitted-but-redundant, and stdlib §6.8's `Domain.Spawn(...): owned Task` spells it on an announced return as documentation with emphasis — while the use side does not: `return move x;` under an `owned` return, `throw move x`, `_ := move x;`, and a marked store into an owning slot are all errors at their marker-free positions. The symmetry option — admit the use-site redundancy so that "every consume says `move`" — was put and is declined, on three grounds. First, it is an owner-ruled precedent of this same programme: proposal 0090's Q2 put exactly this choice and the owner ruled it rejected-as-redundant, the changelog recording the ruling's cost being paid in the same breath (three corpus lines of owned-slot stores, newly rejected, migration drop-the-marker); a ruling enforced at that price is not reopened by a symmetry argument carrying no new evidence. Second, the two sides are different things, and the line is nameable: declaration-side `owned` on a move-only type or a `Task`-family return is type-level documentation — written once, read by every signature reader, true of the slot — where use-site marker redundancy is per-expression spelling variance — written per call, introducing two spellings for one site, the both-ways surface 0090's 633-line migration existed to eliminate; 0090's reading model is precisely that `owned` means *state* at declarations and `move` means *action* at sites. Third, the grep argument fails on its own terms: "every consume says `move`" is unachievable anyway — spec §28.3's marker-free positions consume without a marker by design — and admitting the marker at *some* of them makes the grep less reliable, not more. Not foreclosed: the reverse tightening (rejecting the redundant declaration-side `owned`) is a separate question with its own costs — §28.1's per-instantiation erasure makes the redundancy substitution-dependent, and stdlib §6.8's explicit spellings are load-bearing documentation — and is not raised here.

**`out var x` adopts; no use-site `owned` remains (proposal 0122 L2).** `out var x` adopts what the resolved parameter announces, and the pre-declared `out x` form takes the same answer in an adoptable state. The retired spelling `out owned var x` was the one use-site `owned` the `move` respell left standing, on the ground that it was a declaration position. It was also the one place the language still demanded a marker for a fresh producer: the adoption clause (spec §28.3, K1.7 + K3.11) lets `var x := Factory();` adopt owned from the callee's declared `owned` return with no use-site spelling, and an `out owned` result is the same situation — a fresh local, a callee-declared owned production. Alternatives weighed: keeping the spelling as a visual cue (declined — `var x := Factory()` lacks the cue already, and the rule contradicted the habit the code-generation reference teaches beside it); spelling it `out move var x` (declined — `move` marks the invalidation of a live binding, and nothing is invalidated). `out using var x` survives for §5.27's reason: the scope-bound binding is a declared constraint, not an inference. Cost accepted: a mechanical rewrite across the test corpus and a classification re-key in the compiler.

**No overload pair differs only by `owned` ([proposal 0185](proposals/0185-owned-overload-pair-retired.md); the 2026-10 subtraction review).** `owned` never distinguishes overloads — on an ordinary parameter as on an `out` parameter — and a pair differing only by it clashes at the second declaration (spec §22.6, §11.3). The language did admit such a pair for a time (the historical note below), and that admission was sound and its premise still holds: the marker makes `M(move x)` and `M(x)` visibly distinct, so a pair *could* be resolved from the call site's spelling. What the subtraction review measured is that nothing uses the capability: the standard library declares zero same-type pairs differing only by ordinary `owned` (the merged `WhenAll`/`WhenEach` are type-distinguished and stay; `FromResult`/`FromOwnedResult` never merged, the guard itself keeping them apart), and the test corpus holds three pair declarations, every one a test of the mechanism rather than a program needing it. Against that reach the admission carried about 1,500 words of rule — four applicability clauses, a decidability argument, conjunctive composition across positions, a 460-word erasure test deciding which type parameters admit a pair, a §11.3 reading of constraints for the guard alone, a §2.8.6 re-routing caveat for the owned member added beside a plain one, and a remedy-naming ambiguity diagnostic — plus a guard in the compiler that had already over-rejected once (#4939). The workaround is the one the library had used all along and the one the admission itself kept for every `out` pair: a borrowing form and an owning form of one operation are different operations, and take different names (`TryGetValue`/`TryPopValue`; `Append(item)` beside `Adopt(owned item)`); a dual whose parameter types already differ stays an ordinary overload. No call site changes shape: a single declared member takes `M(move x)` or `M(x)` under §22.3 exactly as before, and a fresh producer is passed unmarked to either. Three alternatives were declined: keeping the admission; keeping the pair at concrete class and interface types only, dropping the guard and the generic arm, which keeps the clauses and the caveat for a capability still used nowhere; and routing a pair by the marker alone, with fresh producers and `null` falling to the plain member, which hands a storing callee a reference that dies at the statement boundary, the hazard the admission's fresh-producer routing existed to avoid. Reopening bar: a measured program whose borrowing and owning forms of one operation cannot take different names.

*Historical note: the admitted pair ([proposal 0091](proposals/0091-owned-overload-lift.md), [#3086](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3086); the v0.250 entry).* Between the `move` respell and proposal 0185, a pair differing only by ordinary `owned` was declarable under a declaration guard, and at a pair position the argument's ownership classification completed *applicability* — an owned production reached the `owned` member, a borrow the plain one, the unclassified rest the ordinary ambiguity rule — with no ranking change, so every selection was derivable from the call site's spelling; fresh producers routed to the `owned` member because a call-bounded borrow of the temporary would hand a storing callee a reference dying at the statement boundary. The guard was an erasure test decided by §14.2's conformance computation, with an interface bound qualifying only where language rule (the `IDisposable` family, closed by §8.7) excluded the erasing substitutions, since conformance to an ordinary interface is open-world. Proposal 0091 records the formulation choices and their declines. Three of its findings still explain today's library. `FromResult`/`FromOwnedResult` never merged: the natural merged member needs `where T: class` (erasure collapse) or `where T: IDisposable` (a domain regression — `OwningTask<T>` admits every interface type), and no §14.2 constraint spells the owning-generic domain, so the erasure geometry rather than the ban's letter keeps the pair apart. The convention-bred duals `WhenAllOwned`/`WhenEachOwned`, type-distinguished and declarable even under the ban, became `WhenAll`/`WhenEach` overloads with no alias; admit-but-don't-rename was declined because the tax the ban levied *was* the shipped vocabulary. And return-side `Owned` names (`Json.ParseOwned<T>`) stay: the return position carries no call-site marker and no overloading-by-return exists, so there the suffix is the §28.3 type-announcement discipline doing its job. The study also observed that every shipped `out` pair is operation-distinct (`TryGetValue` leaves where `TryPopValue` removes).

<a id="sec-uniform-end-closer"></a>
### 3.11 One uniform end-closer rule

One rule governs closers (#1374; one of #1343's recorded seams): `end` may repeat the construct's name (if it has one) or its introducing keyword (otherwise), checked when present, and bare `end;` is legal everywhere. It replaced three rules: named declarations took an optional *name* label (`end Main;`), constructor/destructor/operator bodies an optional *keyword* label (`end constructor;`), and every statement-level block — plus extension and extern, the longest un-labelable bodies in the language — forbade any label, so the Ada-style keyword-labeled closers (`end if;`, `end for;` — the closer scheme of the very heritage §3 cites, though Ada itself spells every loop's closer `end loop;`) were an error. Collapsing the three was fully additive — zero migration — and gives long blocks the compiler-checked readability the declaration closers always had. The alternative of *requiring* labels on long blocks was declined: label ergonomics are a style choice, and the compiler-checked optional form gives tooling and reviewers the same benefit without ceremony.

<a id="sec-the-binding-atom-names-a-subexpression"></a>
### 3.12 The binding atom names a subexpression (`(var x := E; body)`)

The atom (proposal 0061 §1; owner-deferred 2026-07-21, activated on the owner's direction 2026-07-22) exists for one job: naming a subresult where only an expression is admitted, so it need not be recomputed. The motivating position is the contract clause (spec §22.16) — clauses are expression-only and pure, so without it a clause using a subresult twice must evaluate it twice: `ensures(var n := result.Count; n >= 0 and n <= _capacity)` names it once, harmlessly. Expression-position `case` arm bodies are the second beneficiary; helper extraction remains the doctrine for multi-*statement* logic.

The form passed the atom admission test proposal 0061 records — the test future paren-atom candidates must also meet: (1) it produces a value whose computation is ceremony-heavy in expression position today; (2) it rides existing kernel machinery — clause typing is spec §21.1's, ownership is K2/K3.4 verbatim plus one boundary clause (K8.5: an owned initializer is a *named unbound temporary* bounded by the atom — tighter than the enclosing full expression, escape rejected-never-extended per K8.4); (3) it makes no doctrine-declined path more ergonomic. The same review declined the try-expression and comprehensions under tests (3) and (1) — `DECLINED.md`, 2026-07-21.

Design choices worth recording: the spelling reuses `(` + `var` (a unique two-token commit, like `(if`/`(case`) and the statement declaration's own clause syntax — no new keyword, no new token; `;` separates because no expression contains a top-level `;`, where `in` collides with membership and `=>` with lambdas. The binding is single-assignment by construction — assignment is a statement and the atom admits none — and is additionally not an assignable location (the runtime-`const` rule, spec §21.1), which closes the `do`-bodied-lambda capture hole. The body is deliberately *not* a K3.4 marked-transfer position (an atom cannot launder its own binding into an owned result); lifting that, and admitting all-constant atoms into §17.14 constancy, are recorded liftable bounds. It does not reopen §5.13's block-expression decline: that decline is about statement *sequencing* in value position — effects, `do … end`'s single meaning, the trailing-expression/`yield`/`give` designs — while this atom admits no statements at all; a clause is a pure-shape binding, the body one expression. The distinction the two rules jointly draw: naming intermediate *values* in an expression is cheap and admitted; interleaving *effects* into an expression remains declined.

<a id="sec-lambdas-are-keyword-led"></a>
### 3.13 Lambdas are keyword-led (`lambda(params): T => body`)

A lambda is keyword-led (proposal 0064; owner-directed 2026-07-22). The spelling it replaced, `(params) => body`, was the last major expression form with no committing first token: the reading was decided only at the `=>` after the matching `)`, which cost the parser its one unbounded lookahead, required a dedicated carve so a `when`-guard `(t > 0) => …` was not mis-scanned as a lambda (#1256), and gave the reader the same late signal in argument-position paren soup. The keyword fixes all three at once — `lambda` begins no other expression or statement, so the form commits at its first token, stronger than even the paren atoms' second-token commits — and it opens the one slot the old spelling structurally could not host: a return-type ascription (`): T =>` after a bare parenthesized form collides with dictionary-literal key position), which resolves §27.3's divergent-return case at the site instead of at the enclosing variable. That capability is what distinguishes this landing from the spelling-only churn `DECLINED.md` D-0003 warns against: the change retires the lookahead machinery *and* closes a recorded expressiveness gap, rather than re-spelling for taste.

The keyword is `lambda` because it is already the language's name for the construct — §27's title, the grammar's `LambdaExpr`, the diagnostics — and the term-of-art objection is answered by `async`/`await`/`yield`, which are equally terms of art and equally reserved. `function` was the runner-up (matches "function types" but is heaviest at the hottest sites and implies a free-function category the language lacks); `func`/`fn` would be the keyword set's first abbreviations; `method` is free but answers "method of what?" with "nothing". The word is unconditionally reserved, not contextual — reservation was free (the identifier appears nowhere in the corpus), and contextual status would reopen the `Identifier`-lookalike seams the keyword exists to close. The cutover is hard — the old spelling left the grammar in the same revision, the 0057-reversal precedent — because with no external users a deprecation window serves nobody and defers the entire parser payoff. The broader survey (eight syntax families, the verified colon-fatality of the literal Python form, the declined placeholder/brace/`end`-delimited shapes) is recorded in proposal 0064, whose §9 verification record backs every grammar claim here.

<a id="sec-named-destructure-patterns-equals-spelling"></a>
### 3.14 Named-destructure patterns are spelled with `=`

Spec §19.3's named-destructure form `T(Prop = Pattern, …)` (proposal 0065; owner-directed 2026-07-24) — named slots inside the existing destructure parentheses — completes the pattern grammar the way §3.8's per-slot alternatives completed it: same parentheses, no new token, one new list shape inside them. The motivating gap is real. Positional destructuring is deliberately confined to records and choice cases (§5.17 declines class *deconstruction* outright, because extraction and disposal are opposite operations on the same slots), yet a class's public readable properties *are* its encapsulation-respecting match surface, and reading them transfers nothing. Without the named form, matching on them forces bind-then-access with the property names stranded in a `when` guard, away from the pattern they interrogate; the named form puts the interrogation in the pattern.

**Why `=`, not `:=` or braces.** A pattern's data flow is the reverse of construction: a call site *puts a value in* by name with `:=` (`new Rect(Width := 3)`, §22.5), while a pattern *asks whether a value is present* — `Rect(Width = 3)` reads literally as "is `Width` 3?", which is exactly what `=` means (spec §8.4). The `:=`/`=` asymmetry between constructing and interrogating is the load-bearing distinction, not a break in symmetry, and the ML family (F# `{ X = x }`, OCaml record patterns) already spells record patterns with `=`. The collision analysis is clean: `=` is not a pattern relational operator, and the only expression positions embedded in a slot pattern parse below equality's precedence, so a slot's `=` can never be absorbed by a pattern-interior expression. The **brace form** `Rect { Width: var w }` (the original C#-style sketch) was declined under §5.16's recorded no-`{}` posture — `{}` is confined to string interpolation and never becomes a fourth bracket kind — and would also open a second destructure surface where the parentheses already suffice. The **`:=` spelling** was declined because assignment reads false in interrogation position.

**The v1 restrictions are the smallest sound cut.** A slot list is positional or named, never mixed (a structural rule, not a semantic check); bindings are borrows spelled `var w` (a naked identifier after `=` is a constant-or-type reference, never a silent binding — the one place the positional naked-binding shorthand would invert the reading); a constant slot is the `=` equality test; and exhaustiveness counts a named-destructure arm exactly where the corresponding positional or type-pattern arm would count, if and only if every slot is irrefutable — one level deep, no per-property value-space analysis. Each restriction is a compatible widening if evidence arrives (positional/named mixing, computed record properties, interface-typed heads, lambda-parameter named destructuring); the borrow-only, no-transfer-through-named-slots reading is the conservative one that keeps the feature free of any new ownership rule. The full fork record — the declined braces and `:=`, the naked-identifier decision, and the open questions left for later widening — is proposal 0065's.

**Named slots have no transfer spelling ([proposal 0183](proposals/0183-retire-the-marked-named-slot.md)).** Named slots keep the borrow-only reading the restrictions above state, and `move N = …` is a compile error naming the positional form. The positional destructure transfers every obligation-bearing slot at once and `_` discards a sibling with the construct, which is the one real shape of a per-slot peek (borrowing one payload while transferring a sibling in the same list). **Unmarked symmetry** — named slots transferring in owning matches exactly as positional slots do, with no new syntax — is declined: a named slot that transferred silently would invert the borrow reading, and would delete the named form's ability to borrow one payload while another transfers. Also declined: keeping a marked slot for choice cases only; and a `move` on the whole slot list (`Ready(move: conn = var c, …)`), which no site asked for and which is the positional destructure with names.

**Move-only value payloads transfer like `owned` (spec defect [#3094](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3094); owner-directed 2026-08-12 — "propose the resolution and resolve 3094").** Two interpretations were on the table: **(1)** a move-only value payload *transfers* into its binding at arm selection in an owning match, exactly as an `owned` payload does, and *borrows* in a borrowing match under §19.3's move-only scrutinee rule applied at the slot; or **(2)** the payload borrows everywhere, extraction staying inexpressible. Interpretation 1 is adopted, completed on both match forms and through nested destructuring (spec §35.5/§19.3/§15.3), on four grounds. *(a)* K3.12 already gives a move-only value unmarked transfer at every other flow position — assignment, parameter passing, return, owning stores; interpretation 2 would have made the owning match the one place a move-only value cannot go, in exactly the construct whose job is consumption. *(b)* The disposal half of the rule was already defined — §35.4's recursive active-case disposal covers a discarded or unlisted move-only slot with no new text — so interpretation 1 completes an existing rule rather than adding one. *(c)* No new kernel row: the transfer is routing within the one consume the owning match already performs (K8.3 for the temporary trigger, K3.4 for the marked scrutinee), the account the marked named slot was given; K3's enumeration gains nothing and no axiom moves. *(d)* it dissolved a contradiction the marked named slot (since retired, below) then carried: §28.1 admits the `owned` marker as admitted-but-redundant on a move-only value field, so a redundantly-marked field (`Fetched(owned data: [4 owned]Connection?)`) satisfied the marked named slot's condition (ii) — an `owned`-marked payload field — while the rejection taxonomy's move-only arm rejected the same slot: two rules claiming one declaration. Interpretation 2 would have needed a second carve to keep them apart; interpretation 1 makes the marked and unmarked field spellings behave identically, which is §28.1's stance verbatim. The ruling executed 0092 Q2's recorded liftable at exactly its recorded bar — its own defect issue, its own ruling, not a side effect of the marked slot. The nested case follows the same geometry: a move-only *choice* payload (the one nested move-only shape with a pattern surface) routes recursively in an owning match — through the slots that transfer, a positional slot and any pattern nested within one; a nested pattern beneath a named slot routes nothing at any depth — inner bindings receiving their payloads at selection, the un-bound remainder at every level disposing at construct completion. §35.5's "nested destructuring of an owned payload does not arise" claim is corrected rather than narrowed: for a class-typed owned payload it arises in the *named* form (§19.3 admits named destructuring with a class head; interface heads stay barred in v1) and routes nothing — each nested named slot a getter read, a borrow minting no obligation (§28.6), the payload never transferred, disposed at construct completion exactly as a discarded slot.

**Borrow-lifetime obligations key on the *holds-borrows* property, not the classification label (spec defects [#3110](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3110)/[#3111](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3111); owner-directed 2026-08-12 — "resolve and push both 3110 and 3111 resolutions", one train).** §35.4's classification cascade stops at the first matching label, so keyed on the label, an owning choice carrying a plain class payload beside its owned one — `Loaded(owned response: Response, log: Logger)` — escaped every §9.7 borrow-lifetime rule, the borrow-lifetime paragraph being scoped to "borrow-bearing choices", the rule-2 label only (#3110, a gate's out-of-scope observation). The resolution is a scoping correction, not a missing rule: §9.7's obligations were always about *carried borrows*, the choice text inherited them through a cascade that stops at the first matching label, and the re-key restores the rule to its own ground. The named condition — a choice **holds borrows** iff any case has a borrow-carrying payload field under exactly §9.7's definition, transitively; §35.4 defines the property first and lets rule 2 read it — is orthogonal to the classification, and every §9.7-derived constraint keys on it (spec §35.4/§9.7/§30.1/§28.3, K5.5's contribution sentence); the classification is untouched and keeps driving copy/move/dispose semantics — the property adds obligations, never subtracts semantics. **The declined alternative**: a fourth classification, "owning-and-borrow-bearing" — it doubles the label vocabulary for one orthogonal bit and forces every classification-keyed rule (boxing, sendability, equality, matching, the compiler-provided combinators' gates) to enumerate a fourth arm; holds-borrows spells the same fact as a second property read at exactly the sites that need it. **Two compositions are stated rather than left to inference.** Class-field placement narrows: §15.3's "a move-only value may be a field of a class" holds only for values holding no borrows, and the owning choice that holds borrows is the *only* move-only kind the condition excludes — an owning inline array's slots are `owned`, so it never holds borrows — since storing one would let the borrows outlive their sources. And §35.12's transfer-closure admission carries the borrow-free conjunct beside #3111's payload-kind-generic completion, as one condition, total over payload kinds (a gate round caught an obligation-bearing quantification that under-quantified: a function-typed payload, never sendable, would have crossed unchecked inside an owning choice holding no borrows): no carried borrows *and* every payload field of every case transfer-compatible — an obligation-bearing payload recursively (an `owned` class payload by its class's sendability, an `owned` interface payload never, an owning-inline-array payload by its element class's — an interface element type never — a nested owning-choice payload recursively, the same reach as §35.4's recursive active-case disposal), every other payload by its own type's sendability, a function-typed payload disqualifying exactly as a function-typed field does — conformed identically at §30.14 rule 2's carrier, §35.12's home, and Guide 4 §5. #3111's other enumeration silence — §15.3's field-placement bullet never naming the choice-payload position, which has full binding semantics — closes with one sentence and the §35.4/§35.1/§35.5 cites. Kernel accounting: no new K row, no axiom — K5.5's clause-5 tracing sentence reads the property, K2's source-liveness reasoning is unchanged, and the freeze and liveness machinery is §28.3's verbatim. **The property's domain is every value kind ([#5603](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5603)).** On a record or an inline array it coincides with borrow-bearing (a record has no owning classification to part the two, §15.3 and §9.7's first paragraph; an owning inline array never holds borrows, §15.1.1), and on a choice it is what it was; so *a value type that holds borrows* is the one name for the union — borrow-bearing record, borrow-bearing inline array, choice that holds borrows — that the borrow-lifetime, async, static-field, consume-site and bare-type-parameter rules all reach. Proposal 0136 S2-16 had accepted borrow-carrying value for the union; that name is declined, since §9.7 uses *borrow-carrying* for the field-level predicate and §28.3 for closures, frames and class-typed values, four live sites the narrow sense would contradict. Leaving the union unnamed, the other option, kept nine phrasings of one condition in circulation. `holds-borrows` is a name the spec already applies to records and inline arrays at §22.12's verdict table, it is the name proposal 0095 §3.3 chose against the `borrow-<participle>` collision, and widening its domain changes no verdict: no rule keyed on the property being choice-only.

*Historical note: the marked named slot ([proposal 0092](proposals/0092-named-destructure-transfer.md), [#3087](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3087); retired by proposal 0183).* Once the `move` respell gave named slots a natural transfer spelling (proposal 0090's gap G5; 0065's Q2 had marked the form liftable), a named slot could carry the take-out verb on its source — `Fetched(move response = var r)` — in an owning match, on an `owned`-marked payload field, with a binding RHS. It rested on one capability, the per-slot peek. The 2026-10 subtraction review found no program using it: zero gtest or lit programs, only the spec and reference examples that introduced the form and copies of them, and no parser arm, against a second transfer spelling, a three-token parse commit, a six-condition admission rule with its own rejection set, a §19.6 clause and an Appendix C row. Proposal 0092 records the spellings it declined: `Field = move x` and `Field = move var x`, whose `move` operand would be a fresh name rather than the location being emptied, and `Field = owned var x`, which puts `owned` at a site performing an action. It also declined `move N = _` as an explicit dispose-here marker: that would be a new consuming site, a row K3's enumeration does not have, for an effect the plain discard already delivers.

<a id="sec-the-unit-type"></a>
### 3.15 The unit type `()`, and where the no-value boundary sits

Bezel has no tuples, so `()` is not a degenerate tuple — it is a freestanding built-in whose one job is to make *returns nothing* a first-class result and instantiation like any other. That buys three things, each with a live client: a single function-type grammar — `(T) -> U` covers every result, so higher-order code never bifurcates (`method Retry<T>(op: () -> T): T` accepts a no-result operation at `T = ()` with no `Retry` twin, and the same holds for every memoize/backoff/`Locked.With`-shaped combinator to come; C#'s `Action`/`Func` split is the counterexample the design avoids — structural function types escape the *naming* half of that split, and only a unit type escapes the *member* half); a no-payload instantiation where the wrapper is thin — `EventSource<()>` (stdlib §6.5; adopted deliberately, proposal 0035); and a typed statement calculus — §21.9's unused-result warning keys on non-`()` results, §27.3 classifies procedure lambdas by the `()` result, and `catch Exception => ()` (§31.2.5) names suppression as a value.

**The language is deliberately hybrid about "no value", and the boundary is a rule, not an accident — but the rule is frequency, not rule-difference.** §30.5's Pattern A/B split keys on the result's *kind* (class vs. copyable), not on arity: a `Task<()>` would differ from `Task` by nothing, since `Task<T>` at a copyable `T` already shares `Task`'s row. What justifies the dedicated type is pervasiveness: the no-result task is the *common* case — every `async` procedure produces one — so a mandatory `<()>` there would be ambient ceremony at the language's highest-traffic seam, while the no-payload event is the *minority* case of its family, where one honest token per declaration is cheap. So: a no-value variant earns a dedicated type where the no-value case is pervasive enough that the instantiation token would be ambient noise; where it is the minority case, it is an instantiation at `()` — and a family's satellites follow its head (`TaskCompletionSource` is `Task`'s completion face, §30.11's "same shape minus the `result` parameter", so it sits with `Task` by family symmetry, not by an independent ruling).

**Unit's near-invisibility is the design working, not an argument for removal.** §22.1's procedure form (no `:` clause) means declarations never spell it; there are no unit locals, fields, or payloads in idiomatic code; a programmer meets `()` only where it carries information — a function type's result, a thin instantiation, a suppression arm. Removing the type would not remove those places, only re-spell them worse. The removal direction and the boundary moves are declined with reasons and bars (`DECLINED.md`); the keyword-led function-type respelling — under which unit would become unwritable in function types — is deferred with its design worked (proposal 0120 §5: the colon-form parse is settled viable; the costs are majority-pessimization, no parser win, and a whole-surface migration), reopenable on the conditions the row states.

<a id="sec-function-value-calls-are-positional"></a>
### 3.16 A call through a function value takes no named argument

Spec §27.1 rejects the named form at an invocation through a function-typed value ([#4085](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/4085)), rather than binding it by position, because a name accepted and then ignored could spell one binding and perform another. Two admissions were weighed and declined. **Binding *by* name:** a function type's parameter names are not part of its identity (the named and unnamed forms are the same type), so an ordinary assignment — exact-match, or the identity-preserving retyping of spec §27.1.1 — may drop or relabel them, and a name-keyed binding would mean one thing through one view of a value and another through the next. An order-checked *label* (a written name admitted only where it equals the declared name at that position): it would make documentation-only names a compatibility contract on every call site that spelled them. Both are recorded in `DECLINED.md`.

<a id="sec-memory-management"></a>
## 4. Memory management

Bezel's ownership model is the part most likely to surprise readers. The high-level shape:

- Class instances are heap-allocated and must be disposed exactly once.
- Records, enums, and primitives need no disposal.
- A small set of types — `string`, `Type`, boxed value types, function values — are **runtime-managed** and exempt from manual disposal.

For the runtime-managed set — `string`, boxed values, function values, and `Type`, and only them — reclamation is the runtime's business rather than the program's. For the first three the mechanism is not specified: reference counting works, region allocation works, a small collector over them works; implementations choose. `Type` is the fixed-mechanism member: the runtime interns one instance per type, holds it for the life of the process, and reclaims none (spec [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-managed-types)). Every class instance, trivial-dispose included, is instead freed by compiler-inserted code at its statically-known owner's release point — no runtime lifetime mechanism exists for user-defined classes at all ([§5.9](#sec-trivial-dispose-classes), [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking); spec §28).

<a id="sec-why-strings-are-runtime-managed"></a>
### 4.1 Why strings are runtime-managed

`string` is runtime-managed (spec [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-managed-types)): a string carries no disposal obligation, so it is passed, stored, returned and captured with no ownership spelling anywhere.

**Strings need no ownership.** A `string` is immutable (spec [§4.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-string)) and holds no references, so no string reaches another value and strings cannot form a cycle. Reference counting reclaims such a value when its last reference goes, as any other exact scheme would; spec §28.9 leaves the mechanism open. No tracing collector is needed and nothing runs in the background, so the no-collector commitment of [§1](#sec-the-big-picture) holds for strings as it does for classes. A string holds nothing but memory, so there is nothing for an owner to dispose.

**Ownership is for resources.** It gives a file, a connection or a native handle a single disposal point that the reader can find. Strings are the most common value in every signature: names, keys, messages, log lines. Putting the ownership rules on them would charge the largest cost to the type that gains least, since every interpolation, concatenation and `ToString()` result would become an owned value to bind, move or dispose.

**The price of the alternative.** Rust puts strings under ownership, and the cost shows: the `String`/`&str` split, `.to_string()` and `.clone()` at call sites, and lifetime parameters on every struct that holds a `&str`. That design is workable, but it is an ergonomic cost that Bezel declines for an immutable, value-like type.

**Copy-on-write is declined because immutability already gives its benefit.** Copy-on-write makes sharing cheap and copies only when a holder writes. An immutable string is shared for free and is never written, so value-type strings with copy-on-write would add a uniqueness check and buy nothing. The mutable form is `StringBuilder` (stdlib [§3.1](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-stringbuilder)), an ordinary owned class whose `ToString()` produces the immutable result.

**Reference-count elision is implementation latitude.** An implementation that counts references can remove most of the count traffic at compile time, as Koka's Perceus, Lean 4's reset/reuse and Swift's optimizer do, and can reuse a uniquely held buffer in place. The ownership and borrow facts the compiler already computes are the input such an analysis needs. This is latitude under spec §28.9, not a language rule, and it is left to a later optimization campaign.

Boxed values and `Type` tokens are runtime-managed on the same footing: a box's payload is fixed at boxing, and a `Type` token is interned for the life of the process (spec §28.9). Function values are [§4.2](#sec-why-function-typed-values-are-runtime-managed)'s subject.

<a id="sec-why-function-typed-values-are-runtime-managed"></a>
### 4.2 Why function-typed values are runtime-managed

A non-capturing lambda is just a function pointer; a capturing lambda owns its captured environment; a method reference (`obj.Method`) carries the receiver. These are heterogeneous enough that requiring callers to track ownership of every function-typed value would force `using var` on every callback.

The simple rule — function-typed values are runtime-managed — makes them as easy to use as any other value while keeping the ownership story for resource-bearing types intact.

Runtime management settles a function value's own lifetime; the rest of this section records how the borrows it captures are tracked. Its source set is the union of its captures' sources, every borrow rule of spec §28.3 applies to the value by that set, and a position that holds or converts a function value at a declared type announcing no source set — a function type, `Object`, or a type parameter substituted with either — admits a capture-free value only. A value type that holds borrows is kept from `Object` and interface-typed positions on the same ground ([§4.2.6](#sec-borrow-erasing-positions)).

<a id="sec-function-value-no-dispose"></a>
#### 4.2.1 No disposal through a function value

**A function value has no `Dispose()`, and no function value is a disposer ([proposal 0200](proposals/0200-dispose-member-only-on-disposable-types.md), [proposal 0201](proposals/0201-no-method-reference-to-dispose.md); [#7260](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7260), [#7067](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7067), [#7110](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7110)).** A function value is not a class and has no `Dispose()` member (§8.7, §28.9), so nothing disposes a closure: a disposal that freed a captured cell under a later call would be non-conformant under every reading. Nor may a function value *perform* a disposal: a method reference to `Dispose` or `DisposeAsync`, bound or type-qualified, is rejected at its formation over every receiver (§27.6). Explicit disposal is a site-announced kill whose post-state liveness records (K3.9); invoked through a function value it would happen at a site no liveness can see, through the borrow the reference captured — the ground on which §28.3 already rejects `lambda() => d.Dispose()` over a captured owned `d`, and which the one-character sibling `lambda() => d.Dispose` had escaped. A thunk holding a copy of the receiver cannot empty the holder's slot, so over an owning collection — whose disposal path reclaims — the holder's release point would free the block again; a slot-capturing disposer, or a reference bound to the scope-end obligation itself, would each be a new closure kind for a spelling with no measured use; admitting the reference over class receivers only would keep the hidden kill and split the rule by receiver kind. A callback that must dispose takes an `owned` parameter and is invoked with the value transferred (`lambda(owned x: D) => x.Dispose()`, §28.9). The three alternatives are `declined-at-proposal` rows.

<a id="sec-closure-source-set"></a>
#### 4.2.2 The capture rule: one source set

**The closure as a borrow-carrying value: one rule, no closed list ([#4154](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/4154); ruled by the owner on design merits, 2026-09-10, proposal 0126).** Runtime management settles the function value's *own* lifetime; the borrows it captures are tracked separately (spec [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-managed-types)), by one criterion stated in the kernel's vocabulary (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules)): a function value's source set is the union of its captures' sources (a receiver-binding method reference's receiver included; nested closures transitively — the same transitive contribution [§5.25](#sec-deferred-activation-capture) computes for frames), and every §28.3 borrow rule applies to the value by that set. Immediate invocation, a `using var` local of function type and an argument to a function-typed parameter are consequences of the rule, not a list of admitted forms. Four grounds. *Uniformity*: §28.3's local-scope bullet governs every other borrow-holding local by structural scope comparison, and §28.9 frames the closure as a borrow-carrier; a second, syntactic list for one holder kind would be a redundant special case. *Soundness unchanged*: the storage and return sinks — a field, a collection, an `out`/`ref` parameter, the method's return — stay closed to every function value with a non-empty source set, on K2.4's ground (a function type announces no source set, so no holder beyond the body could re-establish it and no caller bound it — the ground §28.14 already states for a received callback), which is what keeps §28.3's capture-set contribution ("borrow-free by construction"), [§5.25](#sec-deferred-activation-capture), §28.14's recorded asymmetry and the v0.177 record true verbatim; the only storage route for a capture-bearing closure remains §28.14's `scoped` admission, and what the rule opens is the binding, invocation, transitive-capture and argument positions the scope comparison already decides. *Ergonomics*: a local helper lambda over a borrow needs no `using var` ceremony, and a plain function-typed local may be reassigned, each assignment checked on its own. *It is the rule the compiler implements*: the compiler's only plain-local check is the block-depth comparison the local-scope bullet describes, and its pinned diagnostic ("closure capturing `rr` is assigned to `f`, which outlives it") is a scope comparison, not a form check. **Declined:** a closed list of three admitted forms, with `using var` as the sole capture-scoping marker on a function-typed local; and such a list widened by a fourth form, which would keep a list whose reason nobody recorded (the spec carried one through v0.297, an accident of wording whose history the v0.298 changelog entry records) (`DECLINED.md`, *Closure-capture admission as a closed syntactic list*). The `using var` keeps its non-reassignability role on function-typed locals and nothing else; [§5.27](#sec-why-using-var-is-not-folded-into-var)'s reasons for the keyword are untouched — they are about owned and pinned bindings, which a function value is not.

**One announcement-erasing-position definition; the value-copy register read off signatures; the report shrunk to its claim (proposal [0169](proposals/0169-retrospective-consolidation.md)).** The places a capture-bearing function value could otherwise reach without its set — a field, a collection, a property, an `Object` parameter, a type-parameter substitution, an `as`, a nested parameter slot, a task result, a captured binding, each with its own paragraph below — are one sentence: a position that holds or converts a function value at a declared type announcing no source set (a function type, `Object`, or a type parameter substituted with either) is announcement-erasing and admits a capture-free value only, the set being compared only at a local, an invocation, a capture and a function-typed parameter. Spec §28.3 states that sentence once, in the closure bullet, with *Function-typed holders* and *Set-erasing positions* as its two site lists and one holder bullet rather than a sink rule restated per holder kind. The standard library's value-copy positions are read off signatures the same way, by a signature-shape rule at spec §28.11 (#5717) — an `owned` collection result, a borrowing-family or span receiver taking `IEnumerable<T>`, a read-only span or a `T` with no return, a written span argument — which reads `Counter<K>.Increment` as the store it is and names the four members a shape reads that copy nothing (`Set<T>.IntersectWith`/`ExceptWith` remove; `Span<T>.MoveFrom`/`MoveTo` move); there are no per-declaration markers, because a census is a second statement of the rule (the register the rule replaced was the part #5717 had to amend, and it had missed `Increment`). And spec §28.3's report paragraph states the clause list rather than a per-family site enumeration: the claim's content is "every K4.3 clause", which K4's own sentence keeps enumerable, and an enumeration restated it and was the part 0157 had to amend. Declined: accepting the four over-reads as copies (a sound but needless rejection of `_set.ExceptWith(localArray)`); keeping the markers; retiring provenance clauses 1–6 and the captured-binding union by a no-binding or freeze rule (1,532 lambda-bound locals in the test tree; 0152's 32 reassigned function-typed locals).

<a id="sec-function-typed-holders"></a>
#### 4.2.3 Function-typed holders

**Function values in properties and indexers ([#5303](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5303)).** The closure bullet bars a capture-bearing function value from fields, and an auto-property's assignment is a store to its backing field, so the property spelling is the field store under another name (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *property and indexer stores*). The rule reads every property or indexer assignment of a function-typed value as a store, whatever the accessor form, and gives a block-form setter's `value` the empty set. Three alternatives were declined. *Admitting property-backed storage under an ownership or release rule*, such as a `scoped` or `owned` property or a release obligation on an auto-property's backing field, would give properties a storage tier that fields lack. A property declares neither modifier, a plain field admits only a capture-free function value, and the one tier that stores a capture-bearing callback is the `scoped` field behind a `scoped` parameter, whose bounding-object admission needs a call site that a setter does not have. *Reading the assignment by accessor form*, with an auto-property's assignment as a field store and a block-form setter's `value` as a call-bounded parameter, fails wherever the property is reached through an interface or a `virtual` member. The site cannot see which form the dispatch reaches, and an auto-property implements an interface property (spec §11.2). *Keeping the accessor-form split and barring a function-typed auto-property from implementing or overriding an interface, `virtual` or `abstract` property* closes that seam at the declaration, but it leaves a block-form setter unable to store its `value` at all, so a settable function-typed property reached through an interface could hold nothing. The cost of the adopted rule is narrow: a caller can no longer hand a capture-bearing value to a block-form setter that only invokes or forwards it.
