The rule's other face is the read ([#5602](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5602)): a function value read from a property or indexer carries the empty set, as one read from a field or returned by a method call does, because every value a getter can yield reached it through a store this rule admits only capture-free or through the getter's own return slot, a function-typed holder. Reading such a value as an unannounced source was declined: a callback stored through a property could then never be stored again, and an auto-property would read differently from the field its assignment is a store to.

**Function values in record components and primary-constructor captures ([#5377](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5377), [#5378](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5378)).** A record's function-typed component is a field, so the closure bullet's rule for fields reaches it. The record's generated constructor has no body to check and the component is declared, so construction and `with` are judged at the site as a property assignment is (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *record components of function type*), and the generated parameter carries the empty set. *Classifying the record as borrow-bearing while it carries a capture-bearing function value* was declined: spec §9.7 classifies by declared field types, and a classification that follows the values a record holds is what spec §3.6 rule 1 forbids. A class's primary constructor differs in one respect: whether a parameter becomes a field depends on whether a body references it (spec §22.10). Its plain function-typed parameter is therefore call-bounded as every other plain function-typed parameter is, and the body reference that captures it is the rejected store. *Reading the construction site as the store, as for a record,* was declined: the caller's verdict would depend on whether some body of the class references the parameter, which the caller cannot see and which changes when a method is added or removed.

**Function values in choice payloads and inline-array slots ([#5486](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5486)).** A choice's function-typed payload and a slot of an inline array of function type hold a function value exactly as a record component does, and spec §35.4 and §3.6 leave both holders classification-neutral, so the record ruling applies unchanged: the payload argument at a case construction and every store into such a slot, a local array's included, are judged at the site, and only a capture-free value is admitted (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *choice payloads and inline-array slots of function type*). *Classifying the choice or array by what it holds* is declined on the record ground, spec §3.6 rule 1. A local inline array's slots are fields too (*Function values in local collections* below).

**Function values in local collections ([#5499](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5499), [#5486](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5486)).** Reading a purely local collection as a local binding for a call-bounded callback, while the closure bullet gives a function value read from any collection the empty set, would let `var xs: Array<() -> int> := []; xs.Add(f); _field := xs[0];` store a received callback in a field. A function value is therefore not stored in any collection while its set is non-empty, a local collection and a local inline array included, following the local-record precedent (#5377). *Making a local collection or local inline array a carrier whose element reads carry its elements' union set* is declined: the collection is a value or a reference that copies into returns, fields, record components, plain arguments and `Object`, none of which announces a set, so each would have to become a sink and element reads a traced provenance; that is a design change with no measured want. Frames keep the exemption: a task or query minted by invoking a callback, or a frame carrying it, may sit in a purely local collection until it is consumed, which is the `Task` fan-out idiom (`pending.Add(cb()); await Task.WhenAll(pending);`). The cost is that a fixed table of received or capture-bearing callbacks is not kept in a collection even within one body; bind each to its own function-typed local.

<a id="sec-set-erasing-positions"></a>
#### 4.2.4 Set-erasing positions

**Function values at set-erasing positions ([#5375](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5375); proposal [0133](proposals/0133-set-erasing-positions.md), owner-accepted 2026-10-08).** A *set-erasing position* is a place that holds or converts a function value at a declared type that is not a function type: a position declared `Object`, or one declared at a bare type parameter that the instantiation substitutes with a function type or `Object`. Such a type announces no source set, so a closure that passed through it would come out with its captured borrows forgotten, read back as carrying the empty set and free to be stored and invoked after its captures have died. The rule is therefore the sink rule's: at a set-erasing position a capture-bearing function value is rejected and a capture-free one is admitted (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *set-erasing positions*). A `scoped` parameter is never a set-erasing position, being declared at a function type or at a class or interface type other than `Object`, so a callback the callee must keep goes there (spec §28.14). The three paragraphs after this one add `out` parameters, a class field declared at a bare type parameter, and an `as` at `Object`.

*The gap it closes.* Before the rule, two readings disagreed about a function value at a `T` slot. A generic body is checked once, under spec §28.1's class-or-interface assumption, where `_v := x` with `x: T` is a parameter-sourced borrow admitted under the field-store bullet's residual. The caller's closure bullet tracks the function value by its source set, which a plain `T` parameter cannot carry. A rule that names only sinks rejects on neither side, so `new Holder<() -> int>(f)` would store a closure over a scope-bound local in a field whose read "carries the empty set". `Object` erases the set the same way (spec §3.1: a function type reaches `Object` directly), and so does a function-typed parameter whose return slot is a bare `T`. With the rule, over a plain parameter `item: Item` whose `Value()` returns `int`:

```bezel
class Holder<T>
  public V: T;
  public constructor(x: T)  V := x;  end constructor;
end Holder;

var f: () -> int := lambda() => item.Value();
var g: () -> int := lambda() => 42;
using var h := new Holder<() -> int>(f);   // rejected: `x: T` at T = () -> int is set-erasing, and f captures item
using var k := new Holder<() -> int>(g);   // admitted: g is capture-free
Console.WriteLine(f);                      // rejected: `value: Object` is set-erasing
Console.WriteLine(f.ToString());           // admitted: the description prints
```

*How the rule is stated and checked.* The position that erases the set is the conversion to a type that is not a function type, so the rule names that conversion once. It is keyed on declared, pre-substitution types, as every other classification in spec §28.3 is; the bare-type-parameter arm reads the instantiation's substitution, a function type or `Object` ([#5708](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5708)). It is a derivation (K2.3 + K2.4), checked by the caller over the callee's declared signature, and the generic body is never re-checked.

*The cases around it.*

- *`yield return`.* A `yield return` of a function value is judged as a `return` is, since an iterator's element type announces no set and a terminal accessor's result (row 5) would otherwise carry an erased one.
- *Variance.* Neither variance rule of spec §27.1.1 relates a function-typed slot to an `Object` slot, in either direction, so no retyping converts a function value's results or arguments to `Object` without a site.
- *Routes with no caller conversion.* Two routes reach a `T` slot with no conversion at any caller: a method reference formed over the position, and a `T` in a parameter slot of a parameter slot. 0133 closed each on an existing precedent: the method-reference formation bar of spec §28.14, and an instantiation exclusion shaped like the `Task`-family rule. Proposal 0165 later replaced that exclusion with the depth-one bound ([§4.2.5](#sec-function-types-over-a-type-parameter)).

*What it costs.* The cost is a short list of non-storing calls that do not take a capture-bearing argument:

- `Contains`, `IndexOf` and `Remove` with such an argument would find nothing, since no collection can hold one once the routes are closed.
- `Console.Write`/`WriteLine(Object)`, `StringBuilder.Append(Object)` and `Equals(Object?)` reject it; `f = g` compares function values, and a description prints.
- `EventSource<T>.Raise` at a function-type payload rejects it; raise data and let handlers close over what they need.
- A selector `Pick<T>(a: T, b: T): T` rejects it, because its result would carry the erased set into a holder. The expression form `(if c then f else g)` keeps both sets, and a function-typed `Pick` returning a call-bounded parameter is the unbounded-tier limitation of spec §28.14 seen again.
- A received callback cannot be forwarded to a generic or `Object`-typed utility, since a plain function-typed parameter carries itself.

Declined:

- *an instantiation exclusion for generic types with `T`-typed fields*: it rejects capture-free `Holder<() -> int>` uses, misses `Bag<T>` over `Array<T>`, and misses `Object`;
- *a declaration-site rejection of storing a `T` value in a generic body unless its constraints exclude function types*: the one constraint admitting every class and interface type, `where T: class`, admits function types, so every generic container would lose its own element type;
- *a K4.3 trust residual for function values*: it reopens proposal 0126 §2 on no new evidence and makes every function value read from storage carry an unannounced source;
- *a per-member exemption for `Raise`*: the first allowlist outside spec §28.14's register, which a user `IEvent<T>` could not share;
- *`ToString`-only stdlib overloads at function types*: they buy a rendering nobody uses at the price of a surface rule;
- *`scoped` on a bare-type-parameter parameter as the remedy*: its two tiers cannot share one field kind, since a `scoped` field is never read out as a value, while a generic container's `T` field exists to be read;
- *row 5 announcing the union of its arguments' sets at a function-type substitution*: the same parameters may be stored, so the arguments must be capture-free and the announcement would announce the empty set;
- *marking a lambda literal's erased parameter slots instead of excluding the nested-slot instantiation*: sound only with a new per-binding annotation that no forwarding position may drop.

Deferred: *a body-keyed exclusion from a per-body storing fact*, with proposal 0132's fact, as its second dependent; and *exempting the return-slot arm where the member's own return is mint-announcing* (a lazy selector producing closures over a value-typed element, whose results live only in the frame), which is sound and liftable, for one uniform rule.

**The formation bar and `out` parameters; a function-typed `ref`/`out` location after the call ([#5526](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5526); proposal [0140](proposals/0140-formation-bar-out-parameters.md), delegated ruling 2026-10-08).** The formation bar keys on the slots a caller's value reaches, plain and `ref`, and exempts `out`: `var tg := d.TryGetValue;` over `Dictionary<string, () -> int>` is admitted, as its direct call always was. The widened bar (above) exists because a call through a formed reference passes a capture-bearing argument to a plain function-typed slot, which the caller side admits, while the callee receives it at a position that announces no set. An `out` parameter takes no argument value: the call passes a location, and the callee's slot is copied out on normal return (spec §22.3). Everything the callee can place in that slot arrived capture-free, through a set-erasing position, a holder, the return-slot arm or another `out`, so the formed type erases nothing the caller supplies. The same ground decides the location's set after a call. A function-typed `ref` or `out` location is a holder the callee may fill only capture-free, so after the call an `out` location carries the empty set and a `ref` location what it carried before, instead of the union of the call's borrows a class-typed location takes. Declined:

- *keeping the bar uniform over `out`*: it protects nothing at that slot and rejects a capture-free read of a collection, with a lambda as the only remedy;
- *dropping `out` locations from the set-erasing definition*: an `out o: Object` location is a real erasure site in a checked callee body (`o := f`), and the definition keys on where a set is erased, not on which slots a caller's argument reaches, which is the bar's key;
- *keeping the union at a function-typed location*: it rejected `d.TryGetValue(k, out this._g)`, and a later field store of the `out` value, for a borrow the location can never hold.

**A class field declared at a bare type parameter is a set-erasing position.** A `public` field `V: T` of a `Box<T>` written through a `Box<() -> int>` receiver (`b.V := f`) is on the set-erasing list: the store is checked at the writer, the position announces no set, and the induction that keeps an `out` location capture-free already covers every store the generic body itself can make. Without this entry the field would sit on neither list, since the holder list keys on a field's declared type being a function type, while its siblings at a bare type parameter (the parameters, return slots, `ref`/`out` locations, record components, choice payloads and inline-array slots) are set-erasing and a read of it carries the empty set (proposal 0140's critique found the gap). Declined: keying the holder definition on the field's substituted type, so that the field becomes a function-typed holder at that instantiation. The verdict is the same, the holder list is kept on declared types so that a checked body's stores classify without an instantiation, and the set-erasing list is where every substitution-made position lives, the field beside its component, payload and slot siblings.

**An `as` at `Object` is a set-erasing position ([#5675](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5675)).** The `as` is a set-erasing position in its own right, with the rejection at the conversion as for a box, and a function value narrowed back from `Object` carries the empty set, every function value at `Object` being capture-free by construction. The set-erasing list had reached its positions "by implicit conversion", while its borrow-erasing twin in spec §9.7 named the `as` and the typed-pattern binding beside the implicit conversion. A function value has exactly one explicit conversion, to `Object` (spec §3.1), and `f as Object` yields a value whose static type no function-value rule reads: the holders key on the assigned value's static type, and §17.4's "a borrow with the same source as `x`" is written for classified references, which a function value is not (K1.6). So without this entry `_o := f as Object`, `objs.Add(f as Object)`, `s.Put(f as Object)` and a callee's `o := f as Object` into a `ref o: Object?` would each carry a capture-bearing closure past the check, and the read-back `_o as () -> int` would invoke it over dead captures. Declined: tracking the set through the `Object`-typed result by flow analysis (the `Object` local declined in the box paragraph below, in function-value form), and rejecting the `as` for capture-free function values too (the set-erasing positions admit those everywhere, and `Equals(Object?)` and the description print rely on it).

<a id="sec-function-types-over-a-type-parameter"></a>
#### 4.2.5 Type parameters inside function types

**Function types over a type parameter: depth one, and the values a body hands out ([proposal 0165](proposals/0165-function-types-over-a-type-parameter.md), [#5762](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5762), [#5763](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5763)).** A type parameter nested two function types deep carries two hazards that a per-instantiation exclusion stated over a parameter's declared type (proposal 0133's, which the `Task`-family exclusion shared) does not reach: in a field, in a return slot of a function in a parameter slot, and for the iterable family in the body-to-caller direction; and — at depth one — in a function value the body hands out at a `(T) -> …` return, field or `Object` position, which the caller invokes under the call-bounded default with a capture-bearing closure, with its mirror at a mint family, a `() -> T` return whose invocation the caller keys as a mint. The rule is a shape anyone can see — a type parameter occurs in a function type at depth one only, in a generic declaration's types and every type its bodies write, with zero measured sites — and the hand-outs are closed by value: a function value a generic body forms or retypes with a type parameter in a parameter slot is bound, invoked, captured or passed, and never returned (from a lambda either), stored, converted to `Object` or to another generic's bare-`T` slot, or varied away as a type argument. Only such a value carries the hazard — every other value at a `(T) -> …` type was checked where it was formed, against the substituted type — so the declarations themselves stay free: a `(T) -> ()` callback field or return is admitted, and a declaration-site ban on them was dropped in review as redundant. A `() -> T` return, the thunk idiom with fifteen sites, stays and is excluded at the two mint families only. Declined: *a polarity rule over the member's whole declared type* (a per-position computation, and it leaves the untyped routes open, which are in the body and not the signature); *a per-instantiation exclusion for an exported `(T) -> …` slot* (the mirror of 0133 at one slot: a mechanism where the value rule serves, and the untyped routes stay open); *a declaration-site ban on a `T` parameter slot in an exported function type* (redundant with the value rule; it would reject callback fields and three test programs); *reading the hand-out as a source in the value's set* (a frame built over a selector with a `T` parameter would carry it, and no generic wrapper could return `xs.Select(lambda(t: T) => …)`); *keeping the nested-slot exclusion beside the depth bound* (redundant). 0133's declines (an exclusion for `T`-typed fields, a declaration-site store ban on bare-`T` values, a trust residual) stand.

**The keying follows the declaration through dispatch, containers and `scoped` fields; a body's bare-`T` lend stays out of `Object` ([proposal 0172](proposals/0172-function-slot-keying-through-implementations.md), [#5836](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5836), [#5798](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5798)).** The depth-one rule admits a body-formed `(T) -> …` value into a function-typed parameter and into a `scoped` field on one assumption: whoever invokes the value reads its pre-substitution slot. Three invokers would not — a concrete implementation of a generic interface member (`Feeder: ISink<() -> int>` reads `f: (() -> int) -> ()` and feeds it a closure; `Drainer: ISink<IEnumerable<int>>` keys `f()` as a mint), a caller's own container slot (`sink: Array<(Box<T>) -> T>`, invoked as `fs[0](box)` at the caller's type) and a caller invoking a public `scoped` field — and neither would the untyped hand-out proposal 0165 §10 filed: a capture-free lend at a bare-`T` return slot converted to `Object` and narrowed back to `(Box<Task>) -> Task`. Each is closed at the declaration that already carries the keying: an implementation reads such a parameter at the declaring signature, as it already reads an inherited bare-`T` return; the carve-out of a plain parameter's declared type from *Exported mint slots* is a parameter's own function type only (zero measured sites); a `scoped` field is invoked by its declaring type's bodies only (one measured site, respelled through a method); and a body-formed value with `T` at a return slot reaches no set-erasing position and no variance, as a `T`-parameter value already does not (zero sites). Declined: *pre-substitution keying at every invocation through a generic declaration's slot* (over-reaches plain `(T) -> …` holders, whose values were checked at the substituted type, and asks a caller to see a declaration behind a value it holds); *barring the body from passing its value to a member of a generic instantiated at its `T`* (rejects `_ev.Add(lambda(t: T) => …)`, the event idiom, and misses the mint twin); *a visibility rule for `scoped` fields over a type parameter* (`private` still admits a nested class holding a concrete instantiation, and repaired it is the who-invokes rule with a shape condition); *dropping the `scoped`-field exemption* (rejects the generic registrar idiom and closes nothing the invocation rule leaves open); *a caller-side lend reading of a function value narrowed from `Object` to a mint-announcing type* (keys an invocation on provenance the caller cannot see); *a per-substitution iterable exclusion at every parameter slot* (rejects `xs.Select(lambda(x) => x.Items)` at `U = IEnumerable<Item>`, which a parameter's own slot reads soundly).

<a id="sec-borrow-erasing-positions"></a>
#### 4.2.6 Borrow-erasing positions: values that hold borrows

**A box that carries borrows is the twin of a function value: borrow-erasing positions ([#5645](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5645); proposal [0146](proposals/0146-borrow-erasing-positions.md)).** Spec §9.7 rejects the store of a borrow-bearing record into a class field statically, on the posture that a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position. Boxing such a value for a call to a method accepting `Object` would hand the callee a parameter declared `Object`, which it may store under §28.3's field-store residual: `method K(x: Object) F := x;` called with a span would keep a dangling view, and `b.Keep<Object>(span)` would launder the per-instantiation rejection of `b.Keep<Pair>(p)` that the bare-type-parameter field paragraph above states. The callee cannot tell the box from a class reference, and a callee-side rule keyed on the interface's implementers is not modular (the record may live outside the callee's closure); a K4.3 trust clause would reverse §9.7's own ground. So the rule is the one this section already records for closures: a value that holds borrows reaches no position declared `Object` or an interface type, nor a bare type parameter substituted with one — the caller's check over the declared signature, K2.3 + K2.4 — with one exception that is not an erasure: a member-call receiver boxes for the call and the member reads the payload at its own type (spec §9.4). An extension on `Object` or a user interface is not such a receiver: its body reads `this` at the head type and could pass it to a storing parameter, so that receiver is an erasing position. The cost is the set-erasing rule's: `Console.WriteLine(span)` is rejected and `span.ToString()` prints. Declined: the trust clause; a callee-side no-store rule for every `Object` or interface parameter, which retires the back-reference idiom; a register of non-storing standard-library `Object` positions, which would make the twins disagree at one position and be the first allowlist outside §28.14's register that user members could not share, the ground on which *Function values at set-erasing positions* above declines one for `Raise`; `Object` locals with tracked sources. Deferred: a user-declared borrow-view interface, for which no program exists (`DECLINED.md`). The ground is the posture, not a missing backstop: a span's reads pass through its backing object's indexer and meet the flag ([§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans), [#5707](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5707)).

**No reference type is read as holding borrows ([proposal 0175](proposals/0175-retire-the-read-only-span-interface.md); [§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)'s record).** A value that holds borrows converts to `Object` or an interface at one position, the receiver of a member call, so spec §9.7's borrow-erasing bullet, §12.9, §27.1.1, §27.6 and §12.7's narrowing paragraph carry no reference-type exception. The posture — a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position — is simplest to state with no reference type to except from it.

**No route boxes such a value without a conversion written (proposal [0159](proposals/0159-holds-borrows-posture.md) option (b); [#5739](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5739), [#5740](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5740)).** Two routes would reach a declared `Equals(Object?)` or an `Object` field with no conversion in user code: the standard library's equality search, whose body contributes no requirement, and a generic body's `x as T`, read at `T = Object` as §12.7's ordinary run-time test. Both are closed by declaration rather than by a new hashable clause. No value kind declares an `Equals` at `Object?`, so the equality search reaches a record's own-typed `Equals`, the only member it reaches without boxing, the rule enums and choices already had (spec §9.2, §9.7). And the `as` or typed pattern at a type parameter is a requirement of the body's use of that parameter, read at each instantiation substituting `Object` or an interface — proposal 0156's per-instantiation posture, with a dispatched generic method deciding at its body. Declined with the proposal: retiring the storage bans on a value that holds borrows (its option (a), the retrospective review's R5) and the same retirement with a default-build warning (option (a')), each on the posture that such a value is stored past its bound at no position. The choice was soundness first: (a) would turn a compile-time rejection into a checked-mode fault, and in an unchecked build undefined behaviour, at every field, `async`, task-result and `Object` position such a value would newly reach — the posture a class borrow accepts for the back-reference idiom, here for programs the tree neither writes nor needs — while keeping the bans is never less sound than retiring them, a route the enumeration may still leave being, under the flag, at worst that posture at one position. The simplicity (a) offered is real and recorded; the reopening bar is in `DECLINED.md`.

<a id="sec-scoped-at-object-and-interfaces"></a>
#### 4.2.7 `scoped` at `Object` and at interface types

**`scoped` is not admitted on `Object` ([#5525](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5525)).** Spec §28.14 gives `scoped` two contracts keyed on the parameter's declared type. At a function type the callee may keep the callback in a `scoped` field, which is never read out, and the argument carries no call-site marker. At a class or interface type it may keep the borrow in an ordinary non-owned field, and the argument is marked `scoped x` and frozen. `Object` is the one class to which a function value also converts (spec §3.1), so a `scoped o: Object` parameter would take both kinds of argument under one declaration, and neither contract covers the other's: a callback would sit in a readable field from which a `case` recovers it, and nothing says whether it is marked. A position declared `Object` is set-erasing (*Function values at set-erasing positions* above), so the callback half could admit only capture-free arguments anyway. The declaration is therefore a compile error (spec §28.14, *Not at `Object`*). *Admitting it as a class-typed `scoped` parameter*, with the set-erasing rule rejecting a capture-bearing callback at each call, was declined: it keeps a declaration whose only distinct role at a function value is to be rejected, and the marker and storage questions for such an argument would still need answers. The remedy is the class or interface type the callee stores.

**A boxed value at an interface-typed `scoped` parameter ([#5559](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5559)).** A record, enum, primitive or copyable choice reaches an interface-typed parameter by boxing (spec §8.6), and the box is runtime-managed. A value carrying no borrow therefore leaves spec §28.14's bound nothing to protect: it is admitted under every bounding object, and nothing is frozen, the box holding a copy. A borrow-bearing value is rejected, because a `scoped` parameter exists for a store into a field of the bounding object, and spec §9.7 rejects a record-carried borrow in a class's instance field, for regime uniformity, a rule spec §8.6 applies to the boxed form. Declined: *admitting it with its carried sources tested and frozen as a closure's captures are*. That bound would be sound, since the declared-before test and the freeze make every source outlive the bounding object, but it is an exception to spec §9.7's instance-field rule keyed on a parameter modifier, and that rule's stated reason is its uniformity; lifting it is a proposal.

<a id="sec-captured-variables"></a>
#### 4.2.8 Captured variables

**Capturing a `ref` or `out` parameter ([#5534](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5534)).** A closure shares a captured variable with its scope (spec §27.5). For a `ref` parameter that variable is the caller's location, and for an `out` parameter the callee's slot for the call; neither can move into a heap cell without breaking the sharing, since a cell would hide the closure's writes from the caller and the callee's from the closure. The capture therefore holds the location, which exists for the call only, and the closure is bounded by the declaring body's scope as a closure over a captured owned local is: it may run within the call and may not leave it. This is spec §30.1's reason for barring `ref` and `out` from bodies whose frames outlive the call, applied to the closure and to any deferred-activation frame that carries it, the holders a synchronous body creates that could carry the location past the call. A task is the one such frame the body cannot consume, since its disposal does not stop it, so a task-returning call that would carry the closure is rejected there. *Capturing a copy of the value* was declined: the closure would read a stale value after the callee writes the parameter and its own writes would be lost, the one place where §27.5's sharing would silently not hold, and the copy is already one declaration away (`var snap := x;`). *Rejecting every capture* was declined as stricter than the hazard: a closure invoked or passed within the call is sound.

**Definite assignment across a closure ([#5558](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5558)).** A closure body runs at its invocations, and once the closure is bound, passed or captured the enclosing body cannot see them, so definite assignment cannot follow them. The point the enclosing body does see is the lambda's formation, and the body is analysed from the state there (spec §27.5): a captured `out` parameter or non-defaultable local unassigned at formation is unassigned in the body, and a write in the body assigns it nowhere outside. The cost is a rejection where every invocation in fact follows the assignment; the remedy is to assign before forming the lambda. One shape is admitted by name: a lambda that is the whole right-hand side of an assignment to the captured variable reads that variable as assigned, because nothing can invoke the lambda before the store. Without it a recursive closure, `var fact: (int) -> int; fact := lambda(n: int) => … fact(n - 1) …;`, the only spelling of a self-calling closure over captured state (a local's own initializer is outside its scope, spec §21.1), would need a nullable variable and a `!` at every self-call. Declined: *checking reads at the invocations*, which for a closure passed to a callee would need a fact about another body that no signature announces; and *leaving closure bodies unanalysed*, which lets a closure read an `out` slot or a non-defaultable local before anything writes it, the unset read spec §7.9 exists to exclude; and *rejecting the recursive closure's self-reference* as well, as C#'s definite assignment does, for the cost above and no soundness gain.

**A captured binding is counted by every assignment to it ([#5688](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5688); proposal [0152](proposals/0152-captured-binding-union-over-assignments.md)).** Captures share one heap cell (spec §27.5): a closure reads it at invocations the enclosing body cannot see, and a lambda body writes it at points the enclosing body's flow analysis has no edge for. So no assignment to a captured binding can be ordered against a closure's reads, and a closure's set read off the binding at its formation is wrong the moment the binding is re-pointed at a shorter-lived source — by a same-block reassignment the local-scope rule admits, by a store into a captured inline array's slot or a captured record's settable member, or by an assignment inside a lambda body, after which even the binding's own `return` reads a stale source. The escape exists for every value kind that has a null-source form or can be re-pointed. The rule is K2.2's union with the cell as the value: a captured binding has one source set, the union over every assignment to it in any body, and every reader reads that set — a per-body pre-scan, with no new mechanism. The cost is that an assignment counts against reads it cannot reach — one overwritten before the lambda forms, or one made after the closure's last invocation or before a read of the binding itself; zero corpus sites, and the remedy is a second binding. Declined: *a freeze on the captured binding after the closure forms*, which must reach inside every lambda body that captures it, keys a freeze on formation where K5 keys on liveness, and bars both re-pointing a captured binding at a longer-lived source and the in-body cache idiom (`if cache = null then cache := _store.Find(); end`), sound under the union; *rejecting only the in-lambda assignment*, with the union over the enclosing body — the cache idiom's cost again, and two rules where one serves; *a flow-sensitive union cut at the formation*, which gives one cell three position-dependent sets and buys a precision — a pre-formation assignment overwritten before the lambda forms, a read of the binding before a later assignment — that no corpus site uses; and *the status quo*.

<a id="sec-function-values-historical-note"></a>
#### 4.2.9 Historical note

*Historical note* (the v0.333 and v0.334 records). From v0.329 to v0.335 `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` was the one reference type read as holding borrows: a parameter of that type, and the `this` of an extension on it, was bounded as the span is. Because it was an interface, the language's identity-preserving retypings reached it — §27.1.1's covariant return and contravariant parameter slots, §12.9's variance at a type argument, a method reference's formation adapter — and v0.333 ([#5726](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5726); proposal [0156](proposals/0156-span-interface-retyping-is-borrow-erasing.md)) made each such retyping the borrow-erasing conversion, rejected where written, with a run-time type test not succeeding through one either. In a generic body a variant retyping over a type parameter was checked under the class-or-interface assumption, its conditions read per instantiation. Declined there: the status quo, backstopped only at run time; the type-relation exclusion the issue recommended, which needed a formation clause and a generic reading of its own; tracking the span's sources through the retyped `Object`; banning `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` as a type argument; and rejecting at the declaration every variance over a type parameter whose constraints do not establish a reference type. v0.334 (proposal 0159) closed the interface to user implementations, and v0.336 retired it, taking every span clause with it; the user borrow-view interface stays deferred.

<a id="sec-why-classes-are-non-nullable-by-default"></a>
### 4.3 Why classes are non-nullable by default

C#'s opt-in nullable reference types proved that most reference fields are non-null in practice. Bezel goes one step further: non-nullability is the default, nullable types are opt-in via `?`. The cost is `?` on a small minority of types; the benefit is that `null` is noteworthy, not the default value of every reference.

<a id="sec-allocation-failure-fatal"></a>
### 4.4 Allocation failure is fatal

When the runtime cannot obtain memory — for a `new`, a growing collection, a string, a boxed value, or a closure/iterator/async frame — the program terminates: the runtime reports the failure and aborts the process (spec §31.4). Nothing is catchable, no unwinding runs, and the disposal-at-exit pass is skipped — allocation failure joins the abnormal terminations of spec §22.13, where disposal at exit is already excused. The report's form is implementation-defined; the termination is not.

C trained generations of programmers to treat an unchecked `malloc` as a bug, so refusing to surface the failure at all deserves a defense. Making allocation failure catchable — an `OutOfMemoryException` — would turn every `new`, every collection growth, and every closure, iterator, and async frame into a potential throw site: a permanent tax on the exception control-flow graph and on the ownership checker's discharge analysis, paid by every program on every path. What the tax would buy is a handler invoked at the precise moment allocation has stopped working — and OOM handlers almost universally allocate on their way to failing. Programs that genuinely must survive memory pressure do it before the fact — bounding inputs, pooling buffers — not by catching the failure after it happens. `StackOverflowException` had already established that resource exhaustion is uncatchable and fatal; allocation failure follows the precedent.

Declined: a named uncatchable `OutOfMemoryException`, purely for report symmetry with `StackOverflowException`. The report's wording is implementation territory, so the name is cosmetic — and revisitable without breaking anything.

<a id="sec-ownership"></a>
## 5. Ownership

<a id="sec-owned-not-borrows"></a>
### 5.1 `owned`, not borrows

Rust's borrow checker enforces single-writer-or-many-readers at compile time. Most working programmers don't need that guarantee; they need "free this once and don't use it after." Bezel targets the second and skips the first.

`owned` marks a field, parameter, or return as on the disposal hook for its containing object, and the compiler tracks every owned value to exactly one discharge: owned values reach an owned location and do not escape to non-owned references. The model is stated normatively as the ownership kernel — spec [§28.3.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-ownership-kernel), principles K1–K9, with the rules the kernel cannot derive kept in the axiom register beside it ([§5.26](#sec-the-ownership-kernel) carries the reasons). What the kernel does not do is prove that no alias outlives a disposal: a plain borrow may still refer to an object that has been disposed. That case is caught at runtime by the per-instance disposed flag. Disposal is idempotent and flag-setting, and in checked disposal mode — the default build ([§5.30](#sec-checked-disposal-default)) — any later use through any alias faults deterministically as `DisposedAccessException` (K4.1). The compiler rejects statically only what the rules below single out — a silent kill under a live borrow, a fault the analysis already proves certain — and never checks that borrows are exclusive.

Trade-off: programmers can write aliasing bugs that Rust would reject. They can also write a working web server in 200 lines without `Arc<Mutex<T>>`. Two things sit outside the trade-off. Data races are not expressible in safe code: continuations are serial per domain and no mutable state is reachable from two domains ([§12.5](#sec-execution-domains), [§12.6](#sec-sendability-spawning-channels)), so what the trade-off admits is aliasing and use after disposal, both caught at runtime. And a forgotten discharge is not an error: an owned value still held at scope end is disposed there, with a must-use warning on the implicit drop of a plain `var` ([§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model)).

**The freeze.** A silent kill is rejected; an announced one is checked. Consuming an owned binding while a live local borrow of its current value exists is a compile error (spec [K5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-kernel-k5), [§28.3.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-consume-site-freeze)). The consuming positions are the consume table's, among them moving it into an `owned` argument (a domain spawn or a channel send included), transferring it with `move`, storing it into an owned slot, returning it owned, and reassigning it. A kill is exempt — unfrozen — exactly when it is keyword-visible at the kill site and the disposed flag it sets is an admissible backstop for every borrow that survives it. That two-part criterion is where this section's decline of compile-time borrow checking stops, and each placement has its reason:

- Explicit disposal (`Dispose()`, a direct `DisposeAsync()`, the discard), `throw x`, and Pattern B `await` of an owned operand stay legal under a live borrow ([K4.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-kernel-k4)): the call or keyword is the reader's notice, and the flag is the safety net. Freezing them too was declined: it would turn the trade-off above into a de-facto borrow checker for no soundness gain where the backstop is real.
- The silent kills are frozen because none of them sets a flag that backstops the surviving borrows: a transfer moves the obligation and sets no flag at all (K5.1). The case that forced the rule has no runtime backstop of any kind: an object moved to another domain stays alive, no flag is ever set, and a surviving pre-transfer borrow is a live data race (probe-confirmed); spec §30.14's isolation claim holds only with the freeze. Drop-on-overwrite does set the flag, but a bare `x := e;` announces nothing at the site, so it is frozen too (K5.2).
- The freeze applies at every consume site rather than at a subset the compiler knows to transfer, because transfer positions are not closed under composition: any `owned` argument may be forwarded to a spawn. Scoping it to domain-crossing sites was declined as leaky, unpredictable at call sites, and blind to the borrowed reassignment. It composes with spec §28.7's drop-on-overwrite: an unborrowed reassignment stays legal.
- It is body-local by construction (K5.5): liveness is a backward dataflow over one body, which is what keeps it decidable and what keeps it from being a borrow checker. Fields and collection slots are outside it (K5.6): slot aliases are not body-local, and a slot kill disposes, so the flag is real there. A slot kill by `move` is governed by the recorded trust below.
- The criterion's second part fails in exactly two recorded shapes, and there even an announced kill is frozen (K5.7, K5.8). One is a capture admitted under spec §28.14's storage-bounded admission: the stored callback is invoked where the kill cannot see it, and the admission promises a fully static bound. The other is a spec §30.12 guard, whose kills flag the guard while the surviving borrows read the lock's interior. A source a live span views is not a third shape: a span reads through its backing object's accessors, so the flag backstops it ([§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)).

Cost accepted: borrowing, then transferring, then using the borrow is rejected; the mechanical rewrite re-borrows from the new owner.

**Where the analysis already decides, the error is free.** The runtime posture covers a kill the compiler cannot place relative to a use. Where facts the compiler computes anyway decide the case, a rejection costs nothing, and five rules take that step:

- *The join rule* (K7): owned ⊓ explicitly-disposed is maybe-consumed, so a read after a conditional `Dispose()` or discard is a compile error. Per-path consumption tracking exists regardless, because per-path scope-end disposal needs it.
- *A dominated use* (spec §28.2, [proposal 0205](proposals/0205-dominated-use-after-dispose.md)). A use of a local that an explicit `Dispose()`, direct `DisposeAsync()` or discard dominates on every path is a compile error. So is a use through a local holder of a borrow of the disposed value — an alias, a `using var` pin, a span or borrow-bearing value, a closure, a frame — whose every source was so disposed. The dominance the analysis already computes and the provenance the freeze already traces (K5.5) decide the case, and the rule rejects nothing that does not fault when it runs, dead aliases apart. This is not the declined freeze: the kill stays legal under a live borrow, a disposal after the last use changes no verdict, and a kill on only some paths to a holder's use stays the checked-mode fault. A repeated disposal stays a warning, and a reassignment between the kill and the use lifts nothing for a holder of the disposed value (a closure over the variable itself reads it afresh when invoked). The certain case is not left to the flag because the flag is the checked build's: in an unchecked build, the second conformance mode, the measured straight-line shapes segfault or silently read the object that reused the block (the measurement in `docs/research/soundness/RELAXATIONS.md` §2 and its caveat). That qualifies the freeze decline's "no soundness gain" for the certain case only; the freeze decline itself stands. Declined with the proposal: a warning-only extension to borrows (the certain fault would stay a warning while the uncertain one is an error), rejecting the repeated disposal (a defined no-op), and keeping consumes at a warning while reads become errors. Residual, stated: a kill at the end of a loop body reaching the next iteration's use through a holder is not dominated and stays the fault. Freezing the reassignment that follows the kill would close it, and is declined with [#7364](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7364)'s ruling (its reading B), since that store kills nothing.
- *A use after a dominating field write* (spec §28.7, [proposal 0206](proposals/0206-dominated-use-after-field-write.md)), the same step for the one field shape whose fault is certain: a local borrow of an owned field's current value (`var b := _w;`, `var b := h.W;`) used after a disposing write of the same field — a reassignment, an explicit `Dispose()` or `DisposeAsync()` — that follows the borrow with no invalidating operation between them (§7.4's forms — no call, a constructor's included, accessor, user operator, function-value invocation or `await` — and no `yield return`, no disposal of another value, no other write to the path or its root, and no `move` of any field; the write's own right-hand side is outside the window, and a right-hand side that takes the value out is the stated over-rejection) and dominates the use; a read of the field itself after such an explicit disposal in the same window is the same error. K5.6 stands: the write stays unfrozen, since a slot alias is not body-local and a callee can write the field — which is exactly why the rule needs the window: inside it nothing can have taken the value out (the replacing `move`, §28.8.1, is the only way a displaced value survives a store, and it is a visible write of the path), so the measured unchecked read of a freed block (`docs/research/certain-faults/`, probe p10: an empty line where the first value's name should print) is the certain outcome. Declined with the proposal: freezing the write under a live local borrow (the K5.6 flip proposal 0153's option D already declined; it guarantees nothing the flag does not, and misses the field-read shape), widening the rule to holders of the borrow (an alias chain, a closure, a span over the field's buffer, a record, a path of two segments — each needs a provenance the field-source fact does not carry; the backstop holds), and the owning-collection slot leg (the certain-faults hunt's §8: it reopens proposal 0181's retirement of the element-borrow warning on a bar that is unmet, and re-adds the per-slot predicate the subtraction review removed).
- *The dispose capability* (spec §28.6): `Dispose()` requires an owned receiver, so disposing through a tracked borrow is a compile error. The rule reads only the receiver's own ownership classification, already computed at every site, and it leaves the trade-off intact: disposing an owned binding while borrows of it live stays legal. It has no carve-out. Per-element disposal through element borrows found no load-bearing client once the owning family existed ([§5.3](#sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements); axiom A8's tombstone).
- *Captured owned bindings* are borrow-state inside a closure: no consume, no reassignment, and no disposal through the capture. The alternative admitted a silent drop-on-overwrite kill that the freeze — decidable precisely because it is local to one body — could never see.

**Where neither applies, the trust is recorded.** An admission the checker can neither compare statically nor backstop by flag is either rejected or carries an individually recorded trust clause (K4.3). The case is the domain clause: a borrowed-from object transferred to, or made reachable from, another domain stays alive, so no flag is ever set. The recorded clauses are the field-held, collection-held, comparer-held and frame-held borrows, plus spec §28.14's two residuals. The plain back-reference field is the case a programmer meets first (Guide 01 §5): `_owner := owner;` is admitted on the field-held clause. These clauses are the model's honest limit, and the rule that no admission is made without recording its clause or its backstop keeps the set closed. The trust-admission report mode enumerates the set on request, naming each admission with its static remedies — `scoped`, a `SlotHandle` key, `Shared<T>`, the owning family, awaiting to settlement ([§5.31](#sec-trust-admission-report)). Shrinking the set is planned work, and its design is ruled: spec §28.15 gives a class that stores a borrow a source set read off its signatures, and its ramp retires the field-held, collection-held and comparer-held clauses in two later revisions ([§5.32](#sec-borrow-bearing-classes)). The [trust-admissions-by-kind research](research/trust-admissions-by-kind/README.md) sorts the recorded admissions by what the stored borrow is for; its largest group, short-lived helpers, is what §28.15 bounds, with no per-parameter fact computed from a callee's body — the derived retention that [proposal 0132](proposals/0132-derived-retention-study.md) studied is superseded by it. A weak reference over an ordinary owned object, for long-lived observers and registries, waits for the report's data and a proposal of its own. Calls whose body the compiler cannot see keep `scoped` as their static spelling. An opt-in strict mode comes once the report is complete. The work package is [#7509](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7509).

<a id="sec-dispose-is-not-on-object"></a>
### 5.2 `Dispose` is not on `Object`

Records, enums, and primitives don't need disposal. Putting `Dispose()` on `Object` would mean asking the user "should I dispose this `int`?" — a meaningless question. Disposal lives on classes, where the need actually exists.

Code that takes "any disposable thing" accepts `IDisposable`, not `Object`. This documents intent.

**Only disposable types have `Dispose()` ([proposal 0200](proposals/0200-dispose-member-only-on-disposable-types.md); [#7276](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7276), [#7273](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7273), [#7068](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7068)).** The owner's test — calling `Dispose` should mark the object disposed and stop operations on it — rules out a member that does nothing: a no-op `Dispose()` claims a kill it does not perform, would draw §28.2's use-after-dispose warning on a value never disposed, could not be an `IDisposable` implementation (§28.1 clause 6 excludes the runtime-managed types from the bound), and could not be written legally anyway, since §28.6 requires an owned receiver and a runtime-managed value is never owned (K1.6). Marking a string disposed is unsound: strings are shared by assignment, may be interned, and cross domains as shared references (§30.14). So the member exists exactly where disposal does — on classes and on the interfaces that are or extend `IDisposable` or declare it — and `Object`, the runtime-managed types and every other interface type have none, their owned values disposed by the discharge points alone, as an owned `Object` already was (§5.2 above): one rule, "a value exposes the members its type declares", and the discard, `using var` and scope end as the uniform remedy. The alternatives — a no-op member on the runtime-managed types; a `Dispose()` that marks a string; an implicit `Dispose()` on every interface type, the compiler's call-form behaviour, which would make the interface surface unlike `Object`'s — are `declined-at-proposal` rows.

<a id="sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements"></a>
### 5.3 Collections borrow by default; owning collections are named

The most-debated decision in this area. Three options were considered:

1. Collections always own their elements (Add takes ownership; Dispose disposes everything).
2. Borrowing collections, which hold their elements without owning them.
3. Collections optionally own via a generic flag.

Option 1 fails for collections of borrowed references, common when a class has a back-reference list. Option 3 doubles the API surface and forces the choice at type-construction time, which is the moment when the programmer least wants to think about it. Option 2 keeps collections universal, but alone it leaves a holder that owns its elements no honest spelling.

The resolution provides options 2 and 1 side by side: the borrowing types (`Array<T>`, `Dictionary<K, V>`, etc.) are the default, and the owning types are separately named (`OwningArray<T>`, `OwningDictionary<K, V>`, etc.). Users pick the form that matches their ownership intent at the declaration site. Option 3 was tried — an `<owned T>` generic-argument modifier with merged class declarations — and abandoned because the substitution machinery accumulated more complexity each revision; separate named types capture the semantic distinction directly without the substitution rules ([§5.11](#sec-owning-collections)). The borrowing default keeps its original justification: it is the right default for collections of borrows. A holding class that owns its elements commits the collection's type to ownership, which is the honest spelling of what a caller-vouched escape hatch would otherwise assert. That hatch — `DisposeElements()`, and per-element disposal through element borrows — is retired (axiom A8's tombstone): a corpus sweep (#2075) found every use whole-collection-owned and migratable to the owning family, so tracked borrows may not be disposed anywhere, with no residual carve.

Asymmetric ownership in the values-owned direction is `OwningDictionary<K, V>`'s shape by construction; the own-keys direction has no recorded client.

<a id="sec-no-shared-ownership"></a>
### 5.4 Single ownership by default; sharing is opt-in

Every owned value has exactly one owner at a time, and single ownership is the only regime the language applies implicitly: no assignment, argument, return or capture ever creates a second owner. Shared ownership exists — `Shared<T>` and `Weak<T>` (spec [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership), stdlib [§2.16](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-shared-and-weak)) — but only where a program asks for it by type, and every additional strong handle is created at its site by an explicit call — `Clone()`, or `TryUpgrade()` on a `Weak<T>`.

Single ownership is the default because the cases that genuinely need several owners are rare in Bezel's target use cases, and most of what looks like sharing is served without a count. Handle-and-table indirection is a library type: `OwningSlotMap<T>` (stdlib [§4.21](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-owning-slot-map)) owns the table and mints `SlotHandle` values whose staleness after a removal is detected rather than silently reused — a stored key in place of a stored borrow, with no count and no trust clause. Channels move a value from one owner to the next, and shared immutable data such as strings lives in the runtime-managed region ([§4.1](#sec-why-strings-are-runtime-managed)).

Sharing is available because some shapes are "N holders, last one cleans up" and nothing else: caches handing out entries, registries whose entries outlive any single owner, the multi-consumer channel question ([§12.6](#sec-sendability-spawning-channels)). For those shapes a handle table only moves the count into user code, where the compiler cannot see it. It is opt-in and spelled at every site because the count is a cost, and the language's position is that ownership transitions are visible in source. [§5.21](#sec-shared-t) gives the design: why the types are compiler-known, why the handles are ordinary owned objects, how they relate to domains, and the accepted hazard that cycles leak.

Declined, each with its reason in [§5.21](#sec-shared-t) unless stated: implicit handle duplication — an assignment or argument that shares where it would otherwise borrow or transfer (it hides the count's cost at every site, against the no-implicit-conversions discipline of [§14](#sec-operators)); a tracing collector, including any cycle detection over `Shared<T>`, which would be one by another name — the language promises not to have one ([§1](#sec-the-big-picture)); reference counting arbitrary classes through an annotation (it hides the cost and breaks the structural-facts discipline); compiler-inferred sharing by escape analysis (implicit, where ownership transitions are meant to be visible); and sharing as a third reference kind beside owned and borrowed (it would touch every join rule and every feature that dispatches on ownership state, for no expressiveness the handle form lacks).

<a id="sec-local-variable-ownership-is-inferred-not-annotated"></a>
### 5.5 Local-variable ownership is inferred, not annotated

Fields, parameters, and returns carry an explicit `owned`. Locals do not. The compiler infers each local's ownership state from how it was introduced — from the right-hand side's classification, for `using var` and plain `var` alike: `var x := new T()` is owned, `var x := someField` is borrowed, and a `using var` is owned over an owned producer but a scope-pinned borrow with no disposal obligation over a borrow ([§28.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-using-var-initialized-from-a-borrow)). The full rules are in [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows) of the spec.

The asymmetry is signal-to-noise. Annotations on field, parameter, and return positions communicate API intent across module boundaries — they need to be explicit. Annotations on every local would be redundant noise: the compiler can already see what the local was initialized from.

<a id="sec-the-lattice-direction-at-control-flow-joins"></a>
### 5.6 The lattice direction at control-flow joins

The [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows) lattice puts owned ⊑ borrowed: owned is the *stronger* state, borrowed the *weaker*. At a control-flow merge where two branches produced different states for the same variable, the meet would have to be the weaker state — but a meet between owned and borrowed at the same merge is rejected outright, because demoting owned to borrowed silently drops a disposal obligation.

The opposite ordering (borrowed ⊑ owned, with the owned branch winning at the join) is unsound: there is no value to dispose on the borrowed branch, so the merged owned slot would carry an obligation it cannot discharge. The compiler instead requires programs to equalize the branches before the merge — either produce an owned value on both branches by allocation or `Clone`-style copy, or arrange for both branches to bind the variable to a borrow whose source outlives the merge. The latter does not mean "borrow the `using var`": an owned value discharged with `using var` in a branch must do its work and be released *within* that branch; the post-merge variable takes its value from a longer-lived source, because a borrow may never outlive the source it points into (the merge inherits, rather than escapes, that rule).

This is the standard lattice-meet rule in linear/affine type systems applied to Bezel's specific kinds. It is more restrictive than the opposite ordering, which is unsound. The rule is kernel principle K7 (spec [§28.3.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-ownership-kernel); see [§5.26](#sec-the-ownership-kernel)).

<a id="sec-factory-method-ownership"></a>
### 5.7 Factory method ownership

A static method call has no structural borrow source: there is no receiver, and a static method may not re-expose static storage through a non-`owned` return. A method like `File.ReadAllLines(path)` returns a freshly-allocated `Array<string>` — semantically identical to a `new` expression — so its caller must own the result, and the only place that can say so is the signature. Spec [§28.3.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-factory-methods-and-sourceless-returns) therefore rejects at the declaration a static method or operator whose class- or interface-typed return does not type-announce its classification: `owned`, a mint-announcing type (a `Task`-family type, an iterable or enumerator interface), or a bare type parameter with a non-empty potential source set. The caller reads every static call's classification off the declared return type, with no heuristic. This completes [§5.5](#sec-local-variable-ownership-is-inferred-not-annotated)'s boundary-explicitness doctrine: ownership is explicit at signatures and inferred only for locals.

The alternative is to infer: treat an unannotated class-typed return whose borrow source the compiler cannot identify as owned, with a warning recommending the explicit `owned` return type. The language carried that rule as a bridge (axiom A1, now a tombstone in the register; [§5.26](#sec-the-ownership-kernel)), a safety net beside the preferred `owned` annotation for unannotated third-party returns and for a standard library annotated in stages. Both motives discharged. The stdlib annotation pass completed (a 2026-07 sweep found zero static signatures relying on the treated-owned classification), and the module/artifact model (spec §2.8) delivers an unannotated third-party factory's diagnostic at the third party's own build rather than silently rewriting consumers' obligations. What remained was cost: a core ownership rule memorized rather than read, and the instance↔static refactor hazard — moving a method between kinds silently flipped every call site between borrow and owned. Body inference and a call-site error were declined with reasons in [proposal 0048](proposals/0048-explicit-factory-ownership.md) §8. The per-instantiation erasure of `owned` (below) is what made the rejection affordable, making `owned T` spellable over mixed constraint domains.

*Generic signatures.* A static method whose declared return type is a bare type parameter *does* have identifiable sources: its T-reaching plain arguments. Treating `Math.Max<T>(a, b)` as a fresh-owned factory at a class substitution would make the caller a second owner of one of its own still-owned arguments (two owners, one obligation — the exactly-once invariant broken through the argument binding), while the callee-side borrow-return prohibition would make the selector body unwritable in-language. The bare-type-parameter rule (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-bare-type-parameter-returns)) classifies such calls as borrows carrying the union of the receiver's and the T-reaching arguments' source-liveness constraints. Callee-side, the bare-type-parameter return admits parameter- and receiver-sourced borrows, enforced per instantiation category (§3.6 rule 2's pattern): the announcement-by-declared-type doctrine of the iterator and `Task`-family conventions, and what makes the standard library's own `Min`/`Max`/`Clamp` writable at class substitutions. A genuinely sourceless shape (`Create<T>(): T where T: new()`) names no one responsible for the result, so it is rejected at the declaration exactly as a concrete class-typed static return is. Fresh-producing generic signatures spell `owned T`, as `OwningSet<T>.Pop(query: T): owned T` does under `where T: class` (stdlib §4.10).

Two alternatives are declined with the rule. *Post-substitution keying* — classifying each call by its substituted return type — reads plausibly at `Math.Max<Connection>` and is refuted one shelf over: `First<T>(): T` at `T = Task<int>` would substitute into the `Task`-family rule and mint a disposal obligation over a collection-owned element. The sound geometry, shared with the calls-mint rule ([§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends)), is pre-substitution *keying* with per-instantiation *enforcement*: what rule governs a call is fixed by the declaration, while the checks fire only where a substitution gives them content. *Marking selector returns `owned`* fails twice over — as engineering it is marker spam across every generic selector in every library, and as semantics it is a false statement: a selector transfers nothing, and `owned` would mint exactly the second obligation the rule exists to remove. What makes the borrow classification look inevitable in hindsight is the signature grammar it completes, one the standard library had been writing by instinct all along: bare `T` with T-reaching inputs is a **selector** and borrows (`Min`/`Max`/`Clamp`); `owned T` is a **producer** and transfers (`Pop`, `Deserialize`, and every generic factory); bare `T` with no T-reaching inputs names no source and is rejected, its fresh-producing spelling being `owned T`. A generic signature's ownership behavior is readable off its shape — the rule did not add a distinction, it recognized one the signatures already drew.

`owned` on a bare-type-parameter slot is per-instantiation (spec [§28.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-where-the-owned-modifier-can-appear)): the ordinary transfer at class and interface substitutions, erased by the substitution at value and runtime-managed ones. Rejecting the marker at value substitutions instead would leave fresh-producing generics over domains admitting value substitutions (the stdlib §2.19 numeric abstractions; `Create<T>(): T where T: new()`) with no spelling at all, pointing authors at a qualifier the checker then rejected at exactly the substitutions such domains exist for. Stdlib §2.19's producer members and spec §11.8's `Sum<T>` carry the marker, which is what opens the class-conformer arithmetic surface (a heap-backed BigInteger is the recorded prospective occupant). A second, fresh-vs-selector marker was declined — it would duplicate `owned`'s class-substitution semantics keyword for keyword, differing only in value-substitution admissibility; restricting the numeric interfaces to value conformers was declined — spec §11.8's own canonical conformer is a class, and the restriction would only defer this exact design plus a compatibility event. The generic `owned` field rejection (spec [§28.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-fields)) deliberately stands: a field marker commits the containing type's shape — destructor synthesis, derived properties — in a way a call-boundary marker does not.

*The concrete-selector cliff* ([proposal 0088](proposals/0088-concrete-static-selectors.md) stage 1, #3054). The rules above compose into a cliff: the concrete selector signature is unwritable — `static method Pick(a: Res, b: Res): Res` is rejected at the declaration (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-announcement-table)'s announcement table, the plain-concrete row's static cell: an unannounced concrete static — no type announcement in its signature — is indistinguishable from a factory, so admitting it either mints a second owner or requires the retired inference heuristic — the two-owners argument, preserved in full) — while the semantically identical generic twin `Pick<T>(a: T, b: T): T` compiles under the bare-type-parameter rule, whose bare-`T` return *is* the selector's type announcement. The sanctioned remedy is therefore to generify: a type parameter (with a constraint that keeps it meaningful) introduced for no generic reason, purchased to express a fact — "returns one of these arguments" — that the concrete signature grammar cannot announce. The stdlib's own selectors (`Math.Min`/`Max`/`Clamp`) are generic whether or not genericity means anything to them, and the declaration-site rejection's diagnostic is directed to name this remedy (compiler-lane, #3054). The announcement gap is a recorded liftable, not taken: proposal 0088 stage 2 designs the `sourced` return-position marker — the bare-type-parameter rule transcribed at a concrete type, with the unannounced signature staying rejected exactly as today — and it is gated, per the 0051 Part B evidence posture, on the first real concrete-selector want a stdlib wave or user corpus files. Auto-detection without announcement (classify any concrete static whose return type matches a plain parameter's type as a selector) stays declined on this section's own grounds: it reintroduces a heuristic where the design sentence is "read off the declared return type with no heuristic", it is shape-fragile in exactly the refactor-hazard way the heuristic's retirement closed, and it misclassifies real factories (`Clone(template: Res): Res` intends a fresh value).

<a id="sec-collection-literal-ownership"></a>
### 5.8 Collection literal ownership

A collection literal `[1, 2, 3]` targeting `Array<T>` (or another collection class) allocates a heap object. It is semantically a `new` expression with initialization — the fact that the syntax is a literal does not change the allocation behavior.

So a collection literal targeting a heap collection class is an owned production in the producers table, under the same discharge rules as `new`: the literal creates a fresh object, so the binding must own it. The fresh nodes of a nested literal are owned as one aggregate by the literal's owner ([§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking)'s literal-tree rule). (A collection literal targeting an inline array `[N]T` produces a value-typed inline array, with no heap ownership; it participates in the move-only category only when the inline-array slots are themselves `owned`.)

<a id="sec-trivial-dispose-classes"></a>
### 5.9 Trivial-dispose classes

Not every class holds resources. `Random` holds only an internal seed. `StringBuilder` holds a buffer that is plain memory. The `Encoding` singletons hold nothing at all. Demanding `using var` for `new Random()`, or warning without it, would add ceremony for a no-op dispose.

The runtime-managed exemption ([§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-managed-types) of the spec) covers `string`, `Type`, boxed values, and function types — types the language itself creates implicitly and pervasively — but it is a fixed list, and joining it permanently is the wrong model for user classes: what a resource-free class needs is an exemption that is *computed from its declaration* and tracks the class as it changes.

That is the resolution: **trivial-dispose** is a derived type property (spec [§3.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-derived-type-properties), [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-trivial-dispose-classes)). A class with no destructor, no `owned` fields of non-trivial-dispose type, no owning-inline-array fields, and a trivial-dispose base has a disposal that provably runs no user code and releases only memory, and the compiler drops the ceremony — never the tracking: a bare `var r := new Random()` compiles clean and the discard `_ := new StringBuilder();` is legal and purely cosmetic, while the instance keeps its statically-known owner and is freed by compiler-inserted code at that owner's release point (spec §28.9; no runtime lifetime mechanism is involved — [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking) is the decision record). Nothing is demanded *of the program*, so there is nothing to warn about, not even at reduced severity. The exemption is over ceremony only: no runtime reclaim mechanism for trivial instances exists, and [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking) records why.

The class-gains-a-destructor hazard is answered by recomputation, not by ceremony at every use site. Adding a destructor, an `owned` field of non-trivial-dispose type, or an owning-inline-array field ([§15.1.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owning-inline-arrays) of the spec) flips the classification, and formerly-silent sites immediately draw the standard obligations — the must-use auto-drop warning, a discard that now actually runs a destructor. The flip changes ceremony only — both sides are tracked, and the free-or-dispose point is the owner's release point either way — but it is source-affecting by design, which is why spec [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-trivial-dispose-classes) directs API authors to treat a public class's trivial-dispose status as part of its published contract. `using var` remains accepted (naming the owner explicitly, with a free that runs no user code) for code that wants to be robust against the flip in advance.

**§9.7's bullets in a generic body are read per instantiation, never at the declaration under the class-or-interface assumption ([#5629](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5629)).** [§5.7](#sec-factory-method-ownership)'s keying/enforcement split decides this too. A record over a type parameter is classified at each instantiation (spec [§9.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-records-and-disposal)'s declared-field-types paragraph, [§3.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-derived-type-properties) rule 2), so a body's store, copy, capture or return of a `Box<T>` or of a bare `T` gives a bullet nothing to read until a substitution makes the value hold borrows. The ruling on an `owned T` or `where T: value` return ([#5616](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5616), [#5620](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5620)) is one instance, and spec §9.7 states the reading once for every bullet and for a type formed over a parameter. The declaration reading — classifying `Box<T>` as borrow-bearing because `T` may be a class, and rejecting `Wrap<T>(a: T): Box<T> => new Box<T>(a)` outright — was declined: it rejects `Wrap<int>` and every generic pair-builder for a borrow that exists at no value substitution, the declaration-site over-rejection §26.5.1's `First` example already refutes. The cost lands on dispatched generic methods, whose bodies decide requirements at the body ([§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-dispatched-generic-methods)): an override storing its bare-`T` parameter into an `Object?` field is rejected at the body unless the declaring method is bounded `where T: class` (or `where T: sendable`, over values). That verdict already followed from §14.2's body rule read with spec [§8.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-boxing)'s *Boxing of borrow-bearing records*; proposal 0141's survey, which recorded the two banked bodies of that shape as unchanged, weighed only §8.6's move-only half. The verdict stands on spec §9.7's posture that a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position, not on a missing backstop: a span's buffer reads do meet the flag ([#5707](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5707); [§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)'s record). Excluding value types that hold borrows at every call of a dispatched generic method, as 0141 excludes move-only types and inline arrays, would keep those bodies (zero measured call sites at such a type) but is declined: it reverses 0141's recorded choice to leave borrow-bearing arguments admitted, and rejects the read-only bodies at an ordinary record argument that this reading leaves untouched. It needs its own proposal amending 0141; the reopening bar is recorded with the decline.

<a id="sec-the-move-expression-on-owned-fields"></a>
### 5.10 The `move` expression on owned fields

`move` is the one owned read of an owned field or an owning slot ([K9.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-kernel-k9)). `move x.f with e` (and `move coll[i] with e`) yields the location's current value, owned, and stores the owned replacement `e` into it in the same step, with no intermediate state; where the location's declared type is nullable, the clause may be omitted and the bare `move x.f` stores `null` (spec [§28.8.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-move-expression)). So no location ever holds a hole (K9.2): a non-nullable owned field or slot holds a live value at every instant, a nullable one a live value or `null`, and *moved-from* is a state of bindings only. The same verb at a named operand, `move x`, is the transfer marker that consumes a local binding or an `owned` parameter at every marked position (spec §28.3, §28.8.1; [§3.10](#sec-call-site-transfer-marker)). Static owned storage admits no `move` (K9.4): it is the always-live source every borrow of it may be held against as process-lived.

Plain field reads produce borrows (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows)), because fields are *retained*: a class instance exists to be acted upon multiple times, and the natural operation on a field is "read while leaving the value in place." Every plain field read promises the same thing — the field's value is still there afterward.

A language with only that read has no clean form for the standard pattern "replace a resource and return the old one" (a transactional update on an owned field): the nearest spelling is a `Utils.Swap`-via-`ref` exchange of the field with a freshly-built local, and some shapes, such as batch-aggregating owned task results, have no workaround at all. The take-out operation exists for that pattern.

The design choice is to make the move *explicit*. A bare `var x := _resource;` produces a borrow; only the explicit `move _resource with …` form transfers ownership out. Three reasons for the explicitness:

1. **Visibility in code review.** A reader scanning a method body learns the local effect of each line. With explicit `move`, the take-out is spelled at the read site; the reader does not have to reason about subsequent lines to discover that the read was actually a take. This matches Bezel's existing preference for syntactic visibility — `using var` declares scope-bound disposal at the binding site, `owned` declares ownership transfer at the type position, `out` declares output at the call site. `move` is the analogous keyword for the take-from-field operation.

2. **Refactoring stability.** A version of the rule that *inferred* take-out from the pattern "read followed by reassignment" would be sensitive to insertions between the two lines. Adding a `Log(...)` call between read and reassignment, or extracting the reassignment into a helper method, would silently change whether the read was a borrow or a take. Programs that compiled would compile differently after small edits. Explicit `move` makes the take-out a user decision; inserting a log call between the move and the reassignment does not change the move's semantics.
