3. **The exception-path obligation.** A take that left the location empty until a later store would make every throwing edge between the two an exit on which the field holds a hole. The fix is the *build-before-take* discipline — construct the replacement value first, then exchange — which is the same discipline RAII code in C++ and transactional code in functional languages already commits to. The replacing form makes the discipline syntactic: the replacement is an operand, evaluated before the exchange, so the wrong order cannot be written, and reasons 1 and 2 are served at least as well, the replacement being spelled at the same site as the take.

The three reasons argue for an explicit action verb at every take-out site, not only at fields, which is why `move x` is also the spelling for consuming a named binding ([proposal 0090](proposals/0090-transfer-marker-study.md); [§3.10](#sec-call-site-transfer-marker)'s reversal record). Static fields stay outside it, on K9.4's ground above.

**Why an exchange, not a take-and-restore window ([proposal 0174](proposals/0174-replacing-move.md); [#5887](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5887), [#5888](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5888), [#5889](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5889)).** The alternative design takes the value and leaves the location *moved-from* until a restoring store, which must then be proved to happen on every exit path: a restoration rule over the definite-assignment machinery, an inventory of which expressions may throw inside the window, and a vacated state that code inside the window can meet. Every question that design raised was a question about what may happen *inside* the window — what a call, an `await`, a `yield return` or a constructor body run by the restoring `new` observes at the vacated location, and whether the restoring store lands in the slot the move vacated once the index, the holder or the collection can change. Narrowing the window answered each question and raised the next: measured by the third simplification review, the window family was edited in every one of ten consecutive revisions and tied for the largest root-cause count of the three completeness matrices. The exchange answers them by having no window. What the type system says is shorter and stronger: K9.2's invariant above, with no restoration rule, straight-line window, throwable inventory, vacated state, per-slot tracking at a stable index, stable holder or collection freeze — and no state store for them (proposal 0143's; a borrowing-match binding over `h.Slots` reads the replacement after `move h.Slots with fresh`, §9.4's overwrite posture). The spelling is `with`, a reserved word whose tie-break with its two other uses §9.5 already states; no keyword. The migration was measured before the decision: 170 one-line respellings and 50 deleted `:= null` lines (about 30 of the respelled sites are rejection tests of the window itself, which lose their ground rather than keep it), 19 feature-test rows of the hole-until-disposal shape at a non-nullable slot newly rejected (`PopAt`/`PopValue`, `with fresh` or a nullable declaration), 22 reject pins and one rule case flipping to accept the take of a nullable field, whose only ground was K9.2's restoration rule. Declined (proposal 0174 §7): an explicit `with null` at every nullable take, a library exchange primitive (`ref` names no owned location), a new keyword, the status quo, and the bare take of a non-nullable location left empty until its holder's destruction (the window again, with one restoration).

*Historical note: the narrowings that preceded the exchange.* The window's rulings and declines are kept here because the exchange answered them by removal, not by reversal. The throwable inventory's boundary questions ([#3240](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3240)) were ruled for the restoration analysis ([#3080](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3080)'s exception-edge classifier): a `new` was not analysis-throwable — a throwing constructor completes no object, so the restoration analysis loses nothing on that edge, and the hazard the throw does create, the partially-constructed object's owned state, is the constructor's own unwinding territory (spec §22.9; [§5.29](#sec-destructor-body-not-on-ctor-unwind)) — and the opposite reading, which would have outlawed `var old := move _r; _r := new Res();`, then the commonest restoration idiom, was declined; and a compound assignment (and `++`/`--`) was throwable exactly when its §6.6/§17.6 desugared form is, a principle chosen over a list patch for `/=` and `mod=` because alike spellings classify alike and no second enumeration can drift. The first narrowing ([proposal 0163](proposals/0163-restoration-window-straight-line.md); [#5773](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5773), [#5774](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5774), [#5775](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5775), [#5776](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5776)) admitted only the non-throwable forms and `new` between a `move` and its restoring store, withdrew the `finally`- and `catch`-placed restorations, restored a `move coll[e]` only at a constant or unreassigned-local index, and gave a vacated field or slot §9.4's disposed state under K4.1's checked-mode fault; the owned-field cycle the disposal matrix found (a moved slot's value stored into a field of the object it owns) is recorded beside `Shared<T>`'s, with Appendix C.3's acyclicity assumption made explicit. Declined with it: the run-time sentinel with the `finally` form kept (a fault where a compile error is available); banning `new` in the window (the #3240 ruling); a null-guarded restoring store (throwable, against build-before-take); the conservative whole-collection state kept with same-index restoration (a second tracking mode for one-line rewrites); and a static rejection of the body-local cycle shape (a new analysis for zero sites). The second ([proposal 0170](proposals/0170-restoration-window-stable-holder-frozen-collection.md); [#5833](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5833), [#5843](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5843), [#5796](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5796)) made the index local and the holder binding stable over the window and froze the collection binding in a collection window, because a structural member, a closure- or `ref`-written index, `Clear()` or a rebound holder let the restoring store land somewhere other than the vacated slot. Declined with it (proposal 0170 §6): a run-time fault alone where a compile error is available, a tested restoring store (throwable, against build-before-take), `Clear()` kept with a vacated-tolerant cascade, the holding local's overwrite as a restoration, by-object holder tracking, and an `await using` admitted inside a straight-line window.

**The destructor's bare take is retired ([proposal 0177](proposals/0177-destructor-bare-take-retired.md); [#5913](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5913)).** No body takes a non-nullable owned location bare, a destructor body included (K9.3), so K9.2's invariant holds without exception. A destructor exemption would be the last location-level *moved-from* state, and it has a face no per-body rule reaches: a derived destructor taking an inherited `protected owned` field bare, read afterwards by the base destructor through the field's own spelling in another body. Every static repair priced — rejecting a use of `this` after the take, ordering the take after the body's last use of `this`, confining the take to fields the destructor's own class declares — left a face open, a callee reaching the object through a stored borrow or a closure capturing `this` among them, because the hole is in the object, not in the body; a run-time representation of a moved-from field would be a second null-like state at a non-nullable location, tested at every read. Nothing a site defends is lost: every measured take disposed the taken value at the body's end, which the cascade does unprompted; an early release is the field's explicit `Dispose()` (§28.6's early release point, the cascade's later call the no-op); and a transfer out of a destructor takes a nullable declaration, the remedy the exchange gives everywhere else. Declined (proposal 0177 §7): the status quo, the per-body rules, the own-class restriction, and a run-time moved-from representation.

<a id="sec-owning-collections"></a>
<a id="sec-owned-element-collections"></a>
### 5.11 Owning collections

The owning collections are separately named classes: `OwningArray<T>`, `OwningLinkedList<T>`, `OwningSet<T>`, `OwningStack<T>`, `OwningQueue<T>`, `OwningDictionary<K, V>`, plus the owning task type `OwningTask<T>` and the owning iteration interfaces `IOwningEnumerable<T>` / `IOwningEnumerator<T>` (and async variants), all first-class types in the standard library. The borrowing collection types keep the plain names (`Array<T>`, `Dictionary<K, V>`, …), and the `owned` modifier does not propagate through generic type arguments (axiom A14).

The alternative is one generic family with an ownership argument: an `<owned T>` instantiation form, in which `Array<owned Connection>` is a distinct type from `Array<Connection>` and owns its elements, admitted for an opt-in list of generic types. The language had that form and abandoned it for the reasons below; spec [§14.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-as-a-generic-argument) now states that `Array<owned T>` is not a type ([§5.3](#sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements)).

**Why separate types.** The `<owned T>` mechanism accumulated language machinery: an opt-in list, conditional inheritance semantics, substitution rules and form-conditioning constraints, an inheritance-projection rule. Each addition fixed a specific issue and introduced a corner case for the next revision to address. At its fullest the mechanism had three substitution rules, two new constraint forms, a special inheritance-projection rule, and open concerns about how future features (LINQ over owning collections, user-defined owning containers) would interact with it.

The underlying observation: an owning collection's `Add` takes ownership of its argument; a borrowing collection's `Add` takes a borrow. These are *genuinely different operations*, not two specializations of one operation. Trying to express the difference as a parameter modifier substituted through a single generic forced two unrelated semantics through one syntactic vehicle. Separate types capture the semantic distinction directly.

**What was lost.** The `<owned T>` mechanism let one generic algorithm work over both ownership forms. A `Where<T>(items: IReadOnlyList<T>)` would accept both `Array<MyClass>` and `Array<owned MyClass>` via type inference (T inferred as `MyClass` and `owned MyClass` respectively, with the inheritance projection stripping owned at iteration points). With separate types the generic still accepts both — `Array<T>` and `OwningArray<T>` both implement `IReadOnlyList<T>` — but T is always `MyClass`, not parameterized over the ownership form.

For read-only access (the common case), this is no loss: the generic works identically. For *mutating* algorithms over either kind, the user picks one mutating interface (`IList<T>` for borrowing; `IOwningList<T>` for owning) and parameterizes over that. Code that wants to abstract over both mutating forms isn't expressible — but such code is rare and would face the same semantic dilemma the language faced ("does `Add` take ownership?") just at the user's call site rather than at the language layer.

**What was gained.** Substantial simplification:
- The language spec [§14.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-as-a-generic-argument) opt-in mechanism, substitution rules, and constraint forms are gone.
- The `IEnumerator<owned T>` destructive-Current rule becomes a normal interface declaration (`IOwningEnumerator<T>` has destructive Current by its own definition; `IEnumerator<T>` doesn't).
- User-defined owning containers are now expressible via composition over `OwningArray<T>` (or another owning primitive), without any opt-in mechanism.
- The Dictionary "four combinations" complication (the merged form admitted `Dictionary<owned K, V>`, `<K, owned V>`, `<owned K, owned V>`) collapses: `OwningDictionary<K, V>` covers the one realistic case (V is owned; K is value-like).
- The `Stack.Pop` and `Queue.Dequeue` "return-type form-conditioning" mechanism of the merged form becomes a simple separate declaration on the owning class.

**User-defined owning containers.** A user who wants their own owning container builds it by composition over an `OwningArray<T>` (or another owning primitive) — the same composition that language spec [§14.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-as-a-generic-argument) now carries as its worked example:

```bezel
public class OwningCache<T> where T: class
  private owned _items: OwningArray<T>;

  public constructor() _items := new OwningArray<T>(); end;

  public method Add(owned item: T) => _items.Add(move item);   // transfer marker per spec §22.3
  public method Drain(): IOwningEnumerable<T> => _items.Drain();
end OwningCache;
```

Every line is ordinary current-spec code, and each carries the semantics it names. The `owned` field puts the cache's destructor cascade onto the array — and, through it, onto the elements. `Add`'s parameter is a named owned binding, so forwarding it into the stdlib's `Add(owned item: T)` writes the call-site transfer marker, `_items.Add(move item)`, spelling out that the call consumes `item` (§3.10; spec §22.3). The `Drain()` wrapper follows the same convention the stdlib's own `Drain()` uses: the `IOwningEnumerable<T>` return type is itself the transfer announcement — type-announced (spec §28.3's iterator-factory convention — callers discharge the enumerable, typically through the `for` loop's implicit `using var` source binding, and each yielded element arrives owned in the loop variable). Spec §14.4's copy of this class spells the member `Drain(): owned IOwningEnumerable<T>` ([#6586](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6586)). The two spellings are the [announcement table](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-announcement-table)'s rows 2 and 3, which differ in spelling only: both are admitted, at every callee kind, with one contract. This copy keeps the unmarked spelling, which the stdlib's own `Drain()` declarations use. The user's class needs no special opt-in or compiler magic — `OwningArray<T>` is a primitive whose semantics are spec-defined, and the user inherits those semantics by storing one. The question the merged form left open (how can user-defined types opt in to owning generics?) is answered by simplification rather than by adding more language.

**LINQ over owning collections.** Deferred ([§18](#sec-things-deferred)), on grounds the separate types simplify. Under the merged form the design questions (does `Where`'s predicate take a borrow or owned? what does `Select`'s output look like for owning sources?) interacted with the substitution rules in complicated ways. With separate types, LINQ extension methods can be declared in two families — one over `IEnumerable<T>` (borrowing), one over `IOwningEnumerable<T>` (owning) — with appropriate signatures for each. The work is mechanical: each LINQ method gets two declarations. The owning family is added when there is a sufficiently complete design.

**Disposal order is stated over storage, not insertion (spec §28.11's rule).** The two coincide for a collection built by appending alone and diverge as soon as a positional ingress has been used — `OwningArray.Insert(0, x)` makes the newest element the first index, and `OwningLinkedList.AddFirst`/`AddBefore` do the same for the chain. After `OwningArray.Sort` or `Reverse` insertion order is not merely different from storage order but not recoverable from the collection at all: these types store elements, not arrival order, so an insertion-keyed rule would name an order no conforming implementation can produce. Storage order is observable, computable at every point in a collection's life, and equal to the older term wherever the older term was well defined.

<a id="sec-pattern-b-loosening-and-iterator-yield-refinement"></a>
### 5.12 Pattern B loosening and iterator-yield refinement

Two ownership rules are stated over the positions an owned value can reach, not over one syntactic form: the Pattern B consumer rule for an owned-result task (language spec [§30.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-result-lifetime-and-task-disposal)), and the iterator-yield-ownership rule for owned class-typed values (language spec [§29.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-desugaring-of-for)'s "Iterator yield ownership" subsection). Each replaced a narrower first statement whose inconsistency surfaced once an owning aggregator (today's owning `WhenAll` overload) had to be written end to end.

**Pattern B admits any owned-consuming context.** The await of an owned-result task must reach a position whose type system requires the owned value to be discharged. The narrower statement — the await must be the immediate operand of `using var` or `await using var` — had a clear intent: ensure that the owned result reached an owned location with a definite discharge path, and the `using var` form was the canonical such location.

But the `using var` form is one of several owned-consuming contexts the type system already recognizes. An owned-consuming context is any position in the AST where the surrounding expression's type system requires an owned value to be discharged: `using var` is one; an `owned` parameter of a method call is another; an `owned` return expression is a third. In each of these contexts, the type system already enforces that ownership is discharged after the value reaches that position — the same property `using var` provides. The Pattern B rule was distinguishing a single owned-consuming context (`using var`) from the others without a principled reason.

The cost of the narrower rule shows in the aggregator: `results.Add(await task)` is a natural shape, type-correct under the broader rule, and rejected under the narrower one. The workaround is an intermediate `using var t := await task; results.Add(t);` — three statements where one would do, with the intermediate variable serving no purpose other than satisfying the rule.

So the rule admits any owned-consuming context. The safety property is the same: every owned-consuming context is one whose type system already requires the value to be discharged, so the rule still prevents owned values from being silently dropped. The broader rule only admits more syntactic positions where the await can appear.

**The iterator-yield-ownership rule splits on the declared return type.** A single rule — an iterator yielding owned class-typed values has the enumerator own each yielded value, with the consumer's iteration variable a borrow valid only for the iteration body — is right for an iterator whose contract is to let the consumer look at each value. It is wrong for a drain: `Drain()` on every owning collection is an owning iterator, and spec [§28.11](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-collection-element-ownership) makes the consumer's iteration variable from `coll.Drain()` owned. One rule cannot say both.

The owned-class-typed-yield rule therefore has two cases, distinguished by the iterator's declared return type:

1. **Iterator returns a borrowing enumeration interface (`IEnumerable<T>`, `IEnumerator<T>`).** The enumerator owns each yielded value, and the consumer borrows. This is appropriate when the iterator's contract is "let the consumer look at each value briefly" — filtering, formatting, dispatch.

2. **Iterator returns an owning enumeration interface (`IOwningEnumerable<T>`, `IOwningEnumerator<T>`, and the async forms).** Ownership transfers from enumerator to consumer at each `MoveNext`. The consumer's iteration variable is owned and is discharged in the loop body. This is the rule that makes `Drain()` work.

The split is determined at compile time by the iterator method's return type. The same physical implementation (a state machine that pops elements from the source one at a time) compiles to different consumer-side semantics depending on which signature it presents.

The split rule's complexity is justified because the two cases address distinct use patterns. A filter iterator that yields owned values (perhaps because it constructs them on the fly) and wants the consumer to borrow each one briefly is one valid pattern; a drain iterator that yields owned values and wants the consumer to take responsibility is another. Forcing both into one rule would require either making every consumer-borrow scenario also accept the discharge obligation (adding ceremony to filtering use cases) or making every consumer-owned scenario accept the borrow restriction (preventing drain use cases entirely). The split lets each pattern be expressed naturally.

The marker discipline reaches yield position: an owning iterator's yield of a named owned binding is a marker-free consume (the declared return type is the announcement, the owned-return parallel) that joins the consume-site freeze — a yield transfer hands the value alive, so it has no disposed-flag backstop and cannot ride the throw/dispose/discard exemption — while a borrowing iterator's unmarked named-binding yield is a borrow (nothing announces a kill; the enumerator-owns model is scoped to fresh producers and `move` results, and the adopt-then-yield spelling is the warning-free named form). A yield-position transfer marker was declined as grammar without need and stays on the recorded liftable-marker list with the arm positions. Pattern B await is the site-announced-kill family's fourth member (keyword + single-consumer type + disposal-sets-flag), and Pattern A's chained `return await` is unfrozen for the structural reason that the outer task cannot dispose the chained source before the body completes.

The error-message-side complexity is small: an iterator's static signature is known at the call site, and the compiler can produce a clear "this is an owning enumeration — the iteration variable is owned" message when the consumer fails to discharge.

**Owned-parameter iterables are single-consumption ([proposal 0167](proposals/0167-owned-parameter-iterables-are-single-consumption.md); [#5766](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5766)).** An iterator takes an `owned` argument into its state machine and disposes it when the iterator is disposed (spec §29.8), while an `IEnumerable<T>` result is in general re-iterable (§29.3). Without a rule saying whether the parameter belongs to the enumerable or to each enumerator, a second `for` over one bound result re-runs the body over an argument the first enumerator disposed — or, in §29.8's own `using var r := move reader;` shape, moves it twice. Three answers were weighed: make the result single-consumption under §29.11's affine rule; let the enumerable own the parameter and bar the body from moving it; admit `owned` parameters only at the single-use enumerator types. The first is taken. It is one sentence that reuses a rule the language already has — §29.11 already named "a lazy or single-pass source whose backing is itself external, such as a streaming reader" — the argument such an iterator consumes is a reader, a connection or a handle, consumed once, and the compiler already raised `InvalidOperationException` on a second `GetEnumerator()` for the conditionally-discharged shape. The second would reject §29.8's warning-free adoption idiom in every re-iterable iterator; the third would change 87 declarations to keep a replay no program used — five test programs consumed such a result twice, none of them from a guide, the corpus or the reference. A re-enumerable view takes a borrow: `Echo(r)` over `using var r := new Res();`, or materialization at a value element type.

<a id="sec-no-block-expressions"></a>
### 5.13 No block expressions

Bezel does not provide a value-returning `do ... end` form. `do ... end` is a statement-sequencing block with type `()` everywhere it appears — in method bodies, in `case` arms at statement position, in `for` and `while` bodies, and as the body of a multi-statement lambda. Multi-statement logic in expression-position contexts (most visibly, `case` arms inside `var x := (case ...)`) is written as an extracted helper method or a local lambda, both of which produce values.

The absence is a decision, not a gap awaiting a later revision: the ergonomic cost is real, but the answer is to leave it. The reasoning, briefly:

**The alternatives all carry real costs.** We considered four shapes for a value-returning block expression:

1. *Rust-style trailing-expression-without-semicolon* (`do { stmts; final-expr } end`). This fights Bezel's mandatory-semicolon rule ([§1.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-statements-and-termination)). A trailing expression without a semicolon, in a language otherwise strict about semicolons, would be a foreign element learners would trip over.

2. *Explicit `yield expr;` to produce the block's value.* This reuses `yield`, which is already a keyword for iterator methods (`yield return`, `yield break`). Code inside an iterator method that also contained a block expression would have two scopes the `yield` could target, creating a resolution rule learners would need to track.

3. *A new keyword introducing the block* (`expr ... end`, `compute ... end`, or similar) with `return` exiting the block. This requires `return` to have scope-local semantics — exits the innermost enclosing return-bearing scope — which is one more rule on top of method/lambda return scoping. It also expands the language's keyword surface for a use case the helper-method extraction already covers.

4. *A new keyword for the value-producer statement* (`give expr;` or similar) inside `do ... end`, with the block becoming value-returning iff a `give` appears. This introduces a new keyword and creates a third interpretation of `do ... end` (the value-bearing one), partially undoing the consistency the current design has.

Each option has at least one cost we judge larger than the cost of the helper-method extraction it would replace.

**The helper-method extraction is good design independently.** Pulling a multi-statement arm body into a named method gives the per-arm logic a name. Names are valuable: a reader can scan the `case` and see what each arm does without descending into the body. The extracted helper also localizes any side effects the arm performs, which is the harder thing to track — far harder than the syntactic awkwardness of writing the helper. A spec that requires extraction is steering toward better-structured code.

**The use case is uncommon.** In practice, `case` arms are short — a literal, a method call, an arithmetic expression. Arms with substantial logic are rare; when they occur, they usually benefit from a name. Adding language machinery for a rare case is poor leverage.

**The single-meaning `do ... end` is itself a feature.** Across the language, `do ... end` does one thing: sequence statements. It introduces a fresh scope and has type `()`. This consistency makes the construct easy to learn and easy to reason about — wherever you see `do ... end`, you know what it does. Admitting a value-returning variant, regardless of which option above, would make the construct's meaning context-dependent: sometimes value-less, sometimes value-bearing, with the distinction visible only by reading the body or surrounding context. Single meaning beats two meanings here.

The [§19.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-arms) documentation reflects this decision: `do ... end` is described as statement-sequencing, the helper-method extraction is the recommended pattern for multi-statement arm bodies, and no "future revision" promise is carried.

**The bare-statement arm form is not a block expression.** Beside the `=> Expression` and `=> do … end` forms, an arm in statement-position `case` and a `catch` arm may take a third body alternative, `=> Statement`. It removes the ergonomic friction of writing `=> do _x := y; end` for a one-line assignment-shaped arm body; the spelling is `=> _x := y;`. It does *not* relax this section. The four declined designs above were all ways of producing a value from a multi-statement block; the bare-statement form is a syntactic convenience for the single-statement case in arm bodies that already accept `do … end` (which has type `()` and produces no value). No value-producing construct exists; `do … end` retains its single meaning; the helper-method extraction is still the idiom for multi-statement arms in expression-position `case`. The question this section settles — whether to add value-returning blocks — was re-examined when the arm form was added, and the same conclusion was reached: the four candidate designs still carry the same costs, the helper-method extraction still has independent merits, and `do … end`'s single meaning is still worth preserving. The arm form answers a different question (single-statement ergonomics in arm bodies), which this section did not consider.

**The binding atom is not a block expression.** The `(var x := E; body)` atom (spec §17.15; rationale [§3.12](#sec-the-binding-atom-names-a-subexpression)) names subresults inside one expression; it admits no statements — a clause is a binding, the body is one expression, and no effectful statement form can be spelled inside it. The four declined designs above were all ways of producing a value from a *statement sequence*; this section's conclusion — `do … end` has one meaning, multi-statement logic in value position is extracted into a named helper — stands unchanged.

<a id="sec-inline-arrays-and-array-kind-split"></a>
<a id="sec-inline-arrays-and-the-array-kind-split"></a>
### 5.14 Inline arrays and the array-kind split

Bezel has two array kinds: inline arrays `[N]T` and heap arrays (`Array<T>`, `OwningArray<T>`). There is no third, fixed-size runtime-managed `T[]` form between them. The split has three motivations and four supporting design decisions.

**Foundational implementability.** Without a user-level form for per-slot ownership, the standard library's owning collections (`OwningArray<T>`, `OwningDictionary<K, V>`, etc.) would be compiler intrinsics whose slot-take operations on the backing storage could not be expressed in pure Bezel — the language's most safety-critical primitives in a "trust the compiler" position with no path for users to verify or extend. The `[N owned]T?` form makes the *per-slot ownership-transfer operations* — the slot `move` — expressible at the user level, which suffices for fixed-capacity owning containers. Resizable owning collections like `OwningArray<T>` and `OwningDictionary<K, V>` also need *runtime-sized backing storage*, which `[N owned]T?` cannot give because it requires a compile-time N; `OwningBuffer<T>` is a class wrapping runtime-sized owning storage with the same per-slot semantics extended to runtime-determined length ([§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)). The intrinsic surface of the entire owning-collection family therefore reduces to one well-scoped primitive (`OwningBuffer<T>`) plus the inline-array form (`[N owned]T?`); every resizable owning collection is expressible over these in pure Bezel.

**Disposal-discipline clarity.** A `T[]` form allocated with `new` but runtime-managed (no `Dispose`) would straddle two categories — an inconsistency users would have to learn, since every other heap-allocated reference type in Bezel has a real `Dispose` contract. With the split, heap arrays (`Array<T>`, `OwningArray<T>`) are classes with real disposal, inline arrays are value types stored inline, and arrays need no runtime-managed exception. The runtime-managed types are `string`, `Type`, function values and boxed values (spec [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-managed-types)), which are genuinely interned or runtime-tied at the implementation level in ways an array is not.

**FFI ergonomics.** Inline arrays have C-equivalent layout (packed elements, no header). An array form with a Bezel-level header would need marshalling to convert to C arrays at FFI boundaries. With inline arrays `[N]byte` and similar, a Bezel `extern method foo(buf: [16]byte)` declaration matches `void foo(char buf[16])` in C with no marshalling. This is a real performance and clarity win for FFI-heavy code, and verification had repeatedly cited it as a gap.

The supporting design decisions:

**The array-kind split.** There are two array kinds: inline `[N]T` (value-typed, fixed size at compile time, no allocation) and heap (`Array<T>`, `OwningArray<T>`, class types, real `Dispose`). Users pick by use case: "I want a value-typed buffer" picks inline; "I want a resizable container that can grow" picks heap. A separate "heap fixed-size" type (à la `FixedArray<T>`) is deliberately not provided — heap arrays support fixed-size discipline by not calling resize methods. The taxonomic simplification keeps two array kinds (one syntactic form per kind) rather than four.

**Move-only value types.** Inline arrays with `[N owned]T?` or `[N owned]T` slots own their slot contents (the per-slot reference is non-null on `[N owned]T`, and may be null or non-null on `[N owned]T?`). Copying such an array would duplicate ownership of those contents, so copying is forbidden — the array is move-only. "Move-only value types" are a small type-system category, scoped tightly: the owning inline arrays and the owning-classified choices are its two members (spec [§15.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-move-only-value-types); the choices are [§9.6](#sec-choices-why-a-new-kind)'s and spec §35.4's). The concept is foundational (move semantics for owning slots are unavoidable) and its rules are localized to those forms. An inline array without the `owned` marker is copyable, so its element type may not be move-only, and `[N]C` over an owning choice is rejected (spec §15.1). Admitting it as a third move-only form would need the move-only slot category that [§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans) defers for buffers, for no program that needs it; a class holding the choice, in an `[N owned]` array, is the spelling. `T?` at a type parameter substituted by an inline array ([#5511](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5511)) would form the nullable inline array spec §15.1 admits for no written type, so it is rejected at the instantiation site, as the nested form is, and a standard-library member whose signature forms it at the element type is conditionally admitted (spec §14.2), the receiver type staying well-formed. Admitting the formed type as a substitution result was declined: it would be a type no expression spells, with no rendering distinct from the element-nullable `[3]int?`, and spec §17.4's `as` rule rests on its absence; a generic that follows §7.10's advice to store `T?` therefore admits no inline-array argument, and `Channel<[3]int>` has no non-blocking receive, the costs the decline records. Reading a Try member's `T?` out as a non-nullable `T` assigned `default(T)` at an inline-array element (stdlib §1's shape (c)) was declined as well: an inline array of a non-defaultable element, `[2]string`, has no `default` (spec §17.11), and the `*OrNull` tier has no such reading. The reopening form is a spelled nullable inline-array type.

**Collection literals.** The literal `[1, 2, 3]` is contextually typed: it produces whatever target type the context names (`[3]int`, `Array<int>`, etc.). A literal in a position with no target type is a compile error, which makes the construction site explicit about what's being built. The contextual typing reuses the same machinery as `null` (untyped null) and is the right cost-benefit tradeoff for keeping the literal syntax both flexible and unambiguous.

<a id="sec-owned-buffers-and-spans"></a>
### 5.15 Owned buffers and spans

Runtime-sized owning storage is one standard-library class, `OwningBuffer<T>`, not new language syntax, and the views over it, over `Array<T>` and over `string` are five span records, not a new type kind: the buffer- and array-backed spans get their lifetime tracking from the borrow-bearing-record rule the language already has. A span access is bounds-checked against the span's own `Length` and, through a buffer- or array-backed span, flag-checked as a member access on the backing object; low-level operations go through FFI rather than an `unsafe` keyword, and no interface unifies the spans. The section also records the standard library's conditionally-admitted members ([§5.15.9](#sec-buffers-conditional-members)).

**The shape.** One owning buffer type and five span records provide runtime-sized buffer storage and views over it:

| Type | Role |
|---|---|
| `OwningBuffer<T>` ([§4.14](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)) | Owning fixed-at-construction storage. Class T: slots are owned `T?`. Value T: slots are `T` directly. |
| `Span<T>`, `ReadOnlySpan<T>` ([§4.14](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)) | Views over `OwningBuffer<T>`. Read-write and read-only respectively. |
| `ArraySpan<T>`, `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>` ([§4.15](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-arrayspant-readonlyarrayspant)) | Views over heap `Array<T>` (borrowing collection). Uniform mutation API across class/value T because slots are borrows. |
| `ReadOnlyStringSpan` ([§3.3](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-readonlystringspan)) | Read-only view over a contiguous range of code points in a `string`. |

No interface unifies the spans: the read-only span interface that once did is retired (*The read-only span interface is retired*, below; stdlib [§4.16](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-ireadonlyspan) keeps its stub). All spans are records carrying a borrow of their source (`OwningBuffer<T>`, `Array<T>`, or `string`). The buffer- and array-backed spans are borrow-bearing records — the rule of language spec [§9.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-records-and-disposal) gives them lifetime tracking through existing machinery — while the string-backed span's source is runtime-managed, so §9.7 classifies it as *not* borrow-bearing: it carries no tracked lifetime constraint, which is exactly why spec §30.1 exempts it from the async parameter/result restrictions. Indexer access is bounds-checked against the span's `Length`, and flag-checked as a member access on the backing object (*Span reads are flag-checked*, below). Span construction is non-allocating record construction; the standard entry points are `AsSpan` / `AsReadOnlySpan` methods on the source (`string.AsSpan()`, `Array<T>.AsSpan()`, `OwningBuffer<T>.AsSpan()`, etc.) and the explicit constructors.

The remainder of this section gives the reasons for each piece and the alternatives declined. Readers wanting the operational details should follow the §-references into the standard library document.

<a id="sec-owning-buffer"></a>
#### 5.15.1 `OwningBuffer<T>` and the category split

`OwningBuffer<T>` (a class wrapping runtime-sized owning storage) and the buffer-backed spans `Span<T>` (read-write) and `ReadOnlySpan<T>` (read-only) admit both class-typed and value-typed T. Together they complete what inline arrays start ([§5.14](#sec-inline-arrays-and-array-kind-split)) — making the owning-collection family expressible in pure Bezel — and add a safe, no-`unsafe`-keyword escape hatch for working with raw storage of either category.

**What the buffer is for.** `[N owned]T?` admits per-slot ownership semantics at the user level for fixed compile-time N. Resizable owning collections — `OwningArray<T>`, `OwningDictionary<K, V>`, `OwningSet<T>`, etc. — need runtime-sized owning storage, and no language syntax expresses "a class field of N owning slots where N is a constructor parameter." Without the buffer, the per-slot operations would be expressible in user Bezel but the *underlying runtime-sized buffer* would not, and the resizable owning collections would stay compiler intrinsics.

**Why a class, not new language syntax.** A natural alternative would have been to extend inline arrays to admit a runtime-sized form, perhaps `[?owned]T?` with `?` denoting "runtime-determined." That would have been one additional language construct. Adding a stdlib class is the smaller intervention: the class is one new type, its surface is the standard indexer-and-move pattern that already applies to owning collections, and the language adds nothing new. The intrinsic boundary stays inside the standard library rather than expanding into the type-system primitives.

**The class/value category split.** For class-typed T, slots are `T?` (nullable references), the buffer owns each non-null slot's referent, the slot `move` is admitted, and the destructor cascades. For value-typed T (primitives, records, enums, value-like and borrow-bearing choices, non-move-only inline arrays) the semantics simplify substantially: slots are `T` directly (no nullability wrapper); the setter just overwrites; the slot `move` is not admitted (values have no ownership to transfer); the destructor performs no per-slot work, only releasing the buffer's allocation. The two presentations are different specializations of the same generic class — the compiler resolves T's category at instantiation and emits the appropriate semantics.

The motivating use case for value T is binary buffers: `OwningBuffer<byte>` for file-format parsing, network protocols, image headers. A class-only buffer family would exclude `byte`, and the workarounds (use `Array<byte>`, copy data, etc.) all impose costs the design should not require. The honest answer is to admit value-typed T at the language-design level rather than ship a class-only buffer family.

The cost of the generalization is a small amount of additional design machinery: the slot type and several method signatures dispatch on T's category. The compiler must distinguish at instantiation time between "T is a class type" and "T is a value type". This is *type-parameter-category-dependent specialization*, a kind of generic specialization Bezel has nowhere else. It is admitted as a compiler-known behavior specific to these three types (`OwningBuffer<T>`, `Span<T>`, `ReadOnlySpan<T>`); it is not a general user-available mechanism. The one generalization taken since is the choice classification, spec §3.6's second scoped exception (see [§9.6](#sec-choices-why-a-new-kind)); any further one is not committed work.

**Why move-only value types are not yet admitted.** The move-only value types are two ([§15.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-move-only-value-types)): the owning inline arrays and the owning-classified choice. The owning inline arrays `[N owned]T` and `[N owned]T?` are value-typed but move-only (rationale [§5.14](#sec-inline-arrays-and-array-kind-split) and language spec [§15.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-move-only-value-types)). A buffer slot containing a move-only value type would itself be move-only — the slot setter would need to be `set(owned)`-equivalent for the inline-array contents, with the previous slot's owned slots being disposed before the new is set. This is implementable but adds a third category to the slot-semantics dispatch (class T, non-move-only value T, move-only value T) without unlocking a clearly-motivated use case. Deferred. Users who need a runtime-sized array of fixed-size owning arrays today can express it manually with `OwningArray<MyOwningBoxClass>` or similar wrappers.

**Why `OwningBuffer<T>`'s slots are nullable for class T.** The class-T buffer's slot type is `T?` rather than `T`. The slot take `move buf[i]` at a nullable slot yields the owned value and leaves `null` in the same step (spec [§28.8.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-move-expression)). Non-nullable slots would require every take to supply a fresh owned `T` (`move buf[i] with fresh`), which is inconvenient when the slot's intended state is "empty after take." Resizable owning collections need precisely the "empty after take" semantic — `OwningArray<T>` shrinks its used range without disposing the underlying buffer, `OwningDictionary<K, V>`'s buckets routinely hold null between rehashings. The nullable form serves these naturally. For value T the question doesn't arise — `move` isn't admitted, so there's no "empty after take" state to express, and slots are `T` directly.

<a id="sec-spans-are-records"></a>
#### 5.15.2 Spans are records, with no `unsafe`

**Why `Span<T>` and `ReadOnlySpan<T>` are records, not a `ref struct`.** C#'s `Span<T>` is a `ref struct` — stack-only, cannot escape to the heap. This gives it the strongest possible lifetime guarantees but introduces a new type kind that pervades the language (rules for where ref structs can appear, how they interact with generics, etc.). Bezel's spans are records carrying a borrow of an `OwningBuffer<T>`; the existing borrow-bearing-record rule ([§9.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-records-and-disposal) of the spec) gives them lifetime tracking through machinery the language already has. The trade-off: Bezel spans participate in the standard borrow-lifetime tracking — they can be locals, parameters, and return values; a span may not be stored in a class field (spec §9.7's posture: a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position — the ban does not rest on a missing backstop, since a span's reads do meet the flag, the *Span reads are flag-checked* record below); a span *local* can also cross `await` points when its source is co-resident in the same async frame — an owned local, `using var`, or `owned` parameter of that method (spec §30.1); a span may not be an `async` method's parameter or result, since a caller-side source cannot be shown to outlive the state-machine frame (#1438). Programs whose buffer lifetimes cannot be proven sufficient are rejected by the borrow tracker. The C# comparison has narrowed on both sides: neither language admits a span-typed class field, and C# 13 now admits span locals inside async methods though never across an `await`; what the record-plus-borrow-tracking form still buys is the frame-resident case — a Bezel span local may cross an `await` when its source lives in the same async frame — with the lifetime tracker, not a type kind, enforcing the constraint. This trade follows the same logic as inline arrays ([§5.14](#sec-inline-arrays-and-array-kind-split)) — prefer existing mechanisms (borrow-bearing records, lifetime tracking) over new type-system primitives.

**Why no `unsafe` keyword.** C# pairs `Span<T>` with `unsafe` blocks for the cases where the developer needs raw-pointer access. Bezel's path is different: the user-facing surface never exposes raw pointers, pointer arithmetic, or untyped memory access. `OwningBuffer<T>` allocates through `new` (a normal class constructor), indexes through a bounds-checked indexer, and disposes through the standard destructor cascade. `Span<T>` indexes through the buffer's indexer. Operations that would need `unsafe` in C# — direct memory writes, byte-swapping, type punning — go through FFI: declare a C function that does the work and call it. The FFI surface ([§32](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-foreign-function-interface) of the spec) already accepts inline arrays as FFI-boundary types, so the C interop is direct. The cost is one extra named function per low-level operation, versus a free-for-all `unsafe` block; the benefit is that Bezel's user-facing language remains fully checked.

**What this does not solve.** Two deliberate gaps remain in the current span family:

1. *Stack-allocated runtime-sized buffers* (C# `stackalloc`). Bezel's inline arrays handle fixed-size cases; runtime-sized stack allocation would require new language machinery and a story for how the stack-bound lifetime interacts with closures. Deferred — currently low priority.
2. *`Span<T>` over inline arrays `[N]T`*. Would need a new borrow-source kind tying the span's lifetime to a stack frame, with rules for closure capture, exception unwinding, and recursive calls. Substantial design work for a narrow performance gain. Deferred indefinitely; revisit if a specific need arises.

<a id="sec-span-surface"></a>
#### 5.15.3 The span surface and its bounds checks

**The read/write split.** `Span<T>` is the read-write view; `ReadOnlySpan<T>` is the read-only view. `Span<T>` has an indexer setter that delegates to the underlying buffer's owned-setter. The mechanism is the standard one: the span carries a borrow of the buffer, and calling a setter through a borrowed reference is the same operation as calling any instance method on a borrowed reference. No new lifetime rules are needed. `ReadOnlySpan<T>`'s absence of a setter is what makes it usable as an API-boundary contract for "this code will not mutate the underlying buffer through the span."

**The span surface.** The spans carry enough surface for common use cases without going through the underlying buffer: `IsEmpty`, `IndexOf`, `Contains`, `SequenceEqual` (search and equality on both span types); for-loop integration (a direct-indexed-loop desugaring rule in language spec [§29.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-desugaring-of-for), parallel to the inline-array rule). For class-typed `Span<T>`: `Clear`, `MoveFrom`, `Reverse` (bulk mutation by ownership transfer; no `Fill`/`CopyFrom` because class references cannot be implicitly cloned). For value-typed `Span<T>`: `Fill`, `CopyFrom`, `Reverse` (bulk mutation by value copy; no `Clear`/`MoveFrom` because values have no "empty" state and no ownership transfer). The category-dependent mutation API is a direct consequence of the ownership-vs-value-semantics distinction.

**Byte-buffer accessors.** When T is `byte`, the spans are commonly used for binary I/O — file formats, network protocols, image headers, serialization. Extension methods provide endianness-aware reads and writes for the 16/32/64-bit integer types (signed and unsigned) and IEEE 754 floats — the read tier on `ReadOnlySpan<byte>` and `ReadOnlyArraySpan<byte>` (*The read-only span interface is retired*, below), the write tier per writable span (stdlib [§4.14](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-byte-buffer-accessors)). The methods name the endianness — `ReadInt32LittleEndian`, `WriteUInt64BigEndian`, etc. — rather than taking an endianness parameter or relying on a platform default; both alternatives invite the class of bugs where someone forgets that a particular format uses a specific byte order. Each method has a `Try*` variant that returns `false` instead of throwing on insufficient input, for incremental parsers that treat short input as end-of-stream rather than as an error. No offset parameter is provided; callers slice first (`header.Slice(8).ReadInt32LittleEndian()`), keeping the access model uniform with the rest of the span API. The implementation is pure byte-shuffling for integer types and routes through `BitConverter` ([§2.12](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-bitconverter)) for floats. The choice to put `BitConverter` in `System` rather than gating it behind FFI keeps the user-facing language free of bit-level intrinsics while still admitting the operations that serialization, hashing, and exact float comparison need.

**Span-level bounds checking.** Each span indexer checks the index against the span's own `Length`, not only against the underlying buffer's length. A delegating expression body — `public property [i: int]: T? => Buffer[Start + i]` — would check the index only against the buffer: a `Span<int>` with `Length = 5` over a 100-element buffer would let `span[10]` silently succeed, because `Buffer[Start + 10]` is within the buffer's bounds. The whole point of having a separate `Span<T>` type is to enforce the range restriction the span advertises, so each indexer has an explicit block-form body with a span-level bounds check:

```bezel
public property [i: int]: T?
  get => do
    if i < 0 or i >= Length then
      throw new IndexOutOfRangeException();
    end;
    return Buffer[Start + i];
  end;
  set(owned) => do
    if i < 0 or i >= Length then
      throw new IndexOutOfRangeException();
    end;
    Buffer[Start + i] := value;
  end;
end;
```

The same check applies to `Span<T>`'s setter (class T and value T), `ReadOnlySpan<T>`'s getter (class T and value T), and the slot `move` through a span (which bounds-checks against `span.Length` before delegating to the underlying buffer). The bounds-check semantics align with C#'s `Span<T>`/`ReadOnlySpan<T>` and with `ReadOnlyStringSpan`'s.

<a id="sec-string-spans"></a>
#### 5.15.4 String spans

**String spans.** The `ReadOnlyStringSpan` type (stdlib [§3.3](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-readonlystringspan)) is a read-only view over a contiguous range of code points in a `string`, parallel to `ReadOnlySpan<char>` but with a string source instead of an `OwningBuffer<char>` source. The natural companion to `string.AsSpan()` for zero-allocation substring operations — tokenization, parsing, prefix/suffix checks — without materializing intermediate strings. The use case is the same one that motivates C#'s `ReadOnlySpan<char>` over `string`.

The design choice that took time was whether to extend `ReadOnlySpan<char>` to admit a second source type (alongside `OwningBuffer<char>`) or introduce a separate type. Extending the existing type would either change its record shape (breaking its existing callers) or require a general source-polymorphism mechanism (an `IBufferSource<T>` interface or similar) that one new span type should not introduce on its own. The separate type — `ReadOnlyStringSpan` — leaves `ReadOnlySpan<char>` unchanged, makes the source visible at API boundaries, and uses the existing borrow-bearing-record machinery for lifetime tracking (the span's `Source` field carries a borrow of the string).

The cost is that two type names cover the "read-only view of char sequence" space — `ReadOnlySpan<char>` for buffer-backed views, `ReadOnlyStringSpan` for string-backed views. Methods that want either need overloads or to be written against the more common type (typically `ReadOnlyStringSpan`, since most char views in practice come from strings). C# avoids this through implicit conversion from `string` to `ReadOnlySpan<char>`; Bezel's nominal record typing makes the conversion explicit. The friction is real but bounded — most parsing code is written against one type or the other, not both.

**No ASCII byte span ([proposal 0038](proposals/0038-retire-ascii-byte-span.md)).** A read-only byte view over an ASCII string — `ReadOnlyAsciiByteSpan`, parallel to `ReadOnlyStringSpan` but with `byte` elements, constructed via a validated `string.AsAsciiByteSpan()` / `TryAsAsciiByteSpan(out)` — is not in the library; it shipped and was retired as a changed-circumstances removal (the full record is the v0.188 changelog entry, [`Bezel_Changelog.md`](Bezel_Changelog.md)). The FFI half of its motivation never materialized (a borrow-bearing record has no C-compatible form — language spec [§32.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-ffi-compatible-types) admits only the §32.13 C-compatible records, which spans are not), the I/O half is served by span-destination encoding (`Encoding.GetBytes(s, destination: Span<byte>)` into a caller-reused buffer — every string, unconditional, zero per-call allocation), nothing consumed or implemented the type, and its admitted-but-unchecked positional constructor was the standard library's only construction surface documented as silently lossy on misuse. Should profiling evidence ever demand a zero-copy ASCII byte view again, the sanctioned re-addition shape is a *validated-only* view — construction APIs only, no public unchecked constructor — a purely additive stdlib change.

<a id="sec-array-spans"></a>
#### 5.15.5 Array spans

**Array spans.** The `ArraySpan<T>` and `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>` types (stdlib [§4.15](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-arrayspant-readonlyarrayspant)) are span views over an `Array<T>` source, parallel to `Span<T>` and `ReadOnlySpan<T>` (over `OwningBuffer<T>`) but with a borrowing-collection source instead of an owning one. The natural companion to `Array<T>.AsSpan()` for passing a sub-range of an array to a method without copying.

The mechanics diverge from `Span<T>`/`ReadOnlySpan<T>` in one substantive way: `Array<T>` slots store borrows, not owned values. This changes the slot semantics in three places: (a) the indexer return type is `T` directly (the array's element type), not `T?` (no nullability wrapping — the array stores what it was instantiated with); (b) the setter takes the value with the default parameter convention rather than `set(owned)` (no ownership transfer; no dispose-previous); (c) `move arrSpan[i]` is *not* admitted for any T (borrowed slots have no ownership to transfer).

The mutation API simplifies as a consequence. `Span<T>` splits its bulk-mutation surface by T's category — `Clear`/`MoveFrom` for class T (which has owned slots), `Fill`/`CopyFrom` for value T (where copying is well-defined). `ArraySpan<T>` provides a single uniform surface — `Fill`/`CopyFrom`/`Reverse` — for both categories, because borrowed slots have no ownership constraints regardless of T. The category-dependent split was a consequence of ownership semantics that `Array<T>` doesn't have.

The choice of a separate type (vs extending `Span<T>`/`ReadOnlySpan<T>` to accept an `Array<T>` source) is the same one made for string spans: extending the existing types would require source polymorphism, which would either break their record shape or require a general source-polymorphism mechanism the span family does not otherwise need. The separate-type pattern is the established Bezel approach for span family expansion, and the family is five records: `Span<T>` and `ReadOnlySpan<T>` (`OwningBuffer<T>`-backed), `ReadOnlyStringSpan` (`string`-backed), and `ArraySpan<T>` and `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>` (`Array<T>`-backed). Methods that want to operate on "any span" need overloads — Bezel's nominal record typing doesn't admit implicit conversions between them. The friction is real but bounded; most code is written against a specific source type.

The `Array<T>` class itself ([§4.4](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-arrayt)) has eight span factories: four `AsSpan` overloads (no-arg, `(start)`, `(start, length)`, `(r: Range<int>)`) returning `ArraySpan<T>`, and four matching `AsReadOnlySpan` overloads returning `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>`. `AsSpan` returns the writable form by default (matching `Array<T>`'s mutable nature); callers wanting read-only either call `AsReadOnlySpan` directly or call `.AsReadOnly()` on the writable form.

A structural difference from the `OwningBuffer<T>`-backed spans is worth noting: `Array<T>`'s `Count` can change during the array's lifetime, while `OwningBuffer<T>`'s `Length` is fixed. A span's range is set at construction and does not auto-update if the underlying array resizes. The intended usage pattern is short-lived spans — pass the span to a method that reads or writes through it without resizing the source. Programs that need a stable view across mutations should copy the relevant range into a fresh container. The stdlib [§4.15](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-arrayspant-readonlyarrayspant) prose flags this with a "Caveat" paragraph; the design accepts this footgun rather than designing around it (the alternative — making spans observe array resizes, or rejecting span construction over resizable sources — would either require new language machinery or eliminate the use case entirely).

<a id="sec-span-copy-move-and-factories"></a>
#### 5.15.6 Copy, move and the span factories

**Symmetric copy and move, and the buffer's span factories.** Two conveniences complete the span surface: symmetric CopyTo/MoveTo methods, and `AsSpan` / `AsReadOnlySpan` factories on `OwningBuffer<T>`.

The CopyTo/MoveTo methods parallel the CopyFrom/MoveFrom methods. Each concrete span type's mutable form has a symmetric method:

| Source | Method |
|---|---|
| `Span<T>` (value T) | `CopyTo(destination: Span<T>)` |
| `Span<T>` (class T) | `MoveTo(destination: Span<T>)` |
| `ReadOnlySpan<T>` (value T) | `CopyTo(destination: Span<T>)` |
| `ArraySpan<T>` | `CopyTo(destination: ArraySpan<T>)` |
| `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>` | `CopyTo(destination: ArraySpan<T>)` |
| `ReadOnlyStringSpan` | `CopyTo(destination: Span<char>)` |

Each is equivalent to the corresponding CopyFrom/MoveFrom on the receiving side — `source.CopyTo(dest)` is `dest.CopyFrom(source)`. Both directions are provided because the natural call form depends on which side the caller holds. Tight processing loops typically write `dest.CopyFrom(source)` when the destination is the new local being filled; pipeline-style code typically writes `source.CopyTo(dest)` when chaining transformations left-to-right. Neither form is strictly necessary; both reduce friction.

The `ReadOnlyStringSpan` CopyTo destination is `Span<char>` — the OwningBuffer-backed writable form, since there is no writable string-backed span (strings are immutable).

`ReadOnlySpan<T>` for class T does not get CopyTo. Reading from a read-only class-T span produces a borrow; writing into a Span<T> for class T requires ownership transfer (the `set(owned)` setter); the two are incompatible. Code that needs this pattern moves through the underlying buffer or uses a different ownership model entirely.

There is no "copy from any read-only span" member. The destination type varies by source family — `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>.CopyTo` takes `ArraySpan<T>`; `ReadOnlySpan<T>.CopyTo` takes `Span<T>`; the string-family form takes `Span<char>` — and the class-T-borrow vs class-T-owned incompatibility (above) means a copy cannot be uniformly defined across element types: there is no class-T destination span that accepts borrowed-source content via copy. Code that wants "copy from any read-only span" writes an extension method against the concrete span types, accepting one destination shape per source family.

The `OwningBuffer<T>.AsSpan()` / `AsReadOnlySpan()` family is eight methods (four overloads each, parallel to `Array<T>.AsSpan()` and `string.AsSpan()`). The methods replace `new Span<T>(buf, 0, buf.Length)` boilerplate at every span call site, completing the three-way symmetry across the standard collection sources (string for `ReadOnlyStringSpan`; Array for `ArraySpan<T>`/`ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>`; OwningBuffer for `Span<T>`/`ReadOnlySpan<T>`).

`OwningArray<T>.AsSpan()` was deliberately omitted. The class would need either a new span type with owned-slot semantics matching its element-ownership model, or exposure of its internal buffer through a public method. Neither is justified by current demand. Users with an `OwningArray<T>` who want span access can copy the relevant range into a fresh `OwningBuffer<T>` and call `AsSpan` on that — at the cost of one allocation. If real demand for a direct OwningArray span emerges in user code, the missing type can be added.

<a id="sec-span-reads-are-flag-checked"></a>
#### 5.15.7 Span reads are flag-checked

**Span reads are flag-checked ([#5707](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5707); proposal [0153](proposals/0153-span-reads-are-flag-checked.md)).** A read or write through a buffer- or array-backed span is an instance member access on the span's backing object, raising `DisposedAccessException` in checked mode when that object is logically disposed and undefined in unchecked mode, as every stale member access is. That is the mechanism the span bodies declare: this section and stdlib §4.14/§4.15 give the span records indexers that read `Buffer[Start + i]` / `Source[Start + i]` through the backing class's own indexer, a member access spec §28.2's checked mode faults on. The opposite reading — spec §9.7 naming "a span's buffer window" as the canonical access path with no per-access disposed-flag check — had grounded the instance-field ban, §30.1, a kernel exception for spans (K5.9) and the compiler's unguarded span paths, and the two readings could not both stand. Nothing is added: §28.2's flag test already sits on member entry, its quarantine latitude already keeps the flag readable, and a span holds its backing *object*, never a raw pointer (which is why stdlib §4.15's shrink caveat throws rather than reading stale storage). The cost is one flag load per span access in checked builds, in front of the two bounds checks the read already performs, and nothing in unchecked builds. What follows: K5.9 is withdrawn, as proposal 0149 had pre-committed ("if it is decided for the check, K5.9 and its rationale exception are withdrawn with the decision") — its only ground was the missing backstop, and §5.1's decline of freezing explicit `Dispose()` applies to a span as to any borrow once the backstop is real; K5.5's frame-local trace stays, since a silent kill sets no flag. The rules that had cited the missing backstop — §9.7's instance-field and borrow-erasing bullets, §8.6, §30.1's across-suspension rule, §30.5's `Task<T>` row — stand on the posture they also stated, that a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position; no verdict moved, and the declines whose reopening bars asked for exactly this backstop (§4.2's trust clause for an `Object` parameter's store; the `Task<T>` result rows) are kept on that posture rather than reopened. §9.7's collection bullet says what #5691 needs: its store still admits no residual, and a kill of a carried source after the store is §28.11's collection-held clause. The four span routes the borrow-flow matrix re-run found open (#5706, #5691, #5710, #5709) are runtime-checked under the clauses their class-borrow twins already sit in: K5.6's slot posture, §28.11's collection-held clause, the field- and frame-held clauses, §30.1's settlement clause. Declined: keeping the no-check reading with the stdlib bodies restated as models, which leaves the through-a-call slot route (`frame.ScanLine(10)` then `frame.Reset()`) with no body-local static rule; a receiver-mutation freeze (the borrow checker [§5.1](#sec-owned-not-borrows) declines, in miniature); a same-body slot-overwrite freeze under a slot-sourced span local (a span-only key on K5.6); a K4.3 clause for the slot route (none is needed); tracing a local collection as a K5.5 holder (the collection-held clause is the home). Deferred, with a bar: retiring §9.7's instance-field ban for spans, now that a span field could take the field-store residual — kept on the stated posture because the source-field remedy expresses every measured program, and reopened by a measured program it does not.

<a id="sec-span-interfaces"></a>
#### 5.15.8 No span interface

**No writable span interface.** The writable side has substantive semantic differences across span families — `Span<T>` for class T has a disposing `set(owned)` setter; `ArraySpan<T>` has a non-disposing `set` setter taking a borrow; value-T setters are plain copies. Unifying these under a single writable interface would either pick one model and exclude the others, or fork the contract by source kind, defeating the unification. A partial writable interface (covering only the non-owned-slot writable spans) may be added if a real use case emerges.

**Regularization pass ([proposal 0123](proposals/0123-stdlib-regularization-batch-2026-09.md)).** Three decisions on the span families, recorded with the one keep they re-affirmed. *(1) The byte read tier is not mirrored per span type.* The pass replaced four mirrored blocks with one `extension IReadOnlySpan<byte>` (stdlib §4.14): §26.2 admits interface heads, §26.5 rule 2 reaches every conformer, and the receiver box was the same call-bounded box §8.6 sanctions for an interface argument and for the receiver of an extension call with that head — the accessors read `Length` and the indexer and return values, retaining nothing. The write tier stays per writable span because no writable interface exists (above). The single declaration went with the interface: the tier is now declared on the two read-only byte spans (*The read-only span interface is retired*, below), half the duplication the pass removed. At the pass the cost was the interface's dispatch, on the receiver side of thirty-two members (devirtualisation where the static receiver is concrete being an optimisation the language leaves open), and making the accessors `pure` ran through the deferred pure-body `for` over `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` (§9.7; `DECLINED.md`) and its interface-typed-source rejection. *(2) The array family's concrete cross-mode `SequenceEqual` overloads are retired* (stdlib §4.15): they were residue of the array spans' first surface, shipped before the interface existed, and the interface form was then the recorded cross-source path; keeping them with a sentence recording the direct-dispatch keep was the alternative and was not taken, because two rules for one question in one document is the defect. (Cross-backing comparison is now declared per span, below.) *(3) `Encoding.GetString`'s array window is retired for slice-first* (stdlib §3.2), the same subsumption its span twins stated; `String.From`'s window stays with its reason in stdlib §1 — `Array<char>` has no span-shaped route to `string`. *The keep:* the both-directions `CopyFrom`/`CopyTo` and `MoveFrom`/`MoveTo` surface was re-asked at this pass and stands on the *Symmetric copy and move* paragraph above — the natural call form depends on which side the caller holds — with no new evidence against it; stdlib §4.14 points here.

**The read-only span interface is retired ([proposal 0175](proposals/0175-retire-the-read-only-span-interface.md)).** `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` bought two things: one parameter type over any read-only span whatever its backing, and one declaration of the byte read tier reaching four receivers (the regularization pass above). Being a reference type whose values stood for a borrow-bearing record, it needed a clause in every rule that keys a borrow on a value's kind: it was the one reference type read as holding borrows, its retypings were borrow-erasing conversions, it was closed to user implementations and an `as` over it at a type parameter was a per-instantiation requirement, a flow-narrowing of it was read at the declared type, and the equality search compared its boxes by identity — about 2,800 normative words across the two documents and five proposals, for a declaration economy of thirty-two lines, while its own design called the virtual dispatch it introduced the slower path and recommended the concrete types. The third simplification review measured the tree: every value declared at the interface was a probe of the interface itself (seven test files and fourteen rule cases, no program needing breadth), the `SequenceEqual` calls that went through the interface numbered six (one of them across backings), and no user type implemented it. The spans are five records and nothing else. Breadth across backings is spelled by one overload per span type, the advice the interface's own design gave for hot paths; `SequenceEqual` is declared on each span for its own type and for the read-only span of each heap backing, at the receiver's own `T` (a class `T`'s nullable buffer slots compare null-safely with an array span's slots); and the byte read tier is declared twice, on `ReadOnlySpan<byte>` and `ReadOnlyArraySpan<byte>`, the writable spans reading through `AsReadOnly()` — sixty-four members where the regularization pass had retired a hundred and twenty-eight, half the duplication it declined and on a different ground, the interface's cost in the borrow regime rather than performance (`DECLINED.md`'s rows are annotated, not struck). Every position a span reached only through the interface is one a record reaches under spec §9.7's ordinary rules with the same verdict or a stricter one, so the borrow-flow matrix's span row collapses onto its borrow-bearing-record row; the only admissions are two consequences of the interface's absence, neither with a site in the tree: `where T: class` establishes that a dispatched `async` body's `T` parameter holds no borrows, and a class-`T` buffer span compares with a non-nullable array span. Declined with the proposal: a compiler-intrinsic unified `ReadOnlySpan<E>` over either heap backing (its element type must become the type argument, `ReadOnlyStringSpan` cannot join it, it is one new intrinsic, and its migration is five times this one's); keeping the interface; declaring the read tier on all four byte spans; and nullability-exact cross-backing overloads at a class `T`, whose asymmetry no measured site needs.

<a id="sec-buffers-conditional-members"></a>
#### 5.15.9 The standard library's conditionally-admitted members

**Conditionally-admitted members ([proposal 0116](proposals/0116-conditional-members.md)).** A few standard-library members exist only at some instantiations of their enclosing type — `Array<T>`/`OwningArray<T>` natural-order sorting, `OwningBuffer<T>.DangerousPointer()`, and `Result<T, E>`'s four value-payload combinators among them. A user declaration cannot state such a condition (spec §14.2 rule 1). Spec §14.2 carries the general rule, and each standard-library member states its condition at its own declaration (*The register emptied into the declarations*, below). Before proposal 0116 the language said so nowhere: for eighty-nine revisions the rule was stated completely in exactly one place (spec §35.2, in the *Choices* chapter, written entirely about `Result`), and the other members defined themselves by pointing at each other.

**Why no user-writable per-member `where`.** The mainstream design is the other one — Rust, Swift and C++20 all constrain the member itself — and the argument for following them is real: a standard library only the compiler can write is one users cannot audit against the language. It was declined on cost. The clause would reopen `where`'s meaning for four rules that already depend on it (constraint inheritance on overrides, overload ranking, the artifact interface, the breaking-change table), and the language has run this experiment: the merged `<owned T>` form's `where T is owned` family ([§5.11](#sec-owning-collections)) *"required progressively more machinery … each of which fixed one issue and introduced another"*. Against that, the capability it buys is one collection's sort under a shorter name.

**The register emptied into the declarations ([proposal 0186](proposals/0186-conditional-member-conditions-at-the-declaration.md)).** The register (*Historical note: the register*, [§5.15.10](#sec-owned-buffers-historical-notes)) said every row was on its way out and named the second way out for each — "a member-level condition, once admitted, is the member's declared form". Proposal 0186 takes it, for the standard library only. Each conditional member states its condition at its own declaration: `Sort()`, `Sorted()` and `OwningArray<T>.Sort()` carry `where T: IComparable<T>` on the enclosing type's parameter, the form spec §14.2 rule 1 still refuses a user declaration; the four whose conditions are not constraints of §14.2 — the choice classification, the FFI-pointee set, §9.2's first kind, §15.1's inline-array rule — name the section that defines theirs, as their declarations already did (the nullable-companion members, too many and partly extension methods, by the index row and §15.1, which states their condition in full); and the closed set is indexed once, in stdlib §1's *Conditional members* row, where `scripts/check_docs.sh`'s REGISTER check now reads it. Nothing a program sees moved: every condition, every rejection site and every remedy is the one the register's row stated, and the generic-body reading (a constraint-form condition decided by the parameter's constraints, an instantiation-site condition at the instantiations) is restated in rule 2 unchanged. What went is the table, the five dispositions that explained why each row existed and how it would lapse, and the exclusions paragraph that kept the table honest — about 850 words of a mechanism that spent its words recording its own impermanence. The user-writable member-level `where` stays declined on the ground above (its reopening bar unchanged, [proposal 0116 §5.1](proposals/0116-conditional-members.md)); the sorting row's first way out, a rename of the natural-order form, was declined at 0186 because it would change every `Sort()` call site (142 in the test corpus) for no gain the clause does not give; and a `value-like` constraint form, which would retire `Result<T, E>`'s row by opening §26.2 to choices, was declined as a constraint added for one member family that the declaration-site statement already covers.

**One argument was tried here and withdrawn, because it is the wrong kind of argument.** An earlier draft grounded the sorting row partly on the standard library's collections not being written in Bezel, and called that *the operative reason*. It is not a reason a specification may give. How a member is lowered — as a compiler intrinsic or from Bezel source — is invisible to the language; `Sort(comparer)` is an intrinsic too and nobody calls it a privilege. The question a specification can answer is whether the declaration form is one the language admits, and under a rename it is. What survives as a genuine language-level cost is narrower and has nothing to do with implementation effort: `Array<T>` is an always-available name (spec §2.3) whose home module is not auto-imported, so an extension over it would resolve only under an `import` that naming the type does not require — a call compiling or not on an import unrelated to its receiver's type. That asymmetry between §2.3's list and §26.5's resolution is the defect worth its own proposal, and it is larger than the sorting question that exposed it.

**The general lesson, which cost this design two reversals to learn.** A practice small enough to enumerate and old enough that nobody has enumerated it will be *mis*-enumerated by a census that asks what each instance costs without asking what it was for. The first pass counted two members; there were five. The second proposed deleting one whose motivating case — a BLAS binding's double-copy, from an owner-commissioned FFI review — was recorded in a graduated proposal the census never opened. What makes a register worth having is not that it lists things, but that each row must state a reason someone can later check.

<a id="sec-owned-buffers-historical-notes"></a>
#### 5.15.10 Historical notes

*Historical note: the register.* From proposal 0116 until proposal 0186 the conditions sat in a closed register in spec §14.2, and the decision recorded with it was that the register was a holding pen, not a grant. The alternative considered and rejected was registering the practice as a standing privilege of four members — which would have written into a normative table two members the compiler did not have: one it rejected outright, and one it never implemented. Every surviving row instead carried its exit. `DangerousPointer()` was unimplemented, which is not a ground for removal (the change process's §10, owner direction 2026-10-08): it was tracked as a specification-ahead-of-implementation delta (#1717), and its exit was the second of the sorting row's two ways out below — a member-level condition, once admitted, is its declared form. `Result`'s combinators were there because §26.2 closes extensions against choices *for the stated reason* that no constraint names the classification they would need — so admitting that one constraint would retire the row.
