The sorting row was there because §26.5 rule 1 commits resolution by name, and the two ways out — rename the member, or admit a member-level condition — were open design questions the register declined to settle inside a proposal about defining a mechanism.

*Historical note: what the interface was.* `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` was implemented by every concrete span type, with `TElement` set exactly to what the concrete indexer returns (`Span<MyClass>`, whose indexer is `[i: int]: MyClass?`, implemented `IReadOnlySpan<MyClass?>`; `ArraySpan<MyClass>` implemented `IReadOnlySpan<MyClass>`; `ReadOnlyStringSpan` implemented `IReadOnlySpan<char>`), with no implicit conversion across nullability or element types. A span passed at it boxed under the borrow-bearing-record boxing rule (spec [§8.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-boxing-of-borrow-bearing-records)), the box's lifetime bounded by the source of the underlying borrow. Its surface was deliberately minimal — indexer-get, `Length`, `IsEmpty`, `IndexOf`, `Contains` and `SequenceEqual` — with `Slice` omitted so that the interface-typed handle and its virtual-dispatch cost would not propagate through a slicing chain. The cost was opt-in and per call: vtable lookup, indirect call, no inlining at the indexer and at every other interface-routed call — for tight inner loops over many elements typically a 5–10× slowdown, for utility code that doesn't iterate hot negligible — so its design recommended concrete span types (possibly via overloads) in performance-sensitive APIs and the interface in utility or convenience APIs.

<a id="sec-collection-literals-for-complex-collections"></a>
### 5.16 Collection literals for complex collections

Bezel's collection-literal form `[e1, e2, ...]` is contextually typed: the literal has no standalone type and is well-typed only where a target type is determinable (language spec [§15.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-collection-literals)). The targets are the inline arrays, every standard sequence collection with its owning variant, the dictionaries through a colon-pair form, and user-defined sequence types that declare the target conformance.

**Why the existing colon-pair dictionary syntax.** The dictionary form is `[k: v, ...]`, and the syntax was weighed against three alternatives. The arrow form `[k => v]` would overload `=>`, which Bezel uses for case arms and expression-bodied accessors; reusing it for data would make `[x => y]` ambiguous to a reader scanning for control flow. The brace form `{k: v}` would introduce a fourth bracket kind (`()`, `[]`, `<>`, `{}`) competing for visual distinctness, where `[]` already carries collection-literal meaning and `{}` appears only inside string interpolation; the cost is a permanent extra syntactic category for a single use. The tuple-list form `[(k, v), ...]` would require tuple types, which Bezel does not provide (deferred per [§18](#sec-things-deferred)); the named-record workaround `[new Pair(k, v), ...]` is verbose and reads as a list of pairs, not a mapping. The colon-pair form is compact, distinct from the bare sequence literal, familiar from JSON/Python/Swift/Ruby, and overloads no token in a conflicting role. The one parsing subtlety — distinguishing `[a: b]` (dictionary) from a hypothetical type-annotated expression — does not arise because Bezel has no inline expression type-annotation syntax; the grammar disambiguates purely on the presence of a top-level colon in a literal element.

**Why LinkedList / Stack / Queue are targets.** `LinkedList<T>`, `Stack<T>`, `Queue<T>` and their owning variants are sequence-shaped — a literal `[1, 2, 3]` has an obvious meaning for each — so excluding them would be an omission, not a deliberate restriction. The only design question is element-order convention. For lists and queues the convention is unsurprising (textual order is head-to-tail / front-to-back). For stacks there are two defensible readings: left-to-right as push order (first element ends at the bottom) or left-to-right as pop order (first element on top). The literal takes push order — `[1, 2, 3]` means "push 1, push 2, push 3," so 3 is on top — because it lets a reader trace the literal as a sequence of construction operations, consistent with how the list and queue literals read. The pop-order alternative would make the literal read "as the stack looks from the top," which is convenient for some mental models but breaks the uniform "textual order = construction order" rule the other sequence types follow.

**The user-defined sequence-literal protocol.** A user-defined type opts in to sequence-literal construction by declaring conformance to the stdlib §2.19 target pair, whose one static witness is the factory `FromCollectionLiteral` on the type (language spec [§15.4.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-user-defined-sequence-literal-protocol); [proposal 0125](proposals/0125-static-interface-promotions.md)). The design was guided by a comparison with C#'s `[CollectionBuilder]` mechanism, which solves the same problem and surfaced three lessons.

*Mechanism: a static factory on the type, not an attribute.* C# opts a type in via `[CollectionBuilder(typeof(BuilderType), "Create")]` — an attribute naming a separate non-generic builder class that holds the static generic `Create<T>(ReadOnlySpan<T>)`. The indirection through a separate builder type exists because C#'s attribute argument needs a `typeof` that cannot cleanly name a generic instantiation, and because static methods interact awkwardly with the generic type itself in their model. Bezel has no general attribute system, so the natural "explicit opt-in" equivalent is *declaring a conformance whose one static witness is a specifically-named static method*. And a Bezel static method on a generic type may freely reference the type's own parameters — the witness signature does exactly that (`public static method FromCollectionLiteral(elements: ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>): owned C<T>`, spec §15.4.1) — so the factory goes *directly on the collection type* with no builder-type indirection. The result is simpler than C#'s: one method on the type, no attribute, no separate builder class.

*Element type: read from the factory signature, not inferred.* C#'s own design documents flag an unresolved wart: with `[CollectionBuilder]` alone the compiler cannot reliably determine the collection's element type, so C# additionally requires the type to define its iteration type (implement `IEnumerable<T>` or expose a `GetEnumerator`). Bezel's named-factory approach has no such problem — the element type *is* the `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>` (or `OwningArray<T>`) parameter's element type, declared right there in the signature the compiler is already looking up. There is no secondary requirement and no inference. This is a case where examining C#'s difficulty produced a cleaner Bezel design rather than a copy.

*Ownership: two factory shapes, a Bezel-specific concern.* C# has no ownership system, so its single `ReadOnlySpan<T>` factory shape suffices. Bezel's borrowing/owning collection duality requires two shapes: the borrowing form takes `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>` — the borrowing family's view, over an `Array<T>` whose slots borrow their class-typed contents (stdlib §4.15); the compiler synthesizes that array and the span over it, both scoped to the call. The analogy to C#'s `Create<T>(ReadOnlySpan<T>)` would suggest `ReadOnlySpan<T>`, and it does not carry: C#'s `ReadOnlySpan<T>` does not own its elements and Bezel's does — its source `OwningBuffer<T>` owns each class-typed slot (stdlib §4.14), where the borrowing form's whole premise is that the constructed collection does not. The choice is of span family, not of nullability ([proposal 0115](proposals/0115-collection-literal-borrowing-span-family.md)); the owning form takes `owned OwningArray<T>` (the compiler builds a temporary owned array, transferring each owned element expression into it, and hands ownership to the factory, which drains it). Exactly one shape per type; declaring both is rejected because the literal's element-expression ownership would not unambiguously pick a target. The owning form's partial-failure behavior (an element expression throwing mid-construction) folds into the existing `OwningArray<T>`-literal exception-path discharge — no new rule.

*One opt-in path, not two.* C# also supports a duck-typed fallback (implement `IEnumerable<T>` + an `Add` method + a parameterless constructor, no attribute). Bezel deliberately does not replicate this. The specification is careful to keep exactly one structural-dispatch rule (`Contains` for `in`); a second "your type became literal-constructible because it happens to have an `Add` method" path would be exactly the kind of accidental enrollment that rule's deliberate scarcity guards against. C# carries the dual path largely for backward compatibility with pre-existing collection-initializer types; Bezel has no such legacy and chooses the single explicit path. The one structural rule is structural because `Contains` predates `in` on many existing types — `string`, `Range<T>`, `Array<T>`, `Set<T>` and `Dictionary<K, V>` among them — and routing them all through a common `IContainer<T>` interface would add a layer callers never write or see (spec §17.5).

*Two recognition categories, not three.* Bezel has two ways the compiler recognizes a type's participation in a language construct: interface-based (the iteration protocol, [§29.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-protocol), and the collection-literal target pair, stdlib §2.19); and the single structural-by-shape rule (`Contains`/`in`). The literal protocol's opt-in is a declared conformance to `ICollectionLiteralTarget<TSelf, T>` or `IOwningCollectionLiteralTarget<TSelf, T>` — two interfaces because the two forms carry different element ownership and exactly one is admitted per class, and abstract-only statics would otherwise force every conformer to supply both. The factory's name and signature are those of the original by-name protocol, so the conformance costs each conformer one header clause and nothing else; the built-in collections declare the same conformance in their own standard-library declarations and list the factory, which is what lets a constrained generic construct any target and lets the factory be called by name where a reader can find it. Two alternatives were declined: an implicit conformance inferred from the factory alone, which would be a second structural rule beside `Contains` and would leave `TSelf.FromCollectionLiteral` unreachable through a constraint; and a compiler-provided conformance for the built-ins on the primitive-tower model, which spec §12.7 scopes to primitives and which would make ten callable members exist in no standard-library listing.

*Dictionaries stay out, matching C#'s staged path.* C# deliberately excluded dictionaries from collection expressions and is still, two years on, designing dictionary expressions as a separate feature. The pair-shaped literal needs its own factory design (a paired-span or key/value-span shape) and the design space is unsettled even in a mature language. The protocol covers user-defined *sequence* collections only; user-extensible dictionary literals remain deferred, for the same reason C# deferred them.

**What is still deferred.** User-extensible *dictionary*-literal construction (the `[k: v, ...]` form for user-defined map-like types) remains deferred — see [§18](#sec-things-deferred). The built-in `Dictionary`/`OwningDictionary` keep the dictionary-literal form; user map-like types must use ordinary constructors until a future pass designs the paired-factory protocol.

**Value-element inference (`var x := [1, 2, 3];` defaulting to `Array<int>`) — considered and declined (2026-08, elegance review).** The carve-out is coherent for value and string elements, and would delete an annotation from a common binding. Declined on three grounds. (1) *The uniform rule is load-bearing for ownership.* The literal-tree rule (spec §15.4; [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking)) assigns every fresh node of a literal an owner derived from the target: owning-family slots take nodes by transfer, borrowing-family targets aggregate them, inline arrays copy. A literal with no target has no ownership story to compute; a value-element carve-out would make "does this literal need a target?" depend on element classification — recursively, since elements may be literals (`[[1], [2]]` — value leaves under a class-typed spine), splitting one rule into a family. (2) *Container choice is a silent decision.* `[1, 2, 3]` is equally an `Array<int>`, a `[3]int` inline array, a `Set<int>`, a `Queue<int>`; electing `Array<int>` makes the language guess where spec §22.6's resolution rules refuse to ("the compiler declines to guess"), and the guess is sticky — element-type inference (`[1, 2, 3]` as `Array<byte>`? §5.1 constant narrowing is target-driven) would be pinned to `int` by the default in exactly the positions where an annotation today chooses. (3) *The annotation is one type name, and the language keeps it meaningful*: `var xs: Array<int> := [1, 2, 3];` states the two decisions (container, element type) the literal alone cannot. Reopening requires evidence that annotation weight dominates real code *and* an ownership story for target-free literal trees; absent both, the uniform no-standalone-type rule stands.

<a id="sec-why-no-class-deconstruction"></a>
### 5.17 Why no class deconstruction

A recurring request is positional destructuring of class objects — `var (a, b) := someClass;` — paralleling the record-destructuring of [§19.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-patterns). It was evaluated in full and declined. This section records the analysis so the decision is not silently re-litigated.

The motivating case is the multi-owned-value return: a method that produces several owned resources at once. This is already fully expressible. A class may own multiple fields ([§28.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-fields)); a method may return `owned C`; a single `using var` on the result discharges the whole aggregate, the destructor cascade disposing every owned field in reverse declaration order. The `OwningDictionary<K, V>.Drain()` case (stdlib [§4.9](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-owneddictionaryk-v)) is exactly this pattern: `DrainedEntry<K, V>` is a class that owns its `Value`, iterated through `IOwningEnumerable<T>`. No language feature beyond what already exists is needed for the capability.

What class deconstruction would add is therefore *only* notation: referring to `conn` instead of `pair.Connection`. Three considerations made that notation not worth its cost.

First, the naive form — "destructuring disposes the class and yields its fields" — is unsound. A class destructor runs arbitrary user code and, per [§28.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-fields), runs *before* the owned-field auto-dispose cascade; destructor bodies routinely use the very fields a deconstruction would try to extract. "Dispose the container but keep its fields alive" inverts the dependency disposal embodies: disposal *is* the teardown of those fields. Extraction (move the fields out, transferring ownership) and disposal (the container tears the fields down) are opposite operations on the same slots; they cannot both happen.

Second, the sound form is a separate, opt-in `Deconstruct`-style method that *moves* each owned field out (transferring ownership) and consumes the receiver, leaving it moved-from and exempt from the field-restoration rule of [§28.8.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-move-expression) — a new ownership-system rule ("a deconstruct method consumes its receiver, waiving restoration for fully-moved-out fields"). The language already waives restoration in one place — inside a destructor body ([§28.8.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-move-expression), "the field-restoration rule does not require reassignment inside a destructor") — but that waiver rests on the destructor being *the* designated teardown site: the object's lifetime provably ends there and no later code can observe it. A `Deconstruct` method has neither property; it is an ordinary method, the husk survives the call, and other references to the object may still be live. The destructor carve-out therefore does not extend to it, which is exactly why a genuinely new rule (not a reuse of the existing one) would be required. To keep the borrow checker unchanged, the consumption would have to be syntactically visible at the use site (an explicit `move` at the destructuring position, so the existing move-invalidation machinery applies rather than a new borrow concept). This is implementable and coherent, but it adds a rule to the ownership system — Bezel's most cost-sensitive subsystem, where the whole value proposition is that the rules are few and composable. Every rule there raises the baseline cost of the feature that is supposed to be invisible when it works.

Third, the benefit is sugar on a solved problem at low frequency. Named owning classes and `out owned` parameters cover the capability; the recurring instance (`Drain()`) is closed by a pure standard-library addition. Weighing a new ownership rule against "saves writing `pair.` twice" does not clear the bar a change to the ownership system should meet. Bezel's design philosophy already argues, for records-versus-tuples, that a name and a documented shape is a feature rather than a limitation; `pair.Connection` tells a reader what they hold, where a positional `conn` tells them less. The same argument applies to owned aggregates.

The conservative resolution is taken: the capability stays (owning classes), the sugar is declined (no class deconstruction, no tuples as a prerequisite), and the one real friction point (`OwningDictionary` draining) is removed at the library level rather than the language level. If a future revision finds the notation genuinely necessary — driven by evidence from real code, not design aesthetics — the `Deconstruct`-with-explicit-`move` design recorded here is the starting point.

<a id="sec-type-identity-not-reflection"></a>
### 5.18 Type identity, not reflection

Bezel provides `typeof(T)`, `value.GetType()`, the `is`/`as` operators, and a `Type` token carrying name, kind predicates, and lattice queries (stdlib [§2.13](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-the-type-class)). It deliberately does *not* provide member-access reflection: no enumeration of fields, properties, or methods; no by-name member access; no attribute reading; no dynamic instantiation. This section records why the boundary sits exactly there, so the question is settled rather than perennially reopened.

**The boundary is principled, not merely unimplemented.** Member-access reflection is in direct tension with the language's two load-bearing commitments.

*Ownership has no reflective site.* Every ownership contract in Bezel attaches to a static program point: a parameter declared `owned`, a field declared `owned`, a `move` expression, a `using var`. The borrow checker reasons about these points. A reflective field read — `someType.GetField("conn").GetValue(obj)` — has no such point. Is the returned `Connection` a borrow of the still-living object, or a transfer that leaves the field moved-from? The call site is `GetValue`, whose signature is identical regardless of the field's ownership; there is nowhere to write the contract and nowhere for the checker to enforce it. C#-style reflection works precisely because the GC makes the question moot — every reference is equivalent and lifetimes are not tracked. Bezel's whole value proposition is that they *are* tracked, at explicit sites. Reflective member access would be a hole in exactly the mechanism the language exists to provide. This is not a difficulty to be engineered around; it is a contradiction.

*It is the maximal "hidden magic."* The rationale invokes the no-hidden-magic principle repeatedly — every value has a static shape, every dispatch is either statically resolved or an explicit interface call, the one structural-dispatch rule (`Contains`/`in`) is singular and called out as exceptional. Member reflection is the largest conceivable violation: arbitrary code reaching arbitrary members of arbitrary types with no static trace. Admitting it would not bend the principle; it would delete it.

**The line is drawn at lattice metadata because that costs nothing.** Everything the `Type` token exposes — name, kind, base, interface conformance, enum members — is information the compiler already has and the runtime already carries so that `is`, `as`, and `GetType` can function at all. Surfacing it adds no new capability and no new tension; it only stops forcing users to thread `typeof(T)` through their own hand-built side tables. The genuine, ownership-neutral use cases are well served: type-keyed registries and caches, kind-based diagnostic formatting, interface-conformance checks on bare `Type` tokens, and enum reflection (closed, stateless, ownership-free — the safest introspection that exists, and the single most requested). None of these touch instance state, so none of them raises the ownership question.

**What was deliberately excluded from even the metadata surface.** Generic-instantiation decomposition — "is this some `Array<X>`, and what is `X`?" via a `GenericArguments` / `GenericDefinition` pair — is genuinely useful for mapping libraries, but it cannot be expressed without also adding an *unbound-generic* `typeof` (a way to write `typeof(Array<>)` to compare against a `GenericDefinition`), which the `typeof` grammar does not admit (language spec [§17.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-typeof-operator) accepts only closed instantiations). That is a language change wearing a standard-library costume, and bundling it into a metadata revision would hide its true scope. It is therefore deferred as its own explicitly-scoped question ([§18](#sec-things-deferred)), to be decided together with the unbound-`typeof` syntax, not smuggled in.

**Attributes are the realistic future pressure valve, separately.** Several use cases that look like "reflection" (serialization hints, dependency-injection registration) are really *attribute* use cases. Bezel has no attribute system today. If reflection pressure ever genuinely materializes from real programs, the principled response is a minimal attribute system plus attribute-only `Type` queries — never member-access reflection. That, too, is its own scoped question ([§18](#sec-things-deferred)); it is named here so the eventual direction is on record and is not mistaken for a step toward member reflection.

The disposition mirrors [§5.17](#sec-why-no-class-deconstruction): deliver the part that fits the language cleanly (lattice metadata), decline the part that fights its foundations (member-access reflection) *with the reasoning written down*, and keep the genuinely-open adjacent questions (generic decomposition, attributes) explicitly scoped rather than vaguely "deferred." An explicitly reasoned "no" is more useful to users than an open "later": it tells them to architect with compile-time generics and explicit registration now, rather than waiting for a reflective capability that is not coming.

<a id="sec-serialization-without-reflection"></a>
### 5.19 Serialization without reflection

[§5.18](#sec-type-identity-not-reflection) closes the door on reflective serialization. This section records the design that replaces it and why the serialization vocabulary (stdlib [§2.14](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-serialization-vocabulary)) is shaped the way it is; [§5.22](#sec-type-driven-serialization) records how a class's graph shape is read from its field types.

The decision: a type opts in by declaring `ISerializable`/`IDeserializable`, the compiler synthesizes the bodies, and they run against a format-agnostic serializer/deserializer vocabulary — the Swift `Codable` model, which is auto-equality generalized, rather than reflection or an open source-generator subsystem. Customization is a closed per-member `serialized` modifier rather than attributes, a read policy such as unknown-field tolerance lives on the operation, and polymorphism is a closed, explicitly written registry. [§5.22](#sec-type-driven-serialization)'s graph-shape rules build on the owner-before-borrower construction of [§5.19.5](#sec-serialization-back-edges).

<a id="sec-serialization-architecture"></a>
#### 5.19.1 One architecture, and the machinery Bezel already has

**Every reflection-free language converged on the same architecture.** Rust's serde generates `Serialize`/`Deserialize` implementations at compile time via a derive macro and routes them through a format-agnostic `Serializer`/`Deserializer` pair, so a type knows nothing about JSON or any other format. Swift's `Codable` has the *compiler itself* synthesize conformance for types whose stored properties are all `Codable`, routed through a format-agnostic `Encoder`/`Decoder` pair. Even C#, which has runtime reflection, moved its modern JSON path to *build-time source generators* for performance and ahead-of-time-compilation reasons — evidence that compile-time codegen is not a Bezel compromise forced by the lack of reflection but the destination reflection-capable languages migrate *to*. The shared invariant across all three: separate *what a type's shape is* (static, the type's concern) from *how a format encodes it* (the format's concern). Reflection conflates them by discovering shape at runtime; the reflection-free approach keeps them separate and resolves shape at compile time.

**Bezel already does opt-in compile-time codegen — it is called auto-equality.** A body record with only auto-properties automatically receives a synthesized `=`, `GetHashCode`, and `ToString` ([§9.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-body-records)); a record with a disqualifying member instead produces a compile error at the use site, naming the obstruction. This is precisely opt-in-by-structure compile-time generation with a recursive eligibility rule and a disciplined error story — and it needs no attribute system, no macro system, and no general source-generator plugin mechanism. Serialization synthesis is not a new *kind* of feature for Bezel; it is auto-equality generalized from "compare the fields" to "read and write the fields against a format." Choosing the Swift model (a dedicated compiler feature keyed off a declared conformance) over the C# model (an open-ended source-generator subsystem) is therefore the choice that fits machinery the language already has, rather than the choice that requires building the deferred macro system first.

<a id="sec-serialization-the-vocabulary"></a>
#### 5.19.2 The vocabulary

**The naming follows the established interface convention.** Bezel's interface set splits cleanly into capability adjectives a type declares about itself (`IDisposable`, `IEquatable<T>`, `IComparable<T>` — the `-able` family) and agent nouns that act on other values (`IEnumerator<T>`, `IEqualityComparer<T>`). Serialization has one of each: a *type* declares it can serialize itself (`ISerializable`/`IDeserializable`, the `-able` family, by direct analogy with `IDisposable`); a *format* is an agent that serializes values (`ISerializer`/`IDeserializer`, the agent-noun family, by analogy with `IEqualityComparer<T>`). The type-side interfaces are deliberately *parameterless* (not `ISerializable<TFormat>`): a value's shape is intrinsic and format-independent, so format polymorphism lives in the `ISerializer` parameter to the method, never in the type-side interface — the same place serde and Codable put it, and the same shape as the parameterless `IDisposable`. Splitting serialize and deserialize into two interfaces rather than one combined interface follows the existing `IDisposable`/`IAsyncDisposable` and `IEnumerable`/`IAsyncEnumerable` precedent of separating paired concerns, and is additionally forced here by a structural asymmetry: serialization is an instance operation (an existing value writes itself), deserialization a static one (a reader produces a value where none exists yet).

**That asymmetry is why `IDeserializable` is a marker.** A static construction entry point cannot be an interface *method* without static interface members (spec §11.8). They exist, and promoting `Deserialize` to one is a recorded follow-up (#1507, proposal 0125 Part B); until it lands, `IDeserializable` stays a marker. Rather than block deserialization on that promotion, the entry point is recognized *by name* — a static `Deserialize(d: IDeserializer): T` (plain by-value for the record/enum common case; `owned T` only when the declaring type is a class, since `owned` on a non-class return is semantically rejected) whose presence is opted into by declaring `: IDeserializable` — exactly the mechanism, and exactly the forward-compatibility discipline, established for `FromCollectionLiteral` in [§5.16](#sec-collection-literals-for-complex-collections) and language spec [§15.4.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-user-defined-sequence-literal-protocol) before that protocol's promotion to the stdlib §2.19 target pair. The name and signature are chosen so that the promotion, when it lands, turns `Deserialize` into a real interface method with no change to the member; the conformance declaration itself becomes `: IDeserializable<T>` (proposal 0125 Part B). This reuse of an already-justified pattern is deliberate: it avoids inventing a second recognition mechanism for the same shape of problem, and it means deserialization is not gated on the promotion.

**The vocabulary is the keystone.** The format abstraction is the one piece that cannot be retrofitted: if the type-side interface ever names a format, or the format ever inspects type shape, the separation is lost and every later tier is built on sand. It costs no language feature, and its writing direction is usable by hand without synthesis. That is why it was fixed first and alone, and why synthesis, the concrete formats and the customization mechanism are independent, non-breaking additions over it — each forward-compatible because the vocabulary was designed, like `FromCollectionLiteral` before it, to be promotable rather than replaced. The disposition is the one applied in [§5.17](#sec-why-no-class-deconstruction) and [§5.18](#sec-type-identity-not-reflection): ship what fits the language, record the plan for what does not yet, keep every open question explicitly scoped.

<a id="sec-serialization-synthesis-design"></a>
#### 5.19.3 Synthesis

**The synthesis design.** A type that declares `: ISerializable` (and/or `: IDeserializable`) and satisfies the synthesis-eligibility rule has the bodies generated by the compiler (language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)), governed by a recursive structural-eligibility rule that mirrors auto-equality's: a type is synthesis-eligible when every serialized component is itself serializable; a non-serializable component is a compile error at the conformance declaration, naming the offending member, with the same error discipline auto-equality uses for disqualifying members (reported at the declaration rather than the use site, because the conformance declaration is the explicit opt-in). Records (shape known from [§9.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-positional-records)/[§9.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-body-records)) and enums (closed, members known) have no inheritance, identity or owned-field complications — exactly the closed-shape property that makes auto-equality sound for the same set; a class's graph shape is read from its field types ([§5.22](#sec-serialization-field-types-carry-shape)). The ownership concern that made *reflection* unsound does not arise: the synthesized `Deserialize` body is a static program point that calls the type's constructor with read values, so field types, nullability, and definite-assignment are checked normally — exactly where the language wants the contract, and exactly the site reflection lacked. Synthesis is therefore sound for the same structural reason auto-equality is. Hand-writing either direction suppresses synthesis of that direction (as a manual `Equals` suppresses auto-equality); the two directions are independent. The specific wire choices are normative (language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)) so independent implementations agree: enums serialize by member *name* not underlying integer (stable against renumbering, self-describing in text), components in declaration order (matching auto-`ToString`), dictionaries as key/value-pair arrays (admitting non-string keys uniformly rather than forcing string coercion as JSON-object-keyed encodings do).

**A number a component cannot hold.** The vocabulary's reads are 64 bits wide (`ReadInt`, `ReadUInt`, `ReadFloat`; stdlib §2.14), so a synthesized `Deserialize` reading a narrower component can meet a value its type cannot hold: `5000000000` for an `int`, `3.5e38` for a `float`. It raises `SerializationException` (spec §9.8). The value is the input's, so the fault is the input's, classed with the mismatched-component fault. **Declined:** truncation to the component's width, which decodes a different and plausible value with no signal; and §5.2's `OverflowException`, the caller's checked-narrowing fault, which a hand-written `int(d.ReadInt())` still raises, since there the narrowing is the caller's own expression.

<a id="sec-serialization-customization-and-policy"></a>
#### 5.19.4 Customization and where a policy lives

**Field-name customization: a compiler-recognized modifier, not attributes, improving on `CodingKeys`.** Renaming a serialized field, excluding one, or tolerating a missing one has no auto-equality analog (equality needs no external names), so it required a new mechanism. Two off-the-shelf models were rejected. *Attributes* (`[JsonPropertyName("…")]`) were rejected because field-name customization is precisely the canonical case that, in C#, metastasizes into a dozen stacked serialization attributes per member — exactly the proliferation, stringly-typed-magic, and semantics/metadata-blur the attribute-system deferral ([§18](#sec-things-deferred)) is on record against; solving this one narrow need by importing a general attribute system would pay that entire cost to buy one feature, and would prejudice the still-open general-attribute question. *Swift's `CodingKeys`* (a nested `enum: String, CodingKey` listing every coded property) was rejected because it is a footgun by construction: it conflates "which members are coded" with "what they are renamed to" into one exhaustive list, so adding a member and forgetting its case silently drops it from serialization, and a reader cannot distinguish intentional exclusion from an omission — the same "blur" indictment, relocated into an enum. The chosen design is the narrow-modifier escape hatch [§18](#sec-things-deferred) already sanctions for `[Obsolete]`/`[MethodImpl]`-style needs: a closed `serialized` clause expressing three effects — rename (`serialized "name"`), exclude (`serialized skip`), tolerate (`serialized optional`), where rename and tolerate combine (`serialized "name" optional`) and exclude is mutually exclusive with both — attached to the member it affects (language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)). It improves on `CodingKeys` in the specific way the design question demanded: it is *per-member and total* — a member with no clause is coded under its own name, always, so there is no separate list to desynchronize and the silent-drop failure is structurally impossible; the clause is *local* — its effect is visible at the member, with one defined meaning, no reflective indirection, answering [§18](#sec-things-deferred)'s "a reader cannot tell what an annotation does" objection directly; and it is *closed* — a fixed effect vocabulary, no parameters interpreted by external machinery, no user extensibility, so it cannot proliferate (a new effect would be a language change with its own rationale, exactly the friction that prevents the C# explosion). It is forward-compatible: if a general attribute system later arrives, `serialized "x"` re-expresses mechanically as `[Serialized("x")]` with identical semantics, the same promotion discipline used for `FromCollectionLiteral` and recognized-`Deserialize`. The honest cost: it adds grammar — unavoidable, since customization inherently needs somewhere to write the custom name, and the modifier is the *smallest* such addition (two closed productions, no new top-level construct, no attribute-target or retention rules, no reflection dependency) where attributes would have been the largest.

**Unknown-field tolerance is an operation policy, not a type annotation.** The strict default — an input field matching no declared component raises — is correct because silently dropping unrecognized input masks producer/consumer schema drift and field-name typos. But making it *configurable* raised a placement question worth recording, because the answer generalizes to future serialization-policy questions. The natural-seeming move was a type-level `serialized tolerant` clause, parallel to the member-level `serialized` modifier. That was rejected on a category distinction: the member-level effects (rename, skip, optional) are *intrinsic to the type* — `FirstName` *is* `"first_name"` in this type's wire form for every caller, every format, every call site; that is part of the type's serialization contract and belongs on the type. Unknown-field tolerance is not a fact about the type at all: the same type is legitimately read strictly when consuming a trusted internal message (drift *should* error) and leniently when consuming a third-party producer that adds fields freely. That is a property of the *read* — the caller has it, with the input, in a context — not of the type. A type-level annotation would force one answer on every call site, would be strictly less expressive than a per-operation control, and would reintroduce exactly the "inert-looking but load-bearing, and you cannot tell from the call site what it does" objection [§18](#sec-things-deferred) levels at attributes — the same objection the member-level clause was designed to answer. So the knob lives on the deserialization operation: `JsonOptions.IgnoreUnknownFields` and the binary deserializer's constructor parameter (stdlib [§2.15](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-concrete-serialization-formats)), defaulting to strict. Mechanically, the synthesized `Deserialize` is identical regardless of policy — on an unrecognized field it calls `IDeserializer.SkipValue` and continues; whether *reaching* an unknown field is an error is the deserializer's configured behavior. This is the shape/format split extended to shape/policy: the synthesized code expresses structure, the deserializer expresses policy, and they compose without either knowing the other's choices. The general principle, worth stating once for future policy questions: a serialization knob belongs on the type when it is part of the type's wire contract, and on the operation when it is a property of a particular read or write; "which spelling is most consistent with an existing mechanism" does not override "where does this policy actually live." By the same criterion a class's graph shape and its `polymorphic` registry are type-scoped ([§5.22](#sec-type-driven-serialization)): each is an invariant of the type's serialized form for every caller, format and call site.

<a id="sec-serialization-back-edges"></a>
#### 5.19.5 Back-edges and definite assignment

**Back-edges and definite assignment.** A serialized class graph is an owning spanning tree (the `owned` edges) plus back-edges: borrowing fields that point at objects owned elsewhere in the tree. Bezel's `owned`/borrow annotations already say which edge owns — the canonical-owner information that serde and every garbage-collected language must guess is statically present here — so the synthesized `Deserialize` infers nothing. The tree is, for synthesis purposes, a record plus heap allocation and ownership transfer: each owned child is constructed and transferred into its parent's constructor, and the ownership contract lives at that constructor signature, a static program point, exactly as for records. A plain borrowing back-edge is non-nullable, and [§7.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-definite-assignment-for-non-nullable-fields) requires a non-nullable reference field to be assigned in every constructor before it returns, so the back-edge is supplied *as a constructor argument*: a directed acyclic graph admits an owner-before-borrower construction order, every back-edge's target is constructed before its borrower, and §7.9 holds unmodified — not relaxed, not excepted for synthesized code, not worked around with nullability — with no intermediate state to observe, because each object is fully constructed, back-edges included, by its single constructor call (language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)). A cycle has no owner-before-borrower order; it closes through a nullable `Weak<T>?` back-edge on a `Shared<T>` spine instead ([§5.22](#sec-serialization-cycles)). The three obvious escapes from §7.9's requirement are declined.

*Why the three obvious escapes each fail.* (1) *Relax [§7.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-definite-assignment-for-non-nullable-fields) for synthesized deserialization.* Rejected: [§7.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-definite-assignment-for-non-nullable-fields) is not a local convenience, it is the guarantee that a non-nullable reference is never observed unset; a synthesized path that can produce a half-constructed object with an unset non-nullable field reintroduces exactly the null-dereference class the language spent its nullability design eliminating, and it would do so invisibly inside generated code where no reader can audit it. A guarantee with a synthesized exception is not a guarantee. (2) *Topologically order construction so borrowers follow targets.* It is how a DAG is built (above), and rejected as the general solution: it handles a DAG but not a cycle (mutually borrowing objects have no valid order), and even for a DAG it forces construction order to be a function of the borrow graph rather than the ownership tree, which the ownership model — not the serializer — is entitled to define. (3) *Make borrowing fields nullable for deserialization.* Rejected because the type's contract must not be deformed by how it is read (the principle behind the placement criterion above), and independently because it would make every *reader* of such a type handle a `null` that the type's own invariants forbid — pushing the deserializer's difficulty onto every downstream use site, the worst possible place for it. A cycle's `Weak<T>?` back-edge is not this escape: it is nullable because its target can be released, not to serve the deserializer ([§5.22](#sec-serialization-cycles)).

<a id="sec-serialization-polymorphic-dispatch"></a>
#### 5.19.6 Polymorphic dispatch

**Polymorphic dispatch: explicit registration, vindicated by the ecosystem's own trajectory.** A base-typed component that may hold a derived instance needs a wire type-discriminator and a construction registry, and the specific shape is worth recording because the wider ecosystem independently converged on it after learning the alternative the hard way. The mechanism is a closed registry, written explicitly in the class-body `polymorphic` member, mapping a wire discriminator string to each permitted concrete derived type; the synthesized serializer writes the discriminator first, the synthesized deserializer reads it first and dispatches through the registry, and any discriminator or run-time type outside the registry is a `SerializationException`. There is no reflective subtype discovery. This is exactly the [§5.18](#sec-type-identity-not-reflection) explicit-registration-never-reflection principle, and it is not an arbitrary preference: reflective type-discriminator binders (the historical Newtonsoft `$type` model, which resolved an attacker-controlled string to *any* constructible type) became a notorious remote-code-execution vector, and the modern mainstream (System.Text.Json from .NET 7) deliberately moved to *explicitly opted-in, closed* derived-type registration with undeclared types raising at runtime. Bezel's design lands at the same place by principle that the ecosystem reached by incident — the registry is closed because an open one is a security defect, not merely because closed is tidier. Two further design points were settled by the same external evidence: the discriminator is consumed *before* component binding (a serializer that treats it as an ordinary field hits the documented problem that it cannot then be a required data property and cannot participate in normal field machinery — so Bezel makes it structural, never a data field, never subject to field-name customization or the unknown-field policy); and polymorphism is *type-scoped*, the permitted-subtype set being an intrinsic property of the base type's serialization contract exactly as graph shape is, by the placement criterion above. Polymorphic dispatch composes with graph shape: the registry selects *which* concrete type, and the field types govern *how that type's edges reconstruct* ([§5.22](#sec-serialization-polymorphism-and-inheritance)).

<a id="sec-serialization-historical-note"></a>
#### 5.19.7 Historical note

*Historical note.* From v0.119 to v0.140 a class's graph shape was asserted by a header clause — `serialized tree`, `serialized graph`, `serialized cyclic` — that the deserializer enforced at read, and the cyclic tier was built by a two-phase construction window (every object allocated with its back-edges unbound, then the back-edges bound) whose intermediate state was proved unobservable under a self-contained-constructor eligibility rule. The design was worked out reasoning-first in two rationale-only revisions, v0.120 and v0.123, under the premise that the language had no shared-ownership primitive; their conclusions are in those changelog entries. The design's external corroboration was Java, which with full GC and unrestricted mutable identity cannot preserve a cycle through an immutable record's constructor — the limitation the self-contained-constructor rule turned into a compile-time restriction. v0.141 retired the clauses, the window and the eligibility rule once `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` made a cycle constructable; [§5.22](#sec-type-driven-serialization-historical-note) records why.

<a id="sec-one-element-policy"></a>
### 5.20 One element policy, not twelve

The element slot policy (language spec [§28.11](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-element-slot-policy)) is a single table — collection family × element category → slot kind, slot kind → the semantics of every element operation — from which all twelve standard collections derive their element behavior. It is declarative, not behavioral: it changes no collection's documented operations, only their *status* — twelve presentations of one rule rather than twelve independently-stated rule sets.

**Why.** The implementation experience made the cost of the per-collection statement concrete. The compiler hand-wired each collection's element handling separately, and the defect history clustered exactly there: a long series of bugs in which one collection's handling of one element category diverged from its siblings' — string elements compared by `memcmp` in one list operation but structurally in another, dictionary value decoding correct for one value category and garbage for the next, disposal order right in one container and wrong in its peer (#556, #558–#560, #562–#563, #565–#566, #570–#571, #598 among others). Every one of those bugs is, in the table's terms, a cell filled in inconsistently with its column. A normative table makes the column the authority: an implementation can implement the column once, and a divergence between a per-collection clause and the table is *defined* to be a spec defect rather than a judgment call. This is the specification-side half of the direction recorded in #545 (implement the element policy once, type-keyed, instead of per collection); the spec says the twelve behaviors *are* one behavior, so the implementation is licensed to build them that way.

**The table names a policy that already existed.** The owning column's storage embodiment, `OwningBuffer<T>` (stdlib [§4.14](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans), [§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans) above), with its category-dispatched slot semantics, is the table's prototype; the owning family's rejection of runtime-managed element types was likewise already normative ([§14.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-as-a-generic-argument)). The policy did not have to be invented — it had to be *named*, stated once, and the per-collection prose subordinated to it.

**The deferred half: a borrow-storing primitive.** The table's borrowing column has no user-expressible storage embodiment, and the language deliberately does not add one. The blocker is principled: outside the compiler-known borrowing collections, a borrow stored in a field is either rejected (a scope-bound local's) or admitted only on a recorded trust clause, the field-held clause backstopped by the disposed flag ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), K4.3) — the bound is not checked. A user-expressible `Buffer<T>`-of-borrows would need its own lifetime discipline (the collection-as-transit rules of [§28.11](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-collection-element-ownership), generalized and made checkable for arbitrary user containers), which is real design work, not transcription. Until that work is done, borrowing collections remain compiler intrinsics specified by the table. Declined rather than attempted: a quick dispensation ("classes marked somehow may store borrows") would re-open the soundness of the borrow tracker for a convenience the standard library does not itself need — the same shape of reasoning that declined `<owned T>` generic parameterization ([§5.11](#sec-owning-collections)).

<a id="sec-shared-t"></a>
### 5.21 `Shared<T>`: scoped shared ownership

This section gives the design of the opt-in sharing [§5.4](#sec-no-shared-ownership) motivates: `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` (language spec [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership), stdlib [§2.16](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-shared-and-weak)). It keeps that section's posture — single ownership is the default and the only *implicit* regime, and sharing is opt-in with its cost visible at every site (`Clone()` is an explicit call) — and, as the paragraphs below explain, it adds no checker rule.

**Why compiler-known rather than pure library.** Faithful shared ownership cannot be built from unique ownership in user Bezel: N handles must keep one control block alive, and "kept alive by N owners" *is* the feature. The precedent is the collection library — compiler-known types with ownership semantics user code cannot express (§28.11) — and the runtime already manages string and closure lifetimes invisibly (§28.9 deliberately leaves the mechanism unspecified; reference counting is the natural implementation); `Shared<T>` asks for the same kind of bookkeeping, now with user-visible semantics.

**Why the handles are ordinary owned objects.** The design's entire surface area is the *inside* of the type: count, last-one-disposes, and `Get()`'s borrow-from-handle sourcing (the same dispensation owning-collection indexer reads already have). Everything else — `using var`, owned fields, `move`, branch joins, exception-path discharge, storage in owning collections — applies to handles by virtue of their being class instances, with zero new checker rules. A design where sharing was a new *reference kind* (a third state beside owned and borrowed) was declined: it would have touched every join rule, every producer table entry, and every feature that dispatches on ownership state, for no expressiveness the handle form lacks.

**When a handle may cross domains.** `Shared<T>` is sendable exactly when its payload type is in the `Shared`-payload synchronized set — the types declaring the synchronized or the immutable fact and not the self-distributing one (spec [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership)'s *Sendability*; stdlib [§1](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-statics-and-sharing-facts)'s statics-and-sharing facts). For such a payload a cloned handle may move to another domain, the handle count is maintained with synchronized operations, and the value is still disposed exactly once, by whichever domain drops the last handle; `Shared<Mutex>` is the cross-domain sharing idiom. For every other payload `Shared<T>` stays domain-local and is not sendable, because the count is not the only shared state: synchronizing the count makes the handles safe to clone and drop from two domains, but not the value they reach, so a sendable `Shared<T>` over an unsynchronized payload would be a data race through `Get()` — the race spec [§30.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-sendable-types)'s isolation claim excludes. Keying sendability on the payload also keeps the cost where the capability is: a `Shared<T>` that cannot leave its domain is not required to pay for a synchronized count. A separate synchronized type (`SharedAtomic<T>`, or a sendable-`Shared` mode chosen per use) was the recorded follow-on before the payload rule; the rule delivers it with no second type. Dedicated handle-minting types per synchronized type (`MutexHandle` via `NewHandle()`) were declined as duplicating `Shared<T>`'s entire lifecycle for each type (the v0.140 entry). Multi-consumer channels, which would share the receiving end, stay deferred on their own ground ([§18](#sec-things-deferred)). Also declined: refcounting arbitrary classes via an annotation (hides cost, breaks the structural-facts discipline of [§3.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-derived-type-properties)-style reasoning), and compiler-inferred sharing via escape analysis (implicit magic; the language's position is that ownership transitions are visible in source).

**The accepted hazard.** Reference cycles through `Shared<T>` leak, and the compiler does not detect them. This is the standard refcount trade, accepted with the standard mitigation (`Weak<T>`, with `TryUpgrade` in the Try-pattern shape the language already has). Cycle *detection* would be a tracing collector by another name — exactly what the language promises not to have.

<a id="sec-type-driven-serialization"></a>
### 5.22 Type-driven serialization: `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` retire the graph-shape keywords

A class's serialized shape is read from its field types: an `owned` field is an inline owning edge, a `Shared<T>` field an identity participant, and a `Weak<T>` field or a plain borrowing field a back-reference (language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)). No header clause asserts the shape, and a cycle closes through a `Weak<T>` back-edge on a `Shared<T>` spine, constructed in ordinary ownership-valid code. The paragraphs below give the reasons; the historical note at the end records the keyword model this replaced.

The section builds on [§5.19](#sec-serialization-without-reflection): the vocabulary and synthesis recorded there, its placement criterion ([§5.19.4](#sec-serialization-customization-and-policy)), the DAG construction ([§5.19.5](#sec-serialization-back-edges)) and the polymorphic registry ([§5.19.6](#sec-serialization-polymorphic-dispatch)).

<a id="sec-serialization-field-types-carry-shape"></a>
#### 5.22.1 The field types carry the shape

**The field types carry the shape.** Each field's serialized role is read from its type: an `owned` field is an owning edge written inline; a borrowing field — plain or `Weak<T>` — is a back-reference written as a `$ref`; a `Shared<T>` field is an identity participant, written once at its first occurrence and as a `$ref` at every alias. The shape follows from those types, and the safe claim — the only one the argument needs — is that the types are at least as informative as any header clause and never contradict the data: a class with no back-references of any kind serializes as a tree; one with borrowing fields and/or `Shared<T>` may alias; one with `Weak<T>` (or `Shared<T>` reaching an ancestor, the documented Shared-cycle leak, [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership)) may cycle. A shape clause would restate, as a header assertion, information the field types already carry — and could *diverge* from it (a declared tree over a class holding a `Shared<T>` field is a contradiction the compiler would have to police for no gain). The disposition is the one [§5.19](#sec-serialization-back-edges) takes for the owning/borrow split it reads straight from the annotations: no restatement, the shape read from the types. An optional checked shape assertion is declined as well, because a checked assertion is still a second statement of the same fact, with the divergence-policing cost and none of the expressiveness. The shape is type-scoped — the same for every caller, format and call site — by [§5.19](#sec-serialization-customization-and-policy)'s placement criterion: a property of the type's wire contract lives on the type, and a property of one read, such as unknown-field tolerance, on the operation.

<a id="sec-serialization-cycles"></a>
#### 5.22.2 Cycles through a `Shared<T>` spine

**A cycle of plain borrows has no ownership-valid construction** (#703). A serialized graph is an owning spanning tree plus back-edges, where a back-edge is a borrowing reference field or a `Weak<T>` ([§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)). Whether a plain borrowing back-edge can be *populated* by an ownership-valid Bezel program is governed by the borrow-lifetime rule ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows)): a borrow may live in a field only when its source provably outlives the containing object. For a directed acyclic graph this is satisfiable — a topological (owner-before-borrower) construction order builds each back-edge's target first and passes the borrow in as a constructor argument, its source outliving the borrower — which is exactly what the synthesized `Deserialize` does for a DAG ([§5.19](#sec-serialization-back-edges)) and what the §28.5 `_owner` back-reference shape constructs in ordinary code. For a cycle there is no such order: a mutual back-edge would require each node's source to outlive the other, which no construction sequence delivers, so the cycle-closing borrow cannot be shown safe and is rejected (`a.Next := a` is a borrow-into-field whose source is a scope-bound local — refused). A cyclic serialization feature over plain borrows would have a serialize direction with no value the language can construct, and round-trip tests that can only bypass the ownership checker — a test-honesty gap. (A DAG's back-edges are constructable, so it is exercisable end-to-end. The operative rule is the lifetime one; a §28.3 sentence that read as a blanket "borrowing fields are not expressible" overstated it, #717.) With `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` the cyclic back-edge is a `Weak<T>` minted from a `Shared<T>` spine, and the cycle *is* constructable in honest Bezel (below); its round trip passes through the checker like any other program — not because a check was relaxed but because the data has an ownership-valid producer.

**The construction, shown.** A mutual cycle is built as a `Shared<T>` spine with `Weak<T>` back-edges wired after both nodes exist:

```bezel
using var a := new Shared<Node>(new Node(1));   // strong spine
using var b := new Shared<Node>(new Node(2));
a.Get().back := b.Downgrade();                  // Weak<Node>? back-edge, wired post-construction
b.Get().back := a.Downgrade();
```

Every step is ordinary ownership-valid code: `Downgrade()` yields an `owned Weak<Node>` from a live handle, stored into an `owned back: Weak<Node>?` field through the `Get()` borrow; the borrow checker accepts it (verified). The cost is real and stated: the back-edge field is nullable (`Weak<T>?`), assigned after construction rather than in the constructor. That is precisely *why* a cycle needs no special construction mode — definite assignment ([§7.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-definite-assignment-for-non-nullable-fields)) is satisfied trivially by the null initializer, and the later wiring is ordinary mutation, not a synthesized binding of a non-nullable field in an interval that would have to be proved unobservable. A *non-nullable* cycle-closing back-edge would need such a window; a nullable `Weak<T>?` removes the obligation at its root. So no synthesized construction mode escapes §7.9: a DAG's back-edges are bound through the constructor, a cycle's by mutation of a nullable field. The nullability is also the right type: a cycle-closing reference that can dangle (its target released) is nullable in substance, and the type now says so.

**Why this rather than the alternatives.** #703 named three resolutions; recording why the others were declined is the project's discipline ([§5.21](#sec-shared-t)'s declined-alternatives register). *Relax the ownership checker* to track owned-subobject lifetimes (so a borrow to a transitive owner is accepted) was declined: it is an open-ended ownership-analysis enhancement of uncertain blast radius that special-cases serialization back-edges inside the checker, and — decisively — it does not actually reach the cyclic case, because even an accepting checker leaves a *mutual* cycle with no order in which a non-nullable back-edge can be initialized at construction; one would still need a nullable field or a post-construction window. `Weak<T>` already exists, is nullable (so the definite-assignment question simply does not arise), carries last-one-disposes semantics the serializer needs anyway, and expresses the cycle in a first-class type rather than a checker exception. *Document the limitation* (ship cyclic synthesis as construct-from-external-bytes-only) was declined as strictly worse than giving the feature an ownership-valid form: a serialization feature whose serialize direction is unreachable is the test-honesty defect, not a resolution of it.

<a id="sec-serialization-identity"></a>
#### 5.22.3 Identity, disposal and weak-reachability

**No shape is asserted, so none can be violated.** Where a property is not statically verifiable, the serialization design does not let a generic mechanism paper over it: the assumption is made explicit and its violation is a *detected error*, never a silent corruption — serde's silent duplication of shared pointers on deserialize is the documented footgun it refuses. Better still is a bad state that is *unrepresentable* rather than merely detected, the project's preferred disposition wherever it is reachable, and the type-driven model reaches it for shape. A declared shape that the data violates (a declared tree over a graph that aliases) would need detecting; with no declared shape there is nothing to violate: a `Shared<T>` field *is* an aliasing participant by its type, not by an assertion that might be wrong, so "declared tree, actually a graph" cannot be written. *Identity preservation* — binding a `$ref` to the already-built object instead of silently duplicating — is the deserializer's job: the identity table holds the first-reconstructed *handle* and resolves an aliasing `$ref` by `Clone()`ing it (incrementing the shared count) and a `Weak` `$ref` by `Downgrade()`.

**One identity envelope, and the one soundness obligation.** The `$id`/`$ref` identity envelope (stdlib [§2.15](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-concrete-serialization-formats)) carries every shape, so an aliased or cyclic payload round-trips identity-for-identity. The must-not-miss point is the identity *key*. A `Shared<T>`'s identity is the object-reference identity of the value `Get()` borrows — the same object-identity basis the existing `$id` tag→object table already uses (`Object.ReferenceEquals`, language spec [§8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-object-class)) — taken for a non-null live handle; a null `Shared<T>?` takes the ordinary `T?` null path, not the identity table. Two independently `Clone()`d handles to one value are distinct owned objects but their `Get()` borrows resolve to the same underlying object, so they collapse to one `$id` and alias correctly; keying on the *handle* instead would serialize the body twice and destroy the aliasing with no error in an unchecked build. The key is sound because within a single serialize pass every keyed value is simultaneously live ([§5.21](#sec-shared-t)'s last-handle-disposes invariant, normatively [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership)/Appendix C), so no address freed by a disposed value can be reused by another live one mid-pass.

**Disposal on deserialize.** Reconstructing a shared node returns `owned Shared<T>` — the caller's single vote, the handle at count one — and every alias in the graph is reconstructed by `Clone()`ing off the identity table, so the last handle disposes the value exactly once (the exactly-once invariant of [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership)/Appendix C). A class that merely *holds* `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` fields, rather than *being* the shared node, returns `owned C`; the distinction is locally decidable from the entry type. Double-free is structurally impossible: the value is released only at the last handle, behind the disposed guard, and `Weak<T>` never owns. The one behaviour serialization makes visible here is the shared-ownership type's own contract ([§5.21](#sec-shared-t)): a `Shared<T>` value is disposed at the last handle's release, anywhere in the program, not at a single owner's destruction.

**Weak-reachability is enforced at read.** A `Weak<T>` back-reference is meaningful only if its target is *strongly* reached elsewhere in the same serialized graph; a `Weak` whose owning `Shared` is not serialized would resolve to nothing. This is a per-*value* property the field types cannot decide — type identity is not value identity, and the strong owner typically lives in an enclosing object, not a preceding same-class field — so it is enforced dynamically: a `Weak` `$ref` to a target never strongly tagged raises `SerializationException` ([§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)'s established read-side assert-and-enforce). A cheap static pre-check — a `Weak<T>` field is suspect unless some `Shared<T>` of the same `T` is serialized in the same class — is a lint that catches the trivial intra-class mistake early; it is necessary-not-sufficient and does not stand in for the dynamic guarantee. The property the compiler cannot check is checked at read: it is the one obligation the types alone cannot carry.

<a id="sec-serialization-polymorphism-and-inheritance"></a>
#### 5.22.4 Polymorphism and inheritance

**Polymorphic dispatch is orthogonal to graph shape.** The explicit closed registry ([§5.19](#sec-serialization-polymorphic-dispatch)'s *Polymorphic dispatch*) — discriminator written first, dispatched through the registry on read, anything outside it raising, closed because the reflective `$type` binder is a remote-code-execution vector — is independent of how a type's edges reconstruct. The registry is a class-body member declaration listing the discriminator-to-type map, coupled to no shape declaration, and a registered type must be type-driven serialization-eligible (`ISerializable`/`IDeserializable` with serialization-eligible components) ([§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)). It is type-scoped for the reason the shape is (above).

**Inheritance.** A derived class's synthesized form carries its base chain's components, base-first, and its `Deserialize` constructs through an ordinary constructor call that binds the inherited components by name (spec §9.8). Three grounds. An instance's state includes its inherited fields, so a form without them does not round-trip the instance. The `polymorphic` registry already presupposes it: a concrete base registers itself beside derived types that inherit its storage, and each registered type is written in that type's own form (spec §9.8). And the ordinary constructor call the synthesis already makes constructs the base through the constructor's own chain, so there is no second construction path to specify. Each base that declares components must opt in itself, by declaring a serialization interface and leaving its members to synthesis: a base's fields, its private ones included, become wire names and constructor-parameter names only by its own author's opt-in, which already makes them that base's wire contract, so an edit to a base that never opted in cannot change a derived class's wire form; and a base whose author hand-wrote its form has chosen a wire contract that a field-by-field derived form would bypass. Either interface is the opt-in, because the derived class's `Deserialize` constructs through its own constructor and never calls the base's factory; so an abstract base with storage, which has no instance for a factory of its own to construct, opts in with `ISerializable` alone. The requirement is the narrow start: lifting it later admits more classes and breaks none. The derived class's synthesized members are its own conformance's, not declarations hiding the base's (spec §12.2), as the `polymorphic` registry's derived types' already are.

**Declined:** serializing only a class's own components, which leaves a base's state to whatever its constructor chooses and loses data; rejecting synthesis over any base with components, which strands the concrete-base `polymorphic` shape; rejecting only `Deserialize` there, which makes eligibility direction-dependent, as spec §9.8 declines for a record's collection components; synthesizing over a base that never opted in, or that hand-wrote its form, for the reasons above; requiring each base to declare every interface the derived class declares, which leaves an abstract base with storage no way to opt in to a derived class's `IDeserializable`; and nesting a base's components as a sub-object, which gives one instance two objects on the wire and a reserved field name per level, where the flat form needs none.

**A derived instance behind a base type.** The synthesized `Serialize` of a class with no `polymorphic` registry of its own raises `SerializationException` when the run-time type is a derived class (spec §9.8). The class's form omits a derived class's components and reads back as the base, so writing it would drop data or change the type without a sign, which spec §9.8's *Field-name customization* rules out when it says adding a component never silently drops it. The check sits in the member itself, so one rule covers a base-typed component, a base-typed receiver, and a derived class that inherits its base's member without declaring the interface. It is the registry's closed-set rule with the class as its one member; a registry is how a hierarchy widens the set. The rule asks nothing static about derived classes: under separate compilation (spec §2.8) whether a class has subclasses is not known where a component is declared, and every class not `sealed` can have one. The disposition is the one this section takes for weak-reachability: a bad state is made unrepresentable where a static rule can reach it, and detected, never silent, where it is a per-value property the types cannot decide. Whether the value behind a base type is a derived instance is such a property, except for an `abstract` or `closed` class with no registry and a synthesized `Serialize`: every non-null value written through that member would raise and its `Deserialize` has nothing to construct, so such a class is rejected statically where an owning edge or a `Shared<T>` would call the member, and as an `IDeserializable` with no hand-written `Deserialize`. A back-edge never calls the member and stays admitted. **Declined:** a compile error at a derived class that declares components and inherits a synthesized `Serialize` without declaring `ISerializable`. A subclass that is never serialized would have to become serializable: a field of a non-serializable type would need `serialized skip`, which needs a defaultable type, so a non-nullable owned connection would have to become nullable to serve a serializer it never meets, the type deformed by how it would be read (§5.19). Nor would the rule close the base-typed receiver: a subclass that redeclares the interface, reached through its base, still runs the base's member. A warning at the same place was declined for the same reason: it would fire on subclasses that are never serialized, and the language has no spelling for "this class is not serialized". Also declined: rejecting a component whose static type is not `sealed`, which spec §9.8 rules out ("`sealed` is irrelevant to serialization") and which would reject the stdlib's own `Node` example; rejecting it when a derived class exists, which separate compilation cannot decide; and dispatching to the run-time type's own form without a discriminator, which writes a form the static type's `Deserialize` cannot read.

<a id="sec-serialization-hand-written-forms"></a>
#### 5.22.5 Hand-written forms in a hierarchy

**The hand-written edges stay closed.** Three owner decisions keep the current rules. A `serialized skip` or `serialized optional` component's type must be defaultable whatever conformances the class declares ([#5497](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5497)). Reading the requirement as `Deserialize`-only would make eligibility depend on direction, which spec §9.8 declines for a record's collection components and *Inheritance* above declines for a base; a write-only class excludes a non-defaultable component by making it nullable, the cost *A derived instance behind a base type* states. A derived class does not hand-write `Serialize` or `Deserialize` over a base's synthesized member ([#5505](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5505)). The member would be a shadow selected by static type, which §8.1 declines, and exempting `base.Serialize(s)` from the run-time type check would relax the check that keeps a base-typed write from dropping a derived class's data. The design also left unstated which identity tag a `base.Serialize(s)` of `this` writes, and it has no read counterpart, since `base.` is illegal in the static `Deserialize` (spec §12.3). A hierarchy that needs a form per class hand-writes the base's `Serialize` as `virtual`, which a derived class overrides (spec §12.2). A derived class does not declare a recognized static `Deserialize` over a base's hand-written one ([#5506](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5506)): spec §12.2 gives a static member no `override` escape, so a hand-written hierarchy deserializes through one factory, which dispatches as it chooses. That decision is deferred rather than declined: promoting `IDeserializable.Deserialize` to a static interface member (#1507, proposal 0125 Part B) would make each class's factory a distinct conformance witness, and the question is revisited with it.

<a id="sec-type-driven-serialization-historical-note"></a>
#### 5.22.6 Historical note

*Historical note.* From v0.119 to v0.140 the shape was asserted by a class-header clause (`serialized tree`, `serialized graph`, `serialized cyclic`) and enforced at read, and the `polymorphic` registry could appear only beside one. That design was built under one constraint, stated outright in the v0.120 working-out: reaching for Rust's `Rc::new_cyclic`, which hands a closure a `Weak` into the not-yet-initialized allocation, it wrote that "the principled Bezel analog is *not* `Weak` (Bezel has no reference counting and should not grow one for serialization)", and it paid for the structural idea with the two-phase construction window and the self-contained-constructor eligibility rule (v0.123, v0.124) over non-nullable back-edges. That was correct while the language had no shared-ownership primitive. `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` arrived at v0.134 for independent reasons (caches, registries, the multi-consumer-channel question), as the scoped, explicit, opt-in form [§5.4](#sec-no-shared-ownership) had always said it would accept, and v0.141 retired the clauses, the window and the eligibility rule: `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` were admitted to the eligibility set first (purely additive), the clauses were then removed, and the cyclic round-trip tests, which never had an ownership-valid producer, were retired rather than rewritten. The wire form did not change. v0.124's claim that the stack was "complete … no undischarged obligation" was true of the wire-and-synthesis layer and stays true of it; it is premise-bound, not retracted — the cyclic tier was complete but unexercisable, the #703 gap rather than a wire defect.

<a id="sec-the-affine-model"></a>
### 5.23 The affine model: auto-drop with a must-use warning

*(The changelog's v0.150–v0.152 entries ([`Bezel_Changelog.md`](Bezel_Changelog.md)) are the detailed record of this transition; the changelog is not part of the language definition, so the transition's load-bearing reasoning is stated here.)*

An owned value still held at a normal scope exit — neither moved, returned, stored, nor disposed — is disposed there, implicitly, by the same mechanism the exception edge uses: **affine auto-drop**, with a **must-use warning** on the implicit drop of a plain `var`. The alternative is strict linearity on the normal path, where such a value is a hard compile error. The model was strict-linear first, and three pressures broke that position:

1. **The language is affine at the seams anyway.** The exception path disposes live owned values during unwinding (nobody proposes a compile error for "an exception happened while you held a resource"), and *unbound owned temporaries* auto-dispose at end of statement. Strict linearity for named values leaves the absurdity that *naming* a value changes whether the program compiles: `new Foo().Use();` compiles while `var a := new Foo(); a.Use();` would be an error — a refactoring hazard with no safety content.
2. **The error rejects sound programs.** Every program it rejects has a perfectly defined meaning (dispose at scope end) that the exception path already implements.
3. **Precedent.** Rust — the reference point for compile-time ownership — is affine: values drop at scope end, and "you probably meant to use this" is a lint (`#[must_use]`), not a rejection. Strict linearity (Austral-style) buys explicitness at the price of ceremony on every early return and every conditionally-consumed value.

The rule is uniform, for locals and `owned` parameters alike. The two declaration forms keep distinct intent: `using var` means scope-pinned (never movable, no warning — you said what you meant); plain `var` means movable, warned if implicitly dropped. The discard `_ := expr;` ([§3.2](#sec-the-discard-_)) is the explicit "dispose this now" form. Exactly-once disposal on every path is preserved throughout — the move analysis marks transferred values moved-from, and scope-end disposal skips them.
