What was deliberately *kept* strict: `Task`/`Task<T>` discard stays an error (abandoning a running operation is a semantic decision, routed to `Task.FireAndForget`) — and the anonymous `using var _ := task;` is the same decision one scope later, rejected with it: nothing can await an unnamed binding, so the form is abandonment by construction, while the named `using var t` idiom stays, a name being exactly what a later `await` needs; moving out of a `using var` stays an error (scope-pinning is the form's entire meaning — the register's one recorded timing carve against the pin is axiom A12, Pattern A chaining, [§5.26](#sec-the-ownership-kernel)); and fields stay outside it (fields are object state, not scope state — K9; auto-drop of a *field* would be a use-after-free generator, not a convenience).

The trade recorded honestly: the compile-time "you forgot to discharge" error is gone, replaced by a warning. Programs that leak by *intent-mismatch* (the author believed something consumed the value) now compile with a diagnostic instead of failing. Implementation experience since the transition supports the choice — the warning has proven sufficient signal in practice, and an entire class of false-positive discharge errors (branch-dependent consumption) disappeared with it.

The three-pattern doctrine this section grounds is stated once, normatively, in spec §28.3 "Choosing a disposal form": `using var` = declared intent + scope pin; plain `var` = movable, warned on the implicit drop; explicit `Dispose()` = an early/conditional release point (exception-safe by this section's own mechanism — the C# dispose-at-end anti-pattern deliberately does not transfer). The discard of a named owned binding, `_ := x;`, is a member of the site-announced-kill family, under the same no-marker logic as `throw x` and `owned` returns; and the must-use warning is uniform across body kinds (frame-disposed `owned` parameters of iterators and async methods warn at the declaration, the adoption idiom being the warning-free spelling).

<a id="sec-the-full-expression-boundary"></a>
### 5.24 The full-expression boundary and the `case`-scrutinee extension

[§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model)'s model disposes unbound owned temporaries implicitly "at the end of the full expression," and that phrase carries load in a dozen rules — temporary-confined awaits, deferred-activation frame bounds, span admissions — so disposal points turn on what a full expression *is*. The spec defines it once (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-unbound-owned-temporaries), #1795): a full expression is a maximal expression — one that is not a subexpression of a larger expression — with the positions enumerated (initializers, assignment right-hand sides, expression statements, each evaluation of a condition or pattern guard, `for` sources, `return`/`throw`/`yield return` operands, and `case` scrutinees).

Maximality is the only defensible direction: any narrower boundary disposes a temporary while an enclosing expression may still hold a borrow into it, and every sub-maximal candidate we examined was either unsound or an arbitrary line no reader could predict. (It is also the boundary C++ draws for its temporaries — one of the few places that precedent is genuinely simple.) The refinement that earns its keep is per-evaluation boundaries for conditions and guards: a loop condition is a fresh full expression at each iteration, so a condition's temporaries die before the next test runs. Keyed on the lexical occurrence instead, a condition that constructs a probe object would accumulate one undisposed temporary per iteration — an unbounded implicit leak inside the very mechanism §5.23 adopted to make disposal automatic and predictable.

The one place the boundary is deliberately wider than the producing expression is the `case` scrutinee: a scrutinee temporary lives through the whole construct, in statement position exactly as in expression position. Pattern bindings are borrows whose source is the scrutinee, so disposing the temporary at arm *selection* would kill every binding under the selected arm's feet (`case pool.PopFirst() of var f: FastConnection => f.Warm(), … end;`). In expression position this already followed from maximality — the enclosing full expression contains the whole construct — so the ruling aligns statement position with what expression position already did, rather than inventing new machinery. The alternative was the [§9.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-records-and-disposal)-style rejection: forbid binding patterns over temporary scrutinees, as spans forbid temporary sources, and extend nothing. Declined, because the rejection could not be contained — to stay coherent it would have had to outlaw the expression-position form too, and that form was long-admitted and idiomatic. The stretch is recorded as the bounded thing it is: the boundary ends with the construct that syntactically contains the scrutinee, everywhere else §9.7's reject-not-extend posture stands, and Bezel still has no general lifetime-extension mechanism.

**The second recorded stretch: the scoped await-bind ([proposal 0082](proposals/0082-pattern-a-scoped-bind.md), [#3048](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3048)).** The boundary has two recorded stretches. Spec [§30.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-scoped-await-bind)'s scoped await-bind admits `var x := await FetchConfigAsync();` (the `const` spelling, and the completed-task `.Result` read, in lockstep) by desugaring the declaration into the hidden `using var` slot the programmer would otherwise have been told to write. The containment is the same recorded kind as the scrutinee's, and structurally tighter on every axis: one construct (a fresh `var`/`const` declaration whose initializer is exactly the await of a fresh unbound producer — a syntactic form plus one classification the compiler already computes at every await site), one desugaring (the slot *is* a `using var` at the same line; the rule is definable — and was prototyped — as macro-expansion into today's language, the #3048 stage-1 prototype measuring zero new analyses, zero freeze extension, zero checked-mode change), one bound (the innermost enclosing block: the same reader-computable bound the two-line form has, against the declined general mechanism's arbitrary consumer lifetimes). The honest asymmetry with the scrutinee precedent, restated rather than smoothed over: that stretch was *forced* — rejecting it would have outlawed a long-admitted form — while this one is *elective*, since the two-line form always exists; an elective stretch needed evidence, which is why it waited on the 0051 Part B gate (C1 measured at 7/11 strict Pattern A sites; C2 satisfied on the letter with the spirit-reading caveat recorded; C3 answered by prototype). The conceded cost stays recorded with the decision: the invisible lifetime — the line reads as "cfg is the config" while an unnamed slot holds the config until a scope end the line does not mention — which §30.5's teaching diagnostics and error-participation wording narrow but do not remove. The boundary ends with the innermost enclosing block of the one binding form; everywhere else reject-not-extend stands; Bezel still has no general lifetime-extension mechanism.

**A callee that stores a temporary argument ([#5360](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5360)).** A fresh value passed to a plain parameter is a call-bounded borrow of an unbound temporary, and a callee may store that parameter in a field. The borrowed-field rule admits the store from any plain parameter, on the caller's recorded obligation that the source outlives the object (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules)). The call is admitted and the temporary is disposed at its full-expression boundary all the same; a later use through the stored reference is the checked-disposal fault. Two alternatives were declined. *Rejecting the call when the callee's body stores the parameter* would key a caller's verdict on information from inside another declaration's body. No per-body fact records that a parameter is stored (proposal 0105 §1), and the verdict of one call would change when its callee became virtual, moved behind an interface, or moved to another module, where no body is visible. It is also the general form of the extension spec [§15.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-collection-literals) declines for `Add`/`Insert`: "an opaque callee's signature type-announces no retention"; [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking) already recorded that `coll.Add(new X())` and `sink.Consume(new X())` are indistinguishable plain-parameter calls. The language rejects a temporary where a declaration announces the retention: a returned frame, a frame taken by an `owned` parameter, a borrow-bearing record's or choice's field, a `scoped` parameter, a `ref` or `out` location. `scoped` is the spelling that turns a back-reference into a statically checked bound. *Keeping the temporary alive while a callee may retain it* is the general lifetime-extension mechanism the boundary excludes: the disposal point would depend on another body, no reader could see the extended lifetime, and the stored reference would still dangle once the extended lifetime ended. A scope-bound local passed to a storing plain parameter is the same rule at a wider boundary: admitted under the same obligation, disposed at its scope's end.

**Property assignments of borrow-carrying values ([#5387](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5387)).** An auto-property's assignment stores into its backing field (spec §23.1), and a block-form setter's `value` is a plain parameter (spec §23.3), so one assignment drew the field-store verdict under one accessor form and the plain-parameter verdict above under the other. Through an interface or a `virtual` member the assigning site cannot tell which applies. The verdict is read at the assignment, as a store into a non-`owned` field of the receiver (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *property stores of a borrow-carrying value*), as [§4.2](#sec-why-function-typed-values-are-runtime-managed) reads the store of a function value. A settable instance property is the declaration that announces the retention here: any implementation of it may be an auto-property, whose assignment is a store. This is also the reading spec axiom A4 already gives the write face: an assignment through `set(owned)` is a store into an owned field (K3.1) whatever the setter's body does, and an assignment through a plain setter is the store into a non-`owned` field. Two alternatives were declined. *Reading the assignment by the accessor form the site resolves* gives one object two verdicts: `obj.P := local` is rejected through the concrete type and admitted through an interface, and a fresh value admitted through an interface dangles in the backing field it reaches once its statement ends. *Reading every property assignment as a call of the setter* would turn the static rejections that reach an auto-property's assignment, a borrow of a scope-bound local in a field (spec §28.3) and a fresh value in a non-`owned` field (spec §28.6), into the plain-parameter residual for the commonest property form. The adopted rule's cost is narrow: a block-form setter that only reads its `value` can no longer receive a fresh value or a borrow of a scope-bound local, where a method with a plain parameter can, and a block-form setter of a borrow-bearing record type, such as a span, receives only a null-source value; the remedy is a method.

**Static property assignments ([#5487](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5487)).** Static properties are not dispatched, and spec §11.8 defers static interface properties, so a site always resolves the declaration it assigns through. The accessor-form split exists at statics all the same. Spec §22.12 rule 2 admits a mutable static auto-property of class type only at a synchronized-handle type or `Task?`, and there an assignment stores into a static that discharges a fresh value into process lifetime. A block-form setter at the same type takes `value` as a plain parameter, so a fresh value would be disposed at its full-expression boundary even when the setter stores it. The uniform reading carries over: a static property assignment is a store into a non-`owned` static declared as the property is (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *property stores of a borrow-carrying value*). At an admitted type a fresh value is discharged and lives to process exit whatever the setter does; a setter that drops `value` therefore leaves it undisposed until exit, the same bounded cost spec §22.13 accepts for a displaced static. At any other type a settable static property is necessarily block-form, and a fresh value is the fresh-store error, as it is for an instance block-form setter. A `static readonly` auto-property is assignable only in its type's static initialization, and that assignment is the `static readonly` field store spec §22.13 already discharges. The ground is #5360's: rewriting an auto-property in block form must not change a client's verdict or the point at which its value is disposed. Two alternatives were declined. *Reading the assignment by accessor form* changes both when the form changes, and leaves a disposed referent in whatever static a block-form setter stores `value` into. *Rejecting every fresh value at a static property* would withdraw the store semantics spec §22.12 rule 2 gives an admitted static auto-property, the `Task?` cache among them, and the discharge spec §22.13 gives a `static readonly` one. The cost: a block-form static setter of a class type outside rule 2's forms receives no fresh value and no borrow of a scope-bound local; the remedy is a `set(owned)` setter or a static method.

**No caller-side warning keyed on the callee's body ([#5374](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5374)).** Spec [§15.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-collection-literals) warns when a fresh temporary reaches `Add`, `Insert` or the indexer-set of a borrowing collection. Those positions are element stores the standard library defines, so they store on every call; the warning rests on that knowledge, not on the signature, which §15.4 itself says type-announces no retention. A user callee's plain parameter carries no such knowledge, so the same warning at `holder.Hold(new Item())` would need a per-parameter fact computed from the callee's body ("this parameter may reach a field or borrowing-collection store") and carried in its artifact interface. That is a wider sibling of the derived-retention fact proposal 0132 studied and deferred. 0132's fact counts only stores into `readonly` fields of `this`, a narrowing a temporary does not need, since its source dies at the full expression whatever the holder does next. 0132's objections apply to the wider fact unchanged. A body edit would change what another module's build prints, keyed on a fact no signature shows ([§9.7](#sec-pure-declared-not-derived)'s third reason; proposal 0048's refactoring seam). The warning would fire at a statically bound callee and stay silent at a `virtual`, interface or function-value call to the same body. The fact is also a may-fact, a store on some path, short of the certainty §15.4's warning rests on. And the spelling that turns the hazard into a checked bound exists: `scoped`, which rejects a fresh producer at the call, or `owned`, which takes the value. The temporary is the shape in which a caller-side warning would be most certain, since the source dies at the full expression's own boundary and no dominance analysis is needed. It is recorded as an instance for 0132's reopening, not as a separate mechanism. Until then the trust-admission report names the store at the callee (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-diagnostics)).

<a id="sec-deferred-activation-capture"></a>
### 5.25 Callbacks that outlive the call: frame residence and capture-carried sources

The capture rules of spec [§28.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-scoped-callback-parameters) classify a plain function-typed parameter as *call-bounded* — every captured source trivially outlives the call, because the callback is not retained past it. That premise is false for precisely the callees whose point is deferral: an iterator, async iterator, or lazy query operator captures its callback into a frame that runs after the call returns. The async half is answered by the settlement obligation; iterators need a callee-side rule and a caller-side one too. A carried-source enumeration of the frame that counted only the receiver and the plain reference-typed arguments, leaving out *capture sets*, would let an iterator frame built over a capturing callback (`return Doubled(nums, lambda(x) => scale.Apply(x));` with `scale` a `using var`) pass the frame-return check by the letter, while the same frame with `scale` passed as a plain argument is rejected at the same return site: a statically certain dangle, guarded by nothing. The ruling (#1790) closes both halves with the announcement-by-declared-type doctrine this chapter keeps returning to. Callee side, frame residence is the admitted retention and the frame's life is the bound — settlement for a task, completion-or-disposal for an iterator frame, the single consumption point for a lazy query — type-announced by the deferred-activation return type exactly as that type already announces the frame's carried object borrows (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-returning-a-deferred-activation-frame)). Caller side, a callback argument contributes its captured borrows' sources to the frame's carried-source set, so the ordinary borrow machinery — not a new check — rejects the dangling composition at the return site.

The contribution is computed from statically known provenance, transitively (a captured closure contributes *its* sources in turn), and the provenance table's edge rows are where the design earns its keep. A function value fetched from a plain field or a collection contributes nothing — *soundly*, because the closure-escape rules never admitted a borrow-capturing closure into such storage in the first place: plainly-stored function values are borrow-free by construction, and no whole-program analysis is needed. (The one storing location that does hold capture-bearing callbacks — a `scoped` field — contributes nothing only because the read-out is itself barred: passing a `scoped` field's value into a deferred-activation plain position is rejected as an escaping read-out, spec [§28.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-scoped-callback-parameters) — the liftable widening, contributing the field's owner as the source, is recorded there.) And a plain function-typed *parameter* of the enclosing body contributes *itself*: the parameter becomes a carried source that every caller re-establishes against its own actual argument, which is what keeps the forwarding combinator — `Filtered(xs, f) => xs.Where(f)` — compositional. (Rejecting opaque parameter arguments instead was declined as outlawing half the motivation.) This is the same signature-level re-establishment as [§5.7](#sec-factory-method-ownership)'s source-set rule and §12.10's callee-side task rule; the three rulings share one skeleton.

**A captured value that holds borrows is counted by what it carries ([#5677](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5677); proposal [0148](proposals/0148-captured-holds-borrows-value-contributes-its-sources.md)).** A span, a borrow-bearing record or inline array, or a choice holding a class payload lives in its capture cell like an `int`, and the cell is what the closure keeps alive — but the cell holds borrows, and those borrows' sources are what can die. The direct count was already in place: the same value passed as an argument "contributes the sources of the borrows it carries", and a live local of such a type freezes every held borrow's source at a consume site. Provenance clause 1 counts the capture the same way, and the two exemptions ("a value-typed capture contributes nothing"; the §28.14 bound's "captured value-typed local or parameter") reach only values that carry no borrow. One consequence is deliberate: a closure over such a value is now capture-bearing even when every carried borrow is a parameter's, so the sink rule keeps it out of returns and fields as it keeps a closure over a class-typed parameter — the caller of a method returning `() -> int` gets a function value carrying the empty set and could carry it past the array the captured span views. A method reference bound to such a value is counted the same way, its receiver being a by-copy capture. The alternatives were a flat ban on capturing such a value, which rejects the in-place invocation and the in-scope lazy query that §9.7's capture bullet and stdlib §4.16 admit, and a route list in §9.7's capture bullet (invocation results, frames, storage-bounded holders), which is the closure-specific list the closure bullet forbids and reopens at the next route. The set is the one mechanism; adding the item to it is the whole fix.

Two postures were declined by name. Spelling the retention `scoped` is the wrong tool on four counts: `scoped` means storage-bounded-by-an-*object*, with an admission and freeze built for open-ended object lifetimes, while a frame is a flow-tracked value the borrow machinery already bounds; `scoped` is barred on static methods without `owned` returns — a compile error for the dominant iterator shape; its admission forms exclude plain-parameter forwarding, half the motivation again; and it would force a breaking migration of every lazy standard-library operator. And leaving the retention *documented but unverified* — the posture async borrows had before the settlement regime — was declined more firmly than anywhere else it has come up: the settlement regime already rejected that posture for async, and the iterator case is worse, because the frame is long-lived *by design* (lazy evaluation is the feature), so the documented gap would be load-bearing for a statically certain soundness break rather than a hedge over a lifetime the checker cannot compare. What the declines protect: no standard-library signature changes (the lazy operators' plain signatures become the normative instance of the general rule, not a certified exception), async upgrades from trust to rejection — statically-certain dangles at return sites, and categorically, paralleling object-borrow escapes, at store and scope sites, and user code can finally write callback-taking iterators at all.

<a id="sec-the-ownership-kernel"></a>
### 5.26 The ownership kernel: reasons and the axiom budget

Spec §28.3 opens with the ownership kernel — nine principles, K1–K9, from which the chapter's rules derive — and closes its kernel subsection with a register of eleven recorded axioms and four tombstones (A1–A15; A1 retired v0.201, A2 retired v0.258, A8 retired v0.213, A13 retired v0.260): the rules the kernel cannot derive, each a design decision with a recorded reason. This section carries the reasons, and the discipline the register installs.

**Why a kernel.** The ownership chapters grew by accretion of rulings — each individually reasoned, collectively unpredictable: a reader could not derive the rule they hadn't read. Consolidating the load-bearing principles into one normative statement buys three things at once. *Predictability*: the freeze's membership, the admitted await positions, and the equalization patterns all compute from stated principles instead of being memorized as lists. *Auditability*: the "memory rules few enough to learn in full" bet (§1) finally has a count — nine principles plus the axiom register's rows are what "in full" contains (the count is the register's to state at any revision; the learnability claim is the *discipline* — visible append, tombstoned delete — not any fixed number). *Mechanizability*: a finite kernel is a modelable one, and a derivation-tagged chapter is one whose diagnostics can explain rejections by derivation. The kernel is deliberately prose, not formal notation: the spec's register is precise prose for the guides' audience, and the formal artifact has a better home in a mechanized model of the kernel, which can be exact without being normative.

**The budget discipline.** The axiom count is published as the language's honest complexity budget. A future ruling that touches §28/§30.5 states its classification — a derivation (naming the K's), an axiom extension (naming the axiom), or a new axiom (adding a register row with its recorded reason). This is a changelog-entry obligation on the spec-change checklist. Axiom ids are frozen: new axioms append; deletions leave a tombstone row pointing at the deleting revision's changelog entry, so "which axiom did this ruling extend?" stays answerable historically. Two candidate axioms were examined during consolidation and classified derived instead — the property-getter-only static-storage admission (derives from K2.4 + the declaration-site static-return rule — at consolidation time, K2.4 + then-axiom A1: a storage-returning static *method* is signature-indistinguishable from a factory; the property face's read is the sound dual) and the maybe-consumed join (derives from K7 with the free-precision argument of [§5.1](#sec-owned-not-borrows)'s join rule) — the worked precedent that the register resists both inflation and laundering.

**Why K4.2's criterion is two bits, neither redundant.** A site-announced kill must be *keyword-visible* and leave the *disposed-flag backstop*. Each conjunct is decisive against a real kill family, so neither can be dropped: an obligation-moving transfer (the consume table's family-1 rows — `move`, marker-free stores, `owned` returns, owning `yield return`) is keyword- or target-visible yet sets no flag — the value lives on under a new holder — so visibility alone would wrongly admit it as announced; drop-on-overwrite (K3.11) sets the flag for the displaced value yet announces nothing at the site — a bare `x := e;` — so the backstop alone would wrongly admit it. The two-bit form is the smallest criterion that excludes both.

**The axioms' reasons.** Where an axiom's full reasoning already lives in this document, the register row points here and this list cross-references it rather than duplicating:

- **A1 (tombstone, retired v0.201)** — [§5.7](#sec-factory-method-ownership): the v0.60 factory-default bridge, retired when its motives discharged; static class- or interface-typed returns now type-announce in the signature (spec §28.3). The register keeps the tombstone so the extension point stays answerable historically.
- **A2 (tombstone, retired v0.258)** — [§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends): the task/iterable calls-mint split, retired when the fence-accretion ledger met the reversal's new-evidence bar — calls now mint for the iterable family too, the stored-collection view respelled to the property face exactly as v0.190 respelled the stored task; the register keeps the tombstone so the extension point stays answerable historically.
- **A3 (the two-container async design)** — [§12.1](#sec-pattern-a-vs-pattern-b-is-type-directed)–[§12.2](#sec-choosing-between-taskt-and-taskowned-t): fan-out versus acquisition is a surface design choice the producer makes in the signature; the kernel checks both flows, forces neither.
- **A4 (accessor faces are lend-only)** — signature predictability: an accessor read has no marker position, so admitting owned production through a getter would make ownership invisible at every property read; factories are methods, where the `owned` return announces the transfer (a marker-free position). (Recorded here; spec §23/§28.6 state the rule.)
- **A5 (`using var` is a scope pin)** — [§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model): the form's entire meaning is declared intent plus scope-pinning; movability was deliberately kept out at the affine transition so that the two declaration forms keep distinct intents.
- **A6 (`Task` discard rejection)** — [§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model) and [§3.2](#sec-the-discard-_): abandoning a running operation is a semantic decision, not a disposal; the routed spelling (`Task.FireAndForget`) keeps the decision visible. The anonymous `using var _` binding is inside the axiom's reach: unawaitable, it is the discard one scope later.
- **A7 (no `??`-operand/operator-operand transfer)** — the register's liftable bound, half-lifted as designed: [proposal 0056](proposals/0056-arm-position-transfer-marker.md) supplied the arm spelling (`move x` as an if-expression branch and an expression-`case` arm result, a K3.4 derivation riding K7.4's existing per-path machinery, no new axiom), and the row covers only the positions still unspelled. The marker parses in the A7 positions (pinned by `marked_transfer_positions_2080.bzl`), so the axiom's content is the recorded rejection rather than a spelling gap, which the row's wording states ([proposal 0090](proposals/0090-transfer-marker-study.md) §5). The `??`-operand lift is gated on a genuinely new ruling 0056 records (the post-state of a marked nullable binding evaluated while null); operator operands remain unmotivated. No-silent-consume stays uniform: the unmarked read is a borrow arm.
- **A8 (tombstone, retired v0.213)** — [§5.3](#sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements)/[§5.1](#sec-owned-not-borrows): the caller-vouched transit disposal, retired when the 2026-07 corpus sweep (#2075) found zero load-bearing clients — the values-owned shape is `OwningDictionary`'s by construction and the own-keys direction has none; the register keeps the tombstone so the extension point stays answerable.
- **A9 (the two collection families)** — [§5.3](#sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements)/[§5.11](#sec-owned-element-collections): the resolution after the `<owned T>` merged form collapsed under its substitution machinery.
- **A10 (stdlib certification grants)** — a named allowlist of trust granted to specific stdlib types (`CancellationToken`'s source-outlives-settlement certification; the §30.11 completion-source callback admission, a named carve beside §28.14 rather than a derivation of it): by construction not derivable — the grant is the stdlib vouching for its own implementation — and acceptable only because the list is closed, named, and normatively stated at each type.
- **A11 (the §28.14 admission forms and bounds)** — decidability engineering (#1421): the chosen static story for "capture source outlives bounding object" among several sound alternatives; several bounds are recorded liftable, and lifting one swaps an axiom clause for a derivation without touching the principle (K2.3 + K5.7). The handle-holding `owned` field of a form-3 or form-4 admission is not frozen against `move` (spec §28.14, *The registration-lifetime residual*): freezing it would require flow-marking fields by what initialized them, a per-value property a field declaration does not carry, so the registration's extension past the bounding object is recorded as the same residual-trust shape as quiescence, with the checked-mode backstop.
- **A12 (chaining's timing dispensation)** — a recorded timing carve against A5's pin for the one composition where the pinned slot already lives in the async frame: deferral to the outer task's teardown preserves exactly-once transitively and moves nothing.
- **A13 (tombstone, retired v0.260)** — the move-only reassignment carve, recorded as debt (tracked #1385, interacting with the then-open #747 design) precisely so it could not be mistaken for intended design — and retired exactly that way: proposal 0101's study tested the recorded ground ("a partial state the compiler cannot cheaply dispose") against the shipped machinery and found it discharged — the disposal pass #747 gated is normative (spec §15.1.1) and the reference compiler had already implemented the per-slot overwrite disposal against #1385's own sketch — so whole-value reassignment of an owning inline array now takes §28.7's drop-on-overwrite per live slot, no partial state is ever disposed (a moved-from slot contributes nothing; the kill discharges the displaced value's restoration obligations on K9.3's ground), and the exactly-once claim holds without qualification. The static form-(iv) residue rode §22.13's static-overwrite posture, a recorded rule, not an axiom, until v0.334 retired the form (proposal 0162). The register keeps the tombstone so the extension point stays answerable historically.
- **A14 (no `owned` generic arguments)** — [§5.3](#sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements)/[§5.11](#sec-owned-element-collections): the substitution machinery was tried for nine revisions and abandoned; named types capture owning semantics without it.
- **A15 (the self-returning enumerator)** — [§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends): the announcement table's callee-side set is closed and admits no borrow return, and `this` is a borrow, so §29.1's long-standing permission escapes the set rather than deriving from it — K6.3 demands a fresh or owned frame and a receiver return is neither. Recorded as an axiom rather than as a widening because the admission is keyed on three conjuncts (the class implements both enumeration heads; the member is the protocol's `GetEnumerator()`; the returned expression is literally `this`) and no caller-side classification changes; written generally it would be a design change. The half that makes it sound where the receiver is a borrow the caller does not own is the non-reclaiming disposal: the frame's disposal runs `Dispose()` and leaves the reclaim with the receiver's own owner, idempotence (spec §28.2) doing the rest. The *static* receiver-returning form remains deferred (#3255).

**What the kernel deliberately excludes.** Sendability and the domain memory model (§12.5–§12.7), nullability, and definite assignment are adjacent systems with their own rules; the kernel touches them only at named interface points (spawn/send as consume positions; the domain clause as K4.3's recorded limit). A second kernel for the domain model, if ever warranted, is its own proposal.

**Grounds of §28.3's derived paragraphs.** Spec §28.3 states the rules below with their `(derives: …)` tags; their soundness arguments are stated once here. (1) *Set-erasing positions*, clause 1 — why a body's `as` to one of its own type parameters is set-erasing at the declaration: at an `Object` substitution it is the `as` the clause names, and at a function-type substitution it would hand the body, which is not re-checked, a function value at a position declared at the parameter. (2) *Function values a generic body forms*, rule 1 — a deferred-activation frame built over such a value as a selector carries the value's captures only, because the frame's elements are the selector's results, which the `return` clause keeps from being such values; and what the body invokes such a value with are its own `T`-typed values, capture-free by the induction at *Set-erasing positions*. (3) *Implementations and overrides read their function-typed parameters at the declaring signature* — at a value substitution the declaring-signature reading is vacuous, and at a class substitution it agrees with the substituted one (announcement-table rows 4 and 5 coincide), so the rule admits nothing a generic body relies on and costs nothing where no route opens. (4) *Function-typed holders* — a local inline array is no exception to the holder rule: the array is a value that copies into returns, fields, components and plain arguments, none of which announces a source set; and the record's, choice's and inline array's classification and holds-borrows property are unchanged by a function-typed component, a function type being runtime-managed. (5) *Deferred-activation callees* — frame residence type-announces retention for call-site arguments and opens no read-out for stored callbacks, which is why a `scoped` field's value and a `scoped` parameter have no admitted path into a plain function-typed argument position (spec §28.14's callee-side rights — `scoped`-field store, same-bound `scoped` forwarding, invocation — include none). (6) *Returning a deferred-activation frame* — a returned frame whose capture-set contribution reaches a `using var` is rejected because the consumer would enumerate into a closure over a disposed object; the by-declared-type classification is conservative in the caller's favour (a caller of a materialized `IEnumerable<T>` over-constrains its own arguments and loses nothing). (7) *Static stores* — the static-storage-only admission is what every static read already assumes: every admissible static form keeps its referents alive to process exit, which a stored borrow of any other source would falsify.

<a id="sec-why-using-var-is-not-folded-into-var"></a>
### 5.27 Why `using var` is not folded into plain `var` — or into `const`

The proposal recurs naturally, and this section is its standing answer. Local ownership is inferred, not annotated ([§5.5](#sec-local-variable-ownership-is-inferred-not-annotated)); `const` covers non-owned single assignment ([§17.8](#sec-readonly-local-bindings-let-val-readonly-var)); and the affine auto-drop disposes a plain-`var` owned local at the same scope-end point as a `using var` on every non-moved path — the spec's own recap says the forms "never add *safety*; they add declared intent and timing" (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows), *Choosing a disposal form*). So the keyword can look like ceremony over inference: why not let `var` do everything? The question was examined against the full rule surface and declined, for four reasons that are one reason: the keyword is not an inference annotation but a declared constraint on the binding's future, and a checker cannot infer the intent it exists to check. Inference reads the initializer — the past. The keyword constrains what comes after — reassignment, movement, source liveness — which no use pattern present in the code can imply, because the constraint is deliberately stronger than any use.

*First, the pin has no substitute.* A5's guarantees — non-reassignable, never movable out — exist to make *maintenance drift* a compile error at the drift site: a lock guard threaded out of its critical section, a mid-scope re-acquisition, a borrow's source consumed under a view the author meant to hold whole-scope. The borrow-pin (spec [§28.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-using-var-initialized-from-a-borrow)) is the only spelling of "keep this source live to the end of this scope" for a source this body does not own — a borrow-returning call, a field, an indexer, a borrow-state binding; its nearest encoding without the keyword is a dummy use on the block's last line — an invariant written as dead code. (Over a named *owned* binding, whose only kills are this body's own consumes of it, the pin's only content would be a freeze against those consumes, and the unmarked spelling is rejected: adoption, `using var y := move x;`, freezes more strongly, and `using var` on the source itself is A5. A freeze of an outer owned local confined to an inner block, after its view's last use, has no spelling; [proposal 0137 §5.1](proposals/0137-mark-the-using-var-pin.md) records that cost with the decision.) Movability was deliberately kept out at the affine transition so that the two declaration forms keep distinct, checkable intents ([§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model)); that decision is re-affirmed here, not merely inherited.

*Second, the lint and the keyword are one economy.* The must-use warning is the affine model's compensating diagnostic — the affine trade replaced the hard discharge error with a warning precisely because a zero-ceremony declared-intent spelling exists to silence it ([§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model)). Without `using var`, the warning either fires on every idiomatic scope-held acquisition (`using var guard := mutex.Acquire();` is the standard library's own idiom, stdlib [§6.2](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-mutex-and-lock)), or is silenced by a trailing explicit `Dispose()` — ceremony that corrupts the doctrine that an explicit dispose signals an *early or conditional* release — or is retired, reversing the recorded trade and letting the intent-mismatch leak class go dark. The warning is high-signal because the silencer is zero-cost; the silencer is meaningful because the warning exists. The pair stands or falls together.

*Third, the keyword is a loudness channel for API evolution.* When a callee's return type flips between borrowed and `owned`, today's binding forms grade the blast radius at every call site: `const` sites reject (an owned production is inadmissible there, spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations)), plain-`var` sites warn at the new implicit drop, and `using var` sites absorb the change correctly by uniform admissibility. Unification — with the lint necessarily detuned per the previous paragraph — reduces that matrix to silence: sound in every cell, affinity guarantees it, and invisible in exactly the dimension (who disposes, and when) this language undertakes to make visible.

*Fourth, "only `var`" is unreachable anyway.* Whether disposal *awaits* is deliberately not inferable: an implicit disposal point never awaits, so a class whose async cleanup must run needs an `await using var` binding (spec [§28.12](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-asynchronous-disposal)) — and `TaskScope` is consumable by `await using` only (stdlib [§6.10](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-taskscope)). The `out owned` recipient form `out using var` (spec [§22.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-inline-out-declarations)) survives for the same reason. The end state of the proposal is therefore not one binding form; it is the same number of forms with the common one missing.

**Nor does `const` absorb it.** The `const` settlement draws this seam deliberately ([§17.8](#sec-readonly-local-bindings-let-val-readonly-var)): `using var` is the owned, scope-pinned binding with declared disposal; `const` is the non-owned one whose bindings never change state — not by assignment, not by consumption, not by disposal — and the permissive owned-`const` is declined as an undeclared-disposal duplicate of `using var`. The reasons hold from both directions. A `const` that accepted owned producers would hide a real scope-end event behind the one keyword whose entire claim is that nothing ever happens — `const guard := mutex.Acquire()` would release a lock at `end` — and the async surface makes the hiding structural: `await using var` marks a suspension point at scope exit, and an "await const" is incoherent. A `using var` doing `const`'s job forces pin semantics onto every fixed borrow — a `const` borrow's constraint on its source deliberately ends at its last use (spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations)), so the source may legitimately be consumed or disposed afterward, a program the pin rejects — and reads absurdly over plain values, the state from before `const` that §17.8 records as tolerated-not-endorsed (#1360). A single merged fixed-binding form must choose always-pin (rejecting legal `const` programs) or never-pin (deleting the borrow-pin's only spelling, on which the [§29.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-desugaring-of-for) `for`-desugaring and deliberate iterable pinning stand); no intermediate point exists — a pin either is a compiler-enforced guarantee or it is not.

What the examination *conceded* is recorded with equal weight. The keyword adds no safety and no disposal timing on non-moved paths — the spec says so in terms. It does not tell the reader whether the binding is owned: its owned-versus-pin split is dispatched by the right-hand side (spec [§28.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-using-var-initialized-from-a-borrow)); at a bare named owned binding, and at an `if`, `case` or `??` arm-meet of such bindings alone, where both readings would be plausible, the form is rejected, so the split is decided by the right-hand side's visible shape: a producer adopts, a borrow pins. The residual overlap with `const` over inert right-hand sides is real, tolerated for generic and desugaring uniformity, and steered by diagnostics rather than semantics (the vacuous-pin lint, #2073). And the compiler's two-path local classifier is duplication the surface design does not require — the spec defines a `using var`'s state by the same right-hand-side algebra as `var`, so the implementation should compute it through one funnel with the pin and the lint boundary as layered flags (#2071, #2072). Formally, unification introduces no ownership ambiguity: inference is total either way, and always was. What it removes is the reader's instrument, not the checker's — the one binding form whose *scope-boundness* is declared at the declaration, locally visible, and compiler-enforced. The checker never needed `using var`. The reader is who it is for.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking"></a>
### 5.28 Silent tracking: trivial-dispose is a ceremony property, not a lifetime regime

Trivial-dispose is a **ceremony exemption** only — no `using var` needed, no must-use warning, `_ := expr;` cosmetic, what [§5.9](#sec-trivial-dispose-classes) records and what every user-facing surface of the feature teaches. It is not a lifetime regime: every trivial-dispose instance keeps a statically-known owner and is freed by compiler-inserted code at that owner's release point ([proposal 0077](proposals/0077-silent-tracking.md); owner-ruled 2026-08-04). The alternative bundles a second, separable exemption with the first. The **tracking exemption** — references to trivial instances could outlive any statically-known bound, with memory reclaimed by an unspecified runtime mechanism ("reference counting, region allocation, or another scheme") — existed only to make abandonment and two specific static-checking carve-outs sound (the spec's old §15.4 fresh-trivial-literal admission and the §22.5 trivial plain-parameter default), and it was the *only* reason any runtime lifetime machinery for user classes needed to exist. The language carried both exemptions until the tracking one was retracted, keeping the ceremony exemption verbatim.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-why-the-mechanism-went"></a>
#### 5.28.1 Why the tracking mechanism went

**Why the mechanism went.** The owner's objection was structural: a full reference-counting implementation — cycle policy, atomic counts at domain crossings, "not a GC in the sense Bezel avoids" hedging — is a hidden per-type runtime purchased for a feature whose entire recorded value is ceremony. The audit trail sharpened the case. The docs were internally split: §9.7 rejected `new Bag([10, 20, 30])` on the ground that the literal "has a lifetime that ends at the construction expression" — a statement only true under static tracking — while the mechanism sentences said the opposite; the ownership chapter's machinery (producers, borrow-escape, store rules, K8 boundaries, capture bounds) was already type-agnostic and tracking-shaped by its letter. And the implementation had never built the mechanism at all: sema classified every class-typed `new` as owned regardless of trivial-dispose and rejected every trivial borrow-escape probe, the runtime contained no count, registry, or arena for user class instances, and the abandonment surface was a patchwork of silent leaks plus one ICE (#2654, #2655, #2656, #2660 — the stage-2 conformance trackers). Retracting the tracking exemption aligned the spec with both its own dominant text and the implementation's architecture, and made the no-GC claim unconditional for every class a user can write.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-what-it-is"></a>
#### 5.28.2 What silent tracking is

**What silent tracking is.** Every trivial-dispose instance keeps the K1 classification it already carried, now load-bearing: exactly one statically-known owner at every point, with the compiler inserting a free — logical disposal (the §28.2 flag, so checked disposal mode backstops every dangle) plus reclamation, running no user code — at the owner's release point. The backstop clause is a mandatory companion, not an option: without it the retraction would have created the language's only un-backstopped dangling class. Cross-domain moves simply transfer the free obligation with the move (spec §30.14) — deleting what would have been the mechanism's hardest corner, cross-domain reclamation. The ceremony exemption survives verbatim as K1.6's rewritten content: the obligation's *user-visible* content is empty, and its discharge is never demanded of the program.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-literal-tree-ownership"></a>
#### 5.28.3 The literal-tree ownership rule

**The literal-tree ownership rule.** The tracking exemption's one real ergonomic pillar was the nested collection literal (`Dictionary<string, Array<int>>` built in one expression). The replacement (spec §15.4) makes the fresh nodes of a literal an aggregate per maximal borrowing-family subtree, owned by whatever owns the subtree's root — receiving binding, K8 temporary, owning slot, `owned` parameter/return/field, or static storage — and freed, all nodes as one obligation, at that owner's release point. Membership is fixed at construction; borrowed elements never join. Two of its recorded choices deserve their reasons here. *Per-aggregate conservative freeze* (the ruled Q3): a borrow of an interior node freezes the whole displaced aggregate at reassignment — cheap, coarse, and deliberately revisable, since per-node tracking would only admit more programs; it also keeps the implementation's sole-owner proofs simple, the sharpest risk the conformance audit named. *Maximal borrowing subtree, not outermost literal*: an owning collection inside the tree takes its fresh element nodes into its own cascade at construction, so no node ever has two owners.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-trivial-dispose-definition"></a>
#### 5.28.4 What the trivial-dispose definition counts

**The definition amendment (the ruled Q2).** Requiring `owned` on fresh-tree fields (the uniform resolution of the fresh-store ambiguity) collided with the definition: an `owned` field flipped its holder non-trivial, so wrapper types — a user `Counter`-shape holding an `Array<int>` — would have lost the ceremony exemption for holding exactly what the free-cascade handles without user code. The definition therefore disregards `owned` fields of trivial-dispose type, recursively and per-instantiation: the synthesized cascade over such fields frees memory and runs no user code, so "disposal provably runs no user code and releases only memory" — the definition's amended prose — stays true in the sense that matters. The costs were ruled on with it: the definition is carried in artifact interfaces (spec §2.8.2), enters the delta classification (§2.8.6 — where verdict-*gained* is preserving, so the amendment's one-way effect is benign), amends §3.6's property-table row, and composes with sendability (§30.14 rule 2 already admits `owned` sendable-class fields, so a type can be simultaneously trivial-dispose and sendable — exactly the composition the moved-obligation clause carries). It also gives composite stdlib types their honest story: `Counter<K>` and the sorted forms hold backing *intrinsically* — a single-allocation layout fact — and the user-expressible spelling of the same shape is now `owned` trivial fields.

**An owning-choice field is owned-bearing ([#5789](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5789)).** A field of move-only value type — an owning choice or an owning inline array — makes its class owned-bearing: §28.4's generated-`Dispose()` list, §28.9's trivial-dispose definition, §28.5's owned-bearing sentence and §22.13's warning name such fields, both kinds, concretely and at a bare-`T` field's move-only instantiations, as §15.3 and §35.4 say the class's destructor disposes them. Naming only `owned` fields and owning-inline-array fields would leave a class whose one obligation is an owning-choice field trivial-dispose by the letter, its compiler free "running no user code" over a payload with a destructor. Forbidding the field instead would strand §22.12 4(g)'s own recommended spelling for a process-global owning choice and nine measured fields. The compiler already disposed such fields in the cascade; its trivial-dispose predicate lacked the arm, which is the tracked delta.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-explicit-disposal"></a>
#### 5.28.5 Explicit disposal and the holder's release point

**Explicit disposal marks; the holder frees ([proposal 0162](proposals/0162-explicit-disposal-marks-the-holder-frees.md)).** Silent tracking's sentence — every class instance is freed by compiler-inserted code at its statically-known holder's release point — is the rule for every class and every disposal path: an explicit call performs logical disposal only, the holder's release point reclaims once, after logical disposal has completed, and the flag records when disposal begins, so a second call after that is a no-op, while checked mode faults from logical disposal's completion: the disposal's own accesses run on a live instance, and an alias's access inside a direct `DisposeAsync()`'s window reaches an intact block and intact owned fields. The alternative — an explicit `Dispose()` that reclaims immediately after logical disposal, with the holder freeing only on the implicit paths (spec §28.4, §28.12 once read so, with one carve for a trivial-dispose field, §28.6) — is unsound in an unchecked build, as the disposal matrix's first run found: the language itself issues a second `Dispose()` on an explicitly disposed instance — the cascade over an early-released field, drop-on-overwrite after a dispose-then-assign, the per-slot pass after a discard, the exit pass — and that call reads a released block ([#5759](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5759)); and a direct `DisposeAsync()` has two reclamation points, the synchronous path's and the task's terminal state ([#5758](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5758)). The cost is a block held past an early release until a point its holder reaches anyway; the remedy for a hot path is a smaller scope or an overwrite, both release points. Declined (proposal 0162 §9): a per-location released bit, making the repeated call undefined unchecked, vacating the slot at the call (the reference compiler's nulling, which puts a second run-time vacated state on non-nullable fields and a null fault where the chapter reads the flag), a joined second `DisposeAsync()` task, and faulting an alias's access inside the in-flight window while exempting the disposal's own (no flag tested at member entry can tell the two apart).

**The overwrite of an explicitly-disposed local is its release point ([#5832](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5832)).** A moved-from, explicitly-disposed or maybe-consumed local is reassignable (the #5768 ruling). A moved-from left-hand side displaces nothing; an explicitly-disposed one displaces a logically disposed value, and the store is its release point — the no-op `Dispose()` on the set flag, then the free — on every path that reached the store disposed, a maybe-consumed binding taking that per path; and the scope end's skip of an explicitly-disposed binding is the disposal's, not the free's. That is how §28.3, §28.2's list of release points and §28.7's liveness test read together, as proposal 0162's composition note intended; reading the explicitly-disposed left-hand side as displacing nothing would leave the logically disposed block with no release point once the explicit call no longer reclaims. No verdict moves and no run-time state is added (the flag is already set); what the text fixes is where the block is reclaimed. Declined: making the explicit call on a local its own release point, reclaiming at the call — it reopens #5759's alias hazard, a surviving borrow reading a freed flag in an unchecked build, which is the hazard the paragraph above closes.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-fresh-store"></a>
#### 5.28.6 The fresh store into a borrowing collection: from lint to error

**The one regression, and the lint (the ruled Q1).** `groups.Add("odds", [1, 3, 5]);` — incremental construction of nested trivial structures — was ownerless-and-safe only under the mechanism; under silent tracking the argument dies at its K8 boundary and the collection holds a flagged dangle. Q1 declined rejection because it seemed it could not be scoped by signature — `coll.Add(new X())` and `sink.Consume(new X())` are indistinguishable plain-parameter calls, and the recorded reason the literal-only asymmetry exists ("an opaque callee's signature type-announces no retention") is type-agnostic — and chose a targeted lint: under the retained no-warning rule the shape would otherwise compile with zero diagnostics while the compiler *knows* the argument dies at the boundary, and a statically-certain dangle emitted silently is the one place the ceremony exemption's silence stops being honest. The lint was the exemption's counterpart, not its erosion — no `using var` demanded, the program accepted. The documented remedy is the owning family (`OwningDictionary<string, Array<int>>`), whose costs are equally recorded: the owning binding is non-trivial (one `using var` per container), and `OwningDictionary` has no default entry iteration. The same lint shape was extended to fresh non-trivial arguments in the same store positions ([proposal 0081](proposals/0081-abandonment-lints.md), #3047) — the `Task` family foremost (#1813's fresh-slot trap) and every other non-trivial-dispose class with it. Q1's ground has since been answered and the store is now an error over the lint's predicate (*The store lint is promoted to an error*, below); this paragraph keeps the reasoning the error inherited.

**The store lint is promoted to an error ([proposal 0207](proposals/0207-fresh-store-into-a-borrowing-collection-is-an-error.md)).** The ruled Q1 above declined rejection because `coll.Add(new X())` and `sink.Consume(new X())` were indistinguishable plain-parameter calls, so a rejection "cannot be scoped by signature", and chose a lint keyed on the standard collections' store members instead; proposal 0081 confirmed the decline. Three things weigh against that reason. The lint it chose *is* scoped — to the compiler-known store members, not to a signature — and ran at that scope from its implementation (#3364) with no false positive on record, its negative controls pinning `sink.Consume(new X())` silent; the error takes its predicate unchanged. §28.11's store rule distinguishes the two calls for a named source — `coll.Add(local)` of a scope-bound local into an outliving collection is rejected, `sink.Consume(local)` admitted — so the fresh temporary, the narrowest-scoped source there is, was the one source the store rule warned about rather than rejected, a severity inverted against lifetime. And §28.11's *Value-copy members* rule (#5693) states in terms that what a standard-library store member stores is read off its declared signature: for these members the retention is announced, which is the condition the literal-only reason named — and which no user-declared member meets, so for those the admission stands as [§5.24](#sec-the-full-expression-boundary) records it. The unchecked conformance mode decided the severity (measured before the decision): `s.Add(new W()); s.Contains(other)` answers `True` for an object the set never held (the freed block reused), `bs.Add(new Box(7)); bs[0].V` prints `7` from a freed block, and a row of the compiler's own suite passed by reading a disposed value's field — a certain dangle whose only sound continuations never touch the slot, which is the dead code §28.6's fresh-store error and §15.4's literal-site rejection already refuse in the two sibling positions, without a flow condition (a read-conditioned rejection was declined: it admits only dead code at the price of the per-slot liveness §28.11 does not track). The error keeps the lint's full type set, the per-family boundary event and the two remedies, and reaches the four borrowing interfaces with the concrete collections (a concrete-only scope was declined as one shape with two verdicts). The migration is the warning's own remedy, one line per site; the measured reach is the proposal's §5. The largest share is the task family — `tasks.Add(Task.FromResult(1))`, `pending.Add(cb())` — which spec §28.14's fan-out sentence, written before calls-mint ([§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends)), had presented as an accumulation; a task stored that way is abandoned at its boundary, which §28.14 itself names an escape, so the sentence is restated over named bindings, and dynamically-sized fan-in keeps the owning spelling the #1813 ruling gave it (stdlib §6.7). The terminal-accessor and mutation-during-iteration warnings are unchanged, and no per-site suppression spelling exists for either.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-abandonment-lints"></a>
#### 5.28.7 The doctrine's second and third instances: the abandonment lints

**The doctrine's second and third instances ([proposal 0081](proposals/0081-abandonment-lints.md), #3047).** The Q1 lint's design shape — a statically-certain hazard, an admission kept for recorded type-agnostic reasons, a normative warning naming the boundary event and the remedies, no new syntax — applies twice more. *The store lint's type-set extension* (the extension above, now part of the error): for a task the identical store was strictly worse than the trivial case that already warned — the discard spelling of the same abandonment (`_ := call();`) is a hard error, and axiom A6 calls a task's loss "a decision, not a disposal" — yet it compiled with zero diagnostics; and a task-only extension was declined because it would have left the middle band (`conns.Add(new Connection("a"))` — non-trivial, non-task) as the *only* fresh store shape with no diagnostic, an inverted asymmetry stranger than the one being fixed. *The terminal-accessor lint* (spec §29.11; stdlib §5): a class-element terminal accessor (`First()` and family) over a pipeline whose enumerator owns the elements — a minting one, or an adopt-then-yield one (spec §29.11, #3285) — returns a borrow of an element the terminal's own internal enumerator already disposed — dead on arrival, Guide 5's trap #4 — and the checker already computes everything the predicate needs (§29.11's provenance trace; §29.2's per-yield-site ownership classification). Its v1 reach is deliberately same-compilation-unit iterators only — zero false positives, with a cross-module minting iterator escaping the lint and keeping the checked-mode backstop; the widening (carrying yield classification in the artifact interface, spec §2.8.2) is a recorded liftable, not taken. No per-site suppression spelling exists for any of these lints, deliberately: the remedies — bind to a named holder; materialize or take the owning path — are one line each, already sanctioned, and convert the program from hazardous to sound, whereas a suppression spelling would be a new way to write the silent dangle the lints exist to remove; project-level lint tuning remains the only knob (spec §28.3's stated posture for the must-use warning).

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-iteration-lints"></a>
#### 5.28.8 The fourth and fifth instances: the iteration lints

**The doctrine's fourth and fifth instances ([proposal 0129](proposals/0129-iteration-mutation-lints.md)).** Two collection shapes the guides taught as traps became diagnostics on the same terms. *The mutation-during-iteration warning* (spec §29.12): a structural call whose receiver is the very binding the enclosing `for` iterates, from which the loop's next advance is reachable, is certain to raise `InvalidOperationException` at that advance — the runtime rule is unchanged and the warning names it early; the predicate is *advance reachable from the call*, not dominance, because the common trap is the conditional `if p(x) then xs.Remove(x); end;` (certain on the branch that runs it) while the sanctioned `xs.Remove(x); break;` never advances and must stay silent. A modification through a borrow copy, a parameter, or a call is not traced — the same-binding predicate is local and exact, and following aliases would be the borrow-liveness checking §5.1 declines. The predicate is stated over the loop's own callable body: a structural call inside a lambda the loop only defines is not a call the loop advances after, and is outside the warning by construction rather than by exemption. *The element-borrow use-after-removal warning* (spec §28.11; retired, below) named the second shape: an owning collection's `Remove`/`RemoveAt`/`Clear` dispose what they remove, and a borrow taken by the indexer, by default iteration, or by a §29.11-traced accessor and used after a *dominating* such call on the same binding is §28.2's dominating-kill shape with the kill on the collection — K4.2 + K7.4 with the source from K2.1, "free precision" in [§5.1](#sec-owned-not-borrows)'s sense: the per-path state exists, the borrow's source is already resolved, and only the straight-line, same-index subset with no structural operation between the borrow and the kill was claimed; a conditional kill stayed with the checked-mode backstop. An indexer-set at the same constant index (a certain dangle) and a keyed `OwningDictionary` removal after a same-key borrow (the same shape over key equality) were recorded liftable and left unnamed, so the lints and §29.12 shared one operation vocabulary. Neither warning has a per-site suppression spelling.

**The fifth instance is retired ([proposal 0181](proposals/0181-retire-the-use-after-removal-warning.md)).** The element-borrow use-after-removal warning changed no verdict — a program drawing it was accepted — and was believed never implemented when retired — the compiler had in fact emitted it (fix [#4603](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/4603)), and its removal from the compiler is decided under [#6573](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6573), the retirement's other grounds unchanged; its three kill legs were keyed on one collection binding, one index spelling and one straight-line window, with the indexer-set and the keyed removal recorded as chosen silences beside them, so the roughly 500 specification words that defined it described the one shape of a dangle the checked-mode backstop already faults deterministically, and nothing else. The hazard it named stays where its soundness always was: §28.11 states that an element borrow is valid until its slot is mutated and is not statically tracked per slot, §28.2's backstop faults the use, and the remedies (re-take the borrow, take the element out first, mutate after the last use) stay in the code-generation reference. A lint over the same shape is tooling's to offer. The fourth instance, the mutation-during-iteration warning (§29.12), is outside this retirement and unchanged. No per-site suppression spelling exists for any remaining lint of the doctrine.

**The fourth instance stays ([#5936](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5936)).** The criterion that retired the fifth instance was put to the mutation-during-iteration warning and does not carry. The warning is implemented (the compiler emits it, and the rule corpus banks the programs that draw it as passing); its predicate is local and exact — the same binding, the loop's own callable body, the next advance reachable — with no alias tracking; and the shape it names is the common trap the guides teach, the conditional `Remove` inside a `for`, where the fifth instance named a rare shape more narrowly than the hazard. The run-time fault stands behind it in every build mode, so the warning decides nothing; what it buys is the diagnosis before the first run — and independent of the data: the fault fires only on a run in which the guarding condition holds, which a test input may never reach, while the warning reports the shape whatever the inputs — for about three hundred specification words. Its retirement is declined, with the reopening bar recorded in `DECLINED.md`.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-alternatives"></a>
#### 5.28.9 Alternatives considered and declined

1. **Status quo plus the layered runtime** (escape analysis → arenas → domain-local RC with elision → atomic promotion at domain crossings; cycles leak with tooling). Workable and previously sketched — but every layer past the first two would have to be built from nothing (the implementation audit found zero), the cycle policy and the "not a GC" hedging never go away, and the owner's objection stands: a hidden per-type runtime purchased for a feature whose value is ceremony, which the retraction delivers without any of it.
2. **Restricting trivial-dispose to provably acyclic types** (so a simple mechanism without cycle detection would suffice). Declined for non-locality: acyclicity is a property of the global type-reference graph — "can anything I reach, reach me" — not of a type's own declaration. Enforcing it is either non-modular (a private field addition in one module flips classifications in another) or brutally conservative (every `Object`/interface field assumed cyclic, reclassifying the tier's most valuable inhabitants). It would also break §3.6's stated basis that a derived property is computed from the type's *structure* — its declaration and, for a generic, its type arguments: a global-graph property is computed from *other* declarations. (The Q2 amendment does not share this defect, though a fast reading might suggest it: the distinction is direction and boundedness. The trivial-dispose check follows *outgoing* owned-field edges — dependencies the declaration itself names, already carried in artifact interfaces (§2.8.2) and already traversed the same way by sendability's rule 2 — while acyclicity needs the *incoming* edge set, "who can reach me," which no artifact carries and no declaration bounds.)
3. **Uniform loud ceremony** (all classes tracked *and* warned). Declined on [§5.9](#sec-trivial-dispose-classes)'s ground: the warning has no content, which is why it was first softened and then dropped. Silent tracking is precisely this option minus the noise.
4. **A declared opt-out marker** (or the empty-destructor idiom) letting performance-sensitive classes exit the mechanism. Solves nothing structural — the mechanism must still exist for everyone else — and adds an axis. Subsumed: under silent tracking there is nothing to opt out of.
5. **Uniform silence** (the mirror of alternative 3, which the owner posed directly: if trivial-dispose only drops ceremony, why not drop the ceremony for destructor-bearing classes too — Rust-style implicit drop for everything?). Declined, and the retraction is what makes the reason precise. Nothing about ceremony is load-bearing for correctness — every class is tracked, auto-drop disposes correctly on every path, and the compiler could go silent for `Connection` tomorrow without a leak. What ceremony *is*, now that it is provably not a tracking tax, is the announcement of observable disposal effects: a trivial-dispose class sheds it because its disposal has no observable content, while a destructor-bearing class's disposal is an *effect* — a file closes, a lock releases, a subscription unhooks — and *when* it fires is part of the program's meaning. `using var` is the source-visible answer to "where does this effect fire"; the must-use warning is "you created an effect-bearing object and let its effect fire silently — did you mean that?" The ceremony criterion — disposal provably runs no user code — is therefore already maximal: extending silence past it means announcing effects nowhere. The line was never complexity: an arbitrarily large class whose disposal provably runs no user code — no destructor anywhere in its hierarchy, no owning-inline-array fields, its `owned` fields all of trivial-dispose type — is already ceremony-free, while a one-field class with a destructor keeps its announcement — and a wrapper whose only cleanup is cascading into an `owned Connection` keeps it too, because the connection's destructor runs *at the wrapper's drop point*. The external evidence points the same way: the silent-RAII languages retrofitted selective ceremony back in — Rust's `#[must_use]` and the clippy lints applied type-by-type exactly where a drop is suspicious, plus the known guard footguns where an invisible drop point releases a lock earlier than the author pictured (`let _ = guard`). Bezel's position is that arrangement with the annotation *derived from structure* rather than hand-applied: must-use on by default where disposal has content, off by rule where it provably has none. The per-site escape hatches already exist and cost a single spelling each — plain `var` where ownership moves on, `_ := x;` for "release now," explicit `.Dispose()` for early release — and each is deliberately an announcement itself.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking-historical-note"></a>
#### 5.28.10 Historical note

v0.61 committed to ceremony relief only — fully compatible. v0.66 did commit to the mechanism, but as the honesty device that let "tighten the discard" avoid "embrace runtime-managed class instances — it's GC-shaped"; silent tracking *completes* that direction rather than reversing it, removing the last mechanism entirely. v0.184's decline of "backing-storage release as a real obligation — ceremony on every dropped `ToArray()` result" is not un-declined: ceremony stays waived, only the free point becomes static. v0.202's pure-throw carve-out survives with its justification re-derived (the dispose-after-arm is now the flag-setting free), and its recorded future widening — trivial-dispose construction in pure bodies — remains viable, a compiler-freed instance still being effect-free.

<a id="sec-destructor-body-not-on-ctor-unwind"></a>
### 5.29 The destructor body does not run on constructor unwind

On a construction unwind the throwing class's own destructor body never runs; the already-completed owned fields and the completed base subobject, whose destructor body does run, are disposed (spec §22.9; spec defect [#3245](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3245), ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation, per the review program's C10 ruling). Found independently by both owner-commissioned RAII comparative reviews (2026-08-18) as the top spec-clarity defect: spec §22.9's lead cited "the §28.4 pass" for constructor unwinding — and §28.4's pass's *step 1 is the destructor body* — while every §22.9 bullet enumerated only owned-field disposal plus the base chain. The two readings diverge observably (does a half-constructed `FileStream`'s destructor run `NativeClose` on a mid-construction throw?), the implementation had long taken one of them (owned fields + base chain; no derived destructor body — `src/codegen/LlvmBackend_Setup.cpp`, `LlvmBackend_Disposal.cpp`), and no text or test pinned it.

**Why the C++-matching reading is right, not merely implemented.** §7.9's never-observed-unset guarantee for non-nullable fields is issued only at constructor completion; a destructor body running mid-unwind would be exactly the forbidden observer — free to read fields the failed construction never assigned. The alternative (run the body, tolerate unset reads) would need either a null-watermark regime for destructor bodies — a second §22.7-style observation gate, purchased for an edge whose C++ precedent says "don't" — or per-field initialized-tracking at runtime, machinery the model deliberately lacks. The base chain is the principled boundary: the base subobject *completed* construction, so its `Dispose()` — destructor body included — runs soundly; completion is the event that licenses the body, on both sides of the line.

**The example consequence, and the discipline now taught.** §28.4's flagship `FileStream` acquires a raw `_handle: IntPtr` in its constructor body and releases it in its destructor — a shape that leaks the handle if any statement after the acquisition throws, since no owned field holds it and the destructor body will not run. The example itself was verified to conform (the acquisition is the constructor's final statement; the only later action is a `using var` disposal, a destructor §28.4's own rule expects not to throw), and the previously-unstated discipline that makes it conform is now normative teaching at §28.4: one raw resource per owning wrapper, acquired as the constructor's final act; anything richer composes wrappers as `owned` fields, which §22.9's unwinding covers on every edge — whereupon the composite needs no destructor at all (§28.5). The example was deliberately *not* rewritten into a wrapper-plus-factory shape: the single-class form *is* the wrapper the discipline prescribes, and stating why it is safe teaches more than restructuring it. The compiler-side regression test pinning the negative (derived destructor body does not run; base's does) rides #3245, which stays open as the tracker. This record sits here, beside the ownership chapter's disposal records, because no rationale section owned §22.8/§22.9's constructor history — §5.10's #3240 record (which named the partially-constructed hazard as "§22.9's territory") is the nearest neighbor, and this ruling completes exactly that territory.

<a id="sec-checked-disposal-default"></a>
### 5.30 Checked disposal is the default build

Spec §28.2 defines two conformance modes and says which is the language: "unchecked mode is the release-build relaxation of the checked semantics, not a different language." Every recorded trust admission in §28 names checked mode's `DisposedAccessException` as its backstop — kernel K4.1 calls it "the runtime backstop every trust admission names" — and §28.14, §29.11, §15.4 and §28.11 each say the checked backstop "remains the guard" where their static rules are silent. Checked mode is the default build and unchecked mode the explicitly selected relaxation (proposal 0128). The choice of default moves nothing else: the two-mode structure, the equivalence claim for programs that never touch a disposed instance, the cost statement ("unchecked builds pay no cost for the mode's existence"), and K4.1's posture hold whichever build is the default.

**The cost of the default, stated.** A checked build pays the flag test on member entry and retains, per disposed instance, the state that makes the fault deterministic: the disposed flag must stay readable through every alias for as long as any alias might be used, and without a liveness oracle that is the process's life. Spec §28.2's latitude ("quarantine, delayed free") permits deferring reclamation; it cannot permit reclaiming the flag while an alias could still reach it, because K4.1's "faults deterministically" would become "faults within a window" — a windowed quarantine that eventually reuses the block is the unchecked relaxation made probabilistic, and was declined. The reference compiler today retains the *whole* disposed block (its runtime calls this a deliberate leak; `--unchecked-disposal` frees it and elides the test), so under the default a program that disposes continuously grows for its lifetime. That is a known, disclosed cost of the default, not an accepted end state: reducing the retention to the flag's footprint — releasing the payload at disposal and never handing a disposed instance's address to a live one — keeps the fault deterministic and is recorded as a precondition of the reference compiler exercising the default (the tracking issue), a compiler-lane obligation and not a spec matter. Unchecked mode remains the release form for a program that cannot carry the retention at all.

Two things were decided with it.
