The contracts mode keeps the opposite default (spec §22.16): `ensures`/`invariant` non-evaluation is unobservable by construction, so the cheap build is the honest default there; a disposal relaxation turns a defined fault into undefined behavior, so the checked build is the honest default here. And the reference compiler's library API keeps its zero-value — `CodegenOptions.checkedDisposal` stays `false` while `bezelc`'s default flips — because the test suite's memory oracles need real frees: checked mode quarantines disposed blocks (spec §28.2's latitude), and a block never freed is one AddressSanitizer cannot see a use-after-free on, while the curated LeakSanitizer sites would report every quarantined block as a leak. The conformance default is the compiler's user-facing one; an embedder chooses explicitly. Alternatives declined: keeping unchecked as the default (the soundness story would describe a build the user did not get); deleting the relaxation (the cost statement is load-bearing); a compile-time warning on the unchecked switch (a deliberate build choice is not a hazard in a program — §5.28's doctrine lints programs).

<a id="sec-trust-admission-report"></a>
### 5.31 The trust-admission report: the K4.3 register is enumerable, and reportable on request

K4's third clause makes every admission the checker cannot compare statically or backstop by flag carry *"an individually recorded trust clause"*. The clauses are recorded in the text — field-held, collection-held, comparer-held ([proposal 0138](proposals/0138-compiler-known-sendability.md) §7) and frame-held borrows, plus §28.14's two residuals — and at every one of them the compiler already distinguishes the admitted, trust-bearing outcome from the rejected one. Spec §28.3's trust-admission report mode (proposal 0131) says so on request: a per-build mode — off by default, selected explicitly for a build — under which each such admission draws a warning naming the clause, the obligation and the static remedies — `scoped`, a `SlotHandle` key, `Shared<T>`, the owning family, awaiting to settlement — and under which a silent compilation unit provably carries no recorded trust clause.

Three placements were decided. **A warning, not a third diagnostic class**: Appendix D classifies outcomes as accept / warn / reject and nothing else, and a report that was neither would be invisible to every conformance instrument the project has. **In the specification, not only the toolchain**: the zero-report claim is a statement about the spec's own register, meaningful only if every conforming implementation reports the same set — and the K4 sentence that the set "is enumerable, and the report mode enumerates it" is a cheap ratchet on the clause's existing "no new admission without recording its clause". **Not always-on**: §5.28's doctrine reserves default-build warnings for statically-certain hazards, and a trust admission is not a hazard but the taught spelling of the back-reference (Guide 01 §5); a default-build warning on every `_owner := owner;` would erode the default-build lints' signal (§5.28). The mode is an audit: off by default, selected explicitly. Excluded from the report, each with its reason in §28.3: the site-announced kills (K4.2 — announced and flag-backstopped, a different posture), §22.12's statics obligations (already declaration-site warnings), §32.10's documented trust (keyword-visible at every `extern` call), and axiom A10's certification grants (an allowlist, not a recorded clause).

**The transfer entry ([#5713](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5713); proposal [0157](proposals/0157-report-a-transfer-of-a-collection-held-source.md)).** A store into a method-local collection of an element sourced in the body is admitted by the scope comparison, which bounds the element against its source's scope end and nothing more. A transfer of that source while the collection still holds the element is admitted too, because a collection is none of the freeze's traced holders (proposal 0153 declined making it one), and that transfer is where spec §28.11's collection-held clause, with its domain obligation, comes into force. So the register names the transfer, not the store. Reporting every such store was declined: most never meet a transfer, so the report would list every local collection a program builds, and an audit whose lines mostly name no obligation stops being read. Narrowing the zero-report claim to except the shape was declined because the claim is what the mode is for. The kills that set the flag — the site-announced kills and a reassignment's drop-on-overwrite — stay outside the register: a later read through the element meets the flag (K4.1), and a disposed object crosses no domain. The entry does not ask whether the collection still holds the element at the transfer: retention is not body-local, since a callee handed the collection as a plain argument may `Remove` or `Clear` it, and a line under a collection that no longer holds the element is an over-report the audit can dismiss.

**The slot-displaced transfer ([proposal 0210](proposals/0210-report-a-transfer-of-a-slot-displaced-value.md); [#7434](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/7434)).** Spec §28.3.9 calls the slot kill by move — a replacing `move` of an owned field while a borrow of the value it takes survives — a recorded obligation, and the report's other entries name it only where a field, a collection or a frame holds that borrow, at the holder's store. A local borrow has no store, so without this entry `var b := a.f; var c := move a.f with new Q(); Domain.Spawn(W, move c); b.M();` would draw no line: a cross-domain read that no flag catches, and one the executable kernel model reaches (6 of 169,632 accepted programs at five statements). The register therefore names the transfer of the taken value, on proposal 0157's ground: the kill alone meets no obligation, and a line at every replacing `move` under a live borrow would list every swap. The borrow is the local spec §28.7's *Use after a dominating field write* already identifies, one read directly from the moved path, and the entry adds a condition 0157's does not — the local is used after the transfer — because a local's later uses are in the body where a collection's retention is not. Freezing the transfer was not adopted: the model's nearest freeze rejects 552 programs at five statements, 510 of which never fault, because a K5 source set names the holder and not the slot's value; the sharp rule waits on #7434. For the same reason the entry does not reach another local holder of the displaced value (an alias, a closure, a borrow-bearing value) or a borrow read through a member, and no body sees a callee's `move`. That residual is stated in §28.3.9 and among the zero-report claim's exclusions rather than left for a reader to find: proposal 0157's decline of a narrowed claim was of a hole one body could decide, and this one no body can.

<a id="sec-borrow-bearing-classes"></a>
### 5.32 Borrow-bearing classes: an object that stores a borrow carries its sources

Three kinds of value that carry a borrow have always been read with a source set: a record that holds one, a function value, a frame. A class instance with a plain class-typed field is the fourth, and it is left on trust (spec K4.3's field-held clause): its stored borrow is the programmer's promise, backstopped by the disposed flag, and not backstopped at all against a transfer to another domain. [Proposal 0212](proposals/0212-borrow-bearing-classes.md) reads the field as what it is, a declaration that the class holds a borrow, and gives the instance the set (spec §28.15).

**Why a ramp, and why the report first.** The rules change what compiles, and the evidence that prices them is test programs: 5 accepted-tier corpus programs are rewritten and 12 take a `scoped` marker, a floor rather than a measurement, since long-lived registries and async helpers are under-represented in tests. So the design lands in three revisions. The first, v0.357, states the property and the rules, and makes them the classification spec §28.3's trust-admission report prints: *bounded*, *needs `scoped`*, *no static form*, and one line at each other site the rules reject. No verdict changes. The second makes each of those sites a default-build warning and widens the class-typed `scoped` parameter, so that every warning has a rewrite that already compiles. The third makes them errors and retires K4.3's field-held, collection-held and comparer-held clauses: a Ratchet in the spec-change process's sense, whose design is ruled now. Each later stage waits on its gate — the compiler implementing the stage before and its prerequisites, and the report read over real programs (proposal 0212 §7). The stage-2 warning is not the always-on trust report that §5.31 declines: it fires only where a scheduled rejection will land.

**The mint reads the signature.** A constructor call carries every argument, stored or not. The alternative — a marker on each stored parameter — is more precise and was measured: it would add a `scoped` to 53 signatures in the accepted corpus where the signature reading adds 12, to buy one construction site in 312. The signature reading is K6.2's, the rule a frame already uses.

**The callee side.** A plain parameter is call-bounded for an object borrow as it is for a callback. v0.329 declined this rule on the ground that it "retires the back-reference idiom" (§4.2.6). It does not, once the constructor's store has a carrier: the idiom's named form is a constructor, and 93 of the 157 measured stores are one. What changes is a member that is not a constructor, which says `scoped`.

**No opt-out, provisionally.** A per-field marker that keeps the old admission is declined: it would keep the clause, and the domain race with it, under another name. The decline is provisional and is ruled again at the third stage's gate, on the count the first stage's report produces of stores with no static form in programs that are not tests (`DECLINED.md`); stages one and two are the same either way.

**Erasure only at owning positions.** A borrow of an instance may be passed at `Object` or an interface, because what it contributes is read from the argument at the caller. An *owned* instance may not be converted to a type that announces nothing: Rust's `Box<dyn Trait>` defaulting to `'static`, Swift's `~Escapable` opt-in and C# 13's ban on converting a `ref struct` to an interface draw the same line. In a generic body the conversion is a requirement read per instantiation, the mechanism of §9.7's bullets.

**The owning positions are the consume table's.** A set can be lost only where an owned value changes holder, and spec K3 already enumerates those places. The proposal's first draft listed them by hand and missed four — an `out owned` recipient, an owning choice's payload, an `owned` argument to a static callee, a replacing `move` written outside a member — which an adversarial critic found with running programs. Reading the table row by row is what replaced the list.

**A sendable type is never a carrier.** The collections that retain a comparer are sendable at value instantiations, so at those the comparer must be static-sourced; checking each value at a domain transfer instead was declined, because it would let a sendable type's values carry sources and stop `where T: sendable` from meaning what the erasure rule needs (`DECLINED.md`). The price is stated with the rule: at such an instantiation a comparer cannot be forwarded through a constructor, and the one admitted spelling, a `static readonly` instance, draws spec §22.12's every-domain warning. The rewrite is a key record that declares its own equality, the form `SortedDictionary` already requires for an ordering.

**What was not done.** Lifetime parameters (the precision ceiling; nine measured sites over this design, at the price of a second parameter system), a freeze of explicit `Dispose()` under a live instance (§5.1's recorded boundary stands), a ban on destructors reading a stored borrow (it retires flush-on-dispose and is not complete), and the retirement of the frame-held clause, which remains recorded trust. Two refinements wait for the first stage's data: a declared-reach reading of the taken-out rule, sound only where every class involved is `sealed`, ruled at the second stage's gate; and a weak reference over an ordinary owned object for registries, a separate proposal.

<a id="sec-nullability"></a>
## 6. Nullability

<a id="sec-field-narrowing-is-not-safe-across-threads"></a>
### 6.1 Field narrowing is not safe across statements

`if user <> null then user.Method() end;` works for locals because the compiler knows nothing else can reassign them. For fields, the same code is a compile error — not primarily because of threads (a domain's continuations are serial, [§12.5](#sec-execution-domains)), but because the field can be reassigned between the check and the use by paths the compiler cannot see: a method call whose body writes the field (directly or through an alias to the same object), a closure invoked in between, re-entrant code reached from the check's own expression, or — across an `await` — any other continuation of the same domain interleaving at the suspension point. Foreign threads introduced through the FFI remain a residual case ([§16](#sec-ffi), spec §30.12). An uncaptured local has none of these writers, so narrowing it is sound; a local mutably captured by a lambda has exactly one — the closure — which is why spec §7.4 invalidates a captured local's narrowing at any call, function-value invocation, or `await` inside the controlled block.

A `ref` parameter ([#5495](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5495)) has every writer the field has, through the alias: the caller may pass a closure that nulls the referent beside the `ref` argument, name the same storage at a second `ref` argument, or pass the holder of a field it also passed as `ref`. The last two are plain stores inside the callee itself, so no invalidating form could be added short of invalidating at every store to anything that might alias, which is the mutable-field situation under another name. Spec §7.4 therefore never narrows a `ref` parameter and gives it the field remedy, the copy. Two alternatives were declined: invalidating a `ref` parameter's narrowing at every user-code transfer and every store that can reach heap storage or another `ref`/`out` parameter, which leaves narrowing only in a block with no call and no non-local store and must be re-derived with every new store form; and rejecting overlapping caller storage at two `ref`/`out` arguments of one call, which still needs the first alternative for the closure route and cannot see `G(ref h.F, h)`, one location under two spellings. Neither could be conditional on the caller's argument, because a body is checked once against its signature, which does not carry the argument's category. An `out` parameter is a callee-local slot until copy-out (spec §22.3, whose hidden-reference licence covers only storage that is the callee's for the call), which no caller alias names, so it narrows like a local; that is also what discharges spec §22.4's `when true` postcondition inside the declaring body, and treating `out` like `ref` for uniformity was declined for that reason.

The compiler does not narrow mutable fields. The one field kind with *no* writers outside construction — `readonly` (single-assignment, §22.8; no `ref`/`out` escape, §22.3) — narrows, with the single-segment path and construction-window rules spec §7.4 states; the copy-to-local pattern remains for everything mutable:

```bezel
var u := _user;
if u <> null then u.DoStuff(); end;
```

Some languages (TypeScript) narrow property accesses and pay for it with subtle time-of-check/time-of-use bugs; Kotlin refuses to smart-cast mutable properties for exactly this reason. Bezel declines to narrow mutable fields and properties entirely.

([#6707](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6707) with [#6673](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6673); proposal [0198](proposals/0198-try-narrowing-every-statement-may-throw.md)) **Narrowing across `try` models neither throw points nor arm selection** (spec [§7.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-member-access-on-nullable-values) *Narrowing across `try`*, [§31.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-try-catch-finally)). Read as the run-time path set, §7.4's every-path clause admitted `try s := null; s := "y"; catch … end; s.Length` — no statement between the two stores can raise — and a `finally` reading a fact the block established. The compiler does not model either: the `readonly`-field set treats every statement of a protected block as a possible throw point for every arm (#2116), and the #6673 fix extended that to locals because the cruder entry-∩-after-body intersection was blind to a store of `null` followed by a re-establishing store (a probe-confirmed null dereference). Stating the finer model would make the raise classification of every expression form — calls, `!`, `as`, indexing, division, checked arithmetic, conversions, interpolation, `await`, getters, user operators and every compiler-provided operation that invokes user members — part of what a reader must know to predict a narrowing, and every future construct would have to join the list, for the shape `s := null; s := "y";`, which a program writes as one store; arm selection by exception type reaches only written `throw`s, since a call's raise set is undeclared ([§31.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-throw-operand)). An arm and the `finally` therefore see the entry facts less everything the block invalidates anywhere, and nothing it establishes; the point after the `try` is the every-path meet of the block's normal completion and every arm's exit, through the `finally`. Zero in-tree programs changed verdict; the remedy is a re-test at the use. Declined (proposal 0198 §7): the straight-line store refinement (option B) and arm selection by exception type (option C).

<a id="sec-is-postfix-only"></a>
### 6.2 `!` is postfix only

`name!.Length` reads left-to-right; prefix `!name.Length` would have to be parenthesized. There is also a semantic argument: `!` asserts something about the value to its left, and putting the assertion to the left of the asserted value reads backwards.

There is no prefix `!` for boolean negation either; `not` is the boolean operator.

<a id="sec-interpolating-a-nullable-renders-null"></a>
### 6.3 Interpolating a nullable renders `null`

`$"{x}"` over a `T?` is the most common diagnostic line in the language; requiring narrowing or `??` there (the §4.6 strict-ordering template) would tax every log statement for zero safety — rendering is total and loses nothing. Between C#'s empty string and a visible token, the visible token wins: an empty hole *hides* the null that a log line exists to reveal. The format spec is skipped on null rather than dispatched (there is no value to format) but stays compile-checked against `T`, so a bad specifier target is caught regardless of the value's presence.

<a id="sec-operators-do-not-lift"></a>
### 6.4 Operators do not lift over nullable operands

Spec §7.11 rejects a nullable operand at the built-in arithmetic, bitwise, shift and relational operators, with the compound-assignment (§6.6) and `++`/`--` (§17.6) forms and the relational and range patterns (§19.3, §16.6) rejected with them; equality (§8.4) and the marked propagating forms (`??`, `?.`, `?(args)`, `??=`) are retained. The rejected design is C#'s lifting, which Bezel had before this rule: arithmetic, bitwise and shift yielded `T?` (null if either operand was null), and the relational operators yielded a non-nullable `bool` — `false` if either operand was null. The whole ruling turns on one observation: every construct in Bezel that propagates absence carries `?` in its own spelling — `?.`, `?(`, `??`, `??=` — and lifted `+` and lifted `<` were the only two that did not. What is enforced is therefore not that propagation is wrong; it is that *unmarked* propagation is, and rejecting the two unmarked cases makes the language's own convention exceptionless.

**What follows.** No operator produces a value from an absent operand, and none consumes one silently: an absent value is confronted at the operand, in the source, once — the discipline spec §7.4 applies to member access, indexing and calls, with no exception to it. A nullable value therefore reaches a non-nullable position only through a narrowing check, `??`, or `!`, and the operations that do answer over an absent operand — equality (§8.4), the null and type tests (§12.7, §19.5), and interpolation (§18.5) — answer questions that have answers.

The relational arm is the one that had no defence at all. §7.11's *Safety* paragraph claimed that "lifting never silently produces a non-nullable value from nullable operands" while its own *Relational* paragraph, twenty lines above, produced exactly that; §18.5 held that in ordering "a null participant has no defensible result", and §4.6 acted on it for `string?`, while §7.11 asserted for value types that the defensible result is `false`. That collapse is not a smaller three-valued logic — it is a two-valued answer to a question with none, and it fails in both directions at once: on an absent `x`, `x < y`, `x = y` and `x > y` are all `false`, and `not (x < y)` is a definite `true`, so `requires(not (retries > Max))` passes on an absent `retries` while the positive spelling of the same contract faults. `while spent < budget do … end;` never executes when `spent` is absent, and a comparator built from `<` breaks the total order stdlib §4.4 requires, whose stated consequence is an unspecified permutation. This is worse than SQL, the usual cautionary tale for propagation: SQL's three-valued logic keeps `NOT (x < 5)` *unknown* and drops the row, which is precisely the protection Bezel lacked, having taken C#'s two-valued collapse without it.

The arithmetic arm follows from the same principle and from an asymmetry the language was already carrying: §7.11 rejected lifting for user-declared operators and named `!` and `??` as the remedies, so `total += price` silently poisoned an accumulator for `decimal?` and was a compile error for `Money?` — the same program shape, decided by whether the author reached for a primitive or a record, recorded nowhere. The external evidence points the same way and is unusually clean, because it turns on mechanism rather than heritage: the languages that enforce nullability by flow analysis reject lifted arithmetic and lifted ordering — TypeScript, which retrofitted its null-safety, rejects them too while permitting `==` — and the ones that lift represent absence as a runtime wrapper. C# lifted in 2005 because it had `Nullable<T>` and no flow analysis; Bezel has C#'s representation and none of C#'s excuse. Two data points carry most of the weight. Swift shipped lifted ordering and removed it in a breaking release (SE-0121, Swift 3), having concluded it was too easy to write bugs with — and Swift's semantics were strictly better than Bezel's, `.none < .some` being a genuine total order where Bezel collapsed to `false`, so the evidence applies a fortiori; SE-0121 names implicit `T → T?` promotion as the accelerant, which Bezel has (§5.1) — and which the lifted design's mixed-operand rule blessed at the operator. And C#'s designers declined to repeat lifting for C# 8's nullable reference types (csharplang #1865): it "doesn't really make good use of null tracking; just propagates nulls", and would "flag the problem 'at the end' rather than on the first undesired null" — the accumulator complaint, stated by the designers of the language Bezel inherited lifting from, as a reason *not* to lift.

**The compound-assignment ruling it superseded (#2158).** An earlier ruling settled how `x op= e` types when the target is a nullable value type — on the underlying types, with §7.11's present-branch semantics for the operation and the write-back alike — and so made `b += 100` on a `byte?` well-typed, raising `OverflowException` when `b` was present and assigning `null` when it was not. It is not reversed on its own terms: it answered *how the compound form types when the operator lifts*, and the no-lifting rule removes the premise. What it established survives — §6.6's nullable paragraph still rejects a nullable right operand against a non-nullable target and still rejects a nullable reference-typed target, now as one rule with the nullable-target case rather than as boundaries drawn around an admission. The asymmetry it left behind is the one this entry would otherwise have to defend: that write-back was loud for the loss of *range* and silent for the loss of the whole accumulated value. Migration cost was zero outside the compiler and its tests — no example in the specification, the standard library, or the six guides performed a lifted operation, and no conformance case did; what remained was compiler code, its diagnostic families, and a codegen path that is a net deletion.

**The narrowed target (#3686).** The rejection reads the operand's *static* type, and §7.11 has always said what that means — the type after §7.4's flow narrowing, so a narrowed `n: int?` is a non-nullable operand of `n + 1`. The compound and `++`/`--` forms had no sentence of their own, and an implementation read them off the *declared* type — accepting `n := n + 1` and rejecting `n += 1` for the same narrowed `n`. §7.11 decides it: the compound forms inherit the base operator's rule ("rejected with them"), which is narrowing-keyed, so the declared-type reading would have made the compound forms the one place in the language where narrowing is invisible — for no semantic gain. (§6.6's narrow-integer write-back is the one deliberate place where `b += 10` and `b := b + 10` differ, and it is a typing rule, not a nullability one.) Keeping the compound forms declared-type-keyed was declined for the reason the compound-assignment-only rejection below was.

**Declined.** A **runtime null fault** at the operator: it would need no new exception type (`NullReferenceException` fits §31.4's admission principle), and is declined for coherence rather than cost — a faulting operator can never yield `null`, so its result type is `T` and the design is this one with the diagnosis moved from compile time to production, against Appendix C.1's "a nullable value may not be dereferenced without a null check or `!`", against §7.8's explicit-postfix-`!` design (it is an unspelled `a! + b!`), and against the pattern §5.4 and §6.5 both follow of taking static rejection wherever it is available and faulting only on the residual. **Rejecting only the compound-assignment forms**, the narrow option: it carves §6.6's desugaring equivalence for no semantic gain — the posture recorded at §11.6 — and its premise fails independently, since `total := total + p;` reproduces the accumulator verbatim and that option leaves it legal. **Marked lifted operators in the F# shape** (`?+?`, `?<?` — keep the semantics, spell the propagation): a real design that satisfies this entry's own marking principle, declined for surface cost — two operator families and their compound forms to serve a case better written with `??` or a narrowing check. *Reopening bar:* evidence from real Bezel programs that the `??`-and-narrow spelling is materially worse, not merely longer; it is the one recorded re-entry shape for operator-level propagation. **Retaining relational and range patterns over a nullable scrutinee** under a "never matches null" reading: declined because it would reproduce in pattern position the split verdict this rule removes, and because §16.6's claim that range patterns "agree exactly with `in`" is false while `in` requires a non-nullable left operand (§17.5) and the pattern silently declines to match. Constant patterns over a nullable scrutinee are unaffected, and the difference is the ruling's own line: equality over an absent value has an answer, ordering does not. **Lifting of user-defined operators**, which §7.11 had deferred: closed as a decline rather than carried, since with no built-in operator lifting a user-declared one has nothing to be consistent with.

**The null-conditional call `f?(args)` is kept (proposal 0136 §1.3).** It is the function-typed member of the marked propagating family this entry's principle rests on — `?.`, `?(args)`, `??`, `??=` — and it spells its propagation in its own token, as the others do; its evaluation is normative (spec §7.6, #3288). Its value form has no shorter replacement: `(if f <> null then f(x) else null)` names `f` twice and states the absent case by hand. Removing it is declined. *Reopening bar:* evidence from real Bezel programs that the form misleads, not that the `if` spelling exists.


<a id="sec-equality-and-hashing"></a>
## 7. Equality and hashing

<a id="sec-=-invokes-equals"></a>
### 7.1 `=` invokes `Equals`

A perennial C# bug is reference-equality on a class that should have had value equality. Bezel chooses the other way: `=` invokes `Equals`. For records, `Equals` is value equality by default. For classes, `Equals` is reference equality by default but overrideable.

The common case does the right thing. For genuine reference comparison, `Object.ReferenceEquals(a, b)` is available.

<a id="sec-=-cannot-be-overloaded-on-classes"></a>
### 7.2 `=` cannot be overloaded on classes

Operator overloading of `=` on classes invites bugs: the override defines value equality, but the rest of the language treats classes as reference types in many places (collections that hash on identity, `is` checks). Classes customize equality through `Equals`; records and enums may overload `=` and `<>` freely.

<a id="sec-overriding-equals-requires-overriding-gethashcode"></a>
### 7.3 Overriding `Equals` requires overriding `GetHashCode`

The "if you override one, override both" invariant is a source of bugs in other languages because it's a documentation rule, not a language rule. Bezel enforces it at compile time. The cost is a `GetHashCode` on types that may never be hashed; the benefit is that no Bezel type can produce silent dictionary-lookup bugs years later when someone refactors.

([#5993](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5993)) **The obligation is the slot's.** The invariant protects the pair a hash container consults, which is the `Object` slot's `Equals` and `GetHashCode()`. A user base's own `virtual Equals(a: int, b: int)` is comparer-shaped and no container ever calls it, so an override of it obliges nothing, and only the zero-parameter `GetHashCode()` discharges the slot's obligation (a `GetHashCode(seed: int)` is a different member). The any-signature reading — every member named `Equals` that carries `override` obliges `override GetHashCode()` — was declined: it keyed a hash invariant on a name.

**A function value's hash agrees with its equality; the function type stays not hashable ([proposal 0173](proposals/0173-function-value-hash-agrees-with-equality.md), [#5792](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5792)).** Proposal 0161 removed the function *type* from the hashable set because the only hash a function value had was `Object`'s identity hash, which §27.7.1's equality (same method, same receiver) contradicts, and declined a hash definition as "a hash for a key nobody uses". The key is used: a capture-free function value is admitted at `Object` (§28.3), `Set<Object>` and `where T: hashable` at `Object` are legal, and §8.3 states its invariant as a *must* for every type — so `Set<Object>` held two evaluations of one static method as one or two elements by bucket placement, and a generic body relying on the invariant at `Object` was wrong. The hash is defined on the equality's own key (a lambda by closure identity; a method reference by method and class-typed-receiver identity; a value-shaped receiver's reference by its function object's identity), which restores §8.3's invariant everywhere and costs the runtime one kind-dispatched hash beside its kind-dispatched equality. The type stays not hashable: a set of lambdas would still hold every evaluation apart, the reason 0161 kept. Declined: *an identity `Equals` on the `Object` surface* (splits `=` from `Equals`, which §8.4 identifies); *recording the disagreement as a documented exception in §8.3* (leaves `where T: hashable` at `Object` promising what a legal key breaks).

<a id="sec-float-equality-is-reflexive"></a>
### 7.4 Float equality is reflexive

`NaN.Equals(NaN)` is `true`. `(-0.0).Equals(0.0)` is `true`. The relational operators `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` keep IEEE semantics — any comparison involving `NaN` is false.

The asymmetry is intentional. Equality is what hash tables, sets, and `case` patterns use; an irreflexive `=` makes `Set<double>` misbehave when NaN is inserted. Relational operators retain IEEE semantics so ordering-based numeric algorithms work as published. The one published equality idiom this does not cover, the self-inequality NaN test `x != x`, does not port (`x <> x` is `false` for every `x`) and is written `Double.IsNaN(x)`. A warning on a float-typed self-comparison is deferred (changelog v0.308): the expression is certainly constant, but whether its author meant the IEEE test is not, and at a generic `T` the float-typed predicate is known only after substitution. Strict IEEE equality is available as `Double.IeeeEquals`; bit-pattern equality is `Double.BitwiseEquals`.

<a id="sec-equality-skip"></a>
### 7.5 `equality skip`: per-member exclusion from auto-equality

Body-record auto-equality is deliberately all-or-nothing at the *type* level (spec §9.2): the error is loud, never silent reference equality. Without a per-member exclusion, one cache field costs the whole package, and the road back is five member declarations (`operator =`, `operator <>`, `Equals(Object?)`, `Equals(T)`, `GetHashCode` — §9.2's remedy plus §25.2's co-declaration rule) all restating "structural equality over everything except this one field." A recorded deferral ("Asymmetric record auto-equality customization", formerly in [§18](#sec-things-deferred)) named the need before the fix's mechanism existed; `serialized skip` (spec §9.8) then supplied exactly the right affordance — per-member exclusion that also removes its component from eligibility consideration — for only one of the two synthesis packages built on the same §9.2 membership rule. `equality skip` (spec §9.2) mirrors it: one word for one concept across both packages.

The recorded choices: the eligibility gate is one-directional — *serialization's* gate (spec §9.8) reads both packages' skips (a member skipped by either clause is structurally absent for *that* check — the alternative, demanding both clauses on a cache field of a serializable record, was ceremony that reads as meaning something it does not), while the *equality* gate (§3.6's auto-hashable property) reads `equality skip` alone: the landing gate traced the symmetric form to silent reference equality in generated `=` via §9.8's own documented `serialized skip` remedy, the exact footgun §9.2's error exists to prevent, so the unmotivated direction was cut at landing (the v0.244 changelog's gate-round record). Participation never crosses (`equality skip` does not hide a member from the wire; `serialized skip` does not change what `=` compares). The `Equals`/`GetHashCode`-override disqualifier is unrescuable by design — the author who takes over equality takes over all of it (it reaches a homogeneous `=`/`<>` overload on the same ground: [§7.6](#sec-equality-operator-strips-auto-hashable)). All-members-skipped takes the zero-property semantics with no diagnostic (§9.2's own precedent). `ToString` keeps rendering skipped members (one clause, one effect; renderings are not contracts). Declined: a type-level exclusion list (`equality(Lo, Hi)` — the kept-synchronized-list failure mode, [§5.19](#sec-serialization-without-reflection)); auto-excluding private fields (visibility silently changing `=` is exactly what §9.2's error exists to prevent); other spellings (`transient`, `ignore` — vocabulary economy: the sibling clause spells exclusion `skip`); full custom-equality clauses (out of scope — the five-member route, 0012's `DateTimeOffset` precedent, remains the escape for *logic*; the clause covers only exclusion). The corpus honesty note stands from the proposal (0084): no accumulated workaround debt existed — the all-or-nothing rule *deterred* the cache-field shape rather than taxing it visibly; the evidence was the recorded deferral plus the mechanism's arrival, not corpus pressure.

<a id="sec-equality-operator-strips-auto-hashable"></a>
### 7.6 A homogeneous `=` overload strips auto-hashable

A record that overloads `=` itself, homogeneously, is not auto-hashable: spec §9.2's disqualifier list names that overload beside the `Equals`/`GetHashCode` override, and its eligibility list admits only *an overload of a non-equality operator*, so neither list can be read alone and be wrong. The two lists once under-determined the case, the eligibility list admitting non-equality overloads and the disqualifier list naming only the override, so an author who overloaded `=` fell between them. Read one way the record kept auto-hashable and received a synthesized `Equals`/`GetHashCode` computed by a rule its own `=` had just replaced; read the other, the overload disqualified it and §25.2's co-declaration rule supplied the pair. The rule is the second reading.

The decisive argument is not textual but structural: it is the only reading under which §9.2 and §25.2 are independent guards against the same hazard. §25.2 exists to stop the three surfaces (`=`, `Equals`, hashing) diverging — *"`a = b` true while a `Set<T>` treats them as distinct"*. Under the rejected reading §25.2 is the *only* thing stopping it, so any exemption from §25.2 becomes a soundness hole — and that is observably how the `IntPtr` bug arose (v0.275's entry; spec §25.2's retirement parenthetical). Under the ruling the overload strips auto-hashable on its own, §9.2's use-site error blocks equality until the author supplies it, and an exemption is harmless. Three supports follow: §3.6 defines auto-hashable as the property of a type *for which* `=` is synthesized, which an overloading record is not; the word *non-equality* appears in two sections (§9.2, §35.7) and is drafting noise under any other reading, which §9.2's own `equality skip` discipline names as a defect; and the compiler has enforced it since v0.158.

**Heterogeneous overloads (`T = U`) do not disqualify**, aligning §9.2's trigger with §25.2's, which frees them from the co-declaration obligation for the same reason: such an overload defines a comparison the synthesis never provided and takes over nothing. That alignment is what makes §25.2's *"the auto-generated or user-supplied `Equals(Object?)` and `Equals(T)` methods continue to exist"* a live two-case split rather than a sentence with a dead arm.

The stated cost, not minimised: §9.2's recursive component rule admits *another record that is itself auto-hashable*, so a record with an `IntPtr`, `UIntPtr`, or `Ptr<T>` component is not auto-hashable and cannot use `=` or key a hash container — the spec's own `record Sqlite3Db(Raw: IntPtr)` shape. The wart is not created by this ruling; it is created by the component rule saying *auto-hashable* where it arguably means *hashable*. Widening it was declined here and routed, because *hashable* would admit a user-equality record carrying a class-typed field as a component, smuggling reference equality inside a value record's `=` — what §35.7 calls *"virtually never the intended semantics"*. That is a design change owing its own proposal, not a rider on a ruling.

**The rule extends to value-like choices, and extending it forced a second edit nobody asked for.** Spec §35.7 has restated §9.2's operator clause for choices all along, so the ruling reaches them or leaves the two sections disagreeing. But §3.6's hashable row carried its *"nor declares user equality"* rescue for records only — so on the first draft a choice that did everything §25.2's newly-extended co-declaration rule demands was stripped of auto-hashable and then refused as a hash key, unrescuable and undiagnosed. The rescue arm is added rather than the extension dropped, because the alternative leaves a soundness hole open for one type kind on the grounds that nobody has hit it yet; the affected population is zero either way, and a rule with a hole is worse than a rule with a cost. That the gap existed at all is the ordinary consequence of a property defined in one section and consumed in four: §3.6 states what hashable is, and it had never had to consider a type that was outside auto-hashable *by choice* rather than by construction.

The `Equals`/`GetHashCode`-override disqualifier's ground ([§7.5](#sec-equality-skip)) — *the author who takes over equality takes over all of it* — is the same ground, and this is its extension to the surface the author actually writes.

<a id="sec-equality-without-a-definition"></a>
### 7.7 Equality a type does not define: box identity, and the per-instantiation check

A record or choice outside the auto-hashable set has no equality unless its author declares one, and spec §9.2 makes a direct use a compile error. Two routes reach such a value without its static type. The boxed route (an `Object`- or interface-typed receiver) cannot be rejected statically, so it gets a defined answer, member by member: the type's declared member where it has one, and otherwise the inherited identity members (spec §9.2 for records; §35.7 for choices). The answer is sound because a box has identity: identity-equal means the same box, so the same payload and the same hash, and §8.3's contract holds whatever the type declares.

The type-parameter route (`x.Equals(y)` on an unconstrained `T`) is decided differently, because it *can* be checked. Each use's kind (`Equals` against a `T`, `Equals` against anything else, `GetHashCode`) is fixed when the body is checked and read against each substitution, so it reaches that type's own member for the kind where it has one, and an instantiation at a type with none is rejected (spec §9.2, *Through a type parameter*; §3.6 rule 2). `Equals` against a non-`T` argument at a type whose only equality takes its own type compares a boxed argument's payload by that member and is false otherwise, the bridge an enum's box already performs; without it, `where T: hashable` would not cover that use at `IntPtr`, and an interior diagnostic would survive the constraint that §14.2 says replaces it. Two alternatives were declined. *Box identity* gives no sound answer here: a `T`-typed operand of a value substitution is a value, not a box, so `x.Equals(x)` would be false under an implementation that boxes per call and true under one that does not — an irreflexive equality ([§7.4](#sec-float-equality-is-reflexive)'s ground) whose answer turns on a realization strategy spec §11.8 leaves open. A *synthesized structural comparison* is declined on the ground the boxed path's ruling gives: it would answer on the generic path exactly the question §9.2 refuses to answer statically, and for a class-typed component its answer is reference identity, the silent fallthrough §9.2 calls error-prone.

The cost is stated: the instantiation's validity depends on what the generic body does with `T`, not only on its signature. That is the existing model for a property-dependent interior use (spec §14.2's derived-property paragraph speaks of *the per-instantiation re-check for those interior uses*; spec §7.10's copy-demand rejection of a move-only substitution is the same shape), and the remedy is the one that paragraph exists for: `where T: hashable` puts the requirement in the signature and moves the diagnostic to the caller's instantiation. A consequence is stated with it: adding such a use to a public generic's body can reject a client's instantiation with no declared contract changed, which spec §2.8.6 classifies as breaking by its requirement-summary rows, as it does for every interior-use rule; the constraint is how an author makes the requirement a declared one, visible in the signature. The bridge carries a cost of its own: at a record whose only equality is `Equals(T)`, such as `IntPtr`, `x.Equals(o)` through `T` compares a boxed argument's payload, while the boxed `o.Equals(x)` compares box identity (spec §9.2's boxed path), so the two routes can answer one pair differently. It is confined to that record shape, because a record's `Equals(T)` does not fill its box's `Object` slot (spec §25.2) where an enum's or a choice's `Equals` does (spec §35.7), and the record removes it by declaring `Equals(Object?)`. Answering the second kind by box identity instead would be symmetric, but `x.Equals(o)` with `o` a box of `x`'s own value would then be false, the irreflexivity on which box identity is declined above.

([#5492](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5492)) **Rendering through a type parameter is not checked per instantiation.** `ToString` is decided the other way from equality. A choice outside the auto-hashable set has no generated rendering, and a call at its own static type is a compile error (spec §8.5, §35.7); a `T`-typed operand instantiated at it — `$"{x}"` in a generic helper — renders the qualified type name, the answer its box gives, and so does such a choice as a component of a generated rendering (a positional record's field). Rejecting the instantiation was declined on two grounds. No constraint can state the requirement: `hashable` does not imply a rendering, since a value-like choice outside auto-hashable only through its own homogeneous `=` is hashable and has no generated `ToString`, so the rejection would be an interior diagnostic with no signature-side remedy, the shape spec §14.2's derived-property paragraph exists to replace; and it would fall on every generic logging or message helper that interpolates its argument. The qualified name is sound where box identity is not: it is a function of the type alone, so it does not turn on how an implementation realizes generics, and a borrowed owning value renders without being boxed. The cost is that `$"{x}"` prints a type name where the author may have wanted the value, and that a generic body and a concrete one disagree on whether the same rendering compiles; a choice whose rendering matters declares `ToString`.

([#5503](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5503)) **The same holds for a generic choice over a type parameter.** Inside a generic body, `r.ToString()` or `$"{r}"` for `r: Result<T, E>` names a choice type but not a classification: whether `Result<T, E>` is auto-hashable, and so has a generated `ToString`, is decided per instantiation (spec [§35.11](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-choice-generics)). Where it is not and none is declared, the rendering is the instantiated type's qualified name, and the instantiation is not rejected. The first ground above holds unchanged, because no admitted constraint establishes the classification: `where T: hashable` admits a class `T`, at which `Result<T, E>` is owning, and a record declaring its own equality, which is not an admissible component (spec §9.2). Rejecting would also make a more precise static type compile in fewer programs than a less precise one: a helper taking `x: T` renders a `Result<Widget, string>` value that a helper taking `r: Result<T, E>` would be refused. A choice type containing no type parameter (`Result<Widget, string>`) is statically known in a generic body as anywhere else and keeps the use-site error. `Result<T, E>` gains no declared `ToString`: one that rendered payloads at the owning and borrow-bearing instantiations is the case-shaped default the v0.301 entry records as declined, on the ground that owned and borrowed class payloads are not the container's to format, and at the auto-hashable instantiations the generated rendering already exists. A user generic choice whose rendering matters declares `ToString`, which spec §35.2 admits at every classification.

<a id="sec-inheritance"></a>
## 8. Inheritance

<a id="sec-member-hiding-is-forbidden"></a>
### 8.1 Member hiding and name shadowing are forbidden

C# allows a derived member with the same name as a base member to *shadow* (not override) it, with dispatch depending on the static type of the receiver. Almost no one understands the semantics correctly, and the rare cases where it's the right tool are better solved by renaming.

Bezel rejects same-name members in derived classes outright. To override a virtual base member, write `override`. To add something new, choose a different name.

The same ground reaches local names: a declaration may not reuse the name of a local or parameter in scope at it, a setter's implicit `value` included ([§8.1.2](#sec-member-hiding-local-names)). `Object`'s three virtual members block only their own signature, and a class's own-typed `Equals` is that slot's second spelling ([§8.1.3](#sec-member-hiding-object-members)).

<a id="sec-member-hiding-visible-members"></a>
#### 8.1.1 Only visible inherited members are blocked

The blocked set is visible inherited members (§12.2): the recorded motivation above — shadowing and dispatch confusion — requires a member the derived author could see. A base class's `private` (and cross-module `internal`) names stay out of its compatibility surface; the residual cross-module collision creates two distinct members resolved by visibility and static type, recorded as the deliberate scope of the guarantee.

<a id="sec-member-hiding-local-names"></a>
#### 8.1.2 Local names: no redeclaration of a name in scope

([#5370](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5370)) **A local does not redeclare a name of its own scope** (spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations) *Local names*). The same reasoning reaches inside a body at its narrowest point. A second `var s` in one block, or a body-top local named like a parameter, leaves the earlier binding unreachable for the rest of the scope while it is still live: a local or parameter has no qualified spelling (`this.` reaches members, not locals), and the two bindings can carry different ownership obligations. Rust admits same-scope rebinding (`let x = x.trim();`); it is declined. In Bezel, rebinding is the place where an obligation moves, and a new name carries that better (`using var r := move reader;`, the spelling spec §29.8 uses). A `forbid → allow` widening stays compatible where the reverse would break.

([#5382](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5382)) **Nor does a declaration reuse a name of an enclosing scope** (spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations) *Local names*). A nested-block local, a loop variable, an arm binding, a binding-atom clause, or a lambda's parameter, slot or local may not take the name of a local or parameter in scope at it. The ground is the same-scope rule's, one block in: the outer binding stays live under the inner one with no qualified spelling, so for the rest of the inner scope it is unreachable while it may still hold an obligation, and a reader has to find the nearest declaration to know which of two owned values `move x` consumes. The name rules inside a declaration already refuse a shadow: member hiding (above), loop labels (spec §21.5), a local type against a visible type (§21.10) and a method type parameter against an enclosing one (§14.1). The shadows the language admits are of module-level names, a declaration over an import or an always-available name (§2.2, §2.3) and an extension's type-parameter binding over a type, with a warning (§26.4), and each leaves the hidden name reachable by qualification, which a local or parameter never is. C# and Java refuse it within a body too. The test is what is in scope at the declaration, not C#'s whole-block reservation, so sibling blocks, successive loops and the arms of one `case` reuse a name freely, and the compiler's per-declaration keying of definite assignment and ownership state (#5314, #5335, #5267) stays load-bearing for them. A member's parameter may still take a primary-constructor parameter's name (spec §22.10): a parameter name is part of the member's signature, an address for a named argument, and forcing a rename there would let a class's capture leak into its members' interfaces, where a local's name is private to its body. Two alternatives are declined. *Admitting the shadow*, the inner binding hiding the outer for the rest of the inner scope (Rust, Swift, Kotlin): it is the obligation-hiding the same-scope rule refuses, moved one block in, and the compiler's checks met it as defects at least six times (#5314, #5334, #5335, #5267, #3755, #1566), each a check that consulted a name where it needed a binding. *Admitting it across a lambda boundary only* (C# 8 relaxed its rule for lambdas and local functions, so that their parameters and locals may hide an enclosing local or parameter; the C# design team's meeting notes of 2021-11-03 record regret that this admitted a name both captured and shadowed, too late to withdraw): a Bezel lambda captures by reference, and a captured owned binding is a borrow of it (spec §27.5, §28.3), so the hidden name would be a capture candidate inside exactly the construct whose captures the borrow rules reason about; #1566's, #3755's and #5267's lambda rows were this case. Either alternative remains a compatible widening on a recorded need; the ban could not be adopted later without breaking programs, which is C#'s position on its relaxation. The earliest record leans the other way: v0.17's changelog says shadowing "is allowed but discouraged". That entry predates the ownership model (compiler-checked ownership arrived in v0.22) and gave no ground, and the specification does not state it; Appendix B's licence to shadow a setter's implicit `value` is its one remnant, kept by this ruling and left to a separate question, which the #5484 paragraph below decides. Nor is this [§2.3](#sec-auto-imported-extension-modules)'s case for ratifying practice over an unargued restriction: the ban is argued above, and no specification or guide example shadows a nested name, so the only practice is the compiler's, which admitted the shadow while the question was recorded as open.

([#5484](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5484)) **A setter's implicit `value` is a parameter like any other** (spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations) *Local names*, §23.3, Appendix B). Neither rule above exempts any other parameter, and the setter's is where the hazard they close is sharpest. Under `set(owned)` the implicit `value` holds the owned obligation the body exists to discharge, and a nested `var value` hides it for the rest of that block, from the reader and from any check that consults a name where it needs a binding: #5267's and #5252's setter rows were this case. Appendix B's licence rested on `value` not being a true keyword in that role. That fact settles only that the name is not reserved elsewhere. It gives no ground for the setter body to redeclare the parameter, and the licence was the last remnant of v0.17's "shadowing is allowed but discouraged", which the enclosing-scope paragraph above found ungrounded. An author who wants the name for a local gives the local a fresh name; the setter's parameter is always `value` (spec §23.3). The rule reads the same for a property's setter and an indexer's, as Appendix B's sentence did. Two alternatives are declined. *Keeping the exemption*, at body top and nested, leaves one parameter outside both rules on no ground of its own, in the position where an owned obligation most often arrives. *Keeping it at body top only* is the same-scope rebinding the same-scope paragraph above declines, applied to one parameter. Either remains a compatible widening on a recorded need; the reverse could not be adopted later without breaking programs.

<a id="sec-member-hiding-object-members"></a>
#### 8.1.3 `Object`'s virtual members and the own-typed `Equals`

**`Object`'s virtual members block only their own signature** ([#5664](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5664); spec [§12.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-hiding-is-not-allowed)). Every class inherits `Equals(Object?)`, `GetHashCode()` and `ToString()` from `Object` (spec §8.1), so read without a carve-out the rule above would forbid every class a non-`override` `Equals`, `GetHashCode` or `ToString` at any signature — and with it `IEquatable<T>`, `IEqualityComparer<T>` and `IFormattable`, which the stdlib (§2.9, §2.18, §4.8) and spec §18.5 admit on classes, and whose comparer constructors would otherwise have no admissible argument. The ground above — shadowing a member the author can see, with dispatch keyed on the static type — is met in full only by a declaration at `Object`'s own signature, which is the one `override` reaches; a declaration at another parameter list replaces nothing and is selected as every overload is, by its arguments (spec §22.6). One residual of the ground remains, and it is accepted: an `Equals` at another type — `Equals(other: I)` for an interface `I` the class implements — is an overload, so `c.Equals(i)` may answer differently from the same call through an `Object` receiver. Every typed-equality interface at a type other than the class's own carries that split. Spec §8.3's hash rule reads every `a.Equals(b)`, so the author keeps `GetHashCode` in agreement with the typed overload, and overrides the slot where the two answers must agree. So `Object`'s three virtual members block exactly their own signature, and the carve-out is theirs alone: a class's own `Equals(a: K, b: K)` blocks the name in its subclasses as any member does, which keeps the no-overload-across-the-boundary rule intact everywhere a user author could have chosen the base name. Two alternatives are declined. *Reading the rule literally*, so that no class implements the three interfaces: it contradicts four normative statements and leaves stdlib surface unreachable. *Exempting any member that implements an interface member*, whatever base declares the name: it admits the static-type-selected shadow this section exists to refuse, through any interface a derived class lists, and is both wider (any base, through an interface) and narrower (an `Equals(a, b)` declared outside a conformance) than the admission the text needs.

**A class's own-typed `Equals` fills the `Object` slot** ([proposal 0160](proposals/0160-class-own-typed-equals-is-the-object-slot.md), [#5755](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5755), [#5731](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5731)). `Equals(other: Point)` on `class Point` is not an overload beside the inherited `Equals(Object?)` but the slot's second spelling, one virtual member reached by every path — the rule enums and choices have (§9.4 below; spec §9.2, §10.5, §35.2). Read as an overload, the member needed a pairing clause, an operator sentence, a hashable clause, a `C?` carve, a register remedy and three equality-search amendments to keep two members from answering one question differently — at a subclass operand, through a type parameter, in a hash container and through an `Object` surface (the historical note below). Nothing is ranked, because there is one candidate; `Set<Point3>` is hashable through the inherited slot and hash; and the subclass case improves, because the slot is virtual, so a `Point3` that overrides it is compared by its override wherever a `Point3` is compared, which the two-member text could not express (its own objection to reaching the base's non-virtual member). What the #5664 ruling declined — a tie-break keyed on one member name — is not reopened: no tie exists. Three alternatives are declined. *Keeping both members* (the status quo): it is the machinery above, ≈1,100 words for a case with zero measured sites. *Admitting both spellings on one class, the `Object?` one being the slot*: two members again, with the ambiguity the slot rule removes. *A non-virtual slot*, so that `p = q` at two `Point` operands is a direct call: it would compare a `Point3` by `Point`'s member against the subclass's override, the wrong answer proposal 0158 refused, for a dispatch cost a `sealed` class already avoids. `string` keeps one spelling (stdlib §9.6).

**The own-typed `Equals` obliges `GetHashCode`** ([proposal 0155](proposals/0155-class-own-typed-equals.md), [#5668](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5668)). The compiler-enforced pairing (spec §8.3) reads a class's non-`override` own-typed `Equals` as it reads an override, since it fills the same slot; without that, a class with `Equals(other: Point)` and no `GetHashCode` override compiled, and a `Set<Point>` kept equal points apart. That carries the enum and choice decision to the one other kind that can declare such a member, and the divergence it closes is the one spec §25.2's co-declaration rule exists for. A warning was declined because the divergence is a wrong answer, not a style. The obligation stays the declaring class's own: an inherited hash written for another `Equals` does not answer for this one, and a base that seals its `GetHashCode` has fixed its subclasses' equality with it. `=` stays the call `a.Equals(b)`: binding a fixed member would split `=` from the call where the call is unambiguous (`c = 5` against `c.Equals(5)` on `class C: IEquatable<int>`). What the operator adds is the null-safe wrapper (spec §8.4), which reads a present operand at its stripped type, so `p = q` at `q: Point?` and `p = q!` agree.

<a id="sec-member-hiding-two-member-model"></a>
#### 8.1.4 Historical note: the two-member model

Between the #5664 ruling and proposal 0160, a class's own-typed `Equals` was an overload beside `Equals(Object?)`, and two proposals patched the consequences: [proposal 0155](proposals/0155-class-own-typed-equals.md) ([#5670](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5670)) made `p = d` at a subclass-typed `d` §22.6's ambiguity, as `p.Equals(d)` was, declining a tie-break for the operator alone; and [proposal 0158](proposals/0158-equals-ambiguous-type-not-hashable.md) ([#5716](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5716)) made a type whose `Equals` was ambiguous at its own operands (`Point3` deriving from a `Point` declaring `Equals(other: Point)`) not hashable, declining reaching the inherited own-typed member, the nearest `Equals` up the chain, or the `Object` slot through a type parameter. The slot rule removed the ambiguity both answered, and the `Set<Point3>` rejection was withdrawn with it (the v0.332–v0.334 entries).

<a id="sec-class-hierarchy-exhaustiveness-in-case"></a>
### 8.2 Class-hierarchy exhaustiveness in `case`

A sealed hierarchy can be matched exhaustively without a `_` arm: if the base class is `closed` (or abstract with only `sealed` subclasses, recursively forming a closed set — spec §19.6) and every concrete leaf type appears as a pattern, the match is provably total (root and leaves non-generic; the generic case is the record below).

This is the class-shaped form of sum types — useful where it applies, no language additions required. The value-shaped form is the `choice` kind (spec §35); [§9.6](#sec-choices-why-a-new-kind) records the reasons and the line between them — hierarchies for behavior-carrying, identity-bearing sums, choices for value-shaped outcomes.

**Generic hierarchies: the determination rule, retired (proposal 0089, [#3055](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3055)).** A `case` over a closed hierarchy with a generic root or leaf is not proven exhaustive by leaf coverage — the analysis is not attempted — and is completable only by an irrefutable arm. The spec once answered generic-leaf coverage with a *determination rule*: per leaf, compute the root instantiation by composing base-clause substitutions, solve for the leaf's parameters against the scrutinee type under exact invariant identity, filter by constraint satisfaction, and classify exempt/determined/undetermined — with three-valued rigid-parameter identity, the §7.10 `?`-pre-image split, and a census prohibition: roughly two pages of §19's hardest text and ~1,000 lines of sema (#1816, with its own post-landing defect history, #1862/#1874). It was retired by deletion. The deciding evidence was the corpus: after two years of the rule's availability and one year of choices, no organic in-tree code — stdlib, guide, example, or non-§19.6 test — matched a generic sealed hierarchy exhaustively; the rule was exercised exclusively by its own spec-derived test family. Its recorded defense expired with it: the rule's adoption had rejected the blanket never-exhaustive reading as "over-broad: it regresses the determined shape, the canonical sealed-match idiom" — but choices re-homed the value-shaped sum ([§9.6](#sec-choices-why-a-new-kind)), the canonical `Result` is a choice (stdlib §2.20), and the determined shape's flagship inhabitant no longer exists as a class hierarchy anywhere in the tree. The blanket reading is therefore adopted with its rejection's premise expired — recorded here explicitly rather than in silent contradiction of the earlier reasoning. What is lost: `_`-free matching (and spec-§21.7 totality without an irrefutable arm) for generic, behavior-carrying, exhaustively-matched hierarchies — a residual class narrow enough that a one-line dead `_` arm is the recorded price, with virtual dispatch the spelling such hierarchies usually want anyway. Runtime semantics changed nowhere: the deleted proof was sound, and deleting it changed no arm selection. The recorded reopening shape is the existential pattern — `var o: Ok` matching any instantiation of a generic leaf, which would make generic-leaf coverage *expressible* with no determination computation — pinned so a future re-ask starts there rather than at restoring determination; the full design remains in git history and the v0.196 changelog entry.

<a id="sec-one-body-two-interface-members"></a>
### 8.3 One method may meet two coinciding interface members; one override may not fill two virtual slots

([#6577](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6577)) Two neighbouring rules treat a pair of members that coincide at an instantiation differently, on purpose. A class implementing `I<string>`, whose `F(x: T)` and `F(x: string)` coincide there, meets both with one `public method F(x: string)`: spec [§11.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-interface-declaration)'s matching rule is applied once per interface member, and one implementation satisfies every interface member with its signature. An `override` whose signature matches two virtual members of its base, as substituted at the derived class's base clause, is instead a compile error (spec [§12.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-members-are-non-virtual-by-default), [#6302](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6302)). The difference is what the member replaces. An interface member has no body, Bezel having no default interface methods ([§17.4](#sec-default-interface-methods)), so a method meeting two of them replaces nothing: each member is an obligation, and the one body meets both exactly. A virtual member carries a body, and the base's own code reaches each slot separately: in `class B<T>` declaring `Q<U>(e: U, t: T)` beside `Q<U>(e: U, t: int)`, a call whose `t` is typed `T` runs the first slot's body. An override filling both slots at `B<int>` would replace two bodies with one, silently changing what that call runs, and filling only the slot spelled with the concrete type would make the answer depend on spelling ([the decline index](proposals/DECLINED.md) records both readings as `rejected`). Neither rule restricts a caller beyond what overload resolution already does: a call through `I<string>`, or on a `B<int>` receiver, that reaches both members is spec [§22.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-overloading)'s ambiguity either way ([#6220](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6220) (c)). An abstract member is a virtual one (spec [§12.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-abstract-classes-and-members)), so the one-member rule reaches a pair of abstract members that coincide at an instantiation as well ([#6575](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6575)): with one rule over every slot an `override` names, a base turning its abstract pair into `virtual` defaults cannot change whether a subclass's override is legal.

<a id="sec-records-and-classes"></a>
## 9. Records and classes

<a id="sec-why-records-cannot-have-destructors-or-owned-fields"></a>
### 9.1 Why records cannot have destructors or `owned` fields

A record is copied on assignment. If a record had a destructor, every copy would need to track whether it owned or borrowed the underlying resource — C++ value semantics, with their attendant complications.

The clean rule: records hold values and borrowed references. They never own resources. If you need ownership, use a class.

**Four grounds behind spec §9.7's record rules, stated here rather than in the spec.** (1) *Declared field types, not contents* ([#3281](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3281)): a field declared `Object` or an interface type is borrow-carrying although its value may at run time be a box, because the contrary reading would classify a record by its contents, which spec §3.6 rule 1 forbids and spec §2.8.2's computed verdicts cannot carry. (2) *Why no record needs the label/property split that choices need*: a move-only value may not be a field of a record (spec §15.3), and spec §9.7's first paragraph bars owning-inline-array fields, so no record can contain an owning choice that holds borrows — a record that carries borrows *is* a borrow-bearing record. (3) *The null-source quantifier ranges over every reference-typed field, not the borrow-carrying ones alone* ([#3291](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3291); the two phrasings became decidable at #3281, which settled *reference-typed but not borrow-carrying* as a category): the borrow-only reading would let process-global storage hold a function value whose captured variables are a heap-allocated mutable cell reachable and writable from every domain (spec §27.5) with no spec §22.12 rule-4 warning, through a route that rule warns about at every other declaration, and it would make that waiver switchable by adding an unrelated null `Object`-typed field. (4) *Why the null-source form exists at all*: the standard library's sentinel values — `CancellationToken.None` — have semantics defined by the absence of a source rather than by holding one.

<a id="sec-why-records-are-sealed"></a>
### 9.2 Why records are sealed

Inheritance and value-equality conflict. The classic problem: does `Square(5, 5) = Rectangle(5, 5)`? Either answer is wrong in some context. Records side-step this by being sealed. They may implement interfaces, which gives polymorphism without the equality problem.

<a id="sec-auto-equality-on-body-only-records"></a>
### 9.3 Auto-equality on body-only records

A record whose body contains only auto-properties and methods (no explicit fields, no computed properties with side effects) gets auto-generated `=`, `<>`, and `GetHashCode` over those auto-properties. This is what makes `DateTime`, `TimeSpan`, `Guid`, and similar standard types work as hashable without per-type overloads.

The simpler rule — body-only records get no auto-equality — is the one this replaced: it forced every standard library record to write `=`/`<>` overloads explicitly, with no benefit. The refined rule keeps the simple cases simple.

**The use-site error is stricter than C#.** C#'s `record` types always auto-generate equality, but C# *classes* fall back to reference equality on `.Equals(object?)` silently. Bezel records do not have C#'s always-auto-generate guarantee (a body record opts out by including non-auto-property storage), and a silent reference-equality fallthrough on a value type would be an error-prone default: users expect content equality on records, and a body record's reference-equality `Equals` is virtually never the intended semantics. Spec §9.2's compile error at the call site puts the choice in the user's hands.

**Which properties "auto-properties" names for a compiler intrinsic ([#3131](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3131); resolved 2026-08-18 under the owner's delegation, which [§5.10](#sec-the-move-expression-on-owned-fields) quotes).** §8.5's per-type sentence for `Range<T>` ("rendering its auto-properties") and §9.2's "always over storage, never over derived values" pulled apart at the one record whose declaration and storage diverge — the compiler intrinsic: stdlib §9.8 declares six properties in one uniform bodyless spelling, no expression-bodied form and no computed marker anywhere, while the intrinsic stores four slots (`start`, `end`, `step`, `flags`) and derives `IsOpenEnded` and `IsEmpty`. Resolved for the declared surface — all six render. §9.2's exclusion is phrased against *expression-bodied* properties, a syntactic category §9.8 never uses, so a reader of the declaration has no four-member referent available; the storage layout is an implementation fact the spec never promises, and a representation fact cannot shrink a normatively-declared surface. §9.2 scopes its own rule (the storage-vs-derived line is about the declaration surface; for user records the two coincide), the derived pair renders recomputed by the same §16.3 rules member access lowers to — so the rendered component and `r.IsEmpty` cannot disagree, and an open-ended range renders `IsEmpty: False`, §16.3's never-empty rule — and equality is indifferent either way, the derived pair being a function of the stored four: the tension was rendering-only, which is why it went unnoticed. The implemented rendering ([#2661](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/2661)'s lowering) already conformed. The generic-substitution rendering residue recorded on the issue (`Range<char>` reached through an unresolved `T` rendering code points rather than characters) is a codegen bug under the each-component-renders-as-its-own-`ToString` rule, not a spec question — tracked as its own issue, [#3242](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3242).

**A record's own-typed `Equals` is its `Object` slot (proposal [0176](proposals/0176-value-kind-own-typed-equals-is-the-object-slot.md); [#5793](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5793) reading 1).** A record's own-typed `Equals(T)` fills the `Object` slot, as an enum's and a choice's do and a class's does in a second spelling (proposal 0160). The rule it replaced kept `Equals(Object?)` as a record's slot with `Equals(T)` beside it as §25.2's co-declaration member — two members answering one question, and with them one rule of their own (proposal 0159: a record that holds borrows may declare no `Equals(Object?)`, since the equality search would hand that member a `T`-typed argument boxed with no conversion written), a generated positional pair of two members, and a boxed `Money` declaring only `Equals(Money)` comparing by box identity. An earlier decline (the v0.335 entry) kept the two members apart so that a record that holds borrows could declare `Equals(T)` without reaching a box, and because no program compared such a record through `Object`. The first reason does not hold: an own-typed slot member boxes no argument, and the receiver box it needs is §8.6's admitted position, as for every enum, so such a record declares `Equals(T)` on the same terms either way; and with the `Object?` spelling rejected on every value kind the holds-borrows rule has nothing to guard (no record declares a member at `Object?`, so the first kind boxes nothing by construction — stronger than a declaration rule keyed on a classification a generic record decides per instantiation), and three kinds state one rule. The own-typed spelling obliges `GetHashCode` on a record as it does on an enum and a class, because §8.3's invariant must hold through a box; that is the one rejection the rule adds, which reached a handful of test records. Declined with the proposal: the status quo (two members and two rules for a case with zero measured sites); admitting both spellings on a record with the `Object?` one as the slot (#5793's reading 1 as worded: two members again, the shape proposal 0160 declined for a class); and exempting a record's plain own-typed `Equals` from the `GetHashCode` obligation (a slot `Equals` hashed by the box's identity breaks §8.3's invariant at every `Object` surface).

**Declared equality is user equality on every value kind (proposal [0178](proposals/0178-declared-equality-is-user-equality.md); [#5915](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5915), [#5916](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5916), [#5917](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5917)).** It closes three seams the slot rule leaves. First, §3.6's hashable row read *declares user equality* as the operator pair with `Equals(T)`/`GetHashCode` beside it, so a record declaring `Equals(other: Key)` and `GetHashCode` but no `=` — every member §9.2's three kinds reach, and the members a `Set<Key>` consults — was outside hashable by the letter while an enum declaring the same two members was hashable; the arm reads the own-typed `Equals` with the `GetHashCode` §8.3 obliges beside it, with or without an overload, and the type is hashable on the members it declares. Second, §35.7 let a value-like choice's methods "neither qualify nor disqualify", so a choice declaring `Equals(other: Tag)` kept the generated structural `=` beside a replaced `Equals` — the three-surface divergence §9.2's second disqualifier exists to prevent on a record, with one `=` answering two ways at the declaration and through a type parameter; a declared `Equals` or `GetHashCode` disqualifies a choice exactly as it disqualifies a record, the generated package going with it, `=`/`<>` rejected until the overloads are written, and the pair making the choice hashable where it is value-like. Third, no value kind can declare `Equals(other: Object?)`, so an interface member at that signature had no declared witness on a record, an enum or a choice; the member that fills the slot is the witness, on a class as on a value kind — on a value kind its own `Equals`/`GetHashCode`, declared or generated, and a kind with none does not conform, box identity not being reached through a type parameter (§9.2). Declined with the proposal: keeping the operator pair as the only user-equality arm (the pair alone supplies every member the row's consumers reach; zero sites need the operators for hashing); letting `=` on a value kind dispatch to a declared own-typed `Equals` as §8.4 does on a class (it reverses §9.2's recorded use-site error and §25.2's state-all-three discipline, and the pinned tests with it, for a convenience the overloads spell in two lines); keeping §35.7's status quo (a generated `=` beside a replaced `Equals` is the divergence itself); and refusing every value kind the interface (it would make `Object`'s surface the one surface a value kind cannot promise through an interface, with zero sites either way).
