`throw expr` is an expression (with bottom type, valid in any expression position), and so is bare `throw`. A statement-only bare `throw;` would not matter in a clause-form `catch`, where both fit naturally in a statement sequence; in the arm form, where bodies are `=> expression` or `=> do statements end`, it would force a bare re-raise into the heavier `do ... end` form even when the re-raise is the entire body:

```bezel
// Without the change:
catch
  var ex: TransientException => Retry(),
  var ex: Exception => do throw; end       // do/end just for one statement
end;
```

So bare `throw` is an expression form too, the operand being optional: `throw expr` raises the supplied exception, `throw` (no operand) re-raises the current exception. Both have bottom type. The bare form is restricted to catch arm bodies — the same restriction as the statement form `throw;`. The natural arm shape becomes:

```bezel
catch
  var ex: TransientException => Retry(),
  var ex: Exception => throw
end;
```

The statement form `throw;` also exists and means the same thing as the expression form followed by `;`. Inside `do ... end` arm bodies that perform multiple steps before re-raising, the statement form is still natural:

```bezel
catch
  var ex: SqlException => do
    metric.RecordSqlError(ex);
    throw;                                  // statement form, in a sequence
  end
end;
```

Both forms coexist; the choice between them is whether the re-raise is the whole arm body (use the expression form) or one step of several (use the statement form). This is the same expression-vs-statement choice that programmers already make for method calls, `case` arms, and other expressions that can also stand alone as statements.

Without the expression form, the most common idiom in catch arms that re-raise (handle some types specifically, re-raise everything else) would be heavier in the arm form than in the clause form. With it, the idiom reads cleanly:

```bezel
catch
  var ex: TransientException => Retry(),
  Exception => throw
end;
```

<a id="sec-what-catch-and-case-still-dont-share"></a>
#### 11.3.5 What `catch` and `case` still don't share

The unification is structural, not total. Exception dispatch and value dispatch differ in two ways the syntax doesn't try to hide.

The first is the source of the value being matched. `case` matches an explicit scrutinee. `catch` matches the implicit "currently-thrown exception" — there is no scrutinee to name, and the arms can only test types. A non-type `catch` arm pattern (a literal, a range, a record-destructuring) makes no sense and is a syntax error.

The second is the behavior on no match. `case` in expression position raises `MatchFailureException` on no match; `case` in statement position falls through silently. `catch` does neither — unmatched exceptions propagate to the enclosing handler. This is the right behavior for exception dispatch, and it is documented as a third position in [§19.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-exhaustiveness) (alongside expression-position and statement-position `case`).

These differences aren't gaps; they're the parts of exception handling that don't reduce to value pattern matching. The syntax unifies the parts that are the same and leaves the parts that differ to the semantics.

<a id="sec-one-rule-for-failure-signals"></a>
### 11.4 One rule for failure signals: why Bezel is not `Result`-first

The error-model study (proposal 0076, since superseded) asked the maximal question directly: is `Result<T, E>` a better general-purpose error mechanism than exceptions, and should Bezel migrate to it? The answer is doctrine — the failure-signal rule (stdlib §1) — rather than migration, and this section is the citable home of the two declines behind it.

**Why information content is not the band test.** The intuitive line — exceptions for boring failures, `Result` where the failure carries information — was the guides' original teaching, and it was declined as the classification test. It breaks on the environmental fault: an I/O failure carries rich information (path, message, OS detail), yet its consumption site is almost always a boundary — a log, a retry loop, a protocol conversion — that reads the data once and lets it go, which is exactly what `catch`'s copy-out model (spec §31.1) serves. Classifying by information content would evacuate the environmental band into `choice` returns whose exhaustive matches every interior caller would then have to write, converting boundary handling into call-site ceremony. The test that survives is *where consumption happens* (call-site branching vs. boundary handling) *and whether the payload can be value-like* (spec §35.4) — never how much information the failure carries.

**Why maximal `Result` is recorded as not currently constructible.** A `Result`-first Bezel needs four things at once, and each is missing or expensive under landed rules: ownership-polymorphic combinators (`Map`/`AndThen` over owned payloads — the retired-and-deferred territory of spec §14.4), without which owning `Result` chains are match pyramids; a propagation operator, which sits behind proposal 0061 §2's evidence gate and a four-way collision on the `?` token; failure values that cross task seams, where an owned payload makes the instantiation owning and never directly sendable (spec §35.12); and a governed payload vocabulary, without which the error-type space fragments faster than the exception taxonomy it would replace — a choice is a closed sum, so every case addition breaks every exhaustive consumer (spec §35.6). None of this is a taste judgment, which is why the study's verdict is recorded with a reopening bar (proposal 0076 §6's four conditions) rather than as a preference: it is reopenable by evidence, not by re-arguing.

What Bezel adopts instead is the hybrid the rule encodes: `Result` and domain choices confined to the clusters where call-site branching on value-like payloads is the real consumption pattern (decode/deserialize, gathered validation), exceptions keeping the boundary-consumed residual, and one mechanism per fault condition — chosen per condition by the rule, not per language by fashion. The line between the rule's first and fourth bands is visible in the exception's class ([§11.12](#sec-failures-and-defects)).

<a id="sec-enum-prefix-omission"></a>
### 11.5 Enum-prefix omission is a pattern-position affordance, deliberately not an expression-position one

(Proposal 0086 item 5.) Inside a `case` over an enum (or choice) scrutinee, the prefix may be omitted (`case p of Low => …`, spec §10.6/§19.8) because pattern position has exactly one scrutinee, fixed by `of`, whose type is known before any pattern is read — resolution is scrutinee-directed by construction, and a bare constant there can mean nothing else (spec §19.8: "a case name resolves to a case pattern, never a type test"). Expression position has no such anchor: in `p = Low`, the two operands are peers — nothing marks `p` as the side that supplies `Low`'s type, and a rule that resolved `Low` against "the other operand's type" would make name resolution type-directed and bidirectional, which spec §2.7's position-keyed, no-backtracking design deliberately excludes (the recorded decline of C#'s dual-meaning rule). Swift's leading-dot syntax (`p == .low`) shows the feature is buildable — at the cost of a dedicated sigil form and inference-driven member lookup pervading expression syntax; Bezel declines that machinery for the saving of one qualifier. The asymmetry is therefore the composition of two settled designs, not an oversight: patterns are scrutinee-typed, expressions are position-resolved. Write `Priority.Low` in expressions; inside the type's own body, spec §2.7's enclosing-member lookup already shortens it to `Low`.

<a id="sec-is-does-not-bind"></a>
### 11.6 `is` does not bind — the three-construct decomposition, recorded

(Proposal 0086 item 7; spec §12.7 states the decline flatly.) Bezel splits what C#'s `is T name` bundles: `is`/`is not` are the *boolean* type tests (with flow narrowing on locals and `readonly` fields, spec §7.4/§12.7 — the tested variable itself narrows, so the common tested-local case needs no binding at all); `as` is the *nullable-binding* form; `case` typed patterns are the *multi-way binding* form. A fourth spelling, `is T name`, would buy one line at exactly the sites narrowing does not reach (mutable fields, call results) and would import the C# feature's recorded swamp: a pattern variable declared inside a boolean expression must be scoped by definite-assignment flow — bindings escape into `else` branches under negation (`if (!(x is T t)) return;` leaving `t` usable after), into the enclosing scope, and into `&&`/`||` arms — a scoping model so widely misread that it is a staple of C# style guides. Bezel's decomposition keeps every binding at a statement-shaped introduction (`var`, `case` arm) where scope is block-shaped and readable. The decline is of the *spelling*, not the capability: `var d := x as Dog; if d <> null then … end;` is the sanctioned two-step, one line longer, with no new scoping rules. If evidence accumulates that the two-step dominates real code at fields (post-§7.4-readonly-narrowing, the residual is mutable fields and expression results), the recorded lift is `is T name` with true-branch-only scope and no negation flow — the tight-scoping variant — which this entry pre-commits as the only acceptable shape.

**What a *write* through a value-narrowed binding at a boxed location denotes: rejected at compile time (spec defect [#3117](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3117); owner-directed 2026-08-12 — "Please also resolve 3117").** §12.7 narrows the tested variable itself, which is the decomposition's whole point above — but at a variable whose *declared* storage is `Object` or an interface and whose tested type is a value type, the narrowed binding names a §8.6 box, and the spec said nothing about writing through it. The read direction produces the boxed value (#3090), which is what §17.4's inline-array remedy — `if o is [3]int then var a: [3]int := o; end;` — actually needs. An implementation that routes reads and writes alike to the box payload accepts `o[0] := 9`, and the measured behavior is the defect's sharpest statement: `var a: [3]int := [4, 5, 6]; var o: Object := a; if o is [3]int then o[0] := 9; …` prints `9` through `o` and `4` through `a`. Three interpretations were weighed. **(1) Legal, writes the box** — §8.6's wrapper "holds the value", boxing copied it, so the box is a distinct value with its own storage and mutating it is ordinary value-type mutation. Locally self-consistent (the write *is* readable back through the same binding, which is exactly what the `9` measures), and declined anyway, on the ground the local view hides: `Object` is a class and an interface is a reference type (§3.1, §8.1), so such a variable carries the standard reference semantics §3.2 defines, and `var o2: Object := o;` shares the box — the write is mutation of heap state observable through *every* other reference to it — aliased mutation of a value, the one thing value-copy semantics exists to exclude. Interpretation 1 is not a small liberty inside the value model; it is globally unsound against it. It also breaks the runtime-managed kind's own premise: boxed values are runtime-managed (§8.6/§28.9) and §9.7/§35.4 treat runtime-managed payloads as classification-neutral — no borrow tracking, no lifetime constraints — with immutable shared referents doing the work; a writable box payload would demand borrow and freeze machinery for the one kind defined by never needing any. **(2) A compile error** — adopted, on those two grounds, with the issue's own §23.5 analogy kept as the *secondary*, reader-facing one: a reader who writes `o[0] := 9` to update `a` updates neither `a` nor anything they can name, the same family as "would modify a discarded copy", and the diagnostic should echo that register. The adopted rule keys on where the store lands, not on the statement form, so it reaches every position that can put one in the box: the lvalue-chain statements, the same chain in a `ref`/`out` argument (§22.3's assignable-storage-location categories admit `ref o[0]`, and the callee's write lands in the payload — the `Swap` shape, observable through `o2`), and an inline-array argument at an `extern` parameter, which §32.5/§15.1.3 pass by address so that native writes land there too. By the same key it does *not* reach the bare-binding compound and `++`/`--` forms: §6.6 makes `o += 1` equivalent to `o := o + 1`, whose store targets the declared location and mints a fresh box — the legal reassignment below, spelled shorter. Rejecting sugar whose normative expansion is legal would carve §6.6's equivalence for no semantic gain. The analogy's imperfection is recorded rather than glossed: at a §23.5 indexer result the copy is discarded and the write is unobservable, while here the box persists and the write *is* observable through the writing binding — which is precisely why the aliasing ground, not the discarded-copy ground, is primary. **(3) Legal, reflected nowhere** (the pre-#3090 behavior, where the statement emitted nothing) was never live: a silently dropped write. What the rule does *not* touch is the whole-value assignment `o := b`, whose target is the declared `Object`/interface location and whose right-hand side is boxed under §5.1 — a fresh box, other references to the old one unaffected — which is why the diagnostic's copy-out/mutate/re-box remedy makes visible the box mint interpretation 1 hid, and which invalidates the narrowing exactly as §12.7's reassignment sentence has always said. Kernel cost: none — a use-site lvalue admissibility rule, grounded on the value-copy principle and §28.9's immutable-referent premise; no new K row, no axiom. **The scope check the ruling asked for, recorded with its residue:** no value-type *member* surface can mutate its receiver in the shapes that have one — a choice declares no instance storage and only expression-bodied read-only properties, with `this` a borrow (spec §35.2); an enum's instance state is its underlying constant (§10.4/§10.5); inline arrays declare no members and cannot be extended (§26.2) — so for those kinds the spec states nothing, as the ruling provided. The residue is the mutable body record: §9.4 admits a settable property and nothing forbids a record method from assigning one, so a call on a boxed receiver — `o.Bump()` on a narrowed one, or plain interface dispatch `i.Bump()` on an `i: IBump` holding a boxed record, which needs no narrowing at all — is a writable source shape whose meaning turns on whether a value-typed receiver's `this` aliases the receiver's storage or a copy of it. No section states that generally, for records or for value types: the nearest text is §8.5's parenthetical that a boxed receiver's rendering takes a call-bounded copy, said of the `ToString` path and generalized to no other member. Both readings are bad in the same way this entry rejects (an alias reproduces the aliased mutation; a copy reproduces interpretation 3's dropped write), but neither §12.7 nor this ruling can settle it without first ruling on value-receiver binding, and the language has no mutating-member classification a narrowing-site rejection could key on. It was not resolved by side effect: it is spec defect [#3124](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3124), settled by the value-receiver ruling (interpretations 2/3 combined; decision record [§9.13](#sec-value-receivers-are-borrows-rationale), spec §9.4).

<a id="sec-every-throwable-is-an-exception"></a>
### 11.7 Every throwable derives from `Exception`

(Proposal 0080, #3046, re-ruling #771, which admitted any owned class instance as a throw operand.) The any-class capability had no recorded motivating use and no reporting surface — `Message`, `StackTrace`, and `CauseChain` are `Exception`-only — and #771 predated the completion of the exception model, whose reporting surface is written in `Exception` vocabulary throughout (the reason the v0.182 changelog flagged the ruling for owner review). The capability's entire cost was *rules*: a no-universal-catch-all caveat (spec §31.2.3), an `Object`-base carve in the multi-type binding rule (§31.2.1), an asterisk on the deliberate-suppression idiom (§31.2.5), and a hedged parenthetical in §31.1 itself — each a trap for the C#/Java reader who assumes a final `Exception` arm catches everything, with no compensating capability anywhere. The corpus made the case in its strongest form: of ~750 `throw new` sites across the docs and tests at drafting, not one used non-`Exception` throwability *for anything* — the only deliberate uses were the two test files pinning the permissive rule itself.

The throw operand's static type must be `Exception` or a class deriving from it, and all four rule artifacts go; with single inheritance, the nearest common base of any two throwable classes is itself `Exception`-family, so the binding rule's `Object` case is unreachable rather than repealed-with-changed-behavior; and a catch arm — or a generic arm parameter's bound — naming a non-`Exception`-family class errors: provably dead for every such class but `Object`, universally matching for `Object` itself (the bare `Exception` arm is that spelling), the same reject-dead-shapes posture §31.2.1 already applied to non-class generic bounds. The requirement is a pure typing precondition at the throw site — one subtype check — with zero kernel contact: `throw x`'s K3.8/K4.2 site-announced-kill status, the post-throw moved-from machinery, the exception-ownership rules (transfer to the runtime, dispose-after-arm and its two owner-owned exemptions, the borrowed-field residual), and §22.15's pure-throw carve-out (whose conditions are purity and trivial-dispose, not derivation) all quantify over "the thrown owned class instance", never over its base class.

Alternatives declined: the status quo (the spec had carried an open request for this re-ruling); requiring derivation as a warning (a warned-but-admitted non-`Exception` throwable keeps every artifact alive, since the shape remains expressible — the payoff is the deletions, which only a hard rule buys); structural admission of any class exposing the reporting members (Bezel has no structural typing anywhere, and trace capture at throw is `Exception` machinery); and restricting the arm side while keeping the throw side permissive (incoherent — the throw side is the source of the artifacts; the arm side merely reflects it). Like [§11.3](#sec-catch-is-case-for-exceptions)'s unification, the ruling makes the newcomer's prior true: `catch var ex: Exception` is the catch-all — its one qualifier the uncatchable `StackOverflowException` (spec §31.4), a resource-exhaustion termination no arm has ever matched, not a typing carve.

<a id="sec-supersession-keeps-later-wins"></a>
### 11.8 Exception supersession keeps later-wins; the lost diagnosis is snapshot-recorded

(Proposal 0097, charter ruling C3.) Bezel has a single in-flight exception slot and a **later-wins** rule: an exception raised in a non-normal position — a throwing `finally`, a throwing `when` guard, a later throwing disposer in a multi-value cleanup pass (spec §31.2/§31.2.1/§28.4) — replaces the one already propagating, and the replaced one is disposed. Its cost, uncompensated, is that the superseded exception's diagnosis vanishes, and it is frequently the *interesting* one: a `finally` that throws while unwinding a real failure buries the real failure. The reviews' remedy was to flip the priority to **primary-wins** (the Java try-with-resources shape: the body's exception survives, the cleanup's is attached as suppressed). The flip is declined, and the design takes the thing the flip was actually *for* — the diagnostics: at every supersession site the runtime snapshots the superseded exception into the superseding one's `SupersededChain` (spec §31.4), on exactly the `ExceptionInfo` machinery `CauseChain` uses, then disposes it. Later-wins stands, the single slot stands, and the diagnosis is recovered.

**Why snapshot + later-wins beats primary-wins.** The case for primary-wins is entirely a diagnostics case, and the snapshot answers it — the buried failure's type, message, and stack trace survive into `SupersededChain`, printed by the uncaught-exception report and the FFI-boundary abort beside `CauseChain` (spec §32.8/§33.3). What primary-wins would *additionally* buy is that the surviving instance is the primary one, so an upstream `catch (IOException)` still matches the body's failure rather than the cleanup's. What it would cost: a second live exception — the suppressed instance must stay alive and reachable through the primary until handling completes, i.e. one exception owning another, which spec §31.4 declines in a sentence written about a different feature and applying verbatim here (*"a live-instance `InnerException` … would require exception instances to own exceptions, reopening the arm-lifetime rules"*) — primary-wins is that rejected design arriving through the supersession door; a change to the in-flight-slot model, whose single slot is what makes §31.2's dispose-exactly-once rule and its two named exemptions statable at all; and a behavioral reversal for the repeated case — at §28.4's cleanup pass, primary-wins means the *first* disposer's failure escapes and the rest are suppressed, defensible where "the body" is privileged, arbitrary for a scope-end pass over N locals where no disposal is the primary.

**The precedents, cited honestly.** Both mainstream precedents recover the losing exception; they disagree about which one wins, and the disagreement is not the part either is famous for. Java (`Throwable.addSuppressed`/`getSuppressed`, JLS 14.20.3) is primary-wins — but what Java *added* was the list, not the priority: before it, the cleanup exception simply replaced the body's, exactly as Bezel's later-wins does, and the complaint was the lost diagnosis. Python (PEP 3134, `__context__`) is later-wins with the earlier preserved — *"During handling of the above exception, another exception occurred"* — precisely this model. The diagnostics recovery is universal across both precedents; the priority direction is not, and Bezel already sat on Python's side for reasons that predate the proposal (the single slot). The difference from Python is that Bezel's carrier is a snapshot rather than a live instance — the same difference §31.4 already chose for causes, for the same reason.

**The reopening bar:** field evidence from external users that upstream `catch` arms are matching on cleanup failures often enough to matter — arm matching on the primary is the one gain the snapshot does not provide. The snapshot does not foreclose it: a future primary-wins would keep the same chain and only change which instance carries it. Zero new ownership rules were spent — the whole delta is a value-record copy into a field of an object the runtime already owns, at a point the runtime already controls.

<a id="sec-no-bit-syntax-patterns"></a>
### 11.9 No bit-syntax patterns: sub-byte matching stays out of pattern position

(Proposal 0106 §3.5.) Erlang's bit syntax works in *pattern* position — a binary is destructured by a pattern naming bit-widths, binding fields and a tail in one form. Bezel declines the pattern-position half, and the decline is narrow: it is about pattern position, not about bit-granular reading.

The reasons are the pattern system's own. A bit-width pattern element binds a value whose *type* is a function of a width written inside the pattern, so pattern-position binding would acquire a computed-type form that spec §19.3's pattern kinds have nowhere to put. Exhaustiveness has no answer: the pattern kinds Bezel counts for coverage are case names, type tests, and the nullable split, all of which partition a finite declared space, while bit-width alternatives partition an integer's value space — the counting the retired generic-leaves determination rule shows the cost of getting wrong (§8.2). And the tail binding is the sharpest problem: a matched remainder wants to be a sub-view of the scrutinee, which in Bezel is a borrow-bearing span, so a pattern element would mint a borrow whose source is the scrutinee and whose lifetime pattern arms do not currently reason about — a borrow-producing binder, which no pattern kind is.

None of that reaches the *reader*, which needs none of pattern position: a bit-granular reader over a `ReadOnlySpan<byte>` is an ordinary borrow-bearing record whose lifetime the span rules already bound, and the GC-derived objection that forces Erlang's design (a matched tail must outlive the match, so sub-binaries are refcounted) does not transfer, because a Bezel span already *is* a lifetime-checked sub-view. That direction is a standard-library question and is filed as its own proposal (0109), where it must be settled on stdlib grounds; this entry exists so the pattern-position half is settled rather than left dangling behind it.

**Reopening bar:** a design that answers all three — a binding form whose type is not computed inside the pattern, an exhaustiveness account for width alternatives, and a borrow-producing binder with a stated lifetime rule — not a demonstration that bit matching reads nicely.

<a id="sec-exception-rendering-member"></a>
### 11.10 `Exception` declares the rendering its own text promised

(Proposal 0113, [#3259](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3259).) Spec §31.4 says that an exception's diagnostic chain *"survives into logs and `ToString()`"*, and makes the same promise for `SupersededChain` with an output shape attached (*"renders it beside its causes"*). `Exception` declares the `ToString` that delivers both. Without it, `Exception` would carry spec §8.5's compiler-generated class default — the qualified type name alone — and the two texts would not merely under-specify each other, they would conflict: an implementation that rendered the chains from `Exception.ToString()` would violate §8.5, and one that obeyed §8.5 would falsify §31.4. `Console.WriteLine(ex)` would print `System.ArgumentException` and nothing else.

**Declared rather than re-homed.** The smaller-looking fix — strike *"and `ToString()`"* from both sentences and point readers at the runtime reports — was declined on three grounds. It retracts a recorded decision (`SupersededChain`'s output shape), which the change-process classifies as a design change in its own right rather than a wording fix. It leaves the footgun permanently: `Console.WriteLine(ex)` and `$"{ex}"` are what people write, and the language would have no member that renders an exception at all, so every log site hand-assembles four properties. And it is not actually smaller — it amends §31.1, the guides, and every prose site implying an exception renders itself, against one declared member plus its rendering text. Nothing structural was in the way: `Object.ToString` is already `public virtual` (spec §8.1), every class already carries the slot, no derived type's conformance moves, and trivial-dispose (spec §28.9) keys on destructors and `owned` fields, never on member count, so §22.15's pure-throw carve-out is untouched.

**The stack trace is out, deliberately.** Bezel follows Java's split rather than C#'s inclusion. Neither §31.4 sentence promises the trace — both promise the *chains*; `StackTrace` is *"formatted"* with no format stated anywhere and may be `""`; a one-line `Log(ex.ToString())` that expands to a multi-hundred-line dump stops being called, which would leave the promise unfulfilled a second way; and Bezel already has the structural equivalent of `printStackTrace` in triplicate — the uncaught report (§33.3), the FFI-boundary abort (§32.8), and the exit-time disposal report (§22.13) — all three of which do print it. `ToString` is the short half rather than a duplicate of the long one.

**Content normative, arrangement not — and the reports defined independently.** The posture matches what the language already does with exception text everywhere else: §33.3 and §32.8 fix *what* is printed and never *how*. Two arrangement facts are nonetheless fixed, because a reader can rely on them: the qualified type name is always present, so the rendering is never less informative than the default it replaces, and the two chains are distinguishable, since §31.4 gives their orderings different meanings. Stating that the three reports define their own content — rather than defining them as calls to `ToString()` — forecloses two live hazards rather than restating the obvious: double printing, which a shipped implementation was already positioned for (its uncaught report reads the type name through the vtable and `Message`/`CauseChain` by field offset), and a fatal allocation on the termination path, since `ToString` allocates and spec §17.7 makes a failed allocation terminate the process — on the very path whose purpose is to explain a failure. No second §17.7 carve-out was requested.

**Not `pure`, and that is the load-bearing half of the purity question.** Purity here is not about the string allocation (§22.15 admits runtime-managed allocation and string composition). It is about dispatch: §22.15's rules propagate a `pure` override down the whole chain, so marking this member would oblige *every* derived exception type's own `ToString` — user types included — to be declared `pure` and checked under the pure fragment, for a rendering whose whole job is to compose user-declared state. An absent contract is recoverable; a false one is not.

**The recorded cost, in full.** Declaring the member changed the output of every `Console.WriteLine(ex)`, `$"{ex}"`, and `Log(ex.ToString())` in every program, and `base.ToString()` inside a user exception's own override reaches this rendering rather than the type name. No program changed shape. A program parsing `ex.ToString()` for exactly a qualified type name should read `ex.GetType().FullName` (stdlib §2.13), which is stable and says what it means.

<a id="sec-patterns-read-intrinsic-ordering"></a>
### 11.11 Relational and range patterns read the language's own ordering

([#5269](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5269), [#5273](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5273).) Spec §19.3 compares a relational pattern's scrutinee under the ordering of the scrutinee's type, and that ordering is the intrinsic one only: spec §3.5's primitives except `bool`, and `string`'s ordinal ordering (§4.6). The two other answers were the type's declared `operator <` group at relational patterns, with range patterns staying intrinsic, and declared operators at both. A range pattern additionally keeps spec §16.3's element set, the integer types and `char`.

**Why not the declared operators.** The reason is not that a pattern must never run user code. A named slot runs a class getter and a constant slot runs a record's own `=` (spec §19.3), so that line was never drawn. Three reasons belong to this feature. First, a type with an intrinsic ordering has exactly one, and the language fixes it. A declared group is four methods that spec §25.2 obliges only to exist together: not to agree with one another, and not to be an order. A type may also declare several heterogeneous groups (`operator <(a: Money, b: int)` beside `operator <(a: Money, b: Money)`), so "the ordering of the scrutinee's type" would name an overload resolution rather than an ordering. Second, range patterns cannot follow. A range pattern's test is `Range<T>` membership (spec §16.6), and widening `T` past §16.3's intrinsic set is a deferred design change whose open questions include these patterns (`docs/proposals/DECLINED.md`, *`Range<T>` element widening*). Reading declared operators at the relational form alone would give `>= lo` and `lo..`, one test by spec §16.2, opposite verdicts over every ordered user type. Third, a guard already writes the test with nothing lost: `var x when x > limit` resolves `>` as any expression does, at a position where a reader expects a call.

**Why the equality slot is not a precedent.** A constant pattern is spec §8.4's `=` test. A record that declares a homogeneous `=` must declare `Equals(T)` and `GetHashCode` beside it (spec §25.2's co-declaration rule), so the operator a pattern reaches is tied to the surface every other consumer of equality reads. No rule ties a declared `<` to `CompareTo`, or to anything else.

**The cost.** Over `float`, `double`, `decimal` and `string` a relational pattern is admitted and a range pattern is not, because the two read different rules: §3.5's ordering, and §16.3's range element set. Over `float`, `double` and `decimal` the split is the price of the deferred widening. `string` joins them because §16.3 excludes it too, and range patterns over it would leave spec §16.6's agreement with `in` without a `Range<string>` to agree with. No user type is added to the split. An `operator <` declared on a record, enum, choice or class does not reach pattern position, and the guard is the spelling.

**Reopening bar:** a program whose ordering test a guard cannot express, or a `Range<T>` widening that admits user element types, at which point both pattern forms would move together.

**How the bounds are typed** (proposal 0203; [#6887](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6887), [#6897](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6897), [#6935](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6935)). A range literal is target-typed where its position supplies an integer type or `char` — a `Range<T>` location, an index, the `in` operand, a `Contains` argument, a range pattern's scrutinee — when every written endpoint converts to that type under spec §5.1, and is otherwise typed from its endpoints as spec §6.2 types a binary operator's operands, a sign-mixed pair with no common type (`-1..3000000000U`) being an error as `-1 + 3000000000U` is. The expression and the pattern then part: at `in` a literal the operand's type cannot hold falls back to its own type and the operand converts to it, so `b in -100..0` over a `byte` is an admitted `Range<int>` test exactly as `b > 300` is an admitted comparison; a pattern converts its scrutinee to nothing, so a constant or bound the scrutinee's type cannot hold is a compile error, `case b of 300` with `case b of > 300` — spec §5.1's posture that a constant is never silently truncated, and the rule the compiler enforces (#1156). Declined: typing a range from its low endpoint alone (the answer would depend on which endpoint is written first: `n..0` over `n: long` a `Range<long>`, `0..n` rejected); requiring identical endpoint types (every operator admits `int` beside `long`); fixing a literal's type by its endpoints at `in` (it rejects `n in 0..100` over a `long` while the pattern is admitted, against spec §16.6's agreement); a third common type by widening the endpoints' static types (`0..3000000000U` would be `long` at `in` and `uint` in arithmetic); comparing pattern constants mathematically or under promotion (integers compare at a common type everywhere else, and a dead arm is an error, not a warning); and an error for the equal-width constant tie `5U..10`, which spec §6.2 already decides for `5U + 10`.

**A pattern over a `char` reads `char` constants** (proposal 0204). Reading a pattern's constants at the scrutinee's type under spec §5.1 rejects `case c of 65..90` over a `char`, while a constant or relational pattern with an integer constant over a `char` would be spec §8.4's and §3.5's promoted comparison, `int(c) = 65`. One rule covers the three forms: over a `char` scrutinee every pattern constant is a `char`, and an integer constant is a compile error at the pattern. The expression side is untouched — `c = 65` and `c < 122` keep §3.5's promotion — the split being the one 0203 already drew between `b > 300` and `case b of > 300`. Declined: keeping the split, and admitting integer bounds over a `char` by value (no §5.1 conversion exists, a surrogate-valued bound would need an answer, and `'A'..'Z'` is the spelling). Reach: zero sites.

<a id="sec-failures-and-defects"></a>
### 11.12 Failures and defects: the kind is a marker interface on the class

(Proposal 0211.) The failure-signal rule (§11.4) separates a caller's bug from a failure consumed at a boundary. The difference is a fact about the exception's class — it implements `IFailure` or it does not (spec §31.4) — so that a handler can name it: the boundary arm names the marker, where an arm on `Exception` takes both.

**An interface, not a base class.** A base class `Failure: Exception` is the simpler thing to say and needs no rule at the catch arm. It was declined on three grounds. A class has one base, so a band drawn as a subtree cannot hold a class that must also stay under another: the out-of-range `Parse` input, which existing `OverflowException` arms take, and the fault a domain join raises, which existing `DomainFaultedException` arms take. Each would have had to leave its base, and the arms written against that base would have stopped matching. Second, moving a class between bands would be a change of base class, which spec Appendix E classifies as breaking for every consumer, where adding an interface touches only the arms that name it. Third, the precedent is against the subtree: Java drew its checked/unchecked line by base class and filed `NumberFormatException` — malformed input, the plainest failure there is — under the unchecked `IllegalArgumentException`, because its is-a parent was on the other side of the line. A kind that cuts across the is-a tree needs a mechanism that can.

**The cost of the interface, and the rule that pays it.** A binding of interface type exposes only the interface's members, so a boundary arm that bound the marker could not read `Message`, pass the exception to a logger, or chain it as a cause — the three things a boundary arm does. Declaring the reporting members on the marker fixes the first and not the other two. The rule taken is local to the catch arm and general: an interface in an arm selects, and the binding is the exception, typed at the most specific class the arm's class types determine (spec §31.2.1). It is the rule the multi-type alternative already had for classes, extended to the one case its text did not cover.

**A recorded difference from `case`.** [§11.3](#sec-catch-is-case-for-exceptions) says that `catch` arms mirror `case` arms, and in a `case` a type pattern's binding has the pattern's written type (spec §19.3), so `case e of var f: IFailure` binds an `IFailure`. A `catch` arm over the same pattern binds an `Exception`. The two already differed for the multi-type alternative, whose binding is the nearest common base rather than any written type, and for the same reason: a `case` scrutinee may be anything, a caught value is known to be an `Exception`, and a handler's work is done on the exception. Keeping the mirror exact was weighed; it leaves the marker arm unable to log or wrap, which is what the arm is for.

**Why the marker has no members, and who may carry it.** With the binding an `Exception`, a member on the marker would be a second way to reach what the binding already has. Conformance is restricted to `Exception`-derived classes on the ground §11.7 used for arm types: a record or a non-exception class that lists the marker is a shape no program can observe, and Bezel rejects dead shapes rather than carrying them. A second restriction keeps the documents' own classification true: a class under a built-in or standard-library class that is not a failure may not list the marker. Without it `class C: OperationCanceledException, IFailure` would make a failure arm take a cancellation, and a user's failure under `InvalidOperationException` would be taken by every state-defect arm — the swallowing stdlib §4.8 keeps `KeyNotFoundException` clear of. The library's two bridging classes are the only ones across that line, each recorded with its reason; a user hierarchy under a user base stays free to mix kinds, since no arm on a class of the documents is affected.

**Unmarked means defect.** A user exception class is a defect report until it lists the marker. The other default would let a boundary arm swallow whatever a library author forgot to classify, which is the failure this entry exists to remove; the cost of the chosen default is one interface name on each class meant to be handled.

**What the marker does not do.** It forces nothing: no `throws` clause, no obligation to handle (spec §31.3). It changes no ownership rule, and bugs still unwind and clean up (spec K3.15). Abandoning the process or the domain on a defect was weighed and declined: it would skip the cleanup the exception edge guarantees, and it would make a contract violation uncatchable, where spec §22.16 makes it a catchable exception and the test oracle and the supervisor both depend on catching one. It leaves the query/assert tiers alone: a missing key and an empty collection stay defects in their throwing forms, because the program owns the state each tests (stdlib §1, *Failures and defects*); the throwing `Parse` raises a failure, because the text is the outside's. The kind is the condition's, not the tier's.

**Reopening bars.** For the base class: evidence that the catch-arm binding rule misleads readers, together with a migration for the two bridging classes. For re-typing the throwing form of a state test (the `[key]` indexer, `First()`) as a failure: a declared failure surface on members (a `fails` clause), which would let the pairs be derived rather than hand-written.

<a id="sec-async"></a>
## 12. Async

<a id="sec-pattern-a-vs-pattern-b-is-type-directed"></a>
### 12.1 Pattern A vs Pattern B is type-directed

The interaction between async and ownership is the hardest design problem in the language. A class-typed task result must end up somewhere with defined disposal — but that somewhere is sometimes "the task itself, with multiple readers borrowing through it" (a fan-out cache, a future shared between subscribers) and sometimes "a fresh single owner" (a connection from `ConnectAsync`, a token from `AcquireAsync`).

The producer's return type carries the choice:

- `Task<T>` for class T means fan-out. Every awaiter borrows; the task must occupy an owning location — `using var` is the canonical form; plain owned locals, owned parameters, owned fields of `this`, owning-collection slots, admitted static task fields, and an unbound temporary whose borrowed result stays within one full expression also qualify (spec [§30.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-result-lifetime-and-task-disposal)).
- `OwningTask<T>` for class T means single-consumer. One awaiter takes ownership; the task is consumed by `using var x := await t;`. A second await through the same binding is a compile error where the first award dominates it, and raises `InvalidOperationException` where it is reachable only at run time (spec §30.4, §30.5).

The rule reduces to "whatever the type says." Producers commit in their signature; consumers' obligations follow from that signature. There is no syntactic peek at the await context. For an `async` lambda the "type" is, in order, its ascription, a closed target slot, or — with neither — its own body (spec §30.6): the third source is the one place the wrapper is inferred rather than committed in a signature, and it commits to the body's own ownership class — a fresh owned class result infers `OwningTask<C>`, which is why such a lambda is inapplicable at a `Task<R>` slot and applicable at an `OwningTask<C>` one, and why a `Task<R>` slot needs an ascription to take it.

**A second award through a named binding is rejected where the first dominates it ([proposal 0209](proposals/0209-second-award-of-owning-task-binding-rejected.md)).** The static half of the single-award rule is keyed on the award, not on the consume. Keyed on the consume, it would reach a plain owned local only, for a mechanical reason: the award consumes an owned operand (K3.5), so a second await is a moved-from read, while a `using var` cannot be moved out of (spec §28.3), so its award is a borrow and the second await would be left to the run-time `InvalidOperationException` (the #1785 reading, chosen as the one consistent with §28.3, not for a reason about the fault). The fault is as certain through a `using var`, a plain parameter or any other local as through an owned local: §30.4 spends the task at the first award whatever its outcome. The award keying is the layered reading §29.11 already gives the lazy query's enumeration right, spent at exactly one point, rejected where the flow analysis sees a second, raised at run time where it does not. The spent fact is a flow fact of the binding and is read with §7.4's apparatus: it holds where it holds on every path, the `try` model applies, and a reassignment, a `ref`/`out` pass or a mutable capture across the award clears it. Fields and slots are not carriers — another continuation can overwrite a field across the very suspension the first await is — and a loop's back edge is not dominance, so a second iteration's award of a task bound outside the loop stays the run-time fault. Measured reach: one guide example, two rule-corpus cases and three gtest rows, every one a demonstration of the exception. Declined: a mandated warning (a certain fault's end state is not a warning, rationale §5.28), tracking the fact through fields and slots (unsound across suspensions), and a K7.4-style back-edge rule for the loop shape (it rejects the sound first iteration).

The motivating case is `Semaphore.AcquireAsync(): OwningTask<SemaphoreToken>` — an operation whose entire purpose is to produce a fresh, single-consumer token. The consumer writes the canonical shape:

```bezel
using var token := await sem.AcquireAsync();
DoCriticalWork();
```

Producers who want both shapes declare two methods with distinct names — and the second is typically one line: a Pattern B await is an owned expression, so an async façade's `return await CoreOwnedAsync(…);` republishes the owned result through a Pattern A task's owned slot (the adapter shape worked in [§12.2](#sec-choosing-between-taskt-and-taskowned-t)).

<a id="sec-choosing-between-taskt-and-taskowned-t"></a>
### 12.2 Choosing between `Task<T>` and `OwningTask<T>`

*(Historical spelling: revisions v0.78–v0.96 wrote Pattern B as `Task<owned T>`; v0.97 replaced the `<owned T>` generic-argument mechanism with the named `Owning*` types (spelled `Owned*` until v0.168, §3.9) — see [§5.11](#sec-owning-collections) and spec §14.4. The semantics are unchanged.)*

The choice between Pattern A and Pattern B is the producer's — it goes in the method signature and determines what every caller must do. The decision tree below is from the producer's perspective.

**Step 1: Is the result type a value type, `string`, or runtime-managed type?**

If yes, neither pattern applies. Return `Task<T>` and stop here. Value types are copied out; `string` and function values are runtime-managed. No ownership crosses the await boundary.

```bezel
public async method CountAsync(): Task<int>          // value type — no pattern
public async method FetchNameAsync(): Task<string>   // string — no pattern
```

**Step 2: The result is a class type. Will more than one consumer legitimately read the *same result instance*?**

This is the fan-out question, and it is architectural, not incidental: a config cache shared by every caller (spec [§22.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-static-initialization-order) pattern (b)'s static task caches), a lookup result several subscribers await, a task stored once and awaited from multiple places (spec §30.4). Fan-out is announced in a design — it is rarely discovered late.

If yes → **Pattern A: `Task<T>`** — and say why at the declaration:

```bezel
/// Pattern A (fan-out): one fetch, every caller borrows the same Config.
public async method FetchConfigAsync(): Task<Config>
```

The same answer covers **chained republication** — an async body that re-exposes a longer-lived owner's result by chaining (`return await repo.LoadAsync(id);` — the source task's ownership transfers into the outer task, spec [§30.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-result-lifetime-and-task-disposal)'s chained slots) or that shares a fresh value through the task's owned slot. Not "the task borrowing from the repository": a task cannot hold a borrow it did not produce — §30.5/§28.3's return rules forbid returning a bare borrow through a task. Callers borrow *through the task*, whose teardown is the defined owner at every link.

**Step 3: Otherwise — the result is a fresh, single-consumer class value → Pattern B: `OwningTask<T>`.**

This is the default, and it covers more than classic resource acquisition. Connections, lock tokens, and file handles qualify, but so does ordinary fresh *data* the caller will hold: a parsed document, a materialized array, a response object. The consumer writes the form every caller writes unprompted:

```bezel
using var conn := await ConnectAsync(url);    // owned; disposed at scope end
var doc := await ParseAsync(input);           // owned local; affine discharge rules apply
```

**Summary**

| Question | Answer | Pattern | Return type |
|---|---|---|---|
| Result is value type / string / runtime-managed? | Yes | Neither | `Task<T>` |
| Several consumers read the same result instance (fan-out), or the body republishes a longer-lived owner's result (chaining)? | Yes | A — say why at the declaration | `Task<T>` |
| Fresh, single-consumer class result — including plain data the caller will hold? | Yes | **B (the default)** | `OwningTask<T>` |
| Still in doubt? | — | **B** | `OwningTask<T>` |

**When in doubt, choose Pattern B.** *(This default reversed earlier guidance to choose Pattern A; the changelog v0.204 entry carries the evidence.)* Four reasons, each a recorded fact of the current model:

1. *The failure mode is caught.* Under the affine model ([§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model)), an owned result the caller never discharges is auto-dropped at scope end with the must-use warning — forgetting to dispose is not a leak, it is a diagnostic. The argument for A as the conservative choice ("no one can accidentally leak") holds equally for B.
2. *A does not reduce the caller's obligations.* The caller owns the *task* in either pattern ([§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends)). A moves the discharge obligation from the value the caller wants onto the wrapper around it; B puts it on the value itself and lets the await discharge the wrapper.
3. *The consumer form is the natural one.* B's canonical call site is the one-line `using var x := await …;`. A's everyday site is one line too — the `var`/`const` bind of a Pattern A await of a fresh producer desugars to the hidden pinned-slot pair (spec [§30.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-scoped-await-bind), the scoped await-bind) — while naming the task, for multi-award or to hold it, remains the two-line form; so this reason separates the defaults less sharply than the others, and the pattern choice stays the producer's.
4. *B is recoverable; the recovery is one line.* A producer who chose B and later discovers a fan-out need does not narrow the existing signature (that would break owners); it publishes a sibling ([§12.1](#sec-pattern-a-vs-pattern-b-is-type-directed)'s "two methods with distinct names"), and the adapter is the shape §30.5 itself anticipates: a Pattern B await is an owned expression, and a Pattern A async body's `return` of an owned expression fills the task's owned slot.

```bezel
/// Pattern A façade over the Pattern B core, for subscribers.
public async method FetchSharedAsync(url: string): Task<Config>
  return await FetchOwnedAsync(url);   // B result (owned) fills the A task's owned slot
end FetchSharedAsync;
```

The reversibility asymmetry the opposite default rests on is real: A→B remains the mechanical-migration direction (widening `Task<T>` to `OwningTask<T>` breaks callers loudly and compilably — one-line binds keep compiling as owned binds; non-owned-consuming awaits and multi-await sites are rejected with the §30.5 diagnostics, and the fixes are local), and B→A remains breaking (callers wrote `using var x := await t;` and owned the result; under A that same line is rejected). What the default weighs is which one you *start* from: the reversibility argument buys an option — start at A, widen to B once ownership need is proven — that the recorded surface has never needed (no stdlib API started at A and widened; every recorded doubt-case went straight to B), and it pays for that option with the two-line consumer tax on every call site in the meantime, while the reverse risk has the one-line sibling recovery above, which does not touch the existing signature at all. Optimizing the everyday path over an unexercised option is the trade; it is made with the evidence attached, so it can be revisited if the evidence ever inverts.

**The question that settles it** is not "would forgetting to dispose leak?" (the affine model answers that for both patterns) but: *"can a second consumer legitimately need this same result instance?"* If yes, Pattern A — the task is the shared owner. If no — or if you are unsure — Pattern B: hand the caller the value, and let the ownership machinery do what it exists to do.

<a id="sec-no-configureawait"></a>
### 12.3 No `ConfigureAwait`

C#'s `ConfigureAwait(false)` exists because the default is to capture the synchronization context, and library code needs to opt out. Bezel's runtime has no synchronization contexts in this version, so no opt-out is needed. If UI-thread affinity is added later, this will need revisiting.

<a id="sec-cooperative-cancellation"></a>
### 12.4 Cooperative cancellation

Forcible cancellation (Thread.Abort) is unsafe — it can interrupt code holding a lock, corrupt invariants, or skip cleanup. Bezel uses cooperative cancellation: a `CancellationToken` is passed in and the operation checks it at safe points. This is C#'s model (and Go's, via `context`); Rust async reaches the same cooperative end differently — a future is cancelled by dropping it, which takes effect at a suspension point.

The ground reaches domains unchanged, and this is where an Erlang-style forcible teardown (`brutal_kill` on an execution domain) is declined. A domain is not a safer unit to interrupt than a thread; [§30.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-model)'s run-to-completion execution makes it less safe, because a forcible teardown lands wherever the domain's current continuation happens to be — inside a guard's critical section, between an owned value's production and its binding, with [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows)'s disposal obligations mid-discharge — and there is no unwind to run them. Ownership improves the normal and the exception paths, which is what it delivers; it leaves the asynchronous-interrupt path exactly as this entry describes it. The recovery need that motivates a kill primitive is real and is routed elsewhere: the ask-then-stop half to cross-domain cancellation through a shared source (stdlib §6.1), the take-away half to process-level supervision ([§12.14](#sec-recovery-unit)).

<a id="sec-execution-domains"></a>
### 12.5 Execution domains: why the async model is serial per domain

A domain is a unit of serial, run-to-completion execution: nothing else runs between two points of a continuation with no suspension between them. The ownership chapter depends on exactly that. The borrow checker's lifetime reasoning assumes it, and a runtime model that let another continuation — on another thread — dispose a borrow's source mid-use would make the chapter unsound in any program that exercised the freedom. It would also protect against the *smaller* hazard while permitting the larger one: §6.1 refuses to narrow nullable fields because a write can land between check and use, while a concurrent thread could *dispose* the object a borrow points into. The serial model converts the §28 soundness assumptions from accidents of an implementation into normative guarantees, and it cost existing programs nothing: no construct could observe genuine continuation parallelism portably, since any program doing so had data races whose behavior was already undefined. *(Historical note: through v0.130 the spec said async continuations "may resume on any thread-pool thread" and that shared mutable state "requires explicit synchronization," while §5.1 accepted that programmers can write data races; v0.131 resolved the contradiction in favor of the ownership model.)*

Three alternatives were considered and declined. *Send/Sync-style thread-safety typing* (Rust's approach) statically tracks which types may cross threads; it is the right tool for a language committed to shared-memory parallelism, but it infects every generic signature, roughly doubles the constraint vocabulary, and buys safety for a parallelism model Bezel does not actually offer — the cost lands on every user, the benefit on few. *Documenting the status quo* (keep "any thread, synchronize it yourself") was declined because it leaves the flagship feature unsound and outsources the hardest correctness problem to convention — the precise failure C++ shows daily. *Locks as discipline* (rely on `Mutex` use) was declined because nothing checks it; a forgotten lock is exactly as silent as the race it permits. The serial-domain model, by contrast, requires no new constructs, no new annotations, and no migration.

*Parallelism* is multiple domains communicating by ownership transfer over channels, with sendability derived from existing type properties (owned transfer for classes, the §9.7 borrow-bearing computation for value types) — language spec [§30.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-multiple-domains)–[§30.15](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-channels) (#600), reasoned in §12.6 below. Within one domain, I/O-bound concurrency, the dominant async workload, is interleaving at suspension points and is unaffected by serialization of continuations. `Mutex`, `Semaphore`, and `Atomic<T>` (§30.12) exist beside it: foreign threads can still enter through the FFI, and the types serve multi-domain programs.

<a id="sec-sendability-spawning-channels"></a>
### 12.6 Sendability, spawning, and channels

The parallelism half of the domain model: `Domain.Spawn` creates domains, `Channel<T>` moves data between them, and the **sendable** derived type property ([§3.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-derived-type-properties) instance, defined in [§30.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-sendable-types)) is the entire safety story. The design's one invariant — *no mutable state is reachable from two domains* — is what makes the single-domain guarantees survive parallelism unchanged: every crossing value is moved (the origin domain provably loses it, by the same machinery as `move` and owned parameters) or immutable (`string`, value copies), and the channel core is the single compiler-known synchronized exception. Data-race freedom is purchased with the ownership system the language already had, not with a new type system. The decisions, and what was declined:

**Sendable classes must be `sealed` (or `closed` over a fully-sendable closed hierarchy).** The subtyping hole forced this: sendability is checked on static types, but an owned field typed at a sendable open class could hold, at runtime, a subclass instance carrying a back-reference into the origin domain — a data race smuggled past the static check. Three closures of the hole were considered. *Sealing* (chosen): zero runtime cost, compile-time diagnosis, statically airtight — at the price of excluding open classes from crossing domains. *A runtime send-check* (walk the transferred ownership tree at the send site, verifying each concrete class is transfer-closed): sound and more permissive, declined for now because it hides a per-send cost proportional to the transferred graph and converts a compile-time error into a runtime one; it remains the natural widening if sealing proves too narrow, and admitting it later is non-breaking. *Subclass-must-be-sendable inheritance rules* (deriving from a sendable class may not break self-containment): declined because it inverts the dependency — a base author's coincidental sendability would constrain every subclass author, violating the principle that derived type properties are observations, not obligations.

**A generic declaration states the requirement as `where T: sendable`** (spec [§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-derived-property-constraints)'s *Derived-property constraints*; [proposal 0049](proposals/0049-runtime-const-and-deferred-adoptions.md) train B). A derived-property constraint is admitted individually by recorded ruling, nothing propagates unless written, and there is no negated form; `Domain.Spawn` and `Channel<T>` state the requirement in their signatures (stdlib §6.8, §6.9). §12.5's decline of inference-driven Send/Sync typing stands untouched: the constraint is opt-in per declaration and appears only where a signature already traffics in domain crossing.

**Spawn entries are capture-free; data enters through explicit arguments.** The alternative — *move-capturing lambdas* (a closure form that takes ownership of its captures) — was declined: §28.9's capture-is-borrow rule is a single uniform story for every closure in the language, and carving out a second capture mode for one call site would ripple into closure typing, disposal of captured-but-never-run closures, and the §28.3 escape analysis. An explicit `owned arg` parameter is semantically identical, visibly costed at the call site, and required no new machinery. Multiple values aggregate into a sendable class, an `OwningArray`, or flow through a channel.

**Faults cross as `DomainFaultedException`, not as the original exception object.** No exception class is sendable (spec §30.14 *Faults*: the built-in ones carry no sendability statement, and a user-declared one derives from a compiler-known base whose inherited state no section states transfer-compatible — *The compiler-known classes are closed to inheritance* below; exception classes are typically open and carry arbitrary state), and special-casing "exception objects may cross anyway" would reopen the invariant exactly where programs are least disciplined. Copying the message and stack-trace text (strings — sendable) preserves the diagnostic value, and the class the join raises carries the one fact a handler selects on — failure or not ([§11.12](#sec-failures-and-defects)) — with no exception class becoming sendable; programs needing structured fault data return it as a sendable result or send it over a channel. Declined: a sendability requirement on throwable types (intrusive, retroactive) and runtime graph-checking of exception objects (the runtime-check costs above, on the unhappy path).

**Channels are MPSC, with the `Channel<T>` object as the receiving end and `NewSender()` minting sender endpoints.** Declined alternatives: *a `Create` returning two endpoints via `out owned` parameters* — more ceremony at every creation site and a third type, for no expressiveness gain over minting senders on demand; *multi-consumer channels* (receiver cloning) — cloning a receiving end is shared ownership of the consume capability, #603's territory — deferred: `Shared<T>` exists, but a handle crosses domains only over a payload in spec [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership)'s `Shared`-payload synchronized set ([§5.21](#sec-shared-t)), and `Channel<T>` is not in it — its receiving end is single-consumer by ownership (stdlib [§6.9](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-channel)) — so a shared consume needs a design of its own (see the §18 entry). *Bounded channels* were kept apart for the reason they needed one — back-pressure wants an asynchronous `Send` that awaits capacity — and are provided as that separable design: a channel constructed with a capacity bound, whose `SendAsync` awaits capacity while the synchronous `Send` never blocks (spec [§30.15](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-channels)). The unbounded MPSC form covers the dominant fan-in/pipeline shapes, and the remaining deferral is an additive extension. A channel drains with `await for` over `ReceiveAll()` (spec §30.15, stdlib §6.9): the drain is affine (spec §29.11), completes normally at drained-and-closed, and deregisters the pending receive when abandoned; a `catch ChannelClosedException` loop stays legal.

**`CancellationToken` does not cross domains.** Not by a new rule — it falls out: the token is a borrow-bearing record, hence not sendable. The cross-domain primitive lives at the source rather than the token: `CancellationTokenSource` carries the synchronized fact, so `Shared<CancellationTokenSource>` is sendable and a cloned handle is the cross-domain cancellation currency, and a published source is signal-only — it carries no registrations, because a registered callback is a function value whose captures belong to one domain. Where no source is published, the answer is a dedicated channel carrying a cancel message. Stdlib §6.1 carries the ruling.

**The compiler-known classes are closed to inheritance** ([proposal 0138](proposals/0138-compiler-known-sendability.md); [#5613](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5613), [#5605](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5605)). The sealing rule above closes the subtyping hole for declared classes only. The stdlib states several classes sendable whose listings are not `sealed` (`Array<T>` and the other collections, `Mutex`, `Semaphore`, `Atomic<T>`, `Channel<T>`, `ChannelSender<T>`, `Counter<K>`, `OwningBuffer<T>`), `sealed` on a declared class seals only that class, and stdlib §1 keeps the transfer-closed rules, rule 1 included, off compiler-known classes — so without a further rule nothing would bar `class Evil: Mutex` with a non-`owned` class field, and `Domain.Spawn` at static type `Mutex` could carry an origin-domain borrow. Three closures were weighed. *Closing the compiler-known classes to inheritance unless their text opens them* (taken, spec §12.4): one rule for every statement, present and future, at no cost to any program — no test, document or guide derives from a library class other than the opened ones (`Encoding`, `Stream`, `TextReader`/`TextWriter`, the exception family), which are the abstract library classes, whose abstract members exist to be supplied, and the exception family; a concrete class's inherited virtual members (`FileStream`'s `ReadAsync`/`WriteAsync`) or its `Object` overrides do not open it, and `sealed` on a listing is informative. *Sealing implicitly only the classes a sendability statement names* (declined): it closes each statement one at a time, leaves every other library class open for no reason anyone has named, and ties an inheritance verdict to a sendability paragraph a reader of the listing never sees. *The runtime send-check* stays deferred on the grounds above. The mirror question — a declared class deriving from an opened base, `sealed class E: Exception` — is decided by rule 2's compiler-known-base clause: the base's inherited state is read from its section, not from fields the stdlib does not show, and it is transfer-compatible only where the section says so; no section says so, so such a class is not sendable. Declined for now: stating `Exception`'s inherited state transfer-compatible (its surface is strings and arrays of a string record) so that a sealed user exception with sendable fields could cross. A caught exception is a borrow (spec §31.1), so the only owned exception that could cross is one constructed and never thrown, and faults already cross as `DomainFaultedException`; the statement waits for a program that needs it, and it is one sentence in §31.4 when it comes. Neither recorded decline in this area moves: *subclass-must-be-sendable* declined a base's sendability obligating its subclasses, and the clause only lets a subclass's verdict read its base's state, as rule 2 already does for declared bases.

<a id="sec-memory-model-edges"></a>
### 12.7 The memory model is five edges plus atomics

The memory model writes down the happens-before edges the runtime needs: mutex/semaphore release→next-acquire, channel accept→deliver, spawn→entry and settlement→join, task settlement→awaiter (or blocking wait at the process edge), and static publication — the store into an admitted mutable static, and static-init completion, publishing the referent's memory to observing domains. That fifth edge is the one §22.12's untorn-pointer wording presumes, and the one that makes §22.13's cross-domain task cache — and §30.5's borrowed-source chain through it — sound on weakly ordered hardware rather than by x86 accident. Two object-specific guarantees are stated in place and cross-referenced rather than restated: CancellationToken's cross-thread flag visibility (§30.8) and Shared<T>'s drop→last-drop-destruction ordering (§28.13). The striking property is how little there is to say, and that is by construction, not omission: the sendable discipline means no mutable state is ever reachable from two domains, so a Bezel program cannot *write* the racy access whose semantics memory models spend most of their pages on. The only deliberate hole is memory shared with foreign threads outside the §30.12 primitives, which belongs to the platform. Atomics are sequentially consistent, with no acquire/release variants until profiles demand them.

Three neighbouring rulings sit with it. A faulted channel send disposes the item: a channel never retains what it did not accept (the TrySend give-back RFC remains the path to a lossless failure mode). A TCS/OwningTCS source owns its task (every §30.11 example assumes it; the v0.75 changelog entry ([`Bezel_Changelog.md`](Bezel_Changelog.md)) records the alternative's typecheck failure; disposal accounting splits by source kind so plain-TCS multi-await fan-out survives untouched). And a TaskScope live across a yield in an abandonable async iterator is a compile error, keyed on the same class-level fact as §28.12's no-destructor warning: it would otherwise be physically reclaimed with children running, the one silent hole in structured concurrency's quiescence guarantee. The degraded-destructor fallback is recorded but not taken.

**Warned value statics (#1393).** The ruling recorded at #1393 admits primitive-, enum-, and heap-free-inline-array-typed mutable statics with rule 4's warning rather than an error, because the category is the violation-tolerant one: no reference content means ignoring the warning risks stale (or, for the multi-word forms, torn) values, never memory unsafety — a strictly milder violation class than rule 4's own. (It answered a gap between rule 2's text, which made every non-synchronized mutable static an error, and a compiler that never enforced it for value types; the corpus that grew in the gap — the destructor-counter idiom, ~800 declarations in the compiler's own suite — is single-domain and correct.) The same ruling named the owning-inline-array static field as rule 2's form (iv) — the admission §22.13/§11.7 had made (the static slot-pool the exit-disposal pass exists to dispose, necessarily a mutable field since restoration is per-slot assignment), which rule 2's "exactly two forms" sentence had silently contradicted — warned like rule 4 because its owned instances are cross-domain reachable *and* slot-mutable, its violation class being full UB. `string` and record statics were declined, not deferred: a mutable string static's overwrite forces a choice between a reclamation race (UAF through the runtime's own refcounting when the obligation is violated) and an unbounded never-release leak, the same payload-scaled cost declined for §12.8's suppression-list types; and torn record reads fabricate invariant-violating values — a hazard heap-free inline arrays, bare element sequences with no §22.16 invariant surface, do not share, which is the recorded line between the two heap-free categories. Both remain errors with the stdlib's synchronized spellings (`AtomicInteger<T>`, `Mutex`, `Locked<T>`) as the remedies, liftable only by recorded ruling. Forms (iii)/(iv) deliberately get no memory-model publication edge: the obligation, not synchronization, is what makes them sound. (Form (iv) was retired at v0.334; [§12.15](#sec-statics-verdict-grounds).)

<a id="sec-locked-rwlocked"></a>
### 12.8 `Locked<T>`/`RwLocked<T>`: the lock owns the data

Real services share structured state — a session map, a cache, a registry — and without a lock that owns its data the options are the
actor idiom (principled, heavy for a cache) or a `static readonly` collection "guarded" by a *separate* static
`Mutex`, which §22.12 rule 4 rightly warns about: the mutex is not tied to the data, and convention is what §30.14
exists to replace with checking. The shape adopted (from Rust's `Mutex<T>`/`RwLock<T>`, not .NET/Java standalone
monitors): the lock owns its interior, access is only through a scoped guard, and the guard's `Value` is a borrow
whose source is the guard — so the borrow-escape rules the language already has enforce the critical section, with no
new lifetime machinery. One type lifts any wholly-owned interior; the alternative — enumerating `ConcurrentDictionary`,
`ConcurrentSet`, … — grows the stdlib N types for N shapes and still leaves user classes unshareable.

The interior admission reuses the sendable derived property (§30.14) rather than defining a near-twin: a
`Locked<T>` is by design reachable from every domain (statics; `Shared<Locked<T>>`), so a borrow inside `T` would let
a guard-holder chase a reference into another domain's heap outside any lock. "T is a sendable class type" is exactly
the wholly-owned-tree condition, already implemented, already diagnosed.

**The read-guard ruling (recorded at graduation).** `RwLocked<T>`'s concurrent read guards across domains are the
type's point — and Bezel has no read-only borrow, so a "reader" mutating through `Value` would be a silent
cross-domain race. The graduation ruling splits the hazard the way the static-event rule splits it: **stores are
checked where provenance is visible** — through a `ReadGuard`-sourced borrow, every assignment, `move`, and
`ref`/`out` pass is a compile error (expression-site, borrow-provenance-keyed, decidable, and body-local, because
borrow provenance does not cross call boundaries) — and the rest is a recorded programmer obligation: mutating
method calls through the read borrow (no const-method vocabulary exists, and one was declined — it is §12.5's
pervasive-constraint road), and callees reached with the read borrow as a plain argument (borrowed-parameter stores
are a core idiom no caller-side rule can reject). Declined: deferring `RwLocked` (cuts accepted scope and the
read-parallel complement of §12.9); pure residual trust (the flagship path would rest wholly on convention);
per-interior read facades (N types for N shapes again). The lift path, if evidence forces it, is a read-only
view/borrow form designed once with the frozen-view family (§12.9).

**Guard-kill and lock-kill strengthening (recorded at graduation).** §28.3's site-announced-kill carve-out is justified by
the killed object's own disposed-flag backstop; killing a guard sets no flag on the *interior* its surviving borrows
reference — while releasing the lock out from under them — so the carve-out's justification fails and the graduation
strengthens the freeze over guards (the §28.14 admission-freeze precedent): guard kills under live interior borrows,
and body-local lock kills under live guards, are compile errors. Lock destruction that no body-local rule can see —
the cross-domain last-`Shared`-handle drop while another domain holds a guard — takes the §28.2 posture, stated
normatively (checked-mode deterministic fault, unchecked UB) because the casualty here is user data mid-critical-
section, not a runtime lock word.

**Static membership, recast on the store-semantics ground (recorded deviation from 0003's letter).** Proposal 0003 predates the store semantics for admitted mutable statics (spec §22.12; [§12.15](#sec-statics-verdict-grounds), *Overwrite never disposes*); its "join the rule-2 set" spelling would have made a *mutable* static lock sound but overwrite-leaking — a displaced lock leaks its whole interior until exit, a payload-scaled cost §22.12's `Task?`-cache acceptance does not cover — and racing first-use stores could transiently fork a registry into two lock instances (last store wins): memory-sound, silently wrong. No use case demands lock-slot reassignment (the interior's mutability lives behind the guards), so the locks are in rule 4's warning-suppression set — making the `static owned readonly` registry, 0003's actual deliverable, warning-free and exit-disposed — and stay out of form-(i). The same recast as `FrozenArray<T>`'s; liftable by separate ruling if a real mutable-slot use case appears. The memberships are carried by the types' stdlib §1 fact grants — synchronized plus **displacement-unbounded** — and rule 4's set is the derived warning-suppression set ([§12.13](#sec-statics-facts-vocabulary)).

Also recorded: the DI answer. A container's singleton table is a process-global mutable map — the same state made
less visible, resolved at runtime, reflection-shaped (and Bezel has no member-access reflection, §18). Wiring is
constructor parameters from a composition root; when state must genuinely be ambient-and-mutable, a synchronized
static `Locked<T>` is the visible, compiler-checked spelling of what a DI singleton hides. Guard-across-`await` is a
lint, not a ban (a "no-suspend type" concept would be disproportionate); lock ordering stays out of scope (the actor
idiom is the recommendation when ordering gets complicated).

**No poisoning, and the detector that replaces it.** A `Locked<T>` whose guard holder throws releases the lock
by unwind and does not mark the lock poisoned. Rust's flag exists because a panicking holder can leave an interior
that nothing later re-examines; Bezel's interior is *"reachable only through a scoped guard"* (spec §30.12) and must be
a sendable class, so a half-updated interior is structurally unaliasable and observable only through the next guard —
and the next guard's public entry re-evaluates the class invariant, which spec §22.16 evaluates at construction
exit and at the public entry and exit points. That is a detector with content, where a poison flag carries only
"someone threw somewhere". Two limits are recorded rather than assumed: invariants are never evaluated on throw paths,
and checked-contracts mode is not the release default — so in a default build the detector is off, and a torn interior
is caught on the next checked run rather than in production, an asymmetry a program relying on invariant detection must
know it has. Memory safety is untouched either way: what poisoning would add over the invariant is a coarser signal
plus a second failure mode on every acquisition.

<a id="sec-data-parallelism"></a>
### 12.9 Data parallelism: MOVE, FREEZE, and the recorded fork-join design

Bezel had *parallelism* (domains, §12.5–§12.6) but no *data parallelism*: "run this function over this array on all
cores" measured ~60 lines of ceremony (worker channels, a context class per worker, `FireAndForget` supervision
loss). A user-side library cannot close the gap — function values are never sendable, and the capture-free call-site
check is compiler-known machinery — so the combinators are compiler-known, in the position `Domain.Spawn` already
occupies. The surface engages §12.5's Send/Sync decline without reopening it: no constraint vocabulary, no
