declared properties — only boundary checks at call sites, the accepted §12.6 pattern.

Two strategies shipped: **MOVE** (`Parallel.Map`/`Reduce`/`ForEach` — chunk copies or ownership partition; zero new
checking rules, one table extension of the spawn-position category) and **FREEZE** (`FrozenArray<T>` — the `string`
immutable-crossing precedent generalized to bulk data; `Shared<FrozenArray<T>>` sendable via a recorded §28.13
membership ruling, the set's first deep-immutability member). **Freeze-time aliasing is closed conditionally, not
absolutely** (recorded at graduation): the consume-site freeze — with span locals counting as
live borrows of their carried sources — and §9.7's span-escape bullets close every body-local route to a surviving
writer; borrows held in fields, collection slots, or pending async frames are invisible to any body-local rule, so
the domain clause (spec §28.3) extends to consumption into domain-reachable constructions (`FrozenArray.From`, the
`Shared`/`Locked`/`RwLocked` constructors, admitted static stores) — the same recorded-obligation architecture as
every §30.14 residual, and the deliberate alternative to inventing a whole-program alias analysis for one
constructor. Seed semantics for `Reduce` are per-worker and stated plainly (a non-identity seed is incorporated once
per worker) — the alternative, threading one seed through, serializes the reduction; hiding the semantics is how
PLINQ earned its documentation warts. Declined, with reasons that stay binding: a `parallel for` statement (its
safety needs per-index write-disjointness dependence analysis the spec could neither state simply nor check totally;
a runtime check hides per-iteration cost, UB is not in the vocabulary, and a "trust me" independence annotation is
exactly what §3.6 rule 1 forbids — and the library-first posture already governs synchronization, §30.12);
**move-capturing spawn bodies** (§12.6's one-capture-mode decline stands; explicit `owned` arguments are semantically
identical and visibly costed); user-generic parallel libraries (the first step onto §12.5's declined road; the
compiler-known table can widen later without breaking); a runtime send-check (declined in §12.6, undisturbed).

**The recorded fork-join extension (designed, not shipped).** In-place divide-and-conquer — quicksort over one
buffer, `Parallel.Join(lambda() => QSort(lo), lambda() => QSort(hi))` — needs the two banned capabilities at once: shared reads
of frame data without copies, and disjoint mutable writes into one allocation. The design is recorded here so its
review cycle starts from settled rulings; none of it is normative (no grammar, no §3.6 row, no
invariant change):

1. **Blocked-primary `Join`** (ruled): the parent *blocks*, which is both the soundness mechanism (a blocked
   continuation stalls its whole domain — §30.13 run-to-completion — so nothing domain-reachable mutates during the
   extent) and the liveness mechanism (the blocked parent lends its thread to the team; rayon's recruitment). The
   awaiting variant is recorded as weaker *and more novel*: it forfeits work-stealing and needs a frame-wide
   reachability admission with no existing shape to reuse (the TaskScope-sibling attack refutes the §28.14-reuse
   claim). The §30.3 dispensation it needs rests on an *enforced* pure-compute property: task/channel waits already
   fault inside sync closures, and a **fork-extent runtime guard** makes every §30.12 blocking acquisition
   raise `InvalidOperationException` inside a fork extent — the guard's enumeration must cover the whole blocking
   family as of the landing revision, including `Locked<T>`/`RwLocked<T>` (§12.8), reachable through statics by
   name where no capture check sees them.
2. **A `concurrently-readable` derived type property** (§3.6 form, checked only at fork positions): sendable value
   types and `string`; `FrozenArray<T>` and `Shared<FrozenArray<T>>`; admitted read-only views whose source is
   admitted. Locks, channels, and the task family are deliberately excluded — capturing a lock the blocked parent
   holds is a deadlock vector, not a sharing vector (Bezel's Sync-analog is narrower than Rust's, on purpose).
3. **Fork-position closure admission** (`par` marker — a §28.14-family contextual modifier, compiler-known
   declarations only): captures remain §28.9 borrows (no second capture mode — §12.6's decline holds); fork-closure
   bodies are **write-restricted** (stores through captured borrows rejected; only captured lend-span writes and
   closure-local state); captured sources are frozen for the extent (§28.14's freeze, trivially enforced by
   quiescence in the blocked form).
4. **Disjoint span lending**: `SplitAt` witnesses with **provenance-linear** admission (per-binding linearity is
   vacuous for copyable records — the admission traces captures back to a disjointness witness, each witness feeding
   at most one closure) and **opaque lending by type** (ruled): a distinct lend-span family with no `Buffer`
   projection and `Slice`-only narrowing, so buffer recovery is closed by ordinary name resolution — a helper over
   `Span<T>` cannot receive one. The interprocedural body-rule alternative was declined: it requires a
   fork-reachability classification of functions, a whole-program analysis class with no precedent here. One
   inherited residual is named, not absorbed: §22.12 rule-4 warned statics (and rule 2's form-(iii) warned statics; form (iv) was retired at v0.334) are reachable from fork closures exactly
   as from any two domains today — sharpened at graduation: fork closures are lexically the *confining* domain's
   code, so a program honoring rule 4's one-domain confinement obligation would newly race if its own fork
   closures touch the static from team domains; the future landing must extend the confinement obligation (or the
   fork admission) to fork extents explicitly.
5. **The invariant amendment, ratified in principle** and pinned for the landing revision: §30.14's flagship sentence
   becomes location-granular — *"no mutable memory location is ever accessible from two running domains, except that
   a `Join` extent may lend provably disjoint slot ranges of one allocation to distinct fork closures, under the
   fork-admission rules"* — because object-granular reachability is the wrong predicate for the construct
   (its flagship case lends one buffer's disjoint ranges to two running domains). It is deliberately not applied
   yet: nothing normative violates the current sentence, and flagship prose should never be
   weaker than the shipped language requires. A memory-model clause (fork-extent fork/join happens-before edges)
   lands with it.

The `.AsParallel()` endnote stays recorded: the §29.11 affine-query ruling removes PLINQ's two classic hazards
structurally (re-enumeration is already an error; consumption transfers the whole plan), but operator callbacks are
storing signatures and chunking needs an indexable source — high-cost machinery for wins the explicit combinators
already deliver. Evidence first.

*(FREEZE's recorded pressure.)* FREEZE reaches exactly one shape — the buffer. The likely first user
pressure on that boundary is the **frozen class tree** (a build-once object graph shared read-only across domains:
configurations, parsed documents, routing tables, compiled schemas); the pressure record, and the two design
questions a proposal must start from, live at stdlib
[§4.19](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-frozen-array) (proposal 0102 §6).

<a id="sec-calls-mint-storage-lends"></a>
### 12.10 Task ownership at the call site: calls mint, storage lends

A call whose declared, pre-substitution return type is a `Task`-family type produces an owned task with no marker, and since proposal 0096 (v0.258) so does a call whose declared return type is an iterable-family type; a read of storage (a property, field, indexer or local) lends a borrow. An `owned` marker on every such signature was declined because the declared return type already carries the bit the marker would add, and the iterable family's first exemption, which rested on the stored-view idiom, was reversed once the fences it needed were counted. The section closes with how a task disposed before it settles still discharges what its frame delivers and how a faulted task retains the task whose fault it carries, and a historical note on those fences.

[§12.1](#sec-pattern-a-vs-pattern-b-is-type-directed) and §12.2 presume that the caller of `FetchConfigAsync()` owns the returned task. The rule that says so is keyed on the construct rather than on a marker (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-producers), #1786): a call whose declared — pre-substitution — return type is a `Task`-family type produces an owned task, with no marker and no factory warning, whatever the callee kind; a read of storage — a property, field, indexer, or local — remains a borrow of that storage. Calls mint, storage lends: a call is the moment a frame comes into being (or is forwarded by someone who owned it), and storage is the place a task someone else already owns is shared from. Without the rule the producers table would classify a non-`owned` return from an instance method as a borrow of the receiver, so every unmarked instance, interface, or function-value task call would mint a state-machine frame that nobody owned, while §30's entire disposal story (`using var task := client.GetAsync(url);`; both decision trees above) requires the opposite. Nor could a rule key on `async`: it is an implementation-side modifier that no signature or interface exposes (spec [§28.12](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-asynchronous-disposal)). The per-callee-kind matrix this doctrine motivates — declared return kind × callee kind, every cell cited — is spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-announcement-table)'s announcement table.

The declined alternative was the marker: require `owned` on every fresh-task-returning signature. The blast radius alone was disqualifying — every async signature in every program, every task-returning function type, both decision trees above — but the deeper defect is what the *unmarked* spelling would then mean, because a marker whose omission compiles must leave the unmarked form meaning something. As a borrow it is a standing leak-by-omission trap, worst through interfaces, where a caller cannot see whether the implementation behind a signature is async; as a hard error it forces the marker onto every task signature — [§5.5](#sec-local-variable-ownership-is-inferred-not-annotated)'s "redundant noise" verdict, transplanted to the language's most common async surface. The declared return type already carries exactly the bit the marker would add; this is the announcement-by-declared-type doctrine the iterator convention had established, applied to the type family whose calls produce frames by construction. Also declined: post-substitution keying (`First<T>()` at `T = Task<int>` would mint a disposal obligation over a collection-owned element — the decline [§5.7](#sec-factory-method-ownership)'s bare-type-parameter ruling reaffirms). Extending calls-mint uniformly to all deferred-activation kinds was declined at first too — "unsound for iterables, where the stored-collection view idiom (`method Items(): IEnumerable<Item> => _items;`) is legitimate and common", the asymmetry "recorded here so the iterable analogue is not later 'fixed' by copying this ruling blindly" — and is now the rule for the iterable family as well, on the evidence *The reversal* below records.

The rule is the §30.11 precedent made general. For one type, a `TaskCompletionSource` owns its task and consumers reach it through the `.Task` property ([§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges) above; spec [§30.11](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-taskcompletionsourcet-and-ownedtaskcompletionsourcet)) — the source owns, storage lends. The rule draws that line everywhere, callee side included: an unmarked `Task`-family return type is itself container-ownership-transferring, so a non-`async` body must supply an owned task on every return path, and a *stored* task is re-exposed through a property, field, or collection slot — never through a non-`owned` method return. Costs, recorded: the stored-task *method* accessor is inexpressible (the storage-accessor shape belongs to properties — the same property-face resolution as the static idioms of §13.6); and the `owned Task` markers on `Domain.Spawn` and the `Parallel` combinators are emphasis rather than contrast — recorded deliberately against the earlier note that the two spellings were "deliberate, not to be silently harmonized": this is the non-silent harmonization that note demanded.

**The reversal: calls mint for the iterable family too, and A2 retires (proposal 0096; ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation; A2 retired v0.258).** The v0.190 decline above — "unsound for iterables, where the stored-collection view idiom is legitimate and common" — was a migration-cost argument about one idiom, not a soundness argument, and its own closing sentence set the reopening bar: the iterable analogue was "not later 'fixed' by copying this ruling blindly". What reopened it is not a better argument about `Items()`; it is the **fence-accretion ledger** that warning implicitly demanded a future reviser produce — the itemized record of what the split cost after v0.190: the three v0.223 fence rulings (#1787/#1802/#1820), §27.1.1's announcement-bearing family boundary, §27.6's formation bar with its normatively-recorded open question, two self-admitted leak residuals ("a leak until that issue classifies or fences it"), and #3246 as the fourth fence for one seam — at v0.256 roughly 1,900 words of new fence text across five carriers, every entry announcement-coherence machinery whose whole content is *"this family announces, that one does not"*, none of it needed by the `Task` family, which mints. The v0.190 record weighed the stored-view migration; it could not have weighed the fences, because they did not exist yet. **The closure record's central sentence is confronted, not deleted:** "classifying the slot is impossible in either direction — a mint classification double-disposes stored views, a lend classification leaks minted frames" was true, but the impossibility is conditional on the stored-view spelling: a mint classification double-disposes only while a lend-face *method* inhabitant exists, and the property respell removes that inhabitant — `Items(): IEnumerable<Item> => _items;` becomes `public property Items: IEnumerable<Item> => _items;`, the identical remedy v0.190 applied to the stored task, at the cost v0.190's record already called acceptable. After the respell every iterable-returning *method* face mints, every stored view lives on the accessor face whose lend-only guarantee is axiom A4's, and the classification §12.10 recorded as impossible is simply the rule; A2's row is a register tombstone. The unification also retires the v0.223 owning-head declaration-site rejection and its `Drain()`/`ReceiveAll()` §29.11 carve — the carve's ground never fit its occupant (`Drain()` is no deferred LINQ operator; §28.3 filed it under two different grounds two paragraphs apart; §29.11's closed consumption list could not even parse the spec's own `return _items.Drain();`; and the prescribed `owned`-return remedy was unsound at the one member the carve protected, §14.4's `OwningCache` returning the receiver's owned field) — the two members becoming ordinary minting members, at one real, stated cost: `Drain()` must return a genuinely fresh drain-view frame rather than the receiver at the owning static type (stdlib §4.5's receiver-identity optimization retired — the `for` desugaring's `using var __source` disposes the minted frame at loop end, and the frame must not *be* the collection). The v0.256 fence and its conservatism — which had reached the standard library's own query surface, rejecting six §5 members at iterable-head substitutions — are subsumed and refunded: the mint-laundering inhabitants are rejected inhabitant-side (§27.1.1/§27.6 read against declared, pre-substitution slots; a minting lambda at its return path), and the sequence-of-sequences projection over the property face is legal again. Re-declined, each with its ground: the marker (v0.190's own declined alternative transplanted — it would churn every static iterable declaration and stdlib operator to buy an announcement the declared type already carries, and reinstate the leak wherever omitted); the narrow owning-head repair (it needed a drain-through-borrow predicate v0.223 correctly refused to invent); and stopping at the fence (the option the ledger indicts — a fifth fence would have followed the fourth). Kernel accounting: the unified rule is a pure K6.2 + K6.3 derivation; no axiom replaces A2.

**An unsettled task's disposal attaches the frame's discharge; a faulted task retains the task whose fault it carries (proposal 0168).** Spec §30.1 lets a frame run on after its task is disposed, and `OwningTask<T>` has a stated discharger for what the frame delivers afterwards — §30.5's result-disposing continuation; without one for the whole family, a `Task<C>` disposed under the named `using var` drop that §30.10 itself names as legal would leave its kind-1 result, its late-chained source and its fault instance with a teardown that had already run ([#5788](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5788)). And with a faulted task "owning its fault exception" (§30.9) while an awaited operand's disposal waits "until the exception's handling completes" — an event that never comes inside a frame whose body lets the instance escape — `await Inner()` over a faulting `Inner` would have two owners of one instance and no disposal moment for the temporary ([#5787](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5787)). Each gap is closed by a mechanism the text already had, made the rule: every `Task`-family disposal before settlement attaches `OwningTask<T>`'s continuation, which performs at the terminal state every disposal the teardown would have performed; and a task faulted by an instance another task owns retains that task, as the owning `WhenAll` aggregate retains its first non-successful input, the capture ending the frame's handling. Declined: forbidding disposal of an unsettled task (it rejects the unsupervised drop: seven rule-corpus cases, three Guide 04 examples and about fifteen test programs); stating the discharge frame-side (two statements of one fact, and nothing for a late chain's source); moving ownership to the faulting task (two awaiters of `Inner` would lose their instance); re-raising a fresh cause-chained instance (exception identity would change across an `await`).

*Historical note* (the fences, v0.223–v0.256). While the task/iterable split stood, its seams were closed by *announcement coherence* — no value may be viewed through a slot whose type-announced classification contradicts its own. Three 2026-07-24 rulings (v0.223): #1802 excluded calls-mint substitutions from bare-parameter slots (per-instantiation); #1820 fenced the retyping/formation and lambda-body routes (a property reference over a `Task`-family property a formation error, a minting lambda under a concrete slot rejected, return-slot bridging respecting §27.1.1's family boundary); and #1787 announced the fresh-frame-vs-stored-view distinction of instance and extension iterable-returning callees with the `owned` marker, the owning heads taking no unmarked instance spelling (the #1660 declaration-site reject). Copying calls-mint to iterables was declined again then, the marker being the information-bearing bit for iterables as it was *not* for tasks; post-substitution keying and the capture-set-transitive-source alternative were re-declined (wrong axis — the channel is container ownership, not carried sources). The one route left open, a static iterable factory's formed value reaching a lend-classified slot through a bare-parameter substitution or a retyped intermediate, was fenced at v0.256 ([#3246](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3246); the 2026-08-18 back-of-book elegance review's highest-value finding): the calls-mint exclusion mirrored onto the iterable-interface family at bare-parameter function slots, with §27.1.1's family-only covariance for iterable-interface returns. Its closure record held that "classifying the slot is impossible in either direction — a mint classification double-disposes stored views, a lend classification leaks minted frames; that impossibility is why A2 exists"; its cost was the `Task` fence's conservatism one step wider, rejecting sound instance-callee inhabitants with the minting factories. The fence was the minimal soundness closure and right under either outcome, which is why it landed first; the unification above subsumed it and every other fence.

<a id="sec-determinism-stated-hashes-assigned"></a>
### 12.11 Determinism is stated, and identity hashes are assigned

Spec §30.13's *Determinism* subsection adds no rule — every clause of it was already a consequence of serial-per-domain execution, the five memory-model edges, sendability, and the no-object-address invariant. It is stated anyway, for two reasons. First, as an audit obligation on the implementation: once "a domain's execution is a deterministic function of what its edges deliver" is normative, any within-domain nondeterminism given fixed edges — an uninitialized read, an unpinned iteration order, an address leak into an observable value — is a runtime *bug by definition*, not implementation latitude; the paragraph converts a diffuse design intention into a checkable property. Second, as the foundation record/replay tooling needs: a recorded execution is replayable exactly because everything a domain observes arrives through an enumerable set of runtime-mediated edges (proposal 0045 is the tooling design; the spec paragraph is what makes it *possible* rather than heroic). The exclusions are exactly two — elapsed real time, and foreign memory beyond declared writebacks — because those are the two places the runtime genuinely does not mediate.

Two supporting rulings go with it. **Identity hashes are assigned, not derived** (spec §8.3): the .NET lazy-syncblock design with the entropy source replaced by a per-domain deterministic sequence. Without it the determinism statement is false for any program that hashes a class by identity — an address-derived hash is a per-run accident — and the address itself would leak into observable values, which §32.12's no-object-address invariant exists to prevent. Objects never hashed pay nothing; the storage is the runtime's per-object metadata word or a side table (implementation detail); programs are promised only within-run stability and address-independence, so no existing program's meaning changes. **`OwningDictionary<K, V>` iteration is deterministic-given-history, not order-pinned** (stdlib §4.9): pinning insertion order outright would force order metadata into the drain-and-rebuild representation whose economics §4.9 already chose; determinism given the operation-and-hash history is everything replay needs and is what the reference implementation already does. The borrowing containers (`Dictionary`, `Set`) were already insertion-order-pinned, so no frame-by-frame journaling of container iteration is ever needed.


<a id="sec-choosing-the-concurrency-shape"></a>
### 12.12 Choosing the concurrency shape: structured by default

The mechanisms are settled individually — `TaskScope` (stdlib §6.10), `Task.WhenAll` (borrowing and owning overloads, §6.7), `WhenAny`, `Task.FireAndForget`, bare task binds, and `Domain.Spawn` (§30.14) — but the map between them was reconstructible only from scattered passages. The doctrine, in one place:

**Structured is the default.** Spec §30.10 states the posture: concurrent fan-out reaches for the nursery — `await using var scope := new TaskScope();` — and the two discard idioms are the named escape hatches, not peers. The nursery's guarantee is lifetime-shaped: children cannot outlive the scope, and the scope's disposal is the join.

**The fault fork is the real chooser.** `WhenAll` *collects*: it awaits every task and re-raises the first non-success — it never cancels or abandons the laggards (§30.10's aggregation contract). `TaskScope` *supervises*: a child's fault cancels the siblings through the scope token, and `Join` surfaces the failure after quiescence. So: results-of-independent-work → `WhenAll` (all work is wanted regardless of failures); a unit of work whose parts stand or fall together → `TaskScope`. `WhenAny` is a selector, not a supervisor — the losers keep running and remain the caller's to settle. `FireAndForget` is the recorded abandon-with-supervision spelling for work whose result nobody will read (§17.13 routes the `Task` discard here precisely so abandonment is always a visible decision). `Domain.Spawn` is not a concurrency combinator at all — it is an isolation boundary (§12.5), chosen for state separation, with sendability doing the checking.

**The nursery rule is stated by holder** (proposal 0145). A rule stated by binding form (`await using` only) is checkable only at the static type `TaskScope`: a scope reaching any other owning position — an `owned` field or parameter, an owning element, a `Shared<T>` payload, an owning position of `Object` or an interface through an upcast — would discharge on §28.12's implicit synchronous path and skip the drain with children running, voiding the quiescence §28.14 states normatively for the type. So the rule is stated by holder: a `TaskScope` is minted only as an `await using var` initializer at its own type and owned by that binding alone, with borrows free — no implicit discharge runs on a borrow and §28.6 rejects `Dispose()` through one, so no borrow reaches a synchronous disposal path. *A bare conversion ban* (`TaskScope` admitting no §5.1 reference conversion) was declined: it reaches only the upcast face, leaves the own-type holders open, and rejects harmless borrows such as a `Helper(d: IAsyncDisposable)` call. *A per-instantiation check at type arguments* (admitting `List<TaskScope>`, rejecting only owning substitutions) was declined: it would re-run the holder rule through every generic body after substitution, including a new reject-at-substitution clause for `owned T` parameters and returns, which §28.1 erases rather than rejects at substitutions it does not admit, and a check on `new T()`, which `where T: new()` admits at `TaskScope`; the whole-argument ban is §14.2's existing shape (a dispatched generic method admits no move-only value type as a type argument, explicit or inferred), and no borrowing use of a `TaskScope` type argument exists to admit. *The degraded synchronous destructor* (cancel plus detached drain, the #1422 shape) stays where stdlib §6.10 left it — the fallback only if the static rule proves too restrictive, which the holder rule, with zero rejected sites measured across the tests, the documents and the rule corpus, did not.

Why this is a rationale entry rather than new normative text: every sentence above restates a rule the spec already carries; what was missing was the adjacency. (The streaming as-completed combinator is `Task.WhenEach` — stdlib §6.7, graduated from proposal 0023: it *reports* completions in order without supervising or settling anything, the observer complement to `TaskScope`.)

<a id="sec-statics-facts-vocabulary"></a>
### 12.13 The statics-and-sharing taxonomy: facts, not lists

(Proposal 0087, [#3053](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3053); the 2026-08-11 elegance review, finding 6.) Statics and cross-domain sharing are governed by four orthogonal per-type facts — **synchronized**, **immutable**, **self-distributing**, **displacement-unbounded** — declared once at each stdlib type (stdlib §1), from which spec §22.12 rule 2's form-(i) set, rule 4's suppression list, and §28.13's `Shared`-payload set are derived by one-line formulas; the "grown only by recorded ruling" discipline attaches to the facts, and the sets are computed views. Three curated lists maintained in parallel and related by prose set arithmetic ("this set is form-(i) *minus* … *minus* … *plus* the three members") were the alternative and the earlier presentation: each *membership* was a recorded ruling, but the three-list structure itself was defended nowhere, and a normative document stating set differences between its own allowlists is a taxonomy that has outgrown its presentation. The facts reproduced the lists exactly — all thirty memberships (ten types × three sets) — so the move was doc-structural with zero behavior delta. The review's conjecture that *three* facts would suffice was refuted by construction: `Locked<T>`/`RwLocked<T>` are internally synchronized yet excluded from mutable statics for a recorded fourth reason (rule 2's overwrite-never-disposes semantics would strand a payload-scaled displaced value), so the fourth fact is what keeps the derivation exact — without it the data-owning locks would silently join form (i), a behavior change. Declined alternatives: the status quo (checker taxonomy leaking into the user's mental model — one `static` field could meet a hard error, two co-firing warnings, or silence depending on which of three lists its type sat in, with nothing at the type saying which); deriving only §28.13 (most of the cost, half the benefit); a user-declarable trait (the facts are compiler-known *trust grants* about specific implementations — the axiom-A10 family's shape — not self-certifiable structural properties; opening them to user types is a real language design, named so it is not smuggled in as notation); and keeping the lists normative with facts in tooling only (two normative statements of one truth is exactly the drift class `check_docs.sh` exists to catch — instead the facts are the single normative carrier, and a mechanical check recomputes the spec's informative membership parentheticals from the fact lines). The governance clause is the facts': one fact ruling may flow into two sets — that is the feature — so the record requirement is the fact ruling's (it states which facts it grants and confronts each derived-set consequence), and the lists' old vice-versa protection is carried structurally by the two facts that ever made the sets diverge.

**Sendability takes the same home** (proposal 0136 S2-8, owner-accepted). The compiler-known types' sendability classifications live only in each type's stdlib Sendability statement (stdlib §1), and §30.14 states the four forms a statement takes; a per-type prose list in §30.14 beside the stdlib's statements would be the two-carrier shape this section retires for the statics sets. As for the statics sets, the move reproduced the prior classifications with no verdict change. (Where the two documents had disagreed — `OwningBuffer<T>` at a value element type — the stdlib statement took §30.14's verdict, not sendable, and the question was filed; *The copy-state ground* below settles it.) Sendability is not a fifth fact: it is not a boolean property of the type (three of the forms are conditional on an element or payload type), and it is independent of the four facts (`CancellationTokenSource` is synchronized and not sendable; `Channel<T>` declares no fact and is sendable), so no formula over the facts reproduces it. Declined: keeping §30.14's list normative with the stdlib statements pointing to it (two normative statements of one truth, the drift class this section names), and a fifth fact (it would need a conditional fact, and would misstate `Channel<T>`'s recorded fact-less line).

**The unstated default** ([#5593](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5593)). A compiler-known class type whose section states no sendability is not sendable (stdlib §1). This is the facts' own closure ("a type declaring no facts is in none of the derived sets") applied to sendability. A statement is a claim about a type's internal structure, which only the type's section can make, so a type nobody has classified does not cross. Declined: reading spec §30.14's transfer-closed rules over a compiler-known class. Those rules read instance fields, and the stdlib is not normative for a type's internal data structures, so the reading would classify by text that is not the contract: a reference implementation's fields where a section shows one, and nothing where it does not. It would have decided one type only, `Type`, then the one unstated `sealed` library class, making it sendable because no fields are shown rather than because anyone had checked its state; `Type`'s verdict is its own (*The copy-state ground* below). Also declined: a sendable default, which would let a type cross domains before anyone had checked its structure. A type whose structure argues for sending is given a statement in its own section (`Counter<K>` at sendable-value or `string` keys, the first).

**The copy-state ground, `OwningBuffer<T>` at value elements, `Type`, and the comparer-held clause** ([proposal 0138](proposals/0138-compiler-known-sendability.md); [#5614](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5614), [#5578](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5578), [#5615](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5615), [#5604](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5604)). With the subclass seam closed (§12.6, *The compiler-known classes are closed to inheritance*), the sendable form has a fourth ground, *a holder of copy state only*: every member reads and writes the state by value, retains no borrow past its call, and exposes no address of the storage — the issue's wording, kept whole. It is stated by member behaviour, not by storage layout, because stdlib §1 is not normative for internal data structures, and it is what already makes `Array<char>` sendable under the conditional form. `StringBuilder`, `Random` and `Stopwatch` take statements on it. `OwningBuffer<T>` at a sendable value element is the same shape — wholly owned copy state that crosses by move, as `Array<double>` already does — and is sendable; the one thing it has that the three do not, an interior address from `DangerousPointer()`, is an FFI-band value that no borrow tracks whether or not the buffer moves, so spec §32.10's cross-domain bullet covers an address of Bezel-owned storage whose owner has crossed, beside the crossed `Ptr<T>` it already covered. `OwningBuffer<T>` is classified by the conditional form, not by the ground, so the ground keeps its no-address condition and no statement made on it ever reaches §32.10. Declined: dropping that condition from the ground (it would let a future copy-state statement export an untracked address with only §32.10's trust behind it, for no type that needs it); keeping `OwningBuffer<T>` at value elements not sendable (no structural ground distinguishes it from `Array<double>`, and the stdlib had called it sendable). **`Type`** is sendable, crossing as a shared reference exactly as `string` does: it is the one §28.9 runtime-managed type the `string` bullet of §30.14 did not name, its one interned instance is immutable and already reachable from every domain through `typeof`, and `owned Type` is inadmissible, which is why no statement form of the compiler-known classes could carry it — spec §30.14 classifies it directly, as it classifies `string` and the value types, and stdlib §2.13 restates the verdict; the borrowing collections' element condition reads `sendable-value, string or Type` wherever it read `sendable-value or string`. Declined: keeping `Type` not sendable (a domain re-deriving a type token with `typeof` is a workaround for a value that is already shared), and a fifth statement form for shared-reference crossing (the forms classify the stdlib's own classes; the runtime-managed types are the spec's). Not widened: `FrozenArray<T>`'s element restriction, which is its own and has no use at `Type`. **The comparer.** A hash collection built with an `IEqualityComparer<K>` retains a borrow of it (rationale §17.14: injection relocates the container's identity whole), and its sendability statement classifies the type however it was constructed, so a comparer-built `Dictionary<string, int>` could carry an origin-domain borrow across. The statement stays, and the construction carries spec §28.3's domain clause as an individually recorded trust clause, the comparer-held clause (K4.3's *recorded trust, or nothing*), named by the trust-admission report at the constructor call. Declined: a statement that holds only for the comparerless constructors (construction is not in the type, so it is not a type-level rule); requiring a capture-free or stateless comparer (a stateless comparer is still an object its origin domain disposes, so the cross-domain borrow dangles whatever its state). Deferred: a comparer type parameter (`Dictionary<K, V, C>`, or a constructor constrained `where C: IEqualityComparer<K>, sendable`) as a static bound — an API redesign across four types, taken only when a program needs a comparer-built collection to cross; an `owned IEqualityComparer<K>` parameter is not that bound, since an interface-typed field is never transfer-compatible.

**`Type` carries the immutable and self-distributing facts** (0138 §13's filed question; delegated ruling 2026-10-08). The facts, not a listing beside `string` in spec §22.12 rule 3, because the fact line is the one carrier of a library class's statics treatment and a facts ruling has to confront each derived set, which a rule 3 listing would skip while rule 4 still read `Type` as "class type". Immutable is the ground the sendability statement already rests on (one interned, never-written instance per type); self-distributing records that `typeof` is the type's own distribution channel, which keeps it out of §28.13's `Shared`-payload set — where it could not go in any case, `owned Type` being inadmissible. Displacement-unbounded is not granted, and synchronized is not claimed: a mutable `static` of `Type` stays rejected by rule 2's closing sentence, as every class type outside form (i) is. It is not given an admitted form of its own. Such a form would be sound — a pointer-word store of a never-reclaimed instance has none of §12.7's grounds against it, neither the reclamation race nor the leak nor a torn read — but no program needs one: `typeof(X)` is written wherever the slot would be read, a `static readonly` `Type` is warning-free, and a slot that must be rebound after initialization is a field of a class held in the `static owned readonly` `Locked<T>` registry idiom (rule 4(a)). A new mutable form would need store semantics of its own under rule 2 for a slot no program has asked for; it is declined (`DECLINED.md`), reopenable by a program none of those spellings serves. The one verdict it moved is rule 4's: a `static readonly` `Type` field or auto-property is warning-free, where it had taken the class-type row's warning. Zero such declarations exist in the tree, and `typeof(X)` can be written wherever such a static would be read, so the ruling is small; it is recorded because the derived sets are machine-checked and an unrecorded member is a gate failure, not a judgement call.

*Historical note* (*three types held at the default*, [#5606](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5606)). Between v0.325 and v0.327 `StringBuilder`, `Random` and `Stopwatch` carried no sendability statement and were not sendable, though their structure argued for sending. No sendable form of spec §30.14 named a copy-state ground, and adding one was a design change of the same shape as the `OwningBuffer<T>` question ([#5578](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5578)); and the three listings were not `sealed`, while the library classes already stated sendable (`Array<T>`, `Mutex`, `Channel<T>`, `Counter<K>`) shared an open subclass seam ([#5613](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5613)) that three more statements would have widened. A statement on a copy-state ground was deferred until the seam was ruled ([#5614](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5614)), and `Type`, listed `sealed` but inadmissible as `owned Type` (spec §28.9), was a separate question ([#5615](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5615)). §12.6's closure of the seam lifted the deferral (above).

<a id="sec-recovery-unit"></a>
### 12.14 The recovery unit: the error kernel, and process supervision

(Proposal 0106 §3.3/§7.5.3.) Bezel has strong fault containment and, by design, no fault recovery machinery; this entry states what the language is therefore promising and what it is not, because the absence is otherwise only inferable from the shape of what exists.

**The error-kernel doctrine.** Push fallible work outward; keep irreplaceable state in a unit that must not fail. The sendable rules already push programs this way — a domain's state is unreachable from outside it (§12.5–§12.6), an entry is capture-free (spec §30.14), and everything crossing is moved or immutable — so the doctrine names a shape the rules already enforce rather than adding a mechanism. Two consequences worth stating: a unit holding state that cannot be rebuilt should do as little fallible work as possible, and the fallible work belongs in a unit whose loss costs only its own progress. Read against §12.12's map, the actor idiom's serialized domain is a placement decision, not a default: state entangled enough to want serializing is often exactly the state whose loss is unrecoverable, and putting the risky work in the same unit is the arrangement the doctrine warns about.

**The recovery-unit fork, and where Bezel stands.** Recovery admits two answers, and a language must say which it is building. **(1) In-process supervision** — a supervisor owns child specifications, observes each child's exit, and restarts from the specification, as OTP does. Bezel has the hard precondition for it already and for free (spec §30.14's capture-free entry rule means a restarted child provably inherits nothing from the dead one), and lacks every other part: nothing multiplexes child exits, no restart policy vocabulary exists, and a supervisor parameterised over a *stored* entry value is not writable in user Bezel, the entry being a call-site syntactic category (the precedent is stated for `Parallel`, stdlib §6.12). **(2) The OS process as the recovery unit** — the supervisor is outside the program, and the program's job is to fail fast, cleanly, and legibly. Bezel's posture is (2), and the fail-fast band is built for it: allocation failure and stack exhaustion are fatal and uncatchable (spec §31.4), an uncaught exception exits with code 70 after printing the exception (spec §33.3), and the spec already tells programs whose correctness depends on shutdown effects to arrange an external mechanism — *"a separate process holding the lock with a TTL, a write-ahead log, a watchdog"* (spec §22.13). A Bezel service is expected to be run under something that restarts it.

**What this settles and what it leaves open.** It settles the claim: the language does not promise in-process restart, and a program that needs a component rebuilt after a fault gets that from the process supervisor, not from a language mechanism. It does not decline (1) — a supervisor built on stdlib pieces stays a legitimate future proposal, and its cost drops once the pieces behind it exist (a multiplexable completion set; a cross-domain stop signal, which the `Shared<CancellationTokenSource>` ruling of §12.6 supplies). What the posture changes is the target: with process supervision named as the recovery unit, an in-process supervisor is asked to cover only what an OS supervisor covers badly — sub-process-granularity restarts of work whose loss the process should survive — rather than to reproduce OTP. Forcible teardown is not one of the missing pieces; it is declined at [§12.4](#sec-cooperative-cancellation).

<a id="sec-statics-verdict-grounds"></a>
### 12.15 Static members: the grounds behind §22.12's verdicts

(Proposal 0122 L14, [#3845](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3845).) Spec §22.12 states a static field or auto-property's verdict — *ok*, *warn*, or *error*, by mutability against the declared type's kind — in two tables, with the admitted mutable forms (i)–(iii) and the bare-generic-parameter rejection (iv), the store-semantics triple, and rule 4's notes (a)–(i) beneath them. The *grounds* for those verdicts are recorded here rather than inline in the section, keyed by the section's own labels, so that the section states what each rule is and this record states why. Three neighbouring records already carry part of the story and are cited, not repeated: [§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges)'s *Warned value statics (#1393)* paragraph (why forms (iii) and (iv) are admitted warned rather than rejected, why `string` and record statics were declined, and why neither warned form takes a memory-model publication edge), [§12.13](#sec-statics-facts-vocabulary) (why membership in the form-(i) set and the warning-suppression set is derived from per-type facts rather than curated), and [§12.8](#sec-locked-rwlocked) (why the data-owning locks and `FrozenArray<T>` sit in rule 4's set and not in form (i)). Nothing here is normative: the verdict is the spec's, and a ground that stops holding is reopened by ruling, not by re-reading this record.

The grounds fall in three groups: the admitted mutable forms (i)–(iii) and the retired mutable owning-inline-array static ([§12.15.1](#sec-statics-grounds-admitted-forms)); the store semantics every admitted static shares — overwrite never disposes, `static owned` storage is disposed only at exit, and a static store admits no borrow but static storage's ([§12.15.2](#sec-statics-grounds-store-semantics)); and rule 4's notes with rule 5 ([§12.15.3](#sec-statics-grounds-rule-4-and-5)). Several rest on one fact: a static is reachable from every domain and observable across method boundaries, so neither a disposed flag read in one domain nor a per-method window can backstop it.

<a id="sec-statics-grounds-admitted-forms"></a>
#### 12.15.1 The admitted mutable forms and the retired owning-inline-array static

**Form (i) excludes `Channel<T>`.** The channel object is the *receiving* end (spec §30.15), single-consumer by ownership, and a process-global receiving end would readmit the multi-consumer receive that stdlib §6.9 explicitly defers. The spelling the spec steers to — a domain-confined `Channel<T>` with a `ChannelSender<T>` static published in its place — keeps the single consumer where the type puts it.

**Form (ii) excludes `OwningTask<T>?`.** The admitted shape is §22.13 pattern (b)'s `null`-initialized, once-set cache of an asynchronous computation; `OwningTask<T>?` is single-await (spec §30.5), useless as a shared cache, and a displaced owned task would strand its result.

**Form (iii) is warned, not rejected, and its category is closed.** Why the form is warned rather than rejected is [§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges)'s *Warned value statics* paragraph: the category is the violation-tolerant one — no reference content, so ignoring the warning risks stale or torn values and never memory unsafety, a strictly milder violation class than rule 4's undefined behavior. The multi-word forms that paragraph names without enumerating are `decimal`, the nullable forms' flag-and-value pairs, and per-slot mixes of an inline array's elements. The category — primitives, enums, and plain heap-free inline arrays — grows only by recorded ruling, and the shapes outside it were declined, not deferred: mutable statics of `string` (and `string?`), record, choice, and compiler-known value-builtin types (`DateTime`, `Guid`, …), plain inline arrays of any other element type, and inline-array-typed auto-properties. The `string` and record grounds — the overwrite dilemma between a reclamation race and an unbounded leak, and the torn read that fabricates an invariant-violating value — and the stdlib's synchronized spellings as the remedies are §12.7's too; the citations behind them are the displacement-unbounded fact (stdlib §1) for the leak's payload-scaled cost, spec §22.16 for the record invariant, and stdlib §6.4 (record-typed atomics want a `Mutex`; today, `Locked<T>` over a sealed cell class, spec §30.12) for the remedies. A plain inline array of any other element type takes the same grounds component-wise: a `string`- or record-element array imports its element category's dilemma, and a class- or interface-element plain array carries *borrows* (spec §15.1.2) whose cross-domain dangling is memory-unsafe.

**The mutable owning-inline-array static is retired ([proposal 0162](proposals/0162-explicit-disposal-marks-the-holder-frees.md), [#5785](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5785), [#5782](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5782)).** A mutable static field of owning-inline-array type is rejected under rule 2's closing sentence, with every other mutable static that would hold an owned referent. Admitting it — as the warned form (iv) it once was, its slot stores, `move arr[j]` and per-slot restoration "following §15.1.1 unchanged" — meets K9.4's ground (static state is observable across method boundaries, so no per-method window can cover it), which rejects these for a slot exactly as for a whole field, a tension four lanes of the disposal matrix found independently; a slot store into it would have no stated verdict between §15.1.1's per-slot drop and §22.13's static no-drop posture; and a destructor run by the exit pass could store into a slot the pass had already disposed, leaving an object no drain re-enrolled and no flag faulted. Its only uses were the form's own tests. The `static readonly` form, note (h), stays as the one static owning inline array: disposed at exit, written once, its slots fixed — restoration is per-slot assignment ([§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges)'s ground for why the retired form had to be mutable), so a `readonly` array admits no slot store and therefore no `move`. A replaceable process-global pool is a `static owned readonly OwningArray<T>`, whose indexer store disposes the displaced element (spec §28.11), or the `Locked<T>` registry §22.12 names. Declined: keeping the form with `move arr[j]` barred (closes neither the slot-store nor the re-population question for the same reach); keeping it with a stated per-slot drop and an exit drain over re-populated slots (a third overwrite regime and a second drain rule for a form with no use outside its tests); and retiring note (h) with it (it holds only what it was initialized with, and is used).

<a id="sec-statics-grounds-store-semantics"></a>
#### 12.15.2 Store semantics: overwrite, exit disposal, borrows

**Overwrite never disposes.** A racing first use may still hold and await the displaced task, so overwriting an admitted mutable static cannot take spec §28.7's drop-on-overwrite; the displaced referent stays alive until process exit instead — an accepted bounded cost of the same kind as spec §22.13's ownership-discharge exemption, bounded because displacement can occur only during first-use races. The same ground holds at the owning-inline-array form: a racing use in another domain may still hold a displaced slot's value, so statics never take the per-slot drop-on-overwrite that owning-inline-array locals and instance fields take.

**`static owned` storage is disposed only at exit ([proposal 0162](proposals/0162-explicit-disposal-marks-the-holder-frees.md), [#5760](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5760)).** Static owned storage admits no `move`, no slot store and no explicit disposal, K9.4's ground stated once for all three. Every static rule assumes it: K2 classifies static storage as the one always-live source, disposed only at exit; the producers' static rows and §28.3's *Static stores* bullet admit static-storage-sourced borrows into any field or static with no residual because "the always-live holder holds only what is always live" (*Static stores admit no borrow but static storage's* below); K9.4 bars `move` of the same field because static state is observable across method boundaries. An early disposal would falsify all three, and across domains the flag is no backstop (K4.3's third clause), so the borrows stored under the always-live claim would carry no clause. The receiver this excludes — a `static owned` field read by its declaring type as an owned receiver of explicit `Dispose()`, by the declaring-type privacy symmetry that admits an instance field — rested on a consistency argument, not a use case. Measured reach: zero explicit disposals of a `static owned` name in every corpus. Early shutdown of a global is spelled by a `Close()`/`Flush()` method on its type, a `Locked<T>` registry whose guarded value is replaced, or a `Shared<T>` handle. Declined: keeping the receiver and recording the race (a trust clause needs a backstop, and across domains there is none); admitting it per type for types with a defined cross-domain post-disposal fault (a per-type carve for zero sites; §30.12's detection covers live guards, not a later acquisition).

**Static stores admit no borrow but static storage's ([proposal 0151](proposals/0151-static-stores-admit-only-static-sources.md), [#5676](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5676)).** A static store is not judged by the field-store bullet, whose residual admits a parameter- or receiver-sourced borrow under K4.1's backstop. That backstop is a disposed flag read by a later use in the same domain; a static is reachable from every domain, so the source's owner disposes in one domain while another reads the static, and neither the flag nor the domain clause — which names a transfer or a consume of the source, and here there is none — orders the race. Such an admission would also contradict the always-live claim every static read rests on: spec §28.3's *Static field access* producers row and its callee-side list's item 2 say every admissible static form keeps its referents alive to process exit, which a stored borrow falsifies. The rule is now what that claim needs — a fresh value (discharged), `null`, or static storage. Declined: the status quo; a recorded K4.3 static-held clause (a trust clause needs a backstop, and across domains there is none — K4.3's own third clause); rejecting the static-sourced borrow too (it cannot dangle: process-lived storage never disposes a displaced referent, spec §22.13); and a warning (the hazard is a cross-domain use-after-free with no deterministic fault — rule 4's violation class, not form (iii)'s). Reach: no program in `tests/`, the documents or the rule corpus stores a non-static-sourced borrow into a static.

<a id="sec-statics-grounds-rule-4-and-5"></a>
#### 12.15.3 Rule 4's notes and rule 5

**Rule 4(a): the displacement-unbounded four are not mutable statics.** Rule 2's overwrite-never-disposes store semantics would strand a displaced value of unbounded payload until process exit — the payload-scaled cost each type's fact line names, and the cost [§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges) records as declined for the suppression-list types. §12.8 records the recast that placed the locks and `FrozenArray<T>` in rule 4's set rather than form (i).

**Rule 4(e): a function-typed static warns at form (iii)'s hazard class, and does not discriminate captures.** The captured-variable cell is reachable and writable from every domain through the field, with no other declaration at which to name the obligation. The hazard class is form (iii)'s rather than rule 4's because a capture whose source is a local scope cannot reach a static at all — spec §28.9's and §28.3's borrow-escape rules reject a function value that outlives a captured borrow's source — so only runtime-managed and value captures survive there, and what a violated obligation risks is therefore a wrong or torn value, never memory unsafety. Capture-free function values are not distinguished from capturing ones because the initializer's value may come from a call whose captures the declaration cannot see, and no rule of the specification consults another declaration's body to check a declaration.

**Rule 4(g): an owning-classified choice is rejected outright.** An `owned` payload is process-lived with no disposal site even when every borrow-carrying component is `null`, which is why note (f)'s null-source admission cannot reach it; admitted, the form would be a process-lived owned value with no disposal site and no diagnostic, since the program-exit pass enrols only `static owned` fields and owning-inline-array-typed static fields and the `owned` field modifier is inadmissible on a choice-typed field. Rejecting the `readonly` form makes it agree with rule 2's closing sentence, which already rejects the mutable one.

**A borrowed Pattern B operand is spent, not disposed; no static is declared at `OwningTask<T>` ([proposal 0171](proposals/0171-borrowed-owning-task-award-spends.md), [#5834](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5834)).** The await of a *borrowed* `OwningTask<T>` operand — a `using var`, a plain parameter, a field read, a slot, static storage — spends the task and disposes nothing: the holder disposes the task as it disposes any task it holds, finding no result and disposing the fault instance then, which is what the compiler does for a bound operand (it marks the task spent and leaves it to its binder). Pattern B's single award is the spent state §30.4 names, a property of the result buffer, not of the task's life, so no kill is needed, and an implicit disposal at the await — a K4.2 site-announced kill through a borrow — would collide twice: §30.9's retention paragraph promises that a fault escaping the awaiting body stays owned by the operand task, retained by borrow and re-raised to every awaiter as "the same live instance", while the await's terminal act would dispose that operand and its instance at the capture; and K9.4 and §28.6 make static owned storage disposable by the exit pass alone, while the await would dispose a `static owned readonly OwningTask<T>` in place. So a `using var` operand's scope-end disposal is the task's one disposal, not the idempotent no-op after the await's that #1785's reading made it, and in §30.11's source-kind accounting the `OwningTaskCompletionSource<T>`'s own disposal of its task is the task's single disposal. The consume table's K3.5 row, the owned-operand kill, is unchanged. With no soundness defect left, the static face is narrowed anyway, on the owner's standing preference for narrowing a feature of negligible reach over carrying a rule: a `static readonly OwningTask<T>` is awarded once in the whole process, by whichever domain's await arrives first, every other awaiter raising — form (ii)'s own ground for excluding the nullable mutable form, now applied to the `readonly` column as note (j), with the bare-parameter substitution rejected at the instantiation as (g)'s is. Measured reach: no static of `OwningTask<T>` type in `tests/`, the documents or the rule corpus; no program's verdict moves. Declined: deferring the borrowed-operand await's in-place disposal to the faulted task's teardown (keeps a kill through a borrow and patches one of its two collisions); rejecting a borrowed Pattern B await in an `async` body unless every fault is handled; a compile error at an await whose operand is static owned storage; and recording the await as K9.4's one in-place disposal of static storage (single-domain under rule 4).

**Rule 5: the guarantees are carried by the type.** The capture-free admission at every subscribe position declared on `ISharedEvent<T>`/`SharedEventSource<T>` is the enforceable statement of a guarantee whose member-kinded form was unenforceable through an `IEvent<T>` alias — resolved by construction; [§13.6](#sec-retiring-the-event-member-kind) records the retirement that made the type the carrier.

<a id="sec-events"></a>
## 13. Events

<a id="sec-raise-instead-of-invoke"></a>
### 13.1 `Raise` instead of `?.Invoke`

The C# idiom `event?.Invoke(this, args)` carries two operators of boilerplate. The `?.` exists because an empty event is a null delegate. The `Invoke` exists because delegates need explicit invocation. Forgetting the `?.` raises `NullReferenceException` for an empty event.

Bezel uses `event.Raise(payload)` (sync) and `await event.RaiseAsync(payload)` (async). Three properties:

- **No null check.** Zero subscribers is just zero subscribers; `Raise` is a no-op. Whether the implementation backs the event with a null sentinel is invisible at the call site.
- **No `Invoke` indirection.** The verb `Raise` means "call all subscribed handlers." That replaces both `?.` and `Invoke`.
- **Symmetric with `Subscribe`.** The public face — the pattern's property — has type `IEvent<T>` (only `Subscribe`); the producer face — the private field — is `EventSource<T>` (both `Subscribe` and `Raise`). The asymmetry — outside callers can't `Raise`, inside callers can — is enforced by the static types of two declarations, not by a special access rule.

Direct invocation `event(args)` was considered. It's cleaner at the call site but conflicts with the asymmetric inside/outside types — "why is `event(args)` legal here but not there?" is harder to explain than "the type changes."

<a id="sec-payload-types-not-handler-types"></a>
### 13.2 Payload types, not handler types

An event names a payload type: the type argument `T` of `EventSource<T>` is the payload, and `Raise(payload: T)` is a well-typed generic method that means exactly what it says. The handler type `(T) -> ()` is derived mechanically from the payload type and from whether the source is sync or async.

Keying the event on its handler function type instead — `(Button, ClickEventArgs) -> ()` — makes the generic parameter — `TArgs` in `EventSource<TArgs>` — mean "the handler function type". That is a type-system incoherence: `Subscribe(handler: TArgs)` is correct (the handler *is* a function), but `Raise(args: TArgs)` would take a function, not the event's data, and the call site needs pseudo-tuple syntax with no foundation in the type system. *(Historical note: events were declared that way at v0.53; the payload form replaced it at v0.54.)*

Three options were considered:

1. **Compiler-synthesized `Raise`.** Keep the function-type declaration; the compiler generates a per-event `Raise` whose parameters mirror the handler's. This would be the first compiler-generated method not expressible in the generic type — a crack in the "no hidden magic" principle.
2. **Payload types (chosen).** Events name a payload record. `Raise(payload: T)` is a normal generic method. The handler type is derived.
3. **Tuple types.** Add language-level tuples to make the handler's parameter list a first-class type. Tuples are a large feature commitment for a single use case.

Option 2 wins because it requires no new language concepts, keeps `EventSource<T>` fully expressible as a generic class, and aligns with the existing recommendation (stdlib [§6.5](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-events-ievent-eventsource), "Payload records") that event arguments be records. The cost — one record declaration per event — is modest and produces better-documented APIs than multi-parameter handlers.

The subscribe-side trade-off is that handlers take a single payload parameter instead of multiple: `lambda(e) => e.X` instead of `lambda(btn, args) => args.X`. Lambda-parameter destructuring recovers the positional form where it reads better (`lambda(ClickedEvent(_, x, y, _)) => ...`, spec [§27.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-parameter-type-inference), *Lambda-parameter record destructuring*): the head names the parameter's exact record type, the bindings are ordinary positional-field reads (borrows for class-typed fields, spec §28.3), and refutable elements are excluded, since a parameter list is not a `case`. The single-parameter form is clear enough on its own.

**No async payload that holds borrows (proposal [0154](proposals/0154-no-async-event-payload-that-holds-borrows.md); [#5711](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5711)).** `AsyncEventSource<T>.RaiseAsync` holds its payload in the raise frame until the returned task settles — the one standard-library position with an `async` method's retention and no `async` marker (spec §30.1). `AsyncEventSource<T>` therefore takes the rule `Task<T>` takes (stdlib §6.5; proposal 0150): a type-level rejection of a value type that holds borrows, `CancellationToken` excepted, grounded on the frame outliving the synchronous call (K2.1 + K6.1) and on spec §9.7's posture that a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position — a stipulation of regime uniformity, not a missing backstop, so the rule holds with proposal 0153's flag-checked span reads. A rule derived from §30.1's conditional one was tried first and refuted at the gate, which is why the restriction waited for a stated rule. It costs `await _ev.RaiseAsync(span)` awaited in the raising scope, which is sound but which no measured program writes and which buys nothing: a handler cannot pass such a payload to an `async` callee, store it or retain it, so it works synchronously anyway, and a class payload — the buffer object, or a class holding it with an offset and a length — expresses the same program under the settlement clause with its flag. Declined: the status quo (a recorded residual, with no backstop for a span); a flow rule at the abandonment shapes only (a new provenance class for one member's result, admitting a type no handler can use asynchronously); a K4.3 trust clause resting on the flag-checked span read (0150's declined option B at a second member, kept declined on the same posture); and the same restriction at `IAsyncEvent<T>` and `EventSource<T>`, which retain nothing. The move-only leg of the deferred restriction stays deferred, and retiring this rule with the other storage bans on a value that holds borrows rides proposal 0153's deferred option E.

<a id="sec-method-reference-subscriptions"></a>
### 13.3 Method-reference subscriptions

Once payload records are the convention, method-reference subscription becomes the natural idiom for handlers that have a name worth keeping:

```bezel
public method UpdateStatus(e: ClickedEvent)
  // ...
end UpdateStatus;

_clicked += UpdateStatus;           // method reference at the pattern's source field; no lambda wrapping needed
```

The method's signature `(ClickedEvent) -> ()` matches the handler type derived from the event's payload. The compiler resolves the `+=` form to `_clicked.Add(UpdateStatus)` via the desugaring described in spec [§17.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-notes-on-operators). Lambdas remain available for ad-hoc handlers; named methods are preferred when the handler has a stable identity that supports later `-=` removal.

<a id="sec-events-on-interfaces"></a>
### 13.4 Events on interfaces

An interface declares an event as a get-only property of type `IEvent<T>` (sync) or `IAsyncEvent<T>` (async): the public, subscribe-only surface of an event, which is exactly what a contract should expose. The implementing class satisfies it with the property the event pattern already declares (stdlib §6.5), whose getter returns the class's private `EventSource<T>` — `EventSource<T>` implements `IEvent<T>`. No event-specific mechanism is involved: the existing types compose to give interface events for free, with no parens, declaratively read-only.

The motivating use cases are conventional: `INotifyPropertyChanged` and analogous observable contracts, UI component abstractions where multiple concrete types share an event-bearing interface, and plugin extension points where the host subscribes to events declared by the plugin contract. Without interface events, each of these patterns would need either an abstract base class (which consumes the single-inheritance slot) or an awkward `GetEventX(): IEvent<X>` method. C# has supported interface events since C# 1.0 (2002); the precedent is well-established.

*(Historical note: interfaces once declared events with their own member form, the `IfaceEvent` production, satisfied automatically by a class's generated backing field. That form retired with the `event` member kind — §13.6 — and the property shape serves the same use cases; the cost is that the implementer writes the property.)*

<a id="sec-handler-exception-policy-first-error-wins-not-aggregate"></a>
### 13.5 Handler exception policy: first error wins, not aggregate

When a synchronous event handler throws, subsequent handlers in the subscription list are not invoked, and the exception propagates out of `Raise` to the raiser. The same rule applies to async event handlers: if one handler's task faults or is cancelled, subsequent handlers are not invoked and the exception propagates from the awaited `RaiseAsync`. This is "first error wins": the publisher sees one exception and that's the end of the dispatch.

The alternative — running every handler regardless of failures and aggregating the exceptions — was considered. C# went this route for `Task.WhenAll` (which produces an `AggregateException`) and some event-like patterns. Bezel chose first-wins for three reasons.

The first is that aggregation introduces a special exception type that callers must handle differently. `AggregateException` in C# has a complex API (`Flatten`, `InnerExceptions`, `Handle`) and exists primarily because async-with-many-tasks produces multi-failure shapes that ordinary stack-walking exceptions cannot represent. Events are a different shape: a publisher fires once, multiple handlers respond, and the publisher usually does not care about all of them — it cares that *something* failed. Promoting "something failed" into a multi-exception object asks every event consumer to know about an aggregation type.

The second is that handlers that need to run independently can opt in trivially:

```bezel
using var sub := button.Clicked.Subscribe(async lambda(e) => do
  try
    await ProcessAsync(e);
  catch
    var ex: Exception => Log(ex)        // swallow per-handler failures
  end;
end);
```

The handler catches its own exceptions, returns a successful task, and `Raise`/`RaiseAsync` continues to the next subscriber. Each handler decides whether its failure should poison the rest of the dispatch. This puts the policy where the failure semantics are known — in the handler, which knows whether its work is best-effort or critical — rather than in the publisher, which usually doesn't.

The third is that aggregation across handler boundaries can mask bugs. If a handler throws because it has a real defect (a null-deref, a state-machine violation), the publisher silently aggregating and continuing means the bug surfaces later, in a different code path, with the original stack trace already obscured by the aggregation wrapper. First-wins keeps the original exception visible at the point it was thrown.

The cost is real: a publisher that wants every handler to run regardless of failures must implement that policy itself, typically by catching inside each handler at subscribe time. That's the trade we're making — a small amount of subscriber-side ceremony for a simpler default and clearer failure semantics.

The published policy is normative (stdlib [§6.5](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-events-ievent-eventsource), "Handler exceptions"). Future revisions could add an opt-in aggregating raise method (e.g. `RaiseAggregated(payload: T)`) without breaking existing code, but the default would not change.

<a id="sec-retiring-the-event-member-kind"></a>
### 13.6 Retiring the `event` member kind (proposal 0035)

An event is the two-declaration pattern of stdlib §6.5 — `private owned readonly _clicked: EventSource<ClickedEvent> := new();` plus `public property Clicked: IEvent<ClickedEvent> => _clicked;` — and stdlib §6.5 is the normative home of event semantics. There is no `event` member kind (proposal 0035, accepted 2026-07-12; #1550). It existed through v0.185: `public event Clicked: ClickedEvent;` was a one-line declaration that generated a hidden `private owned EventSource<ClickedEvent>` backing field, with the language's only access-site-dependent member-type rule (former spec §24.3 — `IEvent<T>` outside the declaring class, `EventSource<T>` inside). Subscriber-side call sites were unchanged by the retirement.

**Why.** Everything observable about events was already library semantics on the stdlib §6.5 types; the member kind bought one saved line and one saved name, and paid with a carve-out family: two grammar productions (`EventDecl`, `IfaceEvent`), a placement rule (classes/interfaces only), the two-faced typing rule, spec §28.14 form 4's honorary-field clause for the generated backing member, spec §22.12 rule 5's member-kind keying (whose guarantee was unenforceable through an `IEvent<T>` alias — #1647), and its own modifier table. The two-line pattern is the floor for a public event under Bezel's own commitments — declaration-fixed member types, the type fence, no runtime type tests on the producer path — so no in-language shorthand could beat it by more than the one line.

**Alternatives declined** (0035 §5.1.2/§6): expansion sugar (option (a) — retains the member kind and a minted visible name to close a one-line gap) and its bare-name variant (rejected in advance); C3 field-type inference (a new inference form); C4 fused field+property declaration (a new declaration form); C5 primary-constructor capture (wrong lifetime story); C6 public source field (loses the fence); C7 a raiser/source split for instance events (two names, no gain); C8 a `Publisher` base class (consumes the single-inheritance slot); C9 rename-only; C10 an interface-typed field with downcast raise (a runtime type test on the producer path). Deleting the `+=`/`-=` desugaring was separately declined: it would close #1618 by deletion (record-receiver method-reference equality) but churn every producer-side subscription site; the desugaring survives, re-homed to spec §17.2 and keyed on the three source types, with its `readonly` interaction stated for the first time (the desugaring is recognized before `readonly`-assignability checking — previously unstatable, since events rejected `readonly` and the generated field had no name). #1618 stays open as a real bug.

**Statics (S1).** `static event` is replaced by the sealed pair `ISharedEvent<out T>`/`SharedEventSource<T>` — internally synchronized, capture-free at every subscribe position. The capture-freedom is a stdlib-stated entry in spec §28.14's capture-free register, keyed on the resolved callee, made alias-proof by two companion rules: the pair implements neither `IEvent<T>` (an upcast would launder capture admission through `IEvent<T>.Subscribe`'s capture-admitting `scoped` signature — the severed composition is a recorded cost, bridged by a channel relay, stdlib §6.5), and method-reference formation over any capture-free-register position is rejected (the formed plain function type would erase the admission; the bar covers `Domain.Spawn` too, closing the same latent formation hole there). This makes the rule-5 guarantee enforceable for the first time; #1647 closes by construction. User classes cannot be cross-domain publication points in v1 (a user `Subscribe` is retention-incapable under the call-bounded regime; the lift path is §28.14's deferred unbounded tier, #1427). S2 (regressing the guarantee) and S3 (silently synchronizing instance events — reversing v0.163's recorded instance-events-unsynchronized choice) were declined. The shared async twin is deferred until demand appears; until it ships a cross-domain async publication point is not spellable (`static async event`, grammatical before the retirement and unused in every document, has no direct replacement — the migration note says so honestly; the channel bridge is the alternative).

**Small rules that land with the retirement.** (1) A bare read of a `static` `owned` field returned through a non-`owned` return is a borrow of process-lived static storage (spec §28.3/§28.6) — the callee-side rule the static idiom's public property needed, beside the caller-side producers-table row. The admission covers exactly that property face (#1804): a static-*method* arm admitted alongside it at first never had a caller-side row, so a concrete static method call returning storage was factory-classified at every call site, pairing two owners over one obligation; the arm is retired, the pattern's property face plus the caller-side `T.P` row being the complete sound surface. (2) Spec §2.3's always-available list gains all six event types (Q7, spelling (i)) — a recorded reversal of the first draft's "declinable knob" posture: the keyword's import exemption died with the keyword, and requiring `import System.Threading` to declare any event would fight the declaration-cleanliness the retirement exists to serve; relocating the types to `System` (the `Subscription` precedent) stays recorded as the equally-principled alternative. (3) `EventSource<T>`/`AsyncEventSource<T>` gain public parameterless constructors (#1646 tracks the compiler; sequenced first in the implementation roadmap — the pattern is the graduation's front door). (4) `event` moves from Appendix B.1 to B.3 as the language's one reserved-for-future identifier: pre-retirement code fails with a reserved-word diagnostic rather than parsing `event` as an identifier, and a one-line declaration form stays re-introducible without a breaking change (un-reserving is a separately declinable follow-up).

**Recorded costs and drops.** One extra line and one extra name per public event; producer sites read `_clicked` rather than `Clicked`; interface auto-satisfaction is gone (the implementer writes the property — see the §13.4 postscript above); a §28.14 form-4 narrowing for off-idiom discharges (the pattern's property is a getter hop, so an external capture-bearing subscription discharged neither by `using var` nor into an `owned` field of `this` is now rejected — correct-by-default, never unsound; the *transparent-getter* lift restoring form-4 parity for the idiom's property shape is recorded as liftable). Dropped without relocation, each recorded: former §24.1's placement rule (now just §9.7's owned-field rejection in records/enums); §24.1's and §24.7's modifier prose (each declaration's own grammar governs); §24.7's static-events-on-interfaces exclusion (dissolves — a static interface property is unspellable under §11.8, and a static method returning `ISharedEvent<T>` is an ordinary contract); the four-field `ClickedEvent` variant of former §24.2 (a duplicate declaration once the pattern example declares the three-field form). One wording correction rode along, recorded: former §24.5 called snapshot-raise "the same posture as" §29.12's loud-mutation discipline; stdlib §6.5 now states the true relation — both make mutation-during-traversal defined and loud, but the responses differ (snapshot vs fault), because a self-unsubscribing one-shot listener is a legitimate idiom while mid-iteration collection mutation is a bug.

<a id="sec-operators"></a>
## 14. Operators

<a id="sec-++-and-are-statements"></a>
### 14.1 `++` and `--` are statements

Statement-only, no expression form. `i++` vs `++i`, `arr[i++]`, sequence-point confusion, undefined behavior — none of those exist in Bezel because `++` produces no value. The cases where the old or new value is genuinely needed are clearer when written explicitly.
