**And not overloadable (proposal 0122 L4).** `++`/`--` are built-in statements over exactly the operand types §6.6's compound form admits — integer, floating-point, `decimal`, `char` — and a type advances by `x := x + 1` under its own `operator +` or by a named method — the posture §25.1 takes for `not`, and the shape the standard library's own `ISteppable<T>.Step` takes. A user `operator ++`/`operator --`, with `x++` meaning `x := T.operator++(x)`, would be a second desugaring beside the built-in `x := x + 1`, with its own ownership corner — a class-typed `++` producing a fresh owned value into an owned field or an owning slot, rejected into a borrowing slot — that arises nowhere else in the language; the spec needed three paragraphs of §17.6 for it while the overload existed (through v0.288). The removal was the re-measure `DECLINED.md`'s bitwise-overloadability row invites each subtraction pass, and its grounds were tested one by one: the shared `OpToken` cost is real and was paid; the compound-assignment ground does not reach `++` (it is not a compound assignment); and no rationale ground becomes incoherent (§14.4's `~` argument concerns the binary bitwise set, whose keep stands). Reopening bar: a real program whose advance cannot be spelled as `x := x + 1` or as a named method.

<a id="sec-mandatory-parentheses-on-mixed-precedence"></a>
### 14.2 Mandatory parentheses on mixed precedence

Mixing bitwise (`&`, `|`, `^`) with equality, relational, or logical operators in the same expression is a syntax error. `a & 1 = 0` doesn't compile; write `(a & 1) = 0`.

The rule costs nothing on programs that mean what they say and catches the most common precedence pitfalls inherited from C. Shift mixed with arithmetic is the same: `a << 2 + 1` is rejected; parenthesize.

**Range sits in the bitwise band (level 6).** High precedence (just below Primary) would force explicit parentheses around any arithmetic in range bounds; the lowest binary level would break `score in 60..100` and similar `in`-with-range idioms. Level 6 lets both arithmetic-in-bounds (`0..n+1`) and `in`-with-range (`x in a..b`) parse without parentheses, and a bitwise-with-range mix falls to the mandatory-parenthesization rule above (spec §17.2).

<a id="sec-comparison-operators-must-be-defined-together"></a>
### 14.3 Comparison operators must be defined together

`<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` together. `=` and `<>` together. Languages that derive `>=` from `not (a < b)` get the NaN cases wrong. Forcing each to be defined explicitly keeps the semantics explicit.

<a id="sec-not-is-not-user-overloadable"></a>
### 14.4 `not` is not user-overloadable

`not` is the boolean negation operator, valid only on `bool` (spec [§6.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-bitwise-operations)), and it is not among the user-overloadable operators ([§25.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-overloadable-operators)).

The reasoning: `not` reads as a boolean operation. A reader seeing `not x` expects `x` to be (or to behave like) a `bool`. Overloading `not` on arbitrary types would invite confusion — three-valued logic, set-complement semantics, "negate" as in "produce additive inverse" all have superficial appeal, but each interpretation departs from the boolean reading in ways that are easy to misread. Domain-specific inversion is better expressed by a named method (e.g. `.Negate()`, `.Invert()`, `.Complement()`), which makes intent explicit at every call site.

The bitwise complement operator `~` remains user-overloadable. Types that have a meaningful bit-pattern inversion or set-complement operation can overload `~`; types with logical-style negation should provide a named method.

**The binary bitwise operators (`&`, `|`, `^`, `<<`, `>>`) stay user-overloadable (the 2026-08 subtraction review, proposal 0121 §2).** No stdlib type overloads them and no client exists yet, but the keep is structural three ways: `~`'s own ground names the *set-complement* role, which is incoherent without union and intersection beside it; removal would touch the shared grammar productions (Appendix A's `OpToken`/`IfaceStaticOperator`) and permanently strand the compound-assignment desugar for `&=`/`|=`/`^=`/`<<=`/`>>=`; and proposal 0078's checked-enum investigation found, in its words, that "no flags-enum idiom exists in the language or library" — its recorded flags idiom is const integer masks, which need no overloadability — so the keep is structural rather than client-anticipating; one lit guard (#2162) pins the enum-operator composition 0078 left compile-time-unaffected. Re-measured each subtraction pass.

<a id="sec-no-implicit-conversion-operators"></a>
### 14.5 No implicit conversion operators

Implicit conversions make overload resolution unpredictable, hide allocation costs, and let inappropriate values silently flow into APIs. Bezel has no `implicit` keyword — and no `explicit` one either: spec §25.4 admits no user-defined conversion operators at all, implicit or explicit. A conversion between user types is written as a named factory or projection method.

**Why named methods (spec §25.4).** The conversion is visible at the call site (`dto.ToUser()` rather than a cast), and its name can communicate intent (`FromDecimal`, `ToReadOnly`) where a cast cannot. Overload resolution does not have to consider user-defined conversions: the conversion is a regular method call under ordinary resolution. And the pattern composes with the rest of the language (extension methods, fluent chaining) with no conversion-specific machinery. C# supports both `implicit` and `explicit` user-defined conversion operators. The implicit form is widely considered a misfeature in retrospect, even within the C# community: it makes overload resolution opaque and lets unintended conversions slip into expressions silently. The explicit form is less harmful but offers no advantage over a named method with the same compile-time check. Both are omitted, consistent with spec §5.1's rule that an implicit conversion exists only where no programmer-visible information is lost, and with §6.2's prohibition on implicit `decimal`/binary-float mixing.

**Overload resolution tie-breaking (spec §22.6).** An exact match is preferred over any implicit conversion, but built-in implicit conversions (§5.1) are not ranked against one another, so a call that reaches two overloads only through (equally-ranked) conversions is ambiguous and is a compile error citing the candidates. The programmer disambiguates with an explicit cast. The rule is deliberately conservative: §5.1 catalogs the implicit conversions without ranking them, and the tie-break adds no ranking of its own.

We considered importing C#'s finer "better conversion target" ordering (narrower target preferred, so `int→long` would beat `int→float`). We declined it: §5.1 deliberately admits an implicit conversion only when no programmer-visible information is lost (or, for wide-integer-to-float, when an explicit cast would be unjustified noise), and it places every numeric widening in one undifferentiated row — including the precision-losing `int→float`. Ranking those conversions would be net-new semantics layered on top of §5.1, not a clarification of it. The conservative rule keeps faith with Bezel's "explicit over implicit" stance (the same principle that omits user-defined conversion operators, §25.4) and with the existing treatment of overloaded method references (§27.6), which already makes the absence of a unique winner a compile error citing the candidates. The cost is that a few calls that a C#-style rule would silently resolve are instead flagged for an explicit cast; that is the intended trade. A §5.1 constant narrowing is one of those conversions ([#6570](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6570)): a constant that fits a narrower parameter makes the candidate applicable and converts there, so `M(3)` against `M(byte)` beside `M(short)` is flagged for a cast where C# resolves it to the narrower target — the same trade, at the one place a "narrowed constant is exact" reading would have reintroduced the declined ordering: graded exact, the narrowed candidate beats every widening one (`M(byte)` over `M(int)` for a `ushort` constant `3`, C#'s narrower-target preference) and ties with the identity candidate.

<a id="sec-compound-assignment-narrows-back-checked"></a>
### 14.6 Compound assignment narrows back, checked

`b += 10` on a `byte` is the one place every C-family language needs an exception to its no-implicit-narrowing rule, because §6.2-style promotion makes the natural desugaring `b := b + 10` ill-typed. C#'s answer is a hidden cast that silently truncates in unchecked contexts, guarded by an RHS-convertibility test to bound the damage. Bezel's answer (§6.6) is the same hidden cast made *checked*: the write-back raises `OverflowException` out of range, so the only loss is range and range loss is loud — which is why Bezel does not need C#'s guard and admits a runtime `int` RHS (`acc += n`), the same posture as §6.1's checked `a + b`. The line is drawn at the integer family (plus `char`): a binary-float or `decimal` result against a narrower LHS is rejected outright, because §5.2's float narrowings round or truncate *silently* — `b += 1.5` adding-then-flooring is exactly the invisible loss §5.1 exists to exclude, and the explicit `b := byte(b + 1.5)` spelling is the visible version. The carve-out is scoped to the compound form; plain assignment keeps the strict rule everywhere.

<a id="sec-shift-counts-checked"></a>
### 14.7 Shift counts are checked, not masked

The rule (spec §6.5; proposal 0079, owner-ruled 2026-08-11, from the elegance review): the count's domain is `0..width−1` of the left operand's type; outside it, a constant count is a compile error and a runtime count raises `OverflowException` — the same choice as 0078's enum-cast fault and 0086 item 1's range-step fault, one ruling at three sites. Deliberate modular counts are spelled visibly: `v << (k & 31)` — one `&`, in-domain by construction, and the reader sees the masking instead of having to know it.

The C-family alternative reduces the count modulo the left operand's bit width — `1 << 33` on `int` silently computes `2`, a count of `-1` masks to `31` — and is defended by "a shift never raises (it is a bit operation)." That sentence conflates two different things. *Bits shifted out of the left operand are discarded*: that *is* the operation, exactly as `&` discards bits — correct, silent, and kept. *A count outside `0..width−1` silently reduced modulo the width*: that is not the operation — the count is a *quantity* selecting an operation, not a bit pattern being operated on, and quantities out of domain are exactly what the checked posture faults on. `a + b` overflowing raises (§6.1); `a / 0` raises; `byte(300)` raises; a masked `1 << 33` would return `2`. Masking is inherited from C-family hardware behavior, and the honest content of a rationale entry defending it would be "C# did it." Constant evaluation (§17.14) would compound it: it *knows* the count is out of domain and would compute the masked value anyway. *(Historical note: Bezel masked through v0.241 — the earlier #1094 record settled only which operand's width masks, and negative counts were discussed nowhere — and `const Flags: long := 1 << 64;` folded to `1`.)* The precedent survey found the post-C trend uniformly away from silent masking — Rust's checked semantics panic on over-shift (masking only in the unchecked-release concession; Bezel's always-checked §6.1 takes the checked branch of that fork unconditionally), Go faults on negative counts and never masks, Zig types the count at `log2(width)` bits, Swift reserved masking for a separately-spelled `&<<` — and no surveyed language chose silent masking on the merits rather than inheriting it. Codegen cost is honest: LLVM shifts are poison at counts ≥ width, so the backend already spent an unconditional AND on every non-constant shift; the checked rule replaces it with one perfectly-predicted compare+branch, and in-domain constant counts now compile to the bare shift with zero checks. **Declined:** Zig-style typed counts (needs `u5`/`u6` widths the type system should not grow for one operator); Go/Swift-style total non-masking semantics (silently value-producing for what is almost always a bug); faulting on negative counts only (halves the inconsistency, no principled line); a warning instead of an error for constant counts (§17.14's posture: constant-evaluation faults are never deferred). Deliberate modular *bit motion* has named homes in the accepted `BitOperations` toolkit (proposal 0008's rotate/wrapping family) — the same operator-strict/method-explicit split as §6.1 arithmetic.

**The same posture at two more operands and one literal ([proposal 0208](proposals/0208-constant-operand-faults-index-divisor-open-range.md)).** The constant-operand rule — a written operand that fixes the outcome decides the fault at the site, never at run time — reaches conversions (§5.2), enum casts (§5.4), shift counts (§6.5) and whole constant expressions (§17.14), and three more sites whose fault is equally certain: a constant index outside an inline array's `0..<N` (spec §15.1.2 — the extent is in the type, and the same fold that elides an in-range check proves the out-of-range one), a constant zero divisor of `/` or `mod` with a non-constant dividend (§6.1, §6.4 — the dividend's value cannot save it, exactly as a shifted value cannot save an out-of-domain count), and an open-ended range literal written as a `for` source (§16.5 — no execution reaches the body). Each is a compile error at its site; a non-constant index or divisor, and a range reached through a variable or a call, keep their run-time faults. Declined: a constant index into a `const string` (a value fact, not a type fact; zero sites), keeping the open-ended literal at a warning (a mandated warning as the end state of a program that cannot run is the inversion rationale §5.28 reserves warnings against), and widening the literal rule to every iteration context reached through calls (`(10..).Where(…)`: the predicate would read into stdlib semantics the spec states for `GetEnumerator` alone).

<a id="sec-decimal-from-binary-float"></a>
### 14.8 `decimal(x)` reads the shortest round-trip form

A binary float has three candidate decimal values: its exact binary expansion, a fixed-precision rounding of it, and its shortest round-trip form, the digits its no-format `ToString()` prints (stdlib §2.18). Spec §5.2 takes the third, then rounds as §4.4 rounds a literal: `decimal(x)` is the value the `decimal` literal written with `x.ToString()`'s digits denotes. The conversion therefore agrees with what a program already shows for `x`, and `decimal(0.1)` is `0.1`.

The survey (the owner's, 2026-09; sources not re-fetched). Java's `BigDecimal.valueOf(double)` converts through `Double.toString`'s canonical form, which its documentation calls generally the preferred way; `new BigDecimal(double)` gives the exact expansion, with a documented warning. Kotlin's `Double.toBigDecimal()` and `Float.toBigDecimal()` convert through `toString`. Go's shopspring `decimal.NewFromFloat` uses the shortest round-trip digits. Python's `Decimal(float)` gives the exact expansion (`Decimal(0.1)` is `0.1000000000000000055511151231257827…`), and its documentation flags the surprise. .NET's `(decimal)double` rounds to 15 significant digits (7 for `float`), which collapses distinct doubles (`(decimal)(0.1 + 0.2)` is `0.3`) and differs from .NET's own shortest-round-trip `double.ToString()`.

**Declined:** the exact expansion, which puts 28 digits of binary noise into every non-dyadic value; a fixed significant-digit rounding, which needs a precision constant beside the one `ToString()` already defines and maps distinct doubles to one decimal. The shortest form is injective on doubles, so distinct doubles of magnitude at least 10^−12 give distinct decimals. Below that, §4.4's scale bound of 28 merges values, as it must for any rule. The rounding there reads the shortest form, not the binary expansion, so `decimal(2.5e-28)` is a tie that goes to the even `2e-28`, as the literal `2.5e-28m` does.

<a id="sec-binary-float-parse-range"></a>
### 14.9 Binary-float `Parse` faults where the literal does

`double.Parse` and `float.Parse` read a numeral at run time, and stdlib §9.2 gives them the literal's reading: the value rounded once to the type as spec §4.3 rounds a literal, with `OverflowException` exactly where that rounding is not finite. So `double.Parse("1e999")` faults where the literal `1e999` is a compile error, `TryParse` answers `false` there, and `Convert.ToDouble` follows by stdlib §2.3's mirror sentence. Three grounds carry it. One digit string gets one verdict, in source and in text. The `Parse` families fault alike: the integers (stdlib §9.1) and `decimal` (stdlib §9.3) already raise `OverflowException` where the number spelled does not fit the type. And input that spells a number the type cannot hold is a data problem, which the stdlib's strict-fault model (stdlib §3.2) reports rather than substitutes. Spec §5.2's per-target-family split, under which `float(d)` overflows to `±Infinity`, governs converting a value already in a binary type, as §6.3 governs arithmetic on one. `Parse` converts no value: it reads a numeral, and §4.3 declines to let a numeral denote an infinity. Underflow is in range, as for a literal: a numeral that rounds to zero or to a subnormal denotes that value.

The survey (the owner's, 2026-09; sources not fetched). .NET's `double.Parse` returns `±Infinity` on overflow since .NET Core 3.0, and raised `OverflowException` before it. Java's `Double.parseDouble` and Python's `float` return the infinity. Go's `strconv.ParseFloat` returns `±Inf` together with an error whose `Err` is `ErrRange`, and C's `strtod` returns `±HUGE_VAL` and sets `errno` to `ERANGE`. Bezel sides with the earlier .NET contract, and in substance with Go and C, which report the range error rather than hide it. Having no out-of-band error slot, it reports through the fault, and `TryParse` is the branch-shaped tier.

**Declined:** `±Infinity`, the majority's answer, which makes one digit string a compile error as a literal and an infinity as text, and needs a binary-float carve-out in stdlib §2.3; and the infinity with a range error beside it (Go's shape), which needs an error slot the `Parse` family does not have and would be a second mechanism for one fault condition (stdlib §1's no-dual rule).

**The input grammar.** `Parse` reads a `double` literal's shape with a sign before it: digits on both sides of any point, an optional signed exponent, and no whitespace, digit separator or hexadecimal form (stdlib §9.2). It is the integer grammar (stdlib §9.1) carried to a fraction and an exponent, as strict as the integer and `decimal` grammars (stdlib §9.1, §9.3) on whitespace and separators, and every rendering the no-format `ToString()` produces for a finite value lies inside it, so stdlib §2.18's round trip needs nothing wider. The narrow grammar is the compatible starting point: admitting `".5"` or `"0x1p3"` later widens what parses, while retracting either would break programs. An input outside the grammar spells no number, so its `FormatException` comes before any range question. `decimal.Parse` reads the same shape without the exponent (stdlib §9.3), so no numeric `Parse` admits a bare point.

**JSON.** `ReadFloat` takes `Parse`'s reading and range, raising `SerializationException`, the deserializer's fault, where `Parse` raises `OverflowException`: a document never reads back a non-finite value `WriteFloat` refuses to write (stdlib §2.15). A `float` component narrows `ReadFloat`'s `double` as spec §5.2's `float(x)` does, and faults where a finite number does not fit (spec §9.8). That rounds twice. A single rounding of the text to `float` would need a second float read in the deserializer vocabulary (stdlib §2.14), which every `IDeserializer` implementation would then have to supply, or a JSON-private path that synthesis cannot name for other formats. The two readings differ only for a number whose `double` rounding lands exactly on the midpoint between two adjacent `float` values (or between `float.MaxValue` and the overflow threshold), where the second rounding's tie goes to even and the first rounding's direction is lost. The difference is one `float` step, in range or at the edge: `1.0000000596046447753906251` is `1.0000001` to `float.Parse` and `1` in a `float` component, and `3.4028235677973366e38` is `float.MaxValue` to `float.Parse` and a fault in a `float` component. A number `WriteFloat` writes for a `float` value never meets the case, since its digits read back to exactly that value, which the second rounding leaves unchanged; only a number written for some other value can.

**Declined:** `strtod`'s wider input (hexadecimal significands, a bare leading or trailing point), which no literal, rendering or JSON number uses and which would tie the contract to the C library; `±Infinity` from `ReadFloat` and from a `float` component, which would let a document decode to a value `WriteFloat` rejects; and one rounding of the JSON text to `float`, for the vocabulary cost above.

**Non-finite spellings.** Every rendering of a non-finite `double` or `float` — the no-format `ToString()`, an interpolation hole, every floating specifier — spells it `NaN`, `Infinity` or `-Infinity`, and `Parse` reads back exactly those three (stdlib §2.18, §9.2). They are the names of the constants a program already writes (`double.NaN`, `PositiveInfinity`, `NegativeInfinity`) and the spelling spec §4.3 uses for the values, so the text a program prints is the text its author reads in source and documentation; .NET's invariant culture renders the same three tokens. A specifier lays out a number's digits, and a non-finite value has none, so `F2`, `E3` and `P` add no zeros, exponent or `%` to it: one value has one spelling whatever format a hole names. A NaN renders `NaN` whatever its sign and payload: those bits are not part of a number's value, `=` treats every NaN alike (spec §4.3.1), and `BitConverter` (stdlib §2.12) carries them where they matter. `Parse` matches the three exactly: the round trip needs only the rendered spellings, and admitting another later would widen what parses without breaking a program. The finite grammar's one case-insensitive point, the exponent's `e` or `E`, is the literal's own (spec Appendix A's `FloatLiteral` admits both), while no source spelling of `NaN` or `PositiveInfinity` varies in case. `Boolean.Parse` (stdlib §9.4) accepts `true` and `false` in any case and is not the model here: case-insensitive matching would admit spellings no rendering produces, and it remains available later as a widening that breaks no program.

**Declined:** the C library's lowercase `nan`, `inf` and `-inf`, which `%g` produces and no Bezel source spells; `strtod`'s spellings in `Parse` (`infinity`, `nan(…)`, any case), which would tie the contract to the C library, as the grammar above declines for hexadecimal; case-insensitive matching of the three tokens, `Boolean.Parse`'s posture, for the reason above; and `+Infinity` beside `-Infinity`, which no rendering produces — the sign §9.2 admits before a numeral belongs to the numeral grammar, and the non-finite tokens stand outside it.

<a id="sec-strings"></a>
## 15. Strings

Strings appear in nearly every program. The choice of representation affects memory footprint and indexing performance, and the obvious choices each have a real cost.

Three options were on the table:

- **UTF-32, fixed width.** Simplest implementation, O(1) indexing trivially, but pays 4× memory for ASCII text.
- **UTF-8 without O(1) code-point indexing.** What Rust, Go, and Swift do. Smallest memory, efficient sequential operations, but indexing the i-th code point becomes O(i), and the API needs cursor types and view-based abstractions to stay usable.
- **Adaptive width (PEP 393).** Per-string storage of 1, 2, or 4 bytes per code point, chosen at construction. O(1) indexing always; ASCII pays one byte; the representation is invisible to programmers.

Bezel picks PEP 393.

The Swift route was attractive on technical merit but requires a substantial design package — substring views, cursor types, multiple abstraction levels — that Bezel doesn't have time to do well in this revision. Without that package, plain UTF-8 with O(i) indexing puts too much friction on basic string code.

A two-types route ("normal `string` is UTF-32; `Utf8String` is the memory-efficient form") fragments the ecosystem. Every API author chooses a side and every consumer pays for conversions across the boundary.

PEP 393 hides the choice in the runtime. The user model is "string is a sequence of code points indexed in O(1)." The implementation picks 1, 2, or 4 bytes per string. ASCII text pays one byte; mixed-BMP text pays two; only strings with code points above the BMP pay four. Indexing is one branch on the kind, reliably predicted. CPython has thirteen years of experience showing this works.

`char` remains 32 bits — the right type for a code point. `s.IsAscii` gives FFI and IO a runtime predicate to skip encoding work for the dominant case. The char-level non-allocating substring view is `ReadOnlyStringSpan` (stdlib [§3.3](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-readonlystringspan)) — a record carrying a borrow of the string. There is no byte-level view over ASCII strings (`ReadOnlyAsciiByteSpan` is retired, proposal 0038): a borrow-bearing record cannot cross the FFI boundary (spec §32.5 admits only the §32.13 C-compatible records), and the span-destination encoding (`Encoding.GetBytes(s, destination: Span<byte>)`, stdlib [§3.2](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-encoding)) serves the allocation-conscious byte path for every string, not only ASCII ones. Byte access from strings routes through `Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes` in either its allocating one-shot or its buffer-reusing span-destination form. See rationale [§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans) for the full design story.

<a id="sec-convert-is-a-familiarity-alias"></a>
### 15.x `Convert` is a familiarity alias (#1356)

`Convert`'s string↔primitive members exactly duplicate the §9 primitive `Parse`/`TryParse`/`ToString` members. Ruled: kept, explicitly as a C#-familiarity alias — the duplication is documented rather than deleted (deleting would break arriving muscle memory for zero space savings), and `Convert` gains no cross-primitive conversion role: value conversion is the §5.2 checked cast, bit reinterpretation is `BitConverter.Reinterpret` (the recorded split), and `Convert` stays a thin naming veneer over Parse/ToString.

**`Convert.ToString` takes a `float` ([#5500](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5500)).** Without a `float` overload, `Convert.ToString(0.1f)` resolves to `ToString(double)`, `float → double` being its one applicable conversion (spec §22.6), and renders `"0.10000000149011612"` where `0.1f.ToString()` is `"0.1"`: a veneer whose stated contract is the argument's own `ToString()` text (stdlib §2.3) would give a different string for one primitive, and give it silently. `ToString(value: float)` makes the call an exact match, so the equation holds. §15.y's line admits the member on this section's own ground: .NET declares `Convert.ToString(Single)`, and the reader arriving with it expects `"0.1"`. Two alternatives are declined (owner decision). *Keeping the five overloads* leaves the silent divergence. *The rest of .NET's set*, `char` and the narrow integers (`sbyte`, `byte`, `short`, `ushort`, `uint`, `ulong`), has the same arrival ground and is declined on its cost: each such call is a §22.6 ambiguity today, a compile error rather than a wrong string, whose remedy, the value's own `ToString()`, is one call, so the seven overloads would be duplicates that repair nothing. The `float` overload is admitted for the silent divergence it removes, not for arrival alone.

**`Path` is kept on this section's arrival ground.** Stdlib §7.4's six `Path` members (`Combine`, `GetFileName`, `GetExtension`, `GetFileNameWithoutExtension`, `GetDirectoryName`, `GetFullPath`) are the string-level path operations a file-touching service needs, carrying .NET's names and meanings so arriving code reads as written. The set is kept as it stands; a further member is added on a recorded need, not for parity with .NET's wider class.

<a id="sec-stdlib-regularization-v0290"></a>
### 15.y Inherited duplicates are tolerated, new ones are not (proposal 0123)

§15.x's ground is *arrival*: `Convert` stays because deleting it would break muscle memory that .NET readers bring with them, and that ground extends no license to a duplicate that serves no arriving habit (proposal 0074 §7 drew the line, and measured it — 38 in-tree occurrences kept `Guid.NewGuid`). The 2026-09 regularization (proposal 0123) applied the line with counts. **Retired:** `Type.Implements(iface)` — the reflective conformance test has one spelling, `iface.IsAssignableFrom(t)` (0 call sites anywhere; the lattice was unimplemented); `Stopwatch.Restart()` — `Reset(); Start();` (1 test call site); `String.IsNullOrEmpty(value: string?)` — `value = null or value.IsEmpty` (3 test call sites); and the static `String.Compare(a, b[, ignoreCase])`, whose only capability the instance surface lacked — case-folded ordering — is the instance `CompareTo(other, ignoreCase)` beside `Equals(other, ignoreCase)` (12 test lines migrated). **Kept, with the count that keeps each:** `Stopwatch.ElapsedMilliseconds` (19 test / 6 doc / 7 src occurrences — a client), `CancellationTokenSource.IsCancellationRequested` (the reason stands in stdlib §6.x beside it), and `Decimal.Zero`/`Decimal.One` (21 in `tests/`, above the bar that separates them from `ElapsedMilliseconds`; the `int.Zero` asymmetry is real and is an alias pending a re-measure, not a decline).

Two regularizations ride the same principle from the other side. *Overload pairs for an optional trailing argument* were a second idiom for a shape the document already spelled with §22.5 defaults (`Increment(key, delta := 1)`, `token: CancellationToken := CancellationToken.None` at seventeen sites); nine such pairs — thirteen rows — are one defaulted signature each, and stdlib §1 states the rule so it does not regrow. The shapes that keep a pair are an interface-required member beside its widened twin (`CompareTo`/`Equals` with `ignoreCase`), because §11.2's exact-match conformance fixes the shorter signature, and an omitted argument with no nameable default — the to-the-end window forms, and the JSON facade's `JsonOptions`. *`Counter<K>` spelled key membership `ContainsKey` alone* — a name minted with no .NET antecedent, on the one map-like type on which `key in tally` therefore did not compile; it is `Contains(key)`, and stdlib §1 records that `ContainsKey` is the dictionaries' inherited alias and is not minted on new types.

*`Math.Sign` is one generic.* `Sign<T>(x: T): int where T: INumber<T>` replaces four per-type overloads by the principle that retired `Sum`'s (stdlib §5): the per-type forms predated §2.19's abstraction, and `Sign(decimal)` was a symmetry completion added fifty-two revisions after `INumber<T>` shipped. The return type is `int` at every substitution, so no call changes meaning and `DECLINED.md`'s result-type block on `Average` does not reach it; the two costs are stated — `pure` is lost until `INumber<T>`'s members are `pure`-declared (recorded as the follow-on, beside `Min`/`Max`/`Clamp`), and a `char` argument, which converts co-equally to all four per-type parameter types and so was a §22.6 ambiguity under them, is rejected by the `INumber<T>` bound instead (0 sites). **`Abs` stays generic-free** for the reason the same trace exposes: `Abs<T>` returns `T`, so `Math.Abs(floatVal)` — `double` under the per-type overloads, `float → double` being its one applicable §5.1 conversion — would infer `T = float` and change its result type at every `var` and every follow-on (a `short` or `sbyte` argument, co-equal across all four and so a §22.6 ambiguity, would instead newly compile); that is the very mapping the `Average` row records as blocked, and a per-width return-type change with the `pure` loss is not what this evidence earns.

<a id="sec-instance-member-shadows-extension"></a>
### 15.z `OwningSet<T>` pops rather than takes: an instance member shadows a same-named extension

`OwningSet<T>`'s transfer members are `Pop`/`TryPop`, not `Take`/`TryTake`, and the ground is measured shadowing rather than taste. Under language spec §26.5 rule 1 an instance member blocks a same-named extension by name, so an instance `Take(query: T)` on the set made LINQ's `Take(count: int)` unreachable on an `OwningSet<T>` receiver — permanently, for every caller. `Pop`/`TryPop` un-shadow it (`set.Take(2)` resolves to the lazy operator) and put the member in the transfer family the documents already teach: `Get`/`Peek` borrow, `Pop`/`Drain` transfer, which `OwningStack<T>` and `OwningQueue<T>` already spell.

`OwningDictionary<K, V>` keeps `PopValue`/`TryPopValue` with its own reason recorded at stdlib §4.9. `OwningSet<T>` has no such ambiguity — its element and its query are the same type and the same thing — which is why its member can lose the qualifier.

**`CountWhere` is declined, evidence-gated** ([proposal 0098 §6](proposals/0098-stdlib-surface-program.md)). The same block-by-name rule (stdlib §1, *Block-by-name*) makes LINQ's predicate `Count(pred)` unreachable on every receiver that declares or inherits a `Count` member, as it does the `Contains`, `First`, `Last`, `Reverse` and `ToArray` operators on receivers declaring or inheriting those names. A per-name workaround tier — a `CountWhere` beside the blocked `Count(pred)` — fixes one name of six while leaving `FirstWhere`/`LastWhere` undeclared; the one-call composition `coll.Where(pred).Count()` is already idiomatic; no corpus use or filed issue evidences the friction; and the recorded general remedy is the extension-call-disambiguation deferral ([§18](#sec-things-deferred)). The reopening bar: field evidence that the collision costs real users real time on the `Count`/`Contains` names specifically — at which point the question is the `*Where` tier or the disambiguation syntax, not `CountWhere` alone.

<a id="sec-ffi"></a>
## 16. FFI

<a id="sec-hand-written-declarations-not-bindgen"></a>
### 16.1 Hand-written declarations, not bindgen

Auto-generated bindings (Rust's `bindgen`, Python's `ctypesgen`, SWIG) parse C headers and produce language-side declarations automatically. Bezel's FFI is manual: the programmer writes the Bezel-side signature, and the compiler trusts it — the same hand-declared model as Python's `ctypes`.

Auto-generated bindings tend to expose every quirk of the C API; hand-written declarations let the programmer expose only what's needed, with the names they want. The cost is some typing per imported function. A bindgen tool could generate `extern` block source code in the future; nothing precludes that.

<a id="sec-distinct-types-for-cstring-wstring-rawbytes"></a>
### 16.2 Distinct types for `CString`, `WString`, `RawBytes`

C strings, UTF-16 strings, and arbitrary byte buffers have different ownership stories, different encoding assumptions, and different valid operations. Conflating them — as `void*` does — pushes the burden of remembering which is which onto the programmer at every call site. Distinct types let the compiler refuse calls that mix them up.

<a id="sec-typed-pointers-not-marshal"></a>
### 16.3 Typed pointers, not an untyped `Marshal` surface

Foreign memory access (spec §32.12) had two candidate shapes: a Marshal-style static class (`Marshal.ReadInt32(p)` / `WriteInt32(p, v)` over bare `IntPtr` and byte offsets) and typed pointer wrappers. `Ptr<T>` won on §16.2's own argument: an untyped read surface is `void*` with method-name suffixes — `Marshal.ReadDouble(p)` compiles whether `p` came from `sqlite3_column_blob` or `__errno_location`, while `Ptr<double>` vs `Ptr<int>` moves that mistake to compile time. Element-scaled `LoadAt`/`Add` also delete the classic FFI bug class of hand-computed `i * sizeof(T)` with a platform-dependent `sizeof` — the compiler that knows the target stride computes it. The cost was weighed honestly: a generic intrinsic record family with a per-instantiation gate is more machinery than fourteen static methods; §16.2's recorded value judgment outweighs the surface count.

Also declined, with reasons. **Operator pointer arithmetic** (`p + 1`, `-`, `<`): named methods keep the element-scaling in the method's one-line contract, and C itself makes ordering over unrelated allocations undefined; nothing in the binding corpus (sqlite3, curl, BLAS, POSIX) needs them. **`Ptr<Ptr<T>>`**: every `T**` shape in the corpus is expressible as `Ptr<IntPtr>` plus one visible `FromIntPtr` re-typing per indirection level; nested pointee gating buys generality nothing needs. (A record wrapping `Ptr<T>` is itself a valid pointee via the single-field rule — a sanctioned reconstruction of typed double indirection where a binding genuinely wants it, not a loophole: it carries a declared type at every level.) **Panama-style checked foreign memory** (bounds/lifetime-carrying handles): the checking would be exactly as good as the trusted declarations it derives from; `RawBytes.FromForeign(p, length)` *is* the length-carrying form, opt-in where a length exists, and a general checked tier would promise verification §32's trust posture cannot deliver.

<a id="sec-instance-state-and-foreign-callbacks"></a>
### 16.4 Instance state and foreign callbacks: three tiers

A bare C function pointer cannot carry a bound method: a static reference or a non-capturing lambda is all it can hold. Stopping there would leave C's ubiquitous user-data idiom without a typed home, and the owner's question ("marshal an object method that can update the class object it comes from, the way C# does") deserved a designed answer. The answer is three tiers keyed to when native may invoke:

- **Pull** (call-shaped APIs): an ordinary instance method makes the extern call and writes results into `this` from returns, boundary `out`/`ref` (spec §32.5), and `Ptr<T>` reads (spec §32.12). Most of the question dissolves here; nothing about §32 prevents it, and boundary `out`/`ref` and `Ptr<T>` reads supply the expressiveness it needs.
- **Call-bounded push** (`qsort_r`, `sqlite3_exec`): every invocation is bracketed by one synchronous extern call, so the callback's environment is structurally alive — its captures live on the calling frame, and extern bodies cannot suspend. §28.14's call-bounded caller rule ("any closure … every captured source trivially outlives the call") transfers across the boundary with one substitution: the callee obligations, compiler-verified in-language, become §32.10 documented trust. The `env` clause (spec §32.8) declares the two ABI facts no analysis can infer — which parameter is the `void*`, and which callback position receives it — and the compiler synthesizes the trampoline.
- **Retained push** (curl's split registration, buses, signal handlers): native holds the pointer past the registering call; no frame bounds the environment, so no lifetime-checked admission can be sound. The registry idiom (spec §32.8: static callback + integer token + `Locked<OwningDictionary<…>>`) makes the safety structural — synchronized state, token indirection (a stale token is a graceful miss, not a use-after-free), wrapper-ordered teardown with the blocking-acquisition discipline stated.

**Why `env` and not `scoped`** — the natural-looking reuse, declined on three grounds: (i) it would give one keyword opposite meanings — in-language `scoped` marks the *storage-bounded* tier (the callee may retain, bounded by an object), while the extern clause marks the *call-bounded* tier (the callee must not retain); (ii) `scoped` is illegal on extern for a reason that still holds — storage obligations and quiescence are unverifiable across the boundary — and reusing it would reverse that rule; (iii) the marker's grammatical job is ABI slot designation, not a lifetime tier — the paired parameter's lifetime tier is the plain call-bounded default it already has. `ctx` (what C calls the slot) lost to `env` (what Bezel puts there — the closure environment). Slot *inference* (no marker at all) is impossible: `sqlite3_exec` has two pointer-shaped candidate slots, and the callback-side position genuinely varies (first for `sqlite3_exec`'s callback, last for `qsort_r`'s comparator) — both associations must be declared.

**Why the retained tier is a documented idiom, not machinery**: every candidate mechanization (a `registry`-typed extern clause, auto-minted tokens, a compiler-managed handle table) re-derives exactly `Locked<OwningDictionary<…>>` with less visibility, adds grammar for what ten lines of ordinary code state, and forecloses per-binding policy (token reuse, multi-registration, what a "miss" means). It lands as normative guidance text so it is teachable, citable, and testable — the same status as §32.6's ownership patterns.

**The C# `GetFunctionPointerForDelegate` shape** — minting a retained (thunk, environment) pair as an `IntPtr` — is declined: it is precisely a retained environment with no bounding frame, i.e. the storage-bounded tier the boundary cannot verify. C# affords it because the GC roots the delegate via `GCHandle` and the *programmer* remembers to free it — the remembered-convention shape §28 exists to replace. The address-minting surface (`Interop.CodeAddress`) stays static-only so no retained pair is mintable.

**The checked-mode thread guard** rides §28.2's deterministic-backstop posture: foreign-thread invocation of an `env`-paired callback is undefined behavior, and checked mode converts the thread-axis violation into a deterministic abort via a per-activation record in the environment block (correct across concurrent calls and reentrancy; alive exactly while sanctioned invocations are). The temporal axis — invocation after the call returned — deliberately gets no promised backstop: the record the guard would read is itself dead at that point; an implementation may poison the environment block at call exit as a quality-of-implementation strengthening. A recorded asymmetry, not a silent one.

<a id="sec-c-compatible-layout-promise"></a>
### 16.5 The C-compatible layout promise

Struct marshalling (spec §32.13) enters through §3.6's derived-property mechanism because "passable to C" is exactly a structural predicate; §3.6 itself mandates that form. The layout promise is the language's first normative representation guarantee, so its scope is the design's load-bearing feature: classified records get the target C ABI's layout; every other record keeps unspecified layout, preserving future representation freedom.

§3.6's reservation clause ("dispatch of behavior on a derived type property is reserved to compiler-known standard-library types") was engaged head-on rather than lawyered around. A static representation guarantee attaching to *user* records purely by structure is more than admissibility, even though nothing executes differently by classification. Two readings existed: (a) representation is not "behavior", clause untouched — defensible on the letter but eroding the clause's evident purpose (keeping derived properties from silently acquiring semantic weight on user types); (b) the clause needs a scoped, visible exception. Reading (b) was taken and ratified together with the promise; neither stands without the other.

What the promise deliberately excludes: platform-independence (sizes and layouts vary as C's do; *which* C struct matches which platform is the programmer's trusted declaration — glibc vs macOS `struct addrinfo` is the canonical divergence, and each platform gets its own declaration); unions and bitfields (no structural spelling survives §3.6's computed-never-declared discipline, and bitfield layout is ABI-quirky; the workarounds are recorded in the spec text); by-value passing (Stage D: frontend ABI classification — eightbyte splitting, INTEGER/SSE classing, hidden `sret` — is genuinely large, per-target, miscompile-prone work, and the by-address subset unblocks the whole binding corpus except `div_t`-style small-struct returns).

Two recorded deviations-with-reasons ride this area. First, the inline-array element set was extended to the full FFI-compatible scalar set *uniformly* — direct extern parameters and record fields alike — one deliberate step past accepted proposal 0033 §4.1's letter, which committed only the field position: pointer-sized scalars pack exactly as primitives do, and the asymmetry ("`[16]ulong` legal as a `SigAction` field, `[2]IntPtr` illegal as a parameter") would have been a rule with no rationale. (`[N]R` arrays of C-compatible records stay excluded as a recorded liftable bound, spec §32.14.) Second, a plain inline-array extern argument passes by address with no call-site marker, deviating from the marked-mutation doctrine (spec §22.3) — deliberately: by-address is the *only* passing mode an inline array has at the boundary (C's array-to-pointer convention), so there is no unmarked-by-value alternative for a marker to distinguish, and a mandatory marker would mark nothing.

<a id="sec-variadic-imports-per-arity-aliases"></a>
### 16.6 C-variadic imports as per-arity aliases

§17.9's `params` decline is engaged, not contradicted, by spec §32.11's `variadic(N)` clause: no Bezel-side method becomes variadic, no allocation is hidden, and every call site has fixed, declared arity — what changes is only the emitted *calling sequence* for a symbol that is variadic on the C side. The clause exists to close a real trap: binding a variadic C symbol with a plain fixed-arity extern is undefined behavior on ABIs whose variadic callees consult a caller-set register (SysV x86-64's `%al`), and it often works until it doesn't — the worst failure shape. The compiler cannot detect the mismatch (the symbol's C-side arity is invisible), so the spec documents the trap loudly and provides the sound spelling.

Promotions are a compile error, not an implicit conversion: C promotes `float`→`double` and small integers→`int` in variadic slots; Bezel requires the *declaration* to carry the promoted type, so the promotion is visible where the contract is written. One recorded extension past accepted 0033's promoted-type list: single-integer/pointer-field records (spec §32.13's typed handles) are admitted in variadic positions — such a record marshals as its promoted scalar field, so the slot accounting is unchanged, and a typed handle in a `curl_easy_setopt` slot is the natural exemplar. **True call-site varargs** are declined for v1: they need a new call-arity rule and per-call-site promotion checking, and the measured corpus (curl_easy_setopt, open, fcntl, ioctl — all fixed small arities behind one symbol) is fully served by per-arity aliases; recorded as the v2 candidate if real bindings show alias proliferation.

<a id="sec-deferred-ffi-surface-reasons"></a>
### 16.7 Deferred FFI surface, with reasons

Spec §32.14 lists what stays out of the FFI; this section records the reasoning that must survive the deferral.

**Export-to-native**: three blockers — symbol-export/C-ABI entry generation belongs to the module/build layer (#1365, proposal 0034); a foreign process entering a Bezel `.so` on an unregistered thread needs a runtime-initialization story §32.10's foreign-thread obligations presuppose rather than provide; and the binding corpus (0007/0011/0014 and the review exemplars) contains no consumer. **`FnPtr<F>`** (calling pointers received from native) is the single most dangerous primitive in this space — it executes, not merely corrupts — and `Interop.CodeAddress` is one-way precisely so this stays independently deferred behind its own evidence bar. **Stage D by-value structs** wait for a dedicated review cycle with per-target ABI-classification tests (§16.5).

**`ForeignHandle<T>`** (the `GCHandle` analog: object↔`IntPtr`) was evaluated and deferred on four grounds: (1) the pinning half of `GCHandle`'s job does not exist here — Bezel heap objects never relocate, so the handle would provide only address minting plus liveness discipline; (2) the liveness discipline it offers is *weaker* than the registry idiom's — a stale handle resolve is silent undefined behavior, a stale registry token is a graceful miss under a lock, so for retained callbacks (the only tier that wants it) the idiom strictly dominates on safety at the cost of one dictionary lookup; (3) it would break the **no-object-address invariant** (spec §32.12): the only addresses in the language are buffer interiors, foreign values, and code — object identity stays outside the address space, load-bearing for §30.14 and for the unforgeability of §28's dispose-through-borrow rules; (4) the reopening condition is recorded — a measured per-invocation registry cost that matters, or a native API that requires the user pointer to be the object itself — and reopening takes its own small proposal. **The recorded direction for handing a Bezel object to native code**, should the question recur (proposal 0102 §5): for the callback tier — the only tier with a live use case — the shipped mechanism is the `env` user-data marker (spec [§32.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-callbacks)), which makes the callback parameter capture-admitting and lets the runtime recover the function value from the opaque `env` slot without the programmer ever seeing, minting, or storing an object address; for the retained-object tier, the direction is the **registry token** — an integer key into a Bezel-side registry consulted under a lock, whose stale resolve is a graceful miss rather than undefined behavior. Both preserve the no-object-address invariant of spec [§32.12](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-foreign-memory), which is ground (3) above; a proposal that wants the handle must first say what it does with that invariant.

**Declaration-bound destructors** (`extern owned handle Sqlite3Db closes sqlite3_close;`-style) are declined with 0033 §7.2's three reasons: the pattern it automates already works today in ten lines (probe `p9_wrapper`, exit 0); real bindings grow a wrapper class anyway (the destructor is three of its lines); and foreign destructors have *policies* a bound function name cannot express (`sqlite3_close` returns `SQLITE_BUSY`; `fclose` can fail and lose data) — a destructor body expresses the chosen policy, a `closes fn` clause silently discards every return code.

**Startup symbol resolution** (spec §32.11) is pinned eager — every failure before `Main` — with the cost recorded: absent conditional compilation (0034) and runtime loading, a module declaring any platform-absent symbol makes every program linking it unrunnable on that platform even if the call is never reached. Fail-fast was chosen because extern declarations are per-platform artifacts already and a probe-for-availability binding wants conditional compilation, not late failure; the item reopens alongside 0034.

<a id="sec-things-explicitly-rejected"></a>
## 17. Things explicitly rejected

<a id="sec-truthiness-in-conditions"></a>
### 17.1 Truthiness in conditions

`if (user)` is forbidden. Conditions must be `bool`. Truthiness collapses zero, empty string, empty collection, and null.

<a id="sec-string-concatenation-with-non-strings"></a>
### 17.2 String concatenation with non-strings

`"x = " + 42` doesn't compile. Use interpolation: `$"x = {42}"`. Implicit `.ToString()` calls hide allocation behavior.

<a id="sec-goto"></a>
### 17.3 `goto` — declined; labeled `break`/`continue` adopted instead

`goto` remains declined. Multi-level loop exit is labeled `break`/`continue` (spec §21.5; proposal 0050): a loop may carry a `name :` label, and `break name;`/`continue name;` target it. Labeled break keeps the structured subset of goto's power — forward-only, cannot enter a scope, cannot form a loop, target named on a construct the jump is inside — so every scope-exit obligation stays statically enumerable and the disposal story needed zero new rules (the jump reuses the multi-scope unwind edge `return`/`throw` already defined). The closers follow the named-construct rule (`end scan;`); a labeled loop's keyword closer (`end for;`) is rejected per that rule's own "otherwise". Declined alongside goto: numeric break levels (positional targets are fragile under edit) and labeling arbitrary statements (dead weight — only loops are targets). Extraction into a method remains good style where the inner logic deserves a name; it is not the only spelling.

<a id="sec-default-interface-methods"></a>
### 17.4 Default interface methods

Interfaces have only signatures. Default interface methods (C# 8+) blur the line between interfaces and abstract classes and create surprising behavior around explicit implementation. For shared logic, use an abstract class.

<a id="sec-member-hiding"></a>
### 17.5 Member hiding

See [§8.1](#sec-member-hiding-is-forbidden).

<a id="sec-inline-fixed-size-value-type-array-fields"></a>
### 17.6 Inline fixed-size value-type array fields

*A reversed rejection, kept here as its record.* Inline arrays `[N]T` are a first-class type. They can appear as locals, as class fields (including with owned slots via `[N owned]T`), and as record fields when the slots are not `owned` (records hold only copyable value types, so move-only `[N owned]…` inline arrays are not admitted as record fields). A class can have an inline-array field whose storage is part of the class's layout, equivalent to C#'s `fixed` or Rust's `[T; N]`. See language spec [§15.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-inline-arrays) for the type form and [§15.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-move-only-value-types) for the move-only category that supports inline arrays with owned slots. *Historical note:* through v0.97 this entry rejected the feature on the ground that the use case is rare; experience and the v0.97 verification rounds suggested that inline arrays' value extends beyond fixed-size buffers into FFI marshalling, packed records, and as the foundational storage primitive over which owning collections compose, and v0.98 provided them. The entry stays in §17 as the register's precedent that a rejection can be reversed (`DECLINED.md`).

<a id="sec-combined-visibility-modifiers-protected-internal"></a>
### 17.7 Combined visibility modifiers (`protected internal`)

C#'s `protected internal` (union: visible to derived classes *or* same-module code) and `private protected` (intersection: visible to derived classes *only within* the same module) are not provided. Most C# developers cannot state the semantics of either without looking it up. Bezel's four levels — `public`, `internal`, `protected`, `private` — are individually clear and cover the majority of needs. The intersection case (framework-internal override points) is a real gap, but the workaround is acceptable: use `internal` and rely on code review for the "only subclasses should call this" convention. If implementation experience shows the pain is worse than expected, `protected internal` meaning the intersection can be added as a purely additive change in a future revision.

<a id="sec-readonly-local-bindings-let-val-readonly-var"></a>
### 17.8 Readonly local bindings (`let`, `val`, `readonly var`)

Many languages provide a way to declare a local variable that cannot be reassigned after initialization: `let` (Swift, Rust, JavaScript), `val` (Kotlin, Scala), `final` (Java). Bezel's readonly local is `const`, and there is no fourth local-declaration form.

**Local `const` accepts any initializer, classified by form (spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-const-locals); proposal 0049 train A).** A §17.14 constant expression of primitive/`string` type keeps compile-time-constant semantics; everything else is a *runtime const*, a single-assignment binding excluded from compile-time positions. The central ruling is ownership: a runtime `const` rejects owned-producing initializers, keeping the clean division — `using var` is the owned, scope-pinned binding with declared disposal; `const` is the non-owned one whose bindings never change state (not by assignment, consumption, or disposal). The permissive owned-`const` alternative was declined as an undeclared-disposal duplicate of `using var`. The checkable payoff is §7.4's guarantee: a `const` local's narrowing is never invalidated — the invalidation forms are all compile errors on a `const`, so the guarantee costs no new analysis.

`var` is always reassignable. `using var` is non-reassignable and carries disposal semantics; per spec §28.10 a `using var` whose RHS is value-typed, runtime-managed, or a borrow has a no-op disposal, which makes it an *admitted* (if unidiomatic) readonly-local spelling (review finding #1360). That admission is tolerated for generic and desugaring uniformity, not endorsed: `const` is the readonly-local idiom. There is no `let`, `val`, or `readonly var`.

The omission of a fourth form is a cost-benefit judgment. The bug class prevented by readonly locals — accidental reassignment of a local that should have been stable — is real but small relative to the complexity budget. Most locals in well-written code are single-assignment regardless of enforcement, and the ownership system ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows)) already tracks local-variable state far more aggressively than mutability alone. Adding a fourth local-declaration form (`let`, `readonly var`, or similar) would increase the decision burden on every local declaration without a proportionate safety payoff.

C# has lived without readonly locals for 25 years. Java added `final` locals but few teams enforce their use. The practical evidence is that this is a nice-to-have, not a must-have.

<a id="sec-variadic-parameters-params"></a>
### 17.9 Variadic parameters (`params`)

There is no `params` keyword. A method that accepts a variable number of arguments uses `Array<T>` and the caller writes a collection literal:

```bezel
public method Log(messages: Array<string>) ...
Log(["hello", "world"]);
```

The brackets at the call site are syntactic friction but they make the allocation visible. A `params` mechanism that silently constructs an array from bare arguments hides allocation behavior — exactly the kind of implicit cost that Bezel's design philosophy aims to surface. The brackets also make the call site unambiguous when reading: `Log(["a", "b"])` is clearly one argument of type `Array<string>`, while `Log("a", "b")` under a `params` rule could be a two-argument overload or a params call, depending on what's declared.

<a id="sec-auto-ranked-extension-specificity"></a>
### 17.10 Auto-ranked extension specificity (the deleted §26.5.1 rules 2 and 3)

Extension-member resolution ranks equally-applicable candidates by one specificity relation (spec §26.5.1): subtype ordering on receiver-substituted heads over applicable candidates (rule 1). A collision rule 1 does not order is the §26.5 rule-4 ambiguity error, including every one the deleted rules would have ordered. Two further relations were in the spec through v0.185 and are deleted (proposal 0039, accepted 2026-07-12): a recursive more-concrete ordering on declared generic heads (rule 2 — `extension IEnumerable<int>` auto-beating `extension IEnumerable<T>` for an `Array<int>` receiver), and constraint-implication ordering (rule 3 — a `where`-constrained block beating a less-constrained same-head block when every constraint of the loser is "implied by" the winner's).

The case against them was cost without a user. Rule 3's "implied by" was an entailment judgment whose semantics the spec never defined (is `where T: Dog` implied by `where T: Animal`? does implication chain through `where T: U` parameter chains, or through interface inheritance?) — the only constraint-entailment reasoning anywhere in the language; constraint *satisfaction* (spec §14.2) checks concrete arguments at instantiation, a different and decidable question. Rule 2 required a second ordering relation besides subtyping — a recursive concreteness comparison — plus a clarification that rule 1 compares receiver-substituted heads while rule 2 compares declared heads: a two-views subtlety that existed only to keep the two rules from contradicting each other. Neither rule was ever implemented — the reference compiler applied first-declared-wins (#1307), so the spec's own worked example silently returned a declaration-order-dependent answer — and neither was ever exercised: the complete stdlib inventory (proposal 0039 §5, independently re-derived by its critique pass) shows every same-name pair across overlapping stdlib extension blocks differs in arity, so applicability alone decides every stdlib call and zero call sites resolve through rules 2–3. Deleting them also shrank #1307 from "implement a recursive ordering plus an entailment judgment" to "implement subtype ranking plus the rule-4 diagnostic".

What is lost is auto-ranked specialization: a generic block plus a same-head specialized (rule 2) or extra-constrained (rule 3) block sharing one member name and signature, the special form selected automatically. The remedies are the ones the language already records: keep one block and dispatch internally; split the blocks across modules so clients import one; or export the specialization under a distinct name — spec §22.6's posture that when same-name declarations cannot be soundly auto-ranked, the answer is distinct names, the convention the standard library uses everywhere. The terminal state — a rare, loud, remediable ambiguity error — is the terminal state §18's extension-call-disambiguation entry already records as acceptable for extension collisions.

The deletion is the cheap-to-reverse direction, and the re-addition condition is recorded: if demonstrated demand for auto-ranked specialization ever materializes, rule 2 alone may return as a compatible widening — the rules applied in order, so a restored rule 2 would only convert former ambiguity errors into resolutions and could not re-route any program that resolves under rule 1 alone. No re-addition condition is offered for rule 3: it would return with its unspecified entailment judgment, the worst item on the ledger.

<a id="sec-defer-errdefer"></a>
### 17.11 `defer` / `errdefer` statements

A statement registering an action to run at scope exit (`defer`), or only on the failure edge (`errdefer` / `scope(failure)`). Declined on three grounds, one per half of the design (proposal 0099, charter ruling C5).

**(1) The value half is redundant.** Bezel is affine: every owned value's disposal is already scheduled by the binding form that introduced it ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows), [§28.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-using-var)), and the tighter scope a `defer` would emulate is `do … end` ([§21.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-do-end-blocks)). Languages that need `defer` for cleanup need it because their cleanup is not attached to values; ours is. Adding `defer` would give every resource two spellings for one obligation — the both-ways surface the marker discipline is built to avoid.

**(2) The ordering half is already provided, on one stack.** `finally` composes with `using var` and with owned-local discharge on a single establishment-ordered cleanup stack: a `finally` is *"established at entry to the protected block … on the single establishment-ordered cleanup stack it ranks before anything the block itself establishes"* ([§31.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-try-catch-finally)). A `defer` would be a second registration mechanism ranking on the same stack — new syntax for an existing position.

**(3) The failure half needs a primitive the language deliberately lacks.** `errdefer` / `scope(failure)` must ask, at scope exit, *"are we unwinding?"* — D's `scope(failure)` is defined in terms of it and C++'s `std::uncaught_exceptions()` is its canonical spelling. Bezel exposes no such predicate, and adding one would make a program's meaning depend on the ambient unwind state at a point where nothing names an exception — the opposite of the exception model's design, in which the only ways to observe an in-flight exception are `catch` (which names its type) and `finally` (which does not, and therefore cannot branch on it). The commit-flag guard is the sanctioned spelling, and the standard library packages it as `ScopeGuard` (stdlib [§2.21](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-scopeguard)): the flag is a field, the "are we unwinding?" question is replaced by "was `Dismiss()` reached?", and the answer is a fact about the program's own control flow rather than about the runtime's.

**Reopening condition:** field evidence from external users that the `ScopeGuard` spelling is systematically forgotten or mis-placed in a way a statement form would prevent — together with a proposal for the uncaught-exception predicate that names its interaction with `finally`, nested unwinds, and async continuations. Absent that predicate the failure half is not designable, whatever its ergonomics.

<a id="sec-contract-carrying-type-aliases"></a>

### 17.12 Contract-carrying type aliases

An alias declaration carrying a predicate — `type Port = int requires(value in 0..65535)` in sketch — so that a constrained scalar can be spelled once and reused, the Ada constrained-subtype shape. Declined as spelled (proposal 0106 §6.2(a)), on three grounds, any one of which is fatal.

**(1) An alias sees through, so the constraint would buy the wrong half.** A type alias is *"a pure synonym — it does not create a new type"* (spec §27.8): identity, equality, dispatch, and overload resolution all read the aliased type. A constrained alias would therefore deliver range safety and not nominal distinctness — and distinctness is the half a services language actually needs, since the bug a wrapper prevents is passing a user id where an order id was meant, not passing 70000 where a port was meant. Bezel already prescribes the mechanism for the needed half: a single-field record (§27.8).

**(2) There is no boundary to check at.** Because the alias is transparent, no conversion site exists — spec §25.4 admits no user conversions at all, so the language has no position at which "entering the constrained type" happens. A check would have to be attached to assignment, argument, and return positions by a rule invented for this feature: precisely the parallel classification mechanism spec §3.6's closing rule forbids for anything that classifies types.

**(3) It could not appear in a public signature.** Aliases are module-internal, and exportable aliases (`public type`) are their own deferral (§18) inside the module-identity family. A constrained alias would be a validity guarantee that stops at the module boundary — the boundary where validation matters most.

Its useful content survives elsewhere: a validity predicate belongs to the *type* that carries the value, which is the record-invariant design spec §22.16 records as out of scope for this revision and proposal 0106 §6.2(d′) works out. **Reopening bar:** not new syntax for the alias, but new evidence against ground (1) — a demonstration that a transparent constrained type is what the language wants, which would first have to survive §27.8's see-through rule and §25.4's no-conversions posture.

<a id="sec-ambient-authority"></a>
### 17.13 Ambient authority: no object-capability discipline

Owner-ratified decline-with-reasons, 2026-08-20 (proposal 0106 §5bis, Q6). Bezel's I/O entry points are **ambient**: `File.ReadAllText(path)` is a static method on a static class (stdlib §7.3) and needs no capability object; any code that can name the type can reach the filesystem. Languages in the object-capability line (E, Monte, and Pony's authority side) make the opposite choice — no code holds authority it was not handed, so reachable authority is exactly what a caller passed in. The question had never been asked in any of the registers, and it is settled here rather than left silently open.

**Why declined.** Object-capability discipline is not a feature that can be added to a stdlib; it is a property of *every* entry point in it, and of the language's rules about how a reference is obtained. Taking it seriously would mean no static I/O surface anywhere, authority parameters threaded through every layer that touches the outside world, and a story for how a linked artifact obtains its initial authority — the blast-radius shape that declined user-facing allocators (§18) and a mandatory task-return marker (§12.10), here at a larger radius. What it buys — confinement of untrusted *code within one process* — is a guarantee Bezel has not undertaken to give: the compile-time guarantees are about memory and ownership, and §16's FFI posture already states that a program linking foreign code trusts it, since a single `extern` declaration voids the exactly-once guarantee for whatever it touches (spec §32.10). A capability discipline that stops at the FFI boundary would be a confinement claim the language cannot honour.

**What Bezel does promise about reach, stated because the supply-chain era makes the absence worth naming.** A linked artifact can reach every ambient entry point its imports name — that is the whole of the promise, and it is a small one. What legibility exists is at the *module* level, not the value level, and it is thin (spec §2.3): the *typed* foreign-memory surface (`Ptr<T>`, `Interop`) sits behind an import that is deliberately not auto-imported, but that import governs naming, not access. Use without import (spec §2.8.3 rule 2) and the auto-imported `System`-resident FFI-boundary types (`IntPtr`, `CString`, `WString`, `RawBytes`) each reach foreign memory with no such line, so the set of files performing raw access is not enumerable from the import graph. Anyone reasoning about what a dependency can do should audit its resolved callees, and should read neither the absence of a capability parameter nor the absence of an `import System.Interop;` line as a restriction.

**Reopening bar — narrow and named.** Not a general reconsideration of ocap, which would be a different language. What could reopen is a capability-scoped standard-library profile: a bounded, opt-in surface for the multi-tenant and plugin-host cases, where untrusted code runs in-process and the host must bound what it reaches, justified by real demand from such a host rather than by the general principle. Such a profile would still have to answer the FFI boundary before it could claim confinement.

<a id="sec-comparer-injection"></a>
### 17.14 Ordering comparers on sorted collections — and why equality-comparer constructors are not the same case

The sorted collections take no per-instance ordering comparer (declined on the single-order rule — equality, ordering, and hashing live in one place, on the type; the workaround is a wrapper record with the desired `CompareTo`). The hash collections *do* take `constructor(comparer: IEqualityComparer<K>)`. Read alone, the single-order rule indicts the constructors. The ruling that reconciles them: they are not the same operation. An `IEqualityComparer<K>` carries *both* facets a hash container consults — `Equals` and `GetHashCode` — as one object, so injection relocates the container's identity whole: the two functions that must agree — the facets the container consults — travel together, and the type's `CompareTo` is never consulted. (K's own `Equals` remains observable elsewhere, e.g. `DictionaryEntry<K, V>` record equality — the claim is about the container's lookups, not every use of K.) An injected *ordering* comparer would do the opposite: the container would find by the instance's order while the element's own equality stayed the type's — and since a sorted container's identity *is* `CompareTo`-equality (stdlib §4.18), the split would be directly observable in `Contains`/`TryAdd`. The single-order rule's real content is *identity must not fork*; the equality-comparer constructors satisfy it, the ordering injection would not. Both postures stand, and stdlib §4.8/§4.17 cite this record.

<a id="sec-things-deferred"></a>
## 18. Things deferred

Not in this version; the design is open to them in future revisions. An item leaves this list when it ships, and its record then lives in the section that owns the feature. Several of these were priced in study proposals rather than here — proposal 0061 (`docs/proposals/0061-expression-form-studies.md`) and the later `*-study.md` proposals — so a deferral scan of this list alone under-reads what has been examined; read the studies beside it.

- **Sum types beyond `choice`.** Closed nominal sums are the `choice` kind (spec §35; proposal 0041), with the three-way payload-derived ownership classification and static exhaustiveness by case name; the reasons and declined alternatives are [§9.6](#sec-choices-why-a-new-kind), which also declines structural/anonymous unions. What stays deferred is what proposal 0041 deliberately excluded, each recorded liftable there: GADTs, multi-level exhaustiveness counting, and `Result` combinators over owned payloads. The value-payload combinators and declared-result construction target-typing exist (proposal 0075 stages 1–2, spec §35.3, stdlib §2.20); the owning-payload form waits on ownership-polymorphic function parameters, recorded as 0075's stage 3(a).
- **Static interface member promotions.** Static interface members exist (spec §11.8: abstract-only, recurrence-constrained, with compiler-provided primitive conformances), and the `FromCollectionLiteral` promotion is made (stdlib §2.19's target pair; proposal 0125 Part A). Promoting `IDeserializable.Deserialize` to a static interface member is deferred (proposal 0125 Part B, #1507). De-intrinsicking `Range<T>` through an `ISteppable<T>` constraint is declined ([#1508](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/1508)): the admitted element set is a strict subset of the `ISteppable<T>` conformers, so the constraint cannot replace the intrinsic rule without widening it, and the widening is a deferred design change (`DECLINED.md`).
- **Trait/typeclass-style polymorphism.** Interfaces handle most of what traits do; the gap is associated types.
- **Const generics.** Generic parameters that are values, not types.
- **Units of measure at the type-level erased pole.** F#-style unit exponents carried in the type, checked statically, erased at runtime — `float<m/s>` distinct from `float<m>`, with the product of a `<m>` and a `<1/s>` inferred as `<m/s>`. Not in this version, and recorded here rather than in §17 because what stands in the way is unbuilt machinery, not a rejected idea (the placement rule the allocator entry below states). The design space has three poles and the entry reaches exactly one of them. *(i) Type-level erased units* — this entry: its two prerequisites are themselves deferrals on this list, associated types (the trait/typeclass entry above) and const generics (the entry above), since a unit algebra needs type-level exponents that can be added and compared; the standard library records the same wall from the other side, `Average` blocked because no constraint expresses its result-type mapping (stdlib §5). Spec [§14.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-recursive-instantiation) closes the route that would have avoided both: a *normalizing* encoding — one that reduces `Mul<Meter, Div<One, Second>>`, or proves `Mul<A, B> = Mul<B, A>` — is the expanding in-component instantiation shape §14.6 rejects at the declaration, and a non-recursive phantom encoding escapes that rule only by degenerating, since without normalization `Meter * Second` and `Second * Meter` are distinct types and every dimensional identity must be hand-declared. The consequences past the type system are the second half of the price: [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis) synthesis would see only the underlying `double`, [§32.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-c-compatible-records)'s typed-handle admission does not cover `double` single-field records, and an artifact interface would carry a unit-expression type argument whose textual identity is fragile ([§2.8.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-artifact-interface)). *(ii) Units carried by the value* — the dimension travels in the value, arithmetic checks at evaluation, conversion is an explicit boundary operation — needs no language change at all: a value record carrying its dimension, [§25](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-operator-overloading) operator overloads doing the check, and named conversion members, which is what §25.4's no-implicit-conversions posture asks for rather than a fight with it. Its single-dimension case is already shipped: `TimeSpan` (stdlib §2.5) names the unit in every producer (`FromSeconds` and kin) and every readout (`TotalSeconds` and kin), and declares its operators on the dimensionally correct split — `+`/`-` closed under `TimeSpan`, `*` by a scalar. *(iii) Nothing* — the status quo for every other dimension, and the honest description of most services code, whose unit bugs are duration and byte-size confusions at parse boundaries rather than dimensional algebra. **Reopening bar:** the two prerequisite deferrals landing first, together with a use case whose dimensions are not adequately served by pole (ii) — scientific or embedded computation rather than services work, which is the domain this entry's decline is scoped to. *(Proposal 0106 §1.2/§5.4/§6.2(c).)*
- **Reflection beyond `typeof`/`GetType`.** This splits into three pieces with different dispositions. (1) *Type-lattice metadata* — kind predicates, `BaseType`, `IsAssignableFrom`, enum introspection — is provided on the `Type` token (stdlib [§2.13](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-the-type-class)); it is named here only to complete the split. (2) *Member-access reflection* — field/method enumeration, by-name member access, dynamic instantiation — is declined, not deferred: it contradicts the ownership model (a reflective `owned`-member access has no static site to carry its contract) and is the maximal violation of the no-hidden-magic principle; see rationale [§5.18](#sec-type-identity-not-reflection). It is recorded as declined so the question is settled rather than perennially reopened. (3) *Generic-instantiation decomposition* (`GenericArguments`/`GenericDefinition`) remains genuinely deferred — it is useful but cannot be expressed without also adding an unbound-generic `typeof` (`typeof(Array<>)`), which is a language change; it is to be decided together with that syntax, not bundled into a metadata revision. Attribute-only reflection, if reflection pressure ever materializes, is the realistic future direction and is itself a separately-scoped question (the attribute-system entry below).
- **Macros / metaprogramming.** Source generators, compile-time evaluation, syntax-tree macros.
- **Custom collection-literal protocols.** User-defined classes opt into *sequence*-literal construction by conforming to `ICollectionLiteralTarget<TSelf, T>` (borrowing, `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>` parameter) or `IOwningCollectionLiteralTarget<TSelf, T>` (owning, `owned OwningArray<T>` parameter) — stdlib §2.19, language spec [§15.4.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-user-defined-sequence-literal-protocol). What remains deferred: a user-extensible *dictionary*-literal protocol — the `[k: v, ...]` paired form for user-defined map-like types. The built-in `Dictionary`/`OwningDictionary` keep the dictionary-literal form; user map-like types use ordinary constructors until the paired-factory protocol is designed.
