See rationale [§5.16](#sec-collection-literals-for-complex-collections).
- **Synchronization contexts for async.** Needed if Bezel targets desktop UI frameworks.
- **Module-private types.** A `module` modifier restricting visibility to a sub-module rather than the whole assembly.
- **Ref-returning indexers.** Would let `dict[key]++` be one operation instead of two. The language currently has no ref returns ([§22.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-no-params-no-ref-returns) of the spec); adding them is deferred.

  *(Joint note with axiom [A4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-axiom-register); proposal 0102 §4.)* A4 keeps accessor read faces lend-only, on the recorded ground that an accessor read has no marker position available — and A4 is kept: the accessor model is projections in all but name (a property or indexer read *projects* a view of the receiver's state with the mode fixed to "borrow", the write face spelled separately as `set(owned)`), and what A4 forbids is not projection but a *minting* accessor. What would reopen it is recorded here because it is the same design this entry needs: a projection form — a `subscript`-style member that yields a projection whose mode is declared at the yield, as in Hylo — manufactures exactly the missing marker position, and a mutable in-place projection *is* a ref-returning indexer while a transferring projection *is* the minting accessor A4 forbids. The two therefore reopen together or not at all: a proposal for either must address both, because a design that delivers one delivers the other's premise for free, and adopting one while declining the other would leave the language with a projection mechanism it refuses to use at half its positions. A third party rides on the same hinge: the accessor face's guaranteed-lend property is the load-bearing complement of the calls-mint/storage-lends doctrine ([§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends)), which the iterable unification (proposal 0096) completed by making the stored view's property face — A4's guarantee — the respell target, so a projection proposal must also confront the storage-lends half of that doctrine, not only A4's letter. Neither is proposed; the shape is recorded so a future proposal starts from the joint question rather than from the narrower one.

- **User-facing allocators and arena allocation.** A mechanism by which a caller supplies the allocator a callee's allocations draw from — an arena or bump allocator freed as a unit, a pool, a tracking allocator — as Zig's `std.mem.Allocator` parameter convention, C++'s allocator template parameters, or Rust's `allocator_api` provide. Not in this version, and the reason is that Bezel's memory model already collects most of the payoff without the mechanism. Allocation strategy is implementation-defined: nothing in this specification's companion says where a `new` expression's storage comes from, and nothing observes it. Because ownership is tracked at compile time and every owned value's disposal point is *statically known* (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows), [§28.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-reassigning-owned-fields)), an implementation is free to region-allocate a set of values whose lifetimes it can prove coincide and free them as a unit — arena and batch-free become an implementation optimization, invisible in the language, rather than an API the programmer routes by hand. What a *user-facing* allocator would cost is the reason it is not here: an allocator parameter infects every signature that allocates, transitively, so it reaches most of the standard library and most user code — the same blast-radius argument that declined a mandatory `owned` marker on task returns ([§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends)) — and it crosses `owned`-transfer boundaries, where a value allocated from one arena is transferred to a holder whose lifetime the arena does not bound, which is a second, *dynamic* lifetime discipline running beside the static one and answerable only by the programmer. **Reopening condition:** a real embedded or hard-realtime adopter whose target forbids a general allocator, or measured allocation-pressure evidence from a running Bezel service that implementation-side region allocation cannot address. Absent one of those, the mechanism buys placement control the language deliberately does not otherwise expose. *(Proposal 0102 §3, ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation; recorded here in §18 — not §17 — because the reopening condition describes an open design, not a rejected one.)*
- **Stack-allocated runtime-sized buffers (`stackalloc` analog).** `Span<T>` and `ReadOnlySpan<T>` are records carrying borrows of an `OwningBuffer<T>`, so spans view heap memory. What is deferred is a stack-allocated runtime-sized buffer (C# `stackalloc` analog) that would let spans view into stack memory rather than only heap memory. The use case is performance — avoiding heap allocation in hot paths like parsers. Bezel's existing inline arrays (`[N]T`, language spec [§15.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-inline-arrays)) cover the fixed-size case; a runtime-sized stack form would extend the same lifetime story to runtime-N. The design must address how stack-bound lifetimes interact with closures, exception unwinding, and recursive calls. Low priority — the heap-backed `OwningBuffer<T>` is sufficient for almost all use cases; the gap is real but narrow.
- **Spans over additional view sources.** Spans view three sources: an `OwningBuffer<T>` (`Span<T>`, `ReadOnlySpan<T>`), a string (`ReadOnlyStringSpan`, stdlib [§3.3](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-readonlystringspan)), and a sub-range of an `Array<T>` (`ArraySpan<T>`, `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>`, stdlib [§4.15](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-arrayspant-readonlyarrayspant)). Breadth across backings is one overload per span type: the shared read-only interface `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` is retired ([proposal 0175](proposals/0175-retire-the-read-only-span-interface.md)), and so is the byte view over ASCII strings (`ReadOnlyAsciiByteSpan`, stdlib §3.4, proposal 0038 — the allocation-conscious byte path is `Encoding.GetBytes` with a span destination). What remains deferred: `Span<T>` over an inline array `[N]T` — would need a new borrow-source kind that ties the span's lifetime to a stack frame, with rules for closure capture, exception unwinding, and recursive calls. Substantial design work for a narrow performance gain over the existing `OwningBuffer<T>`-backed form. Deferred — the five concrete span types (OwningBuffer-backed pair, ReadOnlyStringSpan, and ArraySpan-pair) cover the dominant use cases.
- **Move-only value types in `OwningBuffer<T>` / `Span<T>` / `ReadOnlySpan<T>`.** Buffers and spans admit value-typed T but exclude the move-only value types — both kinds of language spec [§15.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-move-only-value-types): the owning inline arrays `[N owned]T` and `[N owned]T?`, and the owning-classified choice (language spec [§35.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-choice-classification)). A buffer of move-only values would itself have move-only slot semantics — admissible in principle but requires a third category in the slot-semantics dispatch (class T, non-move-only value T, move-only value T) without an obvious motivating use case. Users who need a runtime-sized container of fixed-size owning arrays today wrap them in a class. Deferred until a motivating use case emerges.
- **Tuples and tuple destructuring.** A built-in anonymous-product type — `(int, string)` as a type, `(42, "hi")` as a value — with positional access and `for var (k, v) in dict do ...` destructuring in patterns and loops. This is an ergonomic convenience, not a capability gap: every use case is already expressible. Bezel's model uses positional records (language spec [§9.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-positional-records)) for value-only products, and *owning classes with multiple `owned` fields* for products that carry owned resources (a class may own its fields per [§28.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-fields); a method may return `owned C`; one `using var` discharges the whole aggregate, cascading to every owned field). The `OwningDictionary<K, V>.Drain()` case — once thought to need tuples — is handled by exactly this pattern via the `DrainedEntry<K, V>` owning class (stdlib [§4.9](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-owneddictionaryk-v)), with no language change. What tuples would add is purely notational: positional `(a, b)` destructuring sugar and one-shot anonymity, versus the named, documented shape that records and classes provide. Bezel's design philosophy already argues (records-vs-tuples) that a name and a documented shape is a feature, not a limitation; the same argument applies to the owned case. Class deconstruction (a `Deconstruct`-style positional surface for classes) was evaluated and declined for the same reason — see [§5.17](#sec-why-no-class-deconstruction). Tuples remain available as a low-priority future convenience; the structural-vs-nominal identity question and the named-vs-positional-fields question would need resolving, but neither blocks any program from being written today.
- **Post-condition loops (`do-while` / `repeat-until`).** A loop that executes the body at least once before checking its termination condition. The current workaround — `while true do ...; if not cond then break; end; end;` — is straightforward but heavier than the dedicated form would be. The workaround composes with flow narrowing: spec §21.3 reads a `while true` loop's exits as its `break`s alone, so a fact every `break` path establishes holds after the loop ([#6772](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/6772)). The open design questions are syntactic: a literal `do ... while (cond);` form clashes with [§21.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-do-end-blocks)'s `do ... end` blocks, so the addition would either need to disambiguate by the presence of `while`/`until` (which parses but reads ambiguously) or introduce a new keyword (`repeat ... until cond;` is the Pascal/Modula-2 form and would fit Bezel's overall vocabulary). The construct is deferred as a decision, not a promise: the payoff is the removal of one `if not cond then break; end;` line from an uncommon loop shape, and every candidate spelling has a real cost — a literal `do … while` collides with the block form, and `repeat … until` spends a new reserved word on that payoff. With the #745 block-form unification RFC declined (DECLINED.md D-0003), no syntax window is open for the form to ride. If it is ever adopted, `repeat StatementList until Expr;` is the presumptive spelling; until a stronger need is demonstrated, the workaround is the decision.
- **Multi-consumer channels / MPMC work queues.** `Shared<T>` exists, and so does the pipeline evidence the deferral once waited for (2026-07 review probe: a 3-worker pool needs one channel per worker, a hand-written sendable carrier class per argument tuple, and blind `i mod N` round-robin — a slow item pins its worker while the others idle; ~90 lines for a trivial pipeline), so the deferral stands on its remaining reason alone: receiver cloning is shared ownership of the *consume* capability, and `Shared<T>` does not supply it: §28.13 makes a handle sendable only over a payload in the `Shared`-payload synchronized set, and `Channel<T>` is not in it (its receiving end is single-consumer by ownership, stdlib §6.9). The honest design options are `Channel<T>` joining the §28.13 synchronized set with cloneable receivers, or a dedicated MPMC `WorkQueue<T>`. Until one is designed, the N-single-consumer-channels + round-robin pattern is the sanctioned pool idiom, its load imbalance acknowledged rather than hidden.
- **Exportable type aliases.** A module-level `type` alias is always module-internal (spec §27.8). Making aliases exportable (`public type`) is deferred to the module-identity work (proposal 0034's family): it touches artifact metadata, §2.7 resolution order, and the collision rules, while the workaround — the importing module declares its own alias — is one line.
- **Extension-call disambiguation syntax.** When two equally-specific extensions collide (spec §26.5 rule 4), the remedies are import surgery or a static-class helper method; there is no call-site form naming the providing module. A first-class spelling is deferred: extension blocks are nameless, so any design must qualify by *module*, dragging §2.7 chain-resolution changes with it — and the collision is rare, loud, and totally remediable today.
- **`owned` as a generic argument on user-defined types.** The library expresses owning-element semantics through separate named classes (`OwningArray<T>`, `OwningTask<T>`, `OwningDictionary<K, V>`, etc.); an `owned T` generic-argument modifier over a hard-coded opt-in list (`Array<owned T>`, `Task<owned T>`) was tried and retired ([§5.3](#sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements), [§5.11](#sec-owning-collections)). What remains deferred is whether and how user-defined generic types should be able to express owning-element semantics: a mechanism by which a user-written `Pool<T>` could declare an "owning T" variant and define the corresponding per-operation semantics (which methods accept owned arguments, which dispose on removal, which transfer ownership out on drain). The inline-array primitive (`[N owned]T?`) gives user-defined owning containers a foundation to compose over (see rationale [§5.14](#sec-inline-arrays-and-array-kind-split)), but expressing this with full generic-argument-level support is deferred.
- **LINQ over owned-element collections.** The standard library's LINQ extensions (`Where`, `Select`, `OrderBy`, `ToArray`, etc.) are declared on `IEnumerable<T>` with non-owning element semantics. Applying them to an `OwningArray<T>` and other `OwningX<T>` collections works today through the borrowing-iteration upcast — owning collections implement `IEnumerable<T>` and produce borrows on iteration — so `ownedArr.Where(predicate)` produces a borrowing pipeline. What is deferred is a parallel LINQ surface declared on `IOwningEnumerable<T>` that preserves ownership through the pipeline (e.g., `Where(predicate: (T) -> bool): IOwningEnumerable<T>`). The design question is whether to provide one method per operation that infers from receiver type, or to add a parallel set of named methods on `IOwningEnumerable<T>`. Code that needs to drain-and-transform today writes the drain loop explicitly.
- **Attribute / annotation system.** A C#-style `[Attribute]` declaration syntax, with attribute classes derived from a base type and reflective query at runtime. Useful for serialization metadata, framework integration, deprecation markers, validation rules, dependency-injection registrations, and other annotations whose effect is interpreted by tools rather than the language itself. Not in this version because most attribute *uses* depend on machinery that is itself deferred — reflection beyond `typeof`/`GetType`, dependency injection, test frameworks; serialization, the use most often cited, ships without attributes, through the closed `serialized` clause ([§5.19](#sec-serialization-without-reflection)), which a later attribute system could re-express mechanically. Designing attributes before designing how they are consumed would lock in syntax for an unbuilt feature, and that syntax would likely need to change once the consuming machinery exists. The corollary: any future attribute design will be tightly coupled to the reflection and serialization designs, and developing them together is the better order than attempting to retrofit one to the other.

  Two narrowly-useful capabilities can be added before the full system, if user demand emerges:
  - **`[Obsolete]`-style compile-time deprecation.** Achievable as a compiler-recognized declaration modifier (an `obsolete` keyword preceding a declaration, or a similar narrow syntax) without needing runtime reflection. The compiler emits a warning at every use site of an obsolete declaration; no runtime machinery required.
  - **Method-implementation hints** (`[MethodImpl]`-style: aggressive inlining, no inlining). Achievable as a small fixed set of compiler-recognized declaration modifiers. No runtime introspection required.

  These two narrow additions don't depend on reflection at runtime and could land independently of the broader attribute design. They are not committed for any particular revision; the deferred-list entry exists to mark the open question rather than to schedule the work.

  C# attributes have known costs that influence the deferral: attributes proliferate in real-world C# codebases (validation rules, serialization hints, ORM mappings, DI registrations stacking on a single class), they invite stringly-typed magic (attribute parameters often interpreted by reflection-based machinery with shallow type checking), and they blur the boundary between language semantics, framework machinery, and inert metadata (a reader cannot tell from an attribute alone what affects compilation, what affects runtime, and what affects nothing). Bezel has time to design around these costs rather than inheriting them.

<a id="sec-changelog"></a>
## 19. Changelog

The per-revision changelog — one entry per revision, the single narrative history of the normative documents — lives in its own companion, [`Bezel_Changelog.md`](Bezel_Changelog.md). Entries there are pinned history: written at their revision, normative for migration, and never the place to look up a current rule.

## 20. Closing

If the opening framed Bezel by what it borrows, the body has shown what it actually contributes: not the features — those have ancestors — but the discipline applied to combining them. The recurring move in this document is the same one every time a hard question arrived. Ship the part that fits the foundations cleanly. Decline the part that fights them, with the reason written down so the question stays settled rather than perennially reopened. Scope whatever genuinely remains open by naming its precise blocker, not by deferring it vaguely. Member-access reflection is declined, not pending. Serialization's hard questions were worked out in prose before their spec text, and when `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` changed a premise, the graph-shape keywords were retired rather than kept beside the type-driven reading ([§5.22](#sec-type-driven-serialization)). Tuples and post-condition loops are conveniences with recorded costs, not gaps. The deferred list is a list of decisions deliberately not yet made — each with its reasoning — never a list of unsolved problems.

That is the claim this document makes for the language: not that it is novel, but that every line in it was chosen rather than defaulted into, and that the choices cohere. The design is, by intent, complete — and it is no longer only on paper. A reference compiler now implements most of it, exercised by a test suite that takes its cases from the spec's own examples, and it has done exactly what a compiler was always going to do: surface concrete questions the spec did not anticipate. Those questions are answered the same way every question here was — the spec stays normative, every divergence is recorded and resolved as its own decision, and what the foundations already imply is determined before anything new is added.
